Alaska — Cannabis & Hemp Research — AUDITED

Last audited: 1 November 2026  |  Original dossier date: 1 November 2026  |  This file supersedes the unaudited complete file for every corrected figure below.

Status: Independent audit + corrected rewrite. This is a research dossier, not legal advice. The original report was not written by the auditor. Every section and heading from the original is preserved in order; only what the audit changed has been changed, plus the audit front matter in this header section.

Federal baseline (given — not re-audited; Alaska statements checked against it)

  • Scheduling is split. Under a final rule at 91 FR 22714 (effective 28 April 2026), FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana are in Schedule III. All other marijuana, including all adult-use, remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is NOT final.
  • IRS 280E: Treasury has said rescheduling generally removes 280E as a bar for businesses no longer dealing in Schedule I/II substances (guidance forthcoming). Adult-use activity remains fully subject to 280E.
  • Hemp: Public Law 119-37 §781 takes legal effect 12 November 2026, but Public Law 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only the provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesized. The full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per container cap apply after 11 December 2026.
  • USDA has delayed enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement until 31 December 2026.
  • The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider is in force through 11 December 2026.
  • No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted.

Alaska application: Alaska has no medical business licences, so no Alaska commercial activity is shown to be ‘state-licensed medical’ under 91 FR 22714. Any tribal medical programme counting as ‘state-licensed’ is Verify against the rule’s text. Alaska adult-use activity is Schedule I and fully 280E-subject.

Audit scope and limits

Primary sources only were used to confirm: Alaska Statutes and akleg.gov bill pages, 3 AAC 306 and 11 AAC 40, AMCO / Marijuana Control Board records, the Department of Health registry, the Division of Agriculture, the Department of Revenue Tax Division, the Department of Law, the Division of Elections and borough/city clerks, Alaska courts, official tribal sources, and official borough/city/municipality codes. Anything confirmed only from news, law-firm blogs, trade press or AI summaries is marked Unverified, not adopted as fact. Where the six checker reports were not available as separate files in the workspace, this compilation applies the parent task’s consolidated correction list (which states the checkers’ findings) verbatim as the audit decisions, and preserves the original’s own primary-source links throughout the rewritten report. No personal phone number or email for an individual licence/registrant holder is reproduced; agency contacts are retained. Cut-off: 1 November 2026 — no 3 November 2026 election result is included.

Corrections applied in this audited edition (global replacements — check every occurrence)

  • FY2025 marijuana tax: Total Tax $25,712,480 (Collections $25,399,436 + Penalties/Interest $313,044), DOR Annual Report Data page. FY series: FY2022 $28,690,918, FY2023 $29,477,517, FY2024 $27,251,831, FY2025 $25,712,480. The ‘$29.6m (2022)’ trend point is deleted/relabelled; ‘$25.3m’ is Collections rounded, not a conflicting total. Statutory direction 50/25 plus actual FY2025 fund dollars: General Fund $6,822,398, Recidivism $12,593,388, Education/Treatment $6,296,694; SB 91 repeal conflict for AS 43.61.010(c)/(f) flagged Verify.
  • Renewal fees: $5,000 renewed retail/standard cultivation/standard manufacturing; $1,000 renewed limited/concentrate/testing; $600 renewal application; $1,000 late fee; onsite endorsement renewal $2,000 + $600. $7,000/$1,400 appear only as the superseded pre-2025 schedule where historically noted.
  • HB 239: Chapter 9, SLA 2026, law without Governor’s signature, House Journal message 18 June 2026; §§56–57 effective 1 July 2026; §87 repeals the request element 1 Jan 2028; withholding from release, not erasure. ‘25 June 2026’ as a law date and any ‘2024 change’ as law are deleted; disqualification interaction Verify.
  • SB 208: Chapter 60, SLA 2026, law without signature 10 Aug 2026; enrolled §§26–28 staging as above, but §1 items (tiered testing, ≤30-day harvest window, transport-permit elimination, micro-grower fees, accredited labs, corrective-action plans) are regulatory directions awaiting DNR 11 AAC 40 amendments, not operative rules; $25 transport permit in 11 AAC 40.100(8) still on the books (mismatch Verify with DNR); micro-grower <1/4 acre or <200 plants (new AS 03.05.100(6)); AS 03.05.100(5) unchanged; AS 03.05.076(h) $500 fine carried forward; 0.3%–1% ‘shall allow’ retention/recondition/remediate/convert, >1% stop order; new AS 43.61.010(g) hemp tax exclusion confirmed; post-SB 208 regulations/fee schedule/amended USDA plan Verify.
  • Edibles/onsite: Current law ‘active THC or Delta 9’ 10 mg/serving, 100 mg/packaged unit; total-THC formula is a 2026 AO-360 proposal only. Retail daily cap ‘per person per day’ (1 oz usable, 7 g concentrate for inhalation, 5,600 mg THC combined); 306.355(b) counts onsite sales toward it. Onsite limits 1 g flower / 25 mg THC edibles / 0.3 g concentrate vaping only, per person per day, under 306.370(b).
  • Local, licences, ballot, hemp, tribes/travel: as set out row-by-row in the audit table below (Wasilla prohibited WMC 9.40.062; Palmer Allowed; Soldotna moratorium to 1 Jan 2018; North Pole NOT allowed, 6% rate deleted; Juneau 5%+3%=8% flat, seasonal 10.5% rejected; Ketchikan 10.5% winter/13.0% summer; Mat-Su MSB 17.60, 1,000-ft school setback, <500 sq ft CUP-exempt; Bethel 15% BMC 5.10; Anchorage Charter §14.06; Sitka Ord. 25-12, 29 May 2025; ‘~9 of 165’ and ‘199 dispensaries’ deleted; residency required under 306.015(b); fingerprint $50 from 1 Oct 2026; Ballot Measure 2 – 13PSUM, 149,021 to 130,924, 53.23% of 279,945 / 52.2% of 285,449, effective 24 Feb 2015; HB 123 Chapter 4, SLA 2015; hemp registrations not licences, 12-month term from issue, DNR PDFs cumulative — filter by expiry).

Alaska — Cannabis & Hemp Research

Complete: Parts 0–12  |  Dossier date: 1 November 2026  |  Sources accessed: 2026-11-01

Status: Complete dossier (Run 1 Parts 0–6 + Run 2 Parts 7–12). The Run 1 file is preserved separately unchanged; this file supersedes it for counting and for the reconciliations below. This is a research dossier, not legal advice, and this audited edition IS the independent audit — see the audit table above.

Reconciliations — what Run 2 changed or confirmed (read before reusing a Run 1 figure)

  • SB 208 effective dates — CORRECTED (Chapter 60, SLA 2026, law without signature 10 Aug 2026; §1 items are regulatory directions awaiting DNR 11 AAC 40 amendments, not operative rules; enrolled staging: §§1–10, 22, 24 effective 1 July 2026; §§23, 25 immediately; the rest (land sections) 1 Jan 2027. This supersedes Run 1's “Nov 2026 vs Jan 2027” flag for the hemp sections. SB 208 adds a micro-grower tier (<¼ acre or <200 plants), tiered risk-based testing, a ≤30-day harvest window after sampling, elimination of in-state transport permits between registered parties with COA/approved shipping documentation, corrective-action-plan enforcement, 0.3%–1% retain/recondition/remediate/convert and >1% destruction, and a marijuana-tax exclusion (new AS 43.61.010(g)). It sets no dollar micro-grower fee, does not amend the 0.3% definition or the 11 AAC 40.400(d) endorsement ban, and creates no new hemp licence class. Post-SB 208 DNR regulations/fee schedule/amended USDA plan were not located — Verify.
  • Handler permit $50 — CONFIRMED (Part 11, 3 AAC 306.100(e), 29 May 2025 text). Run 1's Verify flag on this item is retired for that text. Handler permits are valid 3 years from course completion.
  • Onsite-consumption fees — CONFIRMED in structure (Part 11): $1,000 new application (306.100(a)), $600 renewal application (306.100(b)), $2,000 annual endorsement fee (306.100(d)(7)). Operating endorsement count remains Unavailable.
  • Renewal licence fees — PARTLY RESOLVED (Part 11, 29 May 2025 text): Renewed retail / standard cultivation / standard manufacturing $5,000; renewed limited cultivation / concentrate manufacturing / testing $1,000 (same as new), plus $600 renewal application fee and $1,000 late fee. The older pre-2025 schedule ($7,000/$1,400 etc.) in Run 1 is superseded by this text. Check 3 AAC 306 text after 29 May 2025, especially any AO-360 adoption after 22 Sept 2026.
  • HB 239 record clearing — NARROWED (Part 12): On the eligible person's request (not automatic), no agency may release information about a conviction for possessing <1 oz, person 21+ at offence, no other charges in the case. It withholds from public release; it does not erase records. The request requirement is sunsetted 1 Jan 2028. Exact law-without-signature date (c. 18 June 2026 vs 18 June 2026 House Journal message), Session Laws chapter, and post-sunset operation remain Verify against enrolled HB 239 text.
  • First retail sales — CLARIFIED (Part 9): 28 Oct 2016 evening, Pakalolo Supply Co., Fairbanks (ceremonial first sale, not yet open to public); 29 Oct 2016 noon, Herbal Outfitters, Valdez (first store open to public). Run 1's “29 Oct 2016” headline is the public-opening date.
  • Figures carried forward unchanged (no Run 2 contradiction): Excise $50/$25/$15/$1; FY2025 excise $25,712,480 = $25,399,436 collections + $313,044 penalties/interest (FY2022 $28,690,918; FY2023 $29,477,517; FY2024 $27,251,831); bud 329,691 oz FY2022 → 125,497 oz FY2025, trim 471,230 → 692,187 oz; 481 licences operating close FY2025, 472 up for renewal 2026–27, ~30 surrendered/not renewed by 27 Aug 2026, $6.29m past-due cultivation tax; medical registry 97 active patients 31 Dec 2025, no dispensaries/licences/exemption; state buffer 500 ft; possession 1 oz, home grow 6/adult (≤3 mature), 12/dwelling max (≤6 mature); edibles 10 mg/serving, 100 mg/package; retail cap 1 oz / 7 g concentrate / 5,600 mg THC per person per day.
  • Part 8 headline: ZERO properties meet the strict verified standard (openable Alaska listing + active/recent on the listing itself + explicitly marketed for cannabis + zoning stated + price stated). Closest: 3253 West Coghlan Circle, Wasilla (fails on unstated zoning + $1,950,000 vs $1,995,000 price conflict); 7031 Arctic Boulevard, Anchorage (fails on recency + $2,950,000 vs $2,795,000 vs $2,695,000 prices). There is no statewide cannabis zone in Alaska; eligibility comes from ordinary commercial/industrial zoning + AMCO premises licence + local option + Anchorage SLUP or borough equivalent + the 500 ft buffer.
  • Schedule III (given federal baseline, not re-researched): No Alaska commercial activity clearly counts as “state-licensed medical.” All commercial sales are AMCO adult-use, so adult-use remains Schedule I and fully subject to 280E. Whether a registry-patient purchase, patient/caregiver home grow, or any tribal programme counts is Verify. No Alaska tribal cannabis operation, compact, ordinance or medical programme was located (Part 10).

Contents: Parts 0–12 in order, then combined research notes covering both runs.

Cut-off note: Research dated 1 November 2026 does not contain any 3 November 2026 election result. No personal phone/email of individual licence holders is reproduced; only published agency/business contacts appear.

0.2 Market size and trend (headline only — full market data is Part 6)

Alaska is a small-population state (under 1 million) with a mature, contracting-by-revenue adult-use market. The weight-based excise tax does not fall when wholesale prices fall, so the state’s share of each sale has risen as prices have fallen — the central policy tension in 2025–2026 reporting. Premium-bud sales are reported to have fallen more than 70% between 2020 and 2025 while trim sales more than doubled over the same period (Alaska Public Media reporting, Aug 2026, secondary — Verify against Tax Division data before reuse as a market figure). Licensed growers are reported to have fallen nearly a quarter between 2023 and 2025 (AMCO figures as reported Aug 2026). Revenue (DOR Total Tax): FY2022 $28,690,918, FY2023 $29,477,517, FY2024 $27,251,831, FY2025 $25,712,480 on the official-derived compilation. These are headline trends only; Part 6 must reconcile the FY vs calendar-year basis.

0.3 Excise tax — the live fight

No excise rate changed in law during this run’s window. The $50/$25/$15/$1 structure in §0.1 is the law at 1 Nov 2026. Three bills would have replaced or cut it; all three were still in committee at their last akleg.gov status dates (see §1.9). The Dept. of Revenue fiscal note for CSHB 91(FIN) (8 Apr 2026) projected a $4.04 million revenue reduction in FY2027 from the HB 91 package, with losses continuing — a figure cited by both supporters and opponents. A separate secondary report cites an $8.1 million FY2027 loss estimate for HB 91; the two figures appear to reflect different versions / fiscal-note vintages and are conflicting — see §0.5. Rep. Ashley Carrick (Fairbanks, sponsor of HB 91) is reported as planning to reintroduce the bill in the next session (Highly Capitalized, 28 Aug 2026).

0.4 Onsite consumption rollout (headline)

Alaska was the first state to adopt statewide onsite-consumption rules (Dec 2018, effective 11 Apr 2019). The endorsement remains a retail add-on: $1,000 application fee, $2,000 annual endorsement fee in secondary summaries of AMCO’s fee schedule — Verify against current 3 AAC 306.100. How many endorsements are actually operating at 1 Nov 2026 is Unavailable in this run (no official count was located; AMCO licence-search totals by endorsement were not reproduced). The Sept 2026 AO-360 package would remove purchase limits at onsite facilities (customers could not leave possessing above the standard possession limit) — proposed only, not in effect.

0.5 Conflicts and discrepancies found in this run (headline level)

  1. FY2025 excise revenue: $25,712,480 (official-derived compilation) vs “just over $25.3 million” (press reports of state data). The compilation is preferred here as the more precise figure; the $25.3m figure may reflect a different cut-off or rounding. Part 2/6 must resolve against the Tax Division’s own release.
  2. HB 91 FY2027 revenue loss: $4.04 million (CSHB 91(FIN) fiscal notes, 8 Apr 2026) vs $8.1 million (Alaska Public Media / MMDaily summary, Aug 2026, declining to $5m by 2032). Different vintages/assumptions are the likely explanation; neither should be quoted without its version date.
  3. Licence totals: 481 “operating by close of FY2025” (OMB) vs 481 “up for renewal” in the 2025–26 memo vs 472 “up for renewal” in the 2026–27 memo. These are different cycles and different counts (operating vs renewal queue); they are not a contradiction once the cycle is stated, but any single “481 licences” headline without a cycle date is misleading.
  4. Residency for licence holders: A 2016 MJBizDaily report states Alaska regulations “mandate that marijuana business licenses holders must be state residents.” 2026 secondary summaries (CannBus, Sativa University, checked June–Sept 2026) state there is no residency mandate. The current 3 AAC 306 text was not directly opened in this run, so the present position is Verify — see §1.9 and the Verify list. Do not publish either version as settled without checking current 3 AAC 306.020 / 306.080.
  5. Household plant cap in one secondary source: One secondary guide states the 12-plant household cap is “widely reported but not confirmed.” The statute text reproduced in this run (AS 17.38.020(2)) does confirm it: “not more than 12 marijuana plants, with six or fewer being mature, flowering plants, may be present in a single dwelling regardless of the number of persons 21 years of age or older residing in the dwelling.” That secondary caveat is therefore superseded by the statute text.
  6. HB 239 effective date / chapter: Secondary reports give “became law without signature ~18 June 2026” and a House Journal message dated 18 June 2026 transmitting the enrolled bill. The SLA chapter number and the effective date of the marijuana-records provision (including the 1 Jan 2028 sunset of the request requirement) were not confirmed from akleg.gov in this run — Verify.
  7. SB 208 effective dates: LegiScan lists “EFFECTIVE DATE(S) OF LAW SEE CHAPTER” (11 Aug 2026) and a secondary guide states the non-July-2026 sections take effect 1 Jan 2027. The enrolled chapter text’s effective-date section was not fully reproduced — Verify which SB 208 sections are in force at 1 Nov 2026 vs 1 Jan 2027.

Part 1 — Laws

PART 1 — LAWS

1.1 Legal status: what is legal for adults

Short answer: Both medical (1998) and adult-use (2014, effective 2015) marijuana are legal under Alaska law for their respective eligible persons, subject to the limits below. Adult-use is the only commercial market. Federal law is not changed by Alaska law, and the given federal baseline (adult-use remains Schedule I) still applies.

1.1.1 The constitutional privacy right — Ravin v. State (1975)

  • Case: Ravin v. State, 537 P.2d 494 (Alaska 1975).
  • Constitutional text: Alaska Constitution, art. I, §22: “The right of the people to privacy is recognized and shall not be infringed.”
  • Holding (as summarised in every secondary source located, and in the Court of Appeals’ later restatement): the state constitutional privacy right protects an adult’s possession and use of a small amount of marijuana in the home for personal use. The Alaska Supreme Court was the first (secondary-described) U.S. state or federal court to announce such a constitutional privacy protection for marijuana possession.
  • Limits of Ravin (important, and commonly overstated): Ravin protected possession and ingestion in the home; it did not protect buying, selling, transfer, or public use. A later brief (Connecticut Cannabis Reform Project, Aug 2026) stresses that Ravin “protected possession and ingestion, not cultivation” as originally decided, and “expressly refused to protect buying, selling or transfer.”
  • Practical quantification — the “four ounces at home” rule: After Ravin, the legislature in 1982 decriminalised possession of up to 4 ounces in the home / 1 ounce outside the home. The 1990 recriminalisation initiative (Measure 2, 1990, ~54% yes) purported to re-criminalise all possession, but in Noy v. State, 83 P.3d 538 (Alaska App. 2003), the Court of Appeals held the 1990 law unconstitutional as applied to in-home possession of under 4 ounces, stating the possession law “must be interpreted to prohibit only the possession of four ounces or more of marijuana” for adults in their homes for personal use. The combined Ravin + Noy position therefore remains: adult possession of 1 to <4 ounces in a private residence for personal use is constitutionally protected and non-criminal, even though AS 17.38.020’s statutory personal-use allowance is 1 ounce. Possession of 4 ounces or more, and possession of 1–<4 ounces outside the home, remain criminally exposed under AS 11.71 (see §1.3.6).
  • Source links: Ravin citation via CannBus legal summary Q3 2026 and Wikipedia “Cannabis in Alaska” (secondary, accessed 2026-11-01); Noy via NORML news, 11 Sept 2003, and FindLaw Alaska marijuana laws page. Verify: the full Ravin opinion text was not opened in this run; quotations from it (e.g. the “close and substantial relationship” language) are secondary quotations and are not reproduced here as verified verbatim opinion text.

1.1.2 Adult-use — what is legal, in one table

All rows below are AS 17.38.020 unless stated otherwise; age 21+ throughout.

Activity Legal? Limit / condition
Possess, use, display, purchase, transport marijuana accessories or marijuana Yes 1 ounce or less of marijuana (statutory); plus the Ravin/Noy home protection to <4 oz at home (see §1.1.1)
Possess, grow, process, transport plants Yes 6 plants per adult, ≤3 mature/flowering; 12 plants per dwelling max, ≤6 mature, regardless of number of adults in the dwelling; plus all marijuana produced by those plants possessed on the premises where grown
Transfer without remuneration Yes 1 ounce or less of marijuana and up to 6 immature plants, to a person 21+
Consume Yes, not in public “nothing in this chapter permits the consumption of marijuana in public” — AS 17.38.020(4); public consumption is a violation, fine up to $100 (AS 17.38.040)
Assist / aid another adult 21+ in the above acts Yes AS 17.38.020(5) (paragraph numbering per the 2024 statute text)
Buy from a licensed retail store Yes Per-person-per-day retail sale limits apply (see §1.3.5), not the bare possession limit
Sell / operate commercially without a licence No Requires an AMCO licence under AS 17.38.070 / 3 AAC 306.005
Give / sell to a person under 21 No AS 17.38.220(c): nothing in ch. 38 permits transfer, with or without remuneration, to a person under 21
Drive under the influence No AS 17.38.220(b); AS 28.35.030 DUI statute applies unchanged

1.1.3 Medical — short status

Legal since 1998 (Ballot Measure 8). Registry-based only (AS 17.37). No medical dispensaries, no medical business licences, no medical tax exemption located (see §1.4). A registered patient who is also 21+ has the same adult-use rights as any other adult, plus the AS 17.37 registry protections; a registered patient under 21 has only the AS 17.37 protections.

1.2 Every statute and regulation on cannabis and hemp

Origin key used throughout: [Ravin] = court decision; [M8-1998] = Ballot Measure 8 (1998); [M2-2014] = Ballot Measure 2 (2014); [Bill] = later legislature act with number; [Board] = Marijuana Control Board regulation; [DNR] = Division of Agriculture / DNR regulation; [Tax] = Dept. of Revenue regulation.

1.2.1 Constitutional and court layer

Citation Origin Summary Last amended / status
Alaska Const. art. I, §22 1972 ratification (per secondary brief, Aug 2026) Express privacy right: “The right of the people to privacy is recognized and shall not be infringed.” Constitutional text; Verify ratification date against the official constitution
Ravin v. State, 537 P.2d 494 (Alaska 1975) [Ravin] Privacy protects adult in-home personal possession/use of a small amount Still the governing constitutional backdrop; narrowed/confirmed by Noy (2003)
Noy v. State, 83 P.3d 538 (Alaska App. 2003) [Court] 1990 recriminalisation unconstitutional as applied to <4 oz in-home adult possession Governing interpretation at cut-off on sources located

1.2.2 Medical — AS 17.37 (Title 17, ch. 37)

Citation Origin Summary
AS 17.37.010 — Registry of patients and listing of caregivers [M8-1998], as implemented by SB 94 (1999), ch. 37 SLA 1999 Dept. (now Dept. of Health) “shall create and maintain a confidential registry” of patients entitled to a registry identification card; registry also lists one primary and one alternate caregiver per patient; registry information “is not a public record under AS 40.25.100–40.25.220”
AS 17.37.020 — (medical-use amounts; text located via SB 94 affirmative-defence provisions and secondary summaries) [M8-1998] / SB 94 (1999) Patient + caregiver aggregate: 1 ounce usable marijuana; 6 plants, ≤3 mature/flowering — see §1.4 for the full position and the caveat that the current AS 17.37.020 text was located only through the SB 94 / AS 11.71.090 materials, Verify against current akleg.gov chapter text
AS 17.37.030 — (protections; affirmative defence interface) [M8-1998] / SB 94 (1999) Interface with AS 11.71.090 affirmative defence (see §1.2.4)
AS 17.37.040 — Restrictions on medical use [M8-1998] See verbatim text in §1.2.7 below
AS 17.37.050 — Medical use by a minor [M8-1998] See §1.4.4
AS 17.37.060 — Addition of debilitating medical conditions [M8-1998] Dept. may add conditions by regulation or on petition; petition must be heard/decided within 180 days (per secondary summary — Verify against current text)
AS 17.37.070 — Definitions (“debilitating medical condition”, “department”, “physician”, etc.) [M8-1998] “Debilitating medical condition” = (A) cancer, glaucoma, HIV-positive status, or AIDS, or treatment for any of these; (B) any chronic/debilitating disease or its treatment producing, for a specific patient, cachexia, severe pain, severe nausea, seizures (incl. epilepsy), or persistent muscle spasms (incl. multiple sclerosis), where in the physician’s professional opinion the condition reasonably may be alleviated by medical marijuana; or (C) any other condition approved by the department
AS 17.37.080 — Short title [M8-1998] “Medical Uses of Marijuana for Persons Suffering from Debilitating Medical Conditions Act” (AS 17.37.010–17.37.070)
7 AAC 34 — Medical marijuana registry regulations (Dept. of Health) [Regulation] Registry administration, including the 10-day update duty for name/address/physician/caregiver changes (7 AAC 34.090, per secondary summary — Verify current text)
SB 94 (1999), ch. 37 SLA 1999 [Bill] Enacted the registry mechanics and the AS 11.71.090 affirmative defence; approved by the Governor 1 June 1999, effective 2 June 1999 (per enrolled bill cover)

Verbatim — AS 17.37.010(a) (akleg.gov statute text, via search return):

“The department shall create and maintain a confidential registry of patients who have applied for and are entitled to receive a registry identification card according to the criteria set out in this chapter. The registry must also contain the name of the primary caregiver and the name of the alternate caregiver of a patient, if either is designated by the patient. Only one primary caregiver and one alternate caregiver may be listed in the registry for a patient. The registry and the information contained within it are not a public record under AS 40.25.100 - 40.25.220.”

Verbatim — AS 17.37.040 (akleg.gov statute text, via search return):

“(a) No patient in lawful possession of a registry identification card shall (1) engage in the medical use of marijuana in a way that endangers the health or well-being of any person; (2) engage in the medical use of marijuana in plain view of, or in a place open to, the general public; or (3) sell or distribute marijuana to any person who is known to the patient not to be either in lawful possession of a registry identification card or eligible for such card. (b) Any patient found by a preponderance of the evidence to have willfully violated the provisions of this chapter shall be precluded from obtaining or using a registry identification card for the medical use of marijuana for a period of one year. … (d) Nothing in this section shall require any accommodation of any medical use of marijuana (1) in any place of employment; (2) in any correctional facility; (3) on or within 500 feet of school grounds; (4) at or within 500 feet of a recreation or youth center; or (5) on a school bus.”

Last-amended date for AS 17.37: Verify — the akleg.gov chapter text located does not carry a single last-amended date in the search return, and the “department” is now the Dept. of Health (formerly Dept. of Health and Social Services) after the 2022 department split. Do not cite a last-amended date for ch. 37 without checking the current akleg.gov chapter history note.

1.2.3 Adult-use — AS 17.38 (Title 17, ch. 38) and AS 43.61 (excise tax)

AS 17.38 was enacted by 2014 General Election Ballot Measure No. 2, approved 4 Nov 2014 (53.2% yes, per secondary reports), effective 24 Feb 2015. Later amendments located in the statute history note for AS 17.38.020: “am § 1 ch 53 SLA 2016; am § 57 ch 13 SLA 2019” (editorial conforming changes). The full section list located is: 17.38.010 (purpose), .020 (personal use), .030 (personal cultivation restrictions), .040 (public consumption), .050 (false identification), .060 (marijuana accessories), .070 (lawful operation of facilities), .080 (Marijuana Control Board; appointment), .091, .101, .111, .121 (board rulemaking authority), .131, .140, .150, .190, .200, .210 (local control), .220 (employers/driving/minors/property), plus .900 definitions and related provisions. Verify the complete current section list and each section’s last-amended date against akleg.gov — only the sections quoted below were reproduced in full in this run.

Verbatim — AS 17.38.010 (purpose and findings) [M2-2014]:

“In the interest of allowing law enforcement to focus on violent and property crimes, and to enhance individual freedom, the people of the state of Alaska find and declare that the use of marijuana should be legal for persons 21 years of age or older.”

Verbatim — AS 17.38.020 (personal use) [M2-2014, as amended ch. 53 SLA 2016; ch. 13 SLA 2019]:

“Notwithstanding any other provision of law, except as otherwise provided in this chapter, the following acts, by persons 21 years of age or older, are lawful and are not criminal or civil offenses under state law or the law of any political subdivision of the state or a basis for seizure or forfeiture of assets under state law: (1) possessing, using, displaying, purchasing, or transporting marijuana accessories or one ounce or less of marijuana; (2) possessing, growing, processing, or transporting not more than six marijuana plants, with three or fewer being mature, flowering plants, and possession of the marijuana produced by the plants on the premises where the plants were grown, except that not more than 12 marijuana plants, with six or fewer being mature, flowering plants, may be present in a single dwelling regardless of the number of persons 21 years of age or older residing in the dwelling; (3) transferring one ounce or less of marijuana and up to six immature marijuana plants to a person who is 21 years of age or older without remuneration; (4) consumption of marijuana, except that nothing in this chapter permits the consumption of marijuana in public; and (5) assisting, aiding, or supporting another person who is 21 years of age or older in any [of the acts described above].”

Verbatim — AS 17.38.030 (restrictions on personal cultivation, penalty) [M2-2014]:

“(a) The personal cultivation of marijuana described in AS 17.38.020(2) is subject to the following terms: (1) marijuana plants shall be cultivated in a location where the plants are not subject to public view without the use of binoculars, aircraft, or other optical aids; (2) a person who cultivates marijuana must take reasonable precautions to ensure the plants are secure from unauthorized access; (3) marijuana cultivation may only occur on property lawfully in possession of the cultivator or with the consent of the person in lawful possession of the property. (b) A person who violates this section while otherwise acting in compliance with AS 17.38.020(2) is guilty of a violation punishable by a fine of up to $750.”

Verbatim — AS 17.38.220 (employers, driving, minors, and control of property) [M2-2014]:

“(a) Nothing in this chapter is intended to require an employer to permit or accommodate the use, consumption, possession, transfer, display, transportation, sale, or growing of marijuana in the workplace or to affect the ability of employers to have policies restricting the use of marijuana by employees. (b) Nothing in this chapter is intended to allow driving under the influence of marijuana or to supersede laws related to driving under the influence of marijuana. (c) Nothing in this chapter is intended to permit the transfer of marijuana, with or without remuneration, to a person under the age of 21. (d) Nothing in this chapter shall prohibit a person, employer, school, hospital, recreation or youth center, correction facility, corporation, or any other entity who occupies, owns, or controls private property from prohibiting or otherwise regulating the possession, consumption, use, display, transfer, distribution, sale, transportation, or growing of marijuana on or in that property.”

Note on numbering: the original 2014 initiative text numbered the employers/driving/minors provision as Sec. 17.38.120; the current codification is AS 17.38.220. Secondary sources cite both numbers for the same substance — cite AS 17.38.220 for current law.

Other AS 17.38 provisions (summaries; verbatim text not reproduced in this run — Verify against akleg.gov before quoting):

  • AS 17.38.040 — Public consumption banned; violation, fine up to $100 (bail schedule confirms $100).
  • AS 17.38.050 — False identification by a person under 21 misrepresenting age; bail $400 (Uniform Minor Offense Table, 27 Oct 2026).
  • AS 17.38.060 — Marijuana accessories authorised.
  • AS 17.38.070 — Lawful operation of marijuana establishments (retail, cultivation, product manufacturing, testing) — the statutory basis for the licence types in 3 AAC 306.005.
  • AS 17.38.080–.121 — Marijuana Control Board: composition (five members), appointment, meetings (including the requirement to meet in each judicial district at least once per year, per contemporaneous reporting), and rulemaking authority (AS 17.38.121).
  • AS 17.38.210 — Local control / local option (see Part 3; full treatment is outside Parts 0–1, but the existence of the local-option power is noted here because it qualifies every licence statement in this file).
  • AS 17.38.900 — Definitions, including “marijuana”, “marijuana product”, “usable marijuana”, “mature/flowering” and related terms — Verify current definitions text; product-type limits in §1.3 depend on these definitions plus 3 AAC 306.990.

Excise tax statute — AS 43.61 [M2-2014, §2 of the initiative]:

Verbatim — AS 43.61.010(a)–(c), (f) (akleg.gov text via search return):

“(a) An excise tax is imposed on the sale or transfer of marijuana from a marijuana cultivation facility to a retail marijuana store or marijuana product manufacturing facility. Every marijuana cultivation facility shall pay an excise tax at the rate of $50 per ounce, or proportionate part thereof, on marijuana that is sold or transferred from a marijuana cultivation facility to a retail marijuana store or marijuana product manufacturing facility. (b) The department may exempt certain parts of the marijuana plant from the excise tax described in (a) of this section or may establish a rate lower than $50 per ounce for certain parts of the marijuana plant. (c) The recidivism reduction fund is established in the general fund. The Department of Administration shall separately account for 50 percent of the tax collected under this section and deposit it into the recidivism reduction fund. … (f) The marijuana education and treatment fund is established in the general fund. In addition to the accounting under (c) of this section, the Department of Administration shall separately account for 25 percent of the tax collected under this section and deposit it into the marijuana education and treatment fund.”

The remaining 25% goes to the general fund (see Part 2 for the full distribution; the 50/25/25 split is consistently reported). The tiered rates actually collected ($25 immature/abnormal bud, $15 remainder/trim, $1/clone) are set by regulation, 15 AAC 61.100, under the AS 43.61.010(b) power — see §1.2.5.

1.2.4 Controlled substances — AS 11.71

  • AS 11.71.190 — Schedule VIA: marijuana is listed as a Schedule VIA controlled substance under Alaska law (the lowest-penalty schedule; secondary summaries describe it as “Schedule VI” / “VIA” — cite “Schedule VIA” per AS 11.71.190). This state scheduling coexists with AS 17.38 legalisation: conduct within AS 17.38 / AS 17.37 is lawful and not an offence; conduct outside those chapters is prosecuted under AS 11.71.
  • AS 11.71.050 — Misconduct involving a controlled substance in the fifth degree (Class A misdemeanour): covers, among other things, possessing 1 ounce or more of marijuana (Schedule VIA) outside what AS 17.38 permits, and delivering/manufacturing <1 ounce without a licence — per secondary summary of the statute, Verify against current text.
  • AS 11.71.090 — Affirmative defence; medical use: enacted by SB 94 (1999). In a prosecution under AS 11.71.030–11.71.060 for manufacture, delivery, possession, possession with intent, use or display of a Schedule VIA substance, it is an affirmative defence that the defendant is a registered patient (or primary caregiver), was personally examined/diagnosed by a physician within the previous six months with a written statement that the patient might benefit, the amount was intended for the patient, and the aggregate amount did not exceed 1 ounce usable + 6 plants (≤3 mature). The defence also covers a patient/caregiver transporting ≤1 ounce where not visible to others and limited to direct transport. Note: this is an affirmative defence, not immunity from arrest — MPP’s summary stresses Alaska’s law “only provides an affirmative defense, not protection from arrest.”
  • AS 11.71.900 — Definitions: the statutory definition of “marijuana” explicitly excludes resin, oil, and any compound/preparation made from them, including “natural or synthetic tetrahydrocannabinol” — a definitional quirk that matters for the hemp/intoxicating-cannabinoid analysis in §1.5 (THC concentrates are dealt with as a separate category from “marijuana” as defined). Verify the current AS 11.71.900 text; this point rests on a secondary summary of the definition.
  • Penalties (secondary summary, NORML / FindLaw — Verify against AS 11.71 and AS 12.55 before quoting as law): 1–<4 oz outside the home: Class A misdemeanour, up to 1 year / $10,000 (but no penalty if in the home for personal use, per Ravin/Noy); 4 oz or more: Class C felony, up to 5 years / $50,000; possession within 500 feet of school grounds / recreation or youth centre / on a school bus: Class C felony with an affirmative defence if entirely within a private residence; delivery/sale <1 oz: misdemeanour; ≥1 oz: felony; delivery to a person under 19 by a person ≥3 years older: higher felony exposure. These penalty summaries are not verbatim statute text and are flagged accordingly.

1.2.5 Marijuana Control Board regulations — 3 AAC 306

3 AAC 306 is the Board’s comprehensive marijuana code, first effective 21 Feb 2016 (Register 217). Structure located:

  • Art. 1 (3 AAC 306.005–306.200+): licensing generally — 306.005 (licence required; the four statutory licence families), 306.010 (licence restrictions, incl. 500-foot buffer — see §1.6.5), 306.020 (new-licence application), 306.025, 306.030 (petition for licence in an area with no local government — majority of permanent residents within 1 mile), 306.035 (renewal), 306.040 (ownership change), 306.055 (fingerprints/background), 306.060 (local government notice / 60-day protest), 306.065 (objections), 306.080 (causes for denial), 306.100 (fees), 306.200 (local options).
  • Art. 2/3 (retail, 306.300–306.380): 306.310 (acts prohibited at retail — see §1.6.3), 306.345 (packaging/labelling — retail assurance), 306.355 (limit on quantity sold — see §1.3.5), 306.360 (advertising), 306.365 (warnings/signage), 306.370 (onsite consumption endorsement), 306.380 (exterior window / curbside pickup — referenced in the 2026 drafts; Verify current text and effective date).
  • Art. 4 (cultivation, 306.400–306.480): 306.405/306.410 (standard vs limited cultivation), 306.435 (inventory tracking — cultivation; proposed for repeal/folding into 306.730 in the AO-360 drafts), 306.460 (samples — proposed for repeal/moving to new 306.727), 306.470/306.475 (cultivation packaging/labelling).
  • Art. 5 (product manufacturing, 306.500–306.570): 306.505/306.515 (standard vs concentrate manufacturing), 306.510 (prohibited products — incl. adulterated food/drink and products closely resembling familiar food/drink such as candy), 306.540 (inventory tracking — manufacturing), 306.557 (quality-control sampling), 306.560 (edible potency limits — see §1.3.5), 306.565/306.570 (manufacturing packaging/labelling).
  • Art. 6 (testing, 306.600–306.675): 306.645 (testing compliance for potency), 306.655 (inventory tracking — testing).
  • Art. 7 (general operating, 306.700–306.755+): 306.700 (marijuana handler permits), 306.730 (inventory tracking system — Metrc; see §1.7), 306.740 (waste/disposal), 306.745 (scales), 306.750 (transport manifests), 306.755, plus 306.770 (advertising/marketing/promotions — referenced in the 2026 drafts as the successor advertising section; Verify whether 306.770 has replaced 306.360 or supplements it), 306.840 (fine schedule, adopted by reference — referenced in AO-360 drafts).
  • Art. 9 (definitions, 306.990): 306.990(b)(48) defines “total THC” as “the sum of THC and 0.877 × THCA” (per the AMCO FAQ, 7 Oct 2026); 306.990(b)(42) and (40) are referenced in onsite-consumption and packaging drafts respectively. The full definitions list was not reproduced — Verify.

Last-amended / currency note for 3 AAC 306: The chapter is amended by individual Board projects, not on a single date. Amendments with dates located in this run: original eff. 21 Feb 2016 (Reg. 217); onsite-consumption rules filed 12 Mar 2019, eff. 11 Apr 2019 (Reg. 230); edible potency increase (5 mg/50 mg → 10 mg/100 mg) eff. 1 Sept 2021 (Reg. 239); 3 AAC 306.730 inventory-tracking amendments eff. 20 Oct 2018 (and later). A consolidated April 2025 copy of 3 AAC 306 is cited by secondary guides checked Sept 2026. The AO-360 package debated 2–3 Sept and 22 Sept 2026 would substantially rewrite Arts. 1, 3, 5, 7 — none of it is in effect at 1 Nov 2026 and none of it is described below as current law; where a draft is mentioned it is labelled “PROPOSED — AO-360, Sept 2026, not in effect.”

Official link for the chapter (to be re-opened before publication): Alaska Legislature, Alaska Administrative Code, Title 3, ch. 306 (via akleg.gov AAC pages); AMCO’s own regulations page at commerce.alaska.gov/web/amco. A 2018/2019 consolidated PDF copy is held by Fox Rothschild (not an official copy — the PDF itself disclaims official status).

1.2.6 Hemp — statutes and regulations

Citation Origin Summary
AS 03.05.076 — Industrial hemp programme (DNR commissioner powers) SB 6 (2018), ch. 5 SLA 2018; substantially amended by SB 27 (2021), ch. 32 SLA 2021; amended again by SB 208 (2026), ch. 60 SLA 2026 Authorises DNR/Division of Agriculture to run the industrial hemp programme, including manufacturing and retail sale of hemp products, registration/renewal procedures, isolation distances for seed-purity production (≥1,000 metres under SB 208), tiered risk-based testing frequency, ≤30-day harvest window after sampling, elimination of in-state transport permits between registered parties with approved documentation, and corrective-action procedures prioritising compliance
AS 03.05.078 (as enacted by SB 6) — Industrial hemp as agricultural crop; registration SB 6 (2018) Industrial hemp classified as an agricultural crop; a person registered with the state may produce it; registration requires name, address, GPS coordinates of the production area; registration valid 1 year, renewable annually (original SB 6 text — Verify whether this section number / 1-year term survives the SB 27 / SB 208 rewrites)
AS 03.05.100(5) and related definitions SB 27 (2021) Definitions for the permanent programme (cited in the State’s 2024 summary-judgment motion in the hemp litigation)
AS 11.71 (controlled substances) — hemp exclusion SB 6 (2018) Removed industrial hemp from the state controlled-substances list (per SB 6 sectional analysis)
AS 43.61 — marijuana tax; hemp carve-out SB 208 (2026) Specifies Alaska’s marijuana tax does not apply to compliant industrial hemp or products made from hemp containing ≤0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis (per Marijuana Herald / CannBus summaries of SB 208 — Verify against enrolled ch. 60 SLA 2026 text)
SB 6 (2018) — “Alaska Grown Industrial Hemp Act” [Bill] 30th Legislature Defined industrial hemp as “all parts and varieties of the plant Cannabis sativa L. containing not more than 0.3 percent delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol”; defined “cannabidiol oil” similarly (≤0.3% delta-9 THC); created the pilot programme under the 2014 federal Farm Bill; signed by Gov. Bill Walker, April 2018 (before the 2018 federal Farm Bill)
SB 27 (2021) — permanent programme [Bill] 32nd Legislature, ch. 32 SLA 2021 Created the permanent programme; added the 10-year felony-controlled-substance ineligibility for applicants; authorised DNR to submit a state plan to USDA; signed Sept 2021
SB 208 (2026) — hemp reform + agricultural land + state vegetable [Bill] 34th Legislature, ch. 60 SLA 2026 Senate 20–0 (13 May 2026), House 39–1 as amended (17 May 2026), Senate concurred 17–3 (18 May 2026), transmitted to Governor 23 July 2026, became law without signature 10 Aug 2026, effective date(s) per chapter (LegiScan: 11 Aug 2026). Creates a micro-grower tier (<¼ acre or <200 plants); risk-based testing; ≤30-day harvest window; hemp testing >0.3% but ≤1% delta-9 THC may be retained and reconditioned/remediated/converted to non-intoxicating industrial use; >1% subject to destruction. Verify which sections are effective at 1 Nov 2026 vs 1 Jan 2027 (see §0.5.7)
11 AAC 40 — Industrial hemp regulations (Division of Agriculture) [DNR] Pilot rules eff. 4 Apr 2020; permanent-programme rules eff. 3 Nov 2023 See §1.5 for the operative rules; key sections: 40.400 (endorsement), 40.500–40.525 (retailer registration), 40.520(b)(3) (stop order for delta-9 THC products), 40.900(13) (“consumption”)
USDA Alaska State Plan Federal approval 28 Dec 2021, effective 1 Jan 2022 Alaska runs its own USDA-approved state plan (not the USDA direct programme). Plan contact/reporting: Division reports licensed-producer contact information to USDA monthly via the State HeMP website

Verbatim — SB 6 definition (sectional analysis / bill file, akleg.gov):

“Industrial hemp is defined as the plant Cannabis Sativa L, the same plant as what is commonly referred to as marijuana, but with a THC (tetrahydrocannabinol) level of 0.3% or lower.”

Verbatim — 11 AAC 40.400(a) and (d) (Division advisory notice, Jan 2024, quoting the regulation):

“(a) Before being offered with or without compensation to a consumer, any industrial hemp product processed beyond its raw form and intended for human or animal consumption must be endorsed by the division.” “(d) The division may not endorse a product that contains delta-9-THC or a nonnaturally occurring cannabinoid, including a cannabinoid made from an ingredient extracted from industrial hemp and modified beyond its original form.”

Verbatim — 11 AAC 40.520(b)(3) (same notice):

“The Division may issue a notice of violation and a stop order for any processed hemp product intended for human or animal consumption from a registered retailer if the hemp product contains any delta-9-THC.”

Verbatim — 11 AAC 40.900(13) (same notice):

“‘consumption’ means any method of ingestion of or application to the body, including eating, drinking, inhaling, absorbing, or injecting[.]”

1.2.7 Other laws that touch cannabis/hemp (located, not exhaustively verified)

  • AS 28.35.030 — Operating a vehicle, aircraft or watercraft while under the influence of an alcoholic beverage, inhalant or controlled substance (DUI; applies to marijuana impairment; no per se THC blood level — see §1.3.4).
  • AS 28.35.031 — Implied consent (chemical test of blood/urine on probable cause; refusal triggers licence-revocation proceedings and is usable as evidence — per secondary summary, Verify).
  • AS 23.10.600–23.10.699 — Voluntary employer drug/alcohol testing statute (see §1.8).
  • AS 23.10.325–23.10.370 — (cited in one guide for testing requirements; Verify — the operative voluntary-testing provisions are generally cited as AS 23.10.600 et seq.; the 325–370 citation may be a guide error).
  • AS 18.35.301 et seq. — Smokefree Alaska Workplace Law (cited for workplace-vehicle smoking/vaping prohibition — Verify).
  • AS 45.75.080 — Weights and Measures Act (registered scales, via 3 AAC 306.745).
  • AS 44.62 — Administrative Procedure Act (rulemaking for 3 AAC 306 and 11 AAC 40).
  • AS 17.30 / AS 17.21 — Related drug provisions (illicit synthetic drugs, AS 17.21.010, $500 bail — Uniform Minor Offense Table). Verify relevance to hemp-derived synthetics; not developed in this run.
  • Federal hemp litigation (Alaska): Alaska Industrial Hemp Ass’n v. State (D. Alaska No. 3:23-cv-00253-KFR) — industry challenge to the 2023 endorsement/delta-9 ban; State’s summary-judgment motion filed 25 Sept 2024; a May 2025 federal magistrate decision is reported (secondary) as upholding Alaska’s restrictions on public-safety grounds. Verify the final judgment, appeal status, and whether any injunction is in force — the May 2025 report rests on a single secondary source (LegalClarity) in this run.

1.3 Possession limits, home grow, public consumption, vehicles, gifting, product types

1.3.1 Possession — the two layers that must always be stated together

Layer Limit Who / where
Statutory personal use (AS 17.38.020(1)) 1 ounce or less of marijuana Any person 21+, anywhere in Alaska (subject to public-consumption and property rules)
Constitutional home protection (Ravin + Noy) >1 ounce up to <4 ounces Adult, in a private residence, for personal use — non-criminal; not a retail-sale or transport right
Home-grown harvest (AS 17.38.020(2)) Unlimited weight if it is the marijuana produced by the person’s lawful plants and possessed on the premises where grown The grower, on those premises
Medical registry (AS 17.37 / AS 11.71.090) 1 ounce usable + 6 plants (≤3 mature) aggregate for patient + caregiver Registered patient / listed caregiver
Retail purchase, per person per day (3 AAC 306.355) See §1.3.5 — this is a sale limit, not a possession limit Any retail customer 21+

A person may therefore lawfully possess at home more than they may buy in one day at one store, and more than the 1-ounce statutory figure if it is home-grown harvest kept on the grow premises or Ravin-protected home possession. Conflating these four limits is the commonest error in Alaska summaries; this dossier keeps them separate.

1.3.2 Home grow — verified exact figures

  • Per adult: 6 plants, ≤3 mature/flowering — AS 17.38.020(2) verbatim in §1.2.3.
  • Per dwelling/household: 12 plants max, ≤6 mature/flowering, “regardless of the number of persons 21 years of age or older residing in the dwelling” — AS 17.38.020(2) verbatim. The task’s “6 per adult, 12 per household” is confirmed.
  • Conditions (AS 17.38.030): not subject to public view without binoculars, aircraft or other optical aids; reasonable precautions against unauthorised access; only on property lawfully in the cultivator’s possession or with the lawful possessor’s consent. Violation while otherwise compliant: fine up to $750.
  • Medical home grow: same 6 / ≤3 figures for the patient + caregiver aggregate (AS 17.37 / AS 11.71.090). A medical patient who is also an adult has both regimes available; they do not stack into 12 plants for one person — Verify whether AMCO/DOH guidance treats them as cumulative; no such guidance was located.
  • Landlord / property control: AS 17.38.220(d) lets any owner/occupier/controller of private property prohibit or regulate growing (and possession, consumption, etc.) on that property. A lease ban is therefore enforceable as a property matter even though growing is lawful under state law.

1.3.3 Public consumption

  • General rule: consumption in public is not permitted by AS 17.38.020(4) and is a violation under AS 17.38.040, fine up to $100 (Uniform Minor Offense Table; Supreme Court Order No. 1888 bail schedule, 20 July 2016).
  • What counts as public: parks, sidewalks, bars, and other places open to the public; a motor vehicle on a public roadway or in a publicly accessible place (e.g. a car park) is treated as public consumption for this purpose in secondary guidance (see §1.3.4). The statute text for AS 17.38.040 itself was not reproduced in full in this run — Verify its definition of “public” / “in public” before advising on edge cases (e.g. a private vehicle on private land, a hotel balcony).
  • Exceptions: (a) private property where the owner allows it; (b) a licensed retail store’s designated marijuana consumption area under an onsite consumption endorsement (3 AAC 306.370); (c) medical use is separately barred “in plain view of, or in a place open to, the general public” (AS 17.37.040(a)(2)) and need not be accommodated within 500 feet of school grounds / recreation or youth centres, on a school bus, in a correctional facility, or in any place of employment (AS 17.37.040(d)).

1.3.4 Vehicles and driving

  • Consumption in a vehicle: smoking/vaping marijuana in a motor vehicle on a public roadway or publicly accessible place is public consumption (AS 17.38.040, $100) on the secondary guidance located. No person, driver or passenger, may lawfully smoke in a vehicle in those circumstances.
  • Transport / open container: Secondary guidance (Where’s Weed Alaska guide; eCigator Alaska guide) states product must be in a sealed container, out of the driver’s reach (not the glove compartment), with any open container in the trunk (or behind the last seat if no enclosed trunk). Verify: no Alaska statute or regulation text stating a marijuana open-container rule in those terms was reproduced in this run. Anchorage is reported to have a local open-container rule — Verify against Anchorage Municipal Code (Part 3). Do not cite a statewide “sealed container / trunk” statute without that check.
  • DUI: AS 17.38.220(b) preserves DUI law unchanged. AS 28.35.030 prohibits operating a vehicle/aircraft/watercraft under the influence of a controlled substance, including marijuana. Alaska has no per se THC blood-concentration limit (unlike alcohol’s 0.08): impairment is proved by behavioural observation, field sobriety testing, and drug-recognition evaluation, per secondary summaries. Implied consent (AS 28.35.031) and refusal consequences are as summarised in §1.2.7. Verify the “no per se limit” point against current AS 28.35.030 / AS 28.35.031 text; it rests on secondary summaries in this run.
  • Interstate / air / ferry / federal-land transport: outside Parts 0–1 (Part 10). Not developed here except to note that Alaska-law permission to possess/transport does not authorise transport through Canada, in federal airspace, on federal waters, or on federal land.

1.3.5 Retail sale limits and allowed product types

Per-person-per-day retail sale limit — current law (3 AAC 306.355, eff. 21 Feb 2016, as amended 11 Apr 2019 (Reg. 230) and 18 Oct 2020 (Reg. 236)):

“A retail marijuana store may not sell to any one person per day (1) more than one ounce of usable marijuana; (2) more than seven grams of marijuana concentrate for inhalation, or (3) marijuana or marijuana products if the total amount of marijuana, marijuana products, or both marijuana and marijuana products sold contains more than 5,600 milligrams of THC.” — 3 AAC 306.355(a), as reproduced in the 2019 filing and the 2026 draft papers. “(b) These limits include marijuana or marijuana product sold for onsite consumption under 3 AAC 306.370(a)(2).”

In plain terms, per person per day at a store: 1 oz flower, OR 7 g concentrate for inhalation, OR 5,600 mg THC across products (the three limbs are the sale caps; the 2026 drafts debate whether they are alternatives or cumulative — the regulation text as reproduced states them as separate “more than” prohibitions, and secondary guides present them as the per-day basket). Verify the cumulative-vs-alternative reading against the current 3 AAC 306.355 text and AMCO guidance before advising a retailer.

PROPOSED — NOT IN EFFECT (AO-360, Sept 2026): The Board on 22 Sept 2026 agreed in principle (without a formal vote) to an equivalency system — 1 oz flower = 14 g concentrate = 11,200 mg THC — and separately voted to advance removal of purchase limits at onsite facilities. The redrafted equivalency language was to return at a special meeting before the Board’s regular November 2026 meeting. None of this is law at 1 Nov 2026. The original AO-360 draft would have repealed 306.355(a)(2) and (a)(3) outright (keeping only the 1-oz usable-marijuana limb); the Board did not adopt that version.

Allowed product types (AMCO-licensed market):

Type Status THC / form limit
Flower / bud (usable marijuana), pre-rolls Allowed Sale cap 1 oz/day (above); no statewide potency % cap located — Verify (no flower THC % cap was found in 3 AAC 306 in this run)
Concentrates for inhalation (incl. vape cartridges) Allowed Sale cap 7 g/day; onsite vaping device ≤0.3 g concentrate per person per day (see §1.6.4)
Edibles (eaten/swallowed) Allowed 10 mg total THC per serving; 10 servings / 100 mg total THC per single packaged unit; THC must be homogeneous — 3 AAC 306.560 (eff. 21 Feb 2016; amended 1 Sept 2021, Reg. 239, raising the prior 5 mg / 50 mg limits). PROPOSED (2026, not in effect): redefining the limit from “active THC / Delta 9” to “total THC” (THC + 0.877 × THCA) without changing the 10/100 numbers — AMCO FAQ, 7 Oct 2026. A product that today contains 10 mg THC plus additional THCA would, if the proposal is adopted, have to fit THC + 0.877×THCA within 10 mg/serving
Beverages (marijuana-infused drinks) Allowed as marijuana products, subject to the same edible/manufacturing rules Verify whether any beverage-specific THC cap or container rule exists in current 3 AAC 306 — none was located separate from the 10 mg/serving and 5,600 mg/day sale caps. Note: older onsite-consumption summaries state concentrates and marijuana beverages could not be consumed onsite; the current onsite text (see §1.6.4) must be checked for the beverage position
Topicals, tinctures, capsules, transdermal and other non-inhaled, non-eaten products Allowed as marijuana products Subject to manufacturing, testing, packaging/labelling rules; no separate THC cap located — Verify
Products that are adulterated food/drink or closely resemble a familiar food/drink item such as candy Prohibited to manufacture/sell 3 AAC 306.510(a)(4), (c) (2023 text, per Partnership to End Addiction youth-protection report) — Verify current wording
Concentrates for onsite consumption (other than via the permitted vaping device) Prohibited onsite 3 AAC 306.370(b) (see §1.6.4)

Gifting: Transfer without remuneration of ≤1 oz and ≤6 immature plants to a person 21+ is lawful (AS 17.38.020(3)). “Remuneration” includes indirect payment; a “gift” tied to a purchase is a sale. Retail stores may not give free marijuana or marijuana products (including samples) to consumers as a marketing promotion or for any other reason (3 AAC 306.310 / MJ-03 operating-plan certification, rev. 14 Aug 2023) — sampling rules for licensees/employees are a Part 11 matter and are not conflated here.

1.3.6 What remains criminal (summary — Verify penalties against AS 11.71 / AS 12.55)

Possession of 1–<4 oz outside the home (Class A misdemeanour, up to 1 year / $10,000 in secondary summaries); 4 oz+ (Class C felony, up to 5 years / $50,000); unlicensed manufacture/delivery/sale (graduated by amount and recipient age); possession/use within 500 feet of school grounds / recreation or youth centre / on a school bus (felony exposure, with a private-residence affirmative defence); public consumption ($100 violation); personal-cultivation condition violations ($750 violation); under-21 false identification ($400 bail). Medical patients who wilfully violate AS 17.37 are barred from a registry card for 1 year (AS 17.37.040(b)).

1.4 Medical programme

1.4.1 Qualifying conditions — exhaustive list (AS 17.37.070, “debilitating medical condition”)

Category A (diseases): cancer; glaucoma; positive status for HIV; AIDS — or treatment for any of these.

Category B (symptoms of a chronic/debilitating disease or its treatment, where in the physician’s professional opinion the condition reasonably may be alleviated by medical marijuana): cachexia; severe pain; severe nausea; seizures, including those characteristic of epilepsy; persistent muscle spasms, including those characteristic of multiple sclerosis.

Category C: any other medical condition, or treatment for such condition, approved by the department under AS 17.37.060 (regulation or petition).

Secondary guides count this as “nine” conditions (4 diseases + 5 symptoms). No additional condition added by the department was located in this run — Verify against current 7 AAC 34 / department orders whether any Category C condition has ever been approved. Chronic pain is covered as “severe pain” in Category B, not as a freestanding “chronic pain” listing; guides that list “chronic pain” are paraphrasing.

1.4.2 Physician certification

  • A physician must personally examine and diagnose the patient and provide written documentation stating the patient has been diagnosed with a debilitating medical condition and the physician’s conclusion that the patient “might benefit from the medical use of marijuana” (AS 17.37.010(c)(1) formulation; AS 11.71.090(a)(1) requires the examination/diagnosis within the previous six months for the affirmative defence).
  • The application packet is the “Application for Registry Identification Card for Medical Use of Marijuana” plus the original, signed Physician Statement — photocopies of the Physician Statement are not accepted (per DOH guidance as summarised in secondary guides — Verify against the current DOH packet).
  • There is no state list of “certifying physicians” located, and no requirement in the material located that the physician be registered with a separate state cannabis programme. Verify whether current DOH guidance imposes any physician-registration or bona fide relationship rule beyond AS 17.37.070’s “bona fide physician-patient relationship” definition (referenced in AS 11.71.090(b)(1)).
  • Physician consultation cost ($100–$250) appears only in secondary guides and is not a state fee — do not cite as an official figure.

1.4.3 Registry and cards

  • Administrator: Alaska Dept. of Health, Health Analytics and Vital Records (Bureau of Vital Statistics), Marijuana Registry, P.O. Box 110699, Juneau, AK 99811-0699 (mailing address as given in DOH guidance summaries). The statute still says “Department of Health and Social Services” in older text; the department was split in 2022 — cite “Dept. of Health” for current administration, Verify the current division name on the DOH form.
  • Confidentiality: registry information is not a public record (AS 17.37.010(a)).
  • Fee: $25 new application; $20 renewal before expiry; renewal after expiry charged as new ($25). Non-refundable; cheque/money order payable to the Bureau of Vital Statistics (per DOH guidance summaries — Verify against the current form, including whether card payment is now accepted).
  • Contents of an application (AS 17.37.010(c), as reproduced): patient’s name, address, date of birth, Alaska driver’s licence / ID number; physician documentation; caregiver designations if any; and (for minors) parent/guardian consent (see §1.4.4). A legible photocopy of the Alaska ID of the patient and all caregivers must accompany the form (per DOH guidance summaries).
  • Processing / validity: DOH must review within 30 days of receipt; if the department does not deny and issue the card within 35 days, the application is considered approved under state law (per secondary summary of AS 17.37.010 — Verify against current text). Cards are valid 1 year (per secondary guides — Verify against the card/form). Secondary guides report ~5 weeks / 35 days’ processing in practice.
  • Update duty: cardholders must update the department within 10 days of any change to name, address, physician, or caregiver (7 AAC 34.090, per secondary summary — Verify).
  • Counts only (no personal data in this dossier): 97 active registered patients as of 31 Dec 2025 (DOH via email 10 Feb 2026, via NORML). No more recent official count was located — Unavailable for 2026 year-to-date. The tiny count (97 in a state of ~740,000) is itself a headline: the registry is vestigial for most patients, who can buy as adults without a card.

1.4.4 Caregivers and minors

  • One primary + one alternate caregiver per patient max (AS 17.37.010(a)).
  • Caregiver eligibility (secondary summaries, to Verify against AS 17.37.010(c) / 7 AAC 34): 21+; no felony controlled-substance conviction; not on probation/parole (one guide adds “cannot have certain drug felonies” / “never convicted of a felony controlled substances offense” — the exact disqualifier wording is Verify); a caregiver may serve only one patient unless the patients are relatives in the same household (per MPP summary — Verify).
  • Caregiver functions: controls acquisition, dosage and frequency for the patient (AS 17.37.010(c)(7) formulation in older text); may deliver marijuana to the patient for whom listed; patient may deliver to their listed caregiver (SB 94 text).
  • Minors (AS 17.37.050): a patient who has not reached majority (AS 25.20) or had disabilities removed (AS 09.55.590) may engage in medical use only if: (1) a physician has diagnosed a debilitating condition; (2) the physician has explained risks/benefits to the patient and to a parent/legal guardian residing in Alaska, if any; (3) the physician has provided the written documentation; (4) that parent/guardian consents in writing to serve as primary caregiver and to permit the medical use; (5) the patient applies and receives a registry card; (6) patient + caregiver collectively possess no more than the AS 17.37.020(a)(1)–(2) amounts (1 oz + 6 plants / ≤3 mature); and (7) the caregiver controls acquisition/dosage/frequency (per the older-text formulation — Verify against current AS 17.37.050 numbering).

1.4.5 Absence of medical dispensaries, medical licences, and tax exemptions — and why it matters

  • No medical dispensary licences exist in AS 17.37 or AS 17.38. NORML’s Alaska medical page lists “STATE-LICENSED DISPENSARIES ALLOWED: No” (in the search return, the operational figure is cut off but the structure is clear) and MPP’s Aug 2025 table states for Alaska: businesses “Not allowed, but voters legalized cannabis for adults’ use in 2014. Now, anyone over 21 may purchase” from the adult-use market. AMCO’s licence families (3 AAC 306.005) contain no medical tier.
  • No medical tax exemption was located. The excise tax (AS 43.61) is imposed at cultivation-to-retail/manufacture transfer regardless of the ultimate purchaser’s registry status, and no statute or regulation text exempting registry-card sales from excise or local sales/marijuana taxes was located. Secondary claims that a card “saves up to 5% in taxes at a medical dispensary” (Leafwell) are not applicable to Alaska (there are no medical dispensaries) and should not be repeated for Alaska.
  • No reciprocity: Alaska does not recognise out-of-state medical cards (NORML, 2026). An out-of-state patient who is 21+ may still buy as an adult-use customer.
  • No medical delivery: NORML states medical delivery is not permitted; patients/caregivers obtain product from licensed (adult-use) stores or by home cultivation. Adult-use delivery to consumers is likewise prohibited (see §1.6.3) — the two prohibitions coincide but have different sources.
  • Consequence for Schedule III (restated): because there is no state-licensed medical seller, the only “medical” transaction in Alaska is (a) a registry patient’s purchase from an adult-use retailer, or (b) home cultivation by/for a registered patient. Whether either is “state-licensed medical” under 91 FR 22714 is Verify (see the central answer at the top of this file). No Alaska agency guidance on this question was located in this run.

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

1.5.1 Hemp definition and THC measurement — the two definitions that must not be conflated

Definition Text Applies to
Alaska industrial hemp (SB 6, 2018, as carried into the permanent programme) “all parts and varieties of the plant Cannabis sativa L. containing not more than 0.3 percent delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol” (dry-weight basis, per SB 208 summary for the tax carve-out) Production / programme eligibility; crop compliance
Federal hemp (2018 Farm Bill, pre-11 Dec 2026) ≤0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis Federal baseline before the given 2026 changes
Federal hemp (given, after 11 Dec 2026) Full total-THC definition + 0.4 mg total THC per container cap (PL 119-37 §781 as limited by PL 119-103 §2019) Given, not re-researched; Alaska conformity Verify
Alaska consumable hemp products Zero delta-9 THC, however small — the Division “may not endorse a product that contains delta-9-THC” (11 AAC 40.400(d)), and may issue a stop order for any processed consumable hemp product containing “any delta-9-THC” (11 AAC 40.520(b)(3)) Any processed hemp product beyond raw form intended for human/animal consumption offered to an Alaska consumer
“Total THC” (marijuana edibles, 3 AAC 306.990(b)(48)) “the sum of THC and 0.877 × THCA” Marijuana edible potency (see §1.3.5); not the hemp crop test, which remains delta-9-based in the Alaska materials located

The practical result is a split regime: a hemp crop at 0.25% delta-9 THC is lawful hemp for production purposes, but a gummy, tincture, beverage, capsule, vape, topical or pet product made from it and containing any delta-9 THC may not be sold to an Alaska consumer, endorsed or not. Broad-spectrum / isolate products with delta-9 THC removed (as shown on the final-product certificate of analysis) may be endorsed and sold, if endorsed and sold by a registered retailer.

1.5.2 The 2023 rules and the “50 mg” history (why older guides mislead)

The initial hemp rules allowed a processed consumable hemp product to contain up to 50 mg delta-9 THC per individual product (quoted in the State’s 2024 summary-judgment motion). That allowance was the problem the 2023 rewrite addressed, in part because such products were available without regard to consumer age. The rules effective 3 Nov 2023 replaced it with the zero-delta-9 endorsement bar in §1.5.1. Any guide, product list, or endorsement predating Nov 2023 that shows a delta-9-containing consumable as lawful in Alaska is superseded; the Division’s Jan 2024 advisory notice states expressly: “It does not matter if the product is endorsed. Products containing delta-9-THC may not be sold to a consumer in Alaska” (for products endorsed before Nov 2023, the same bar applies — per secondary summaries of the Division position).

1.5.3 Legal status by cannabinoid — hemp channel vs marijuana channel

Cannabinoid / product (hemp-derived, for human/animal consumption in Alaska) Hemp-channel status (Division endorsement) Marijuana-channel note
Delta-9 THC (any amount, even <0.3%) Not endorsed; sale to a consumer unlawful (11 AAC 40.400(d), 40.520(b)(3)) Delta-9 THC is lawful as marijuana when produced/sold by AMCO licensees under AS 17.38 / 3 AAC 306 — it is not lawful as a hemp product
Delta-8 THC, delta-8 THC-O acetate Not endorsed (non-naturally occurring cannabinoid) See below on the “AMCO channel” claim
Delta-10 THC / THC-O acetate, delta-6 THC / THC-O acetate Not endorsed Same
THCA (hemp-derived) Not endorsed (listed expressly in the Division endorsed-products header and FAQ) THCA-rich flower sold outside the AMCO channel is treated as marijuana in some secondary summaries — Verify the regulation text for this proposition (see §1.5.4)
THCV, THCP Not endorsed Same
HHC, HHCP Not endorsed (synthetic/lab-created) Same
Any other synthetic or chemically converted cannabinoid derived from hemp Not endorsed (11 AAC 40.400(d): “including a cannabinoid made from an ingredient extracted from industrial hemp and modified beyond its original form”) Same
CBD / CBG broad-spectrum or isolate with delta-9 THC removed (COA-proven) Endorsable and saleable by a registered retailer, if endorsed —
Hemp seed-derived products (no cannabinoids; seeds do not naturally contain THC) May be offered without endorsement (Division FAQ) —
Raw / unprocessed hemp (not for consumption) Programme registration rules apply to production; consumable endorsement bar does not apply to non-consumable industrial uses —

Minimum age for hemp products: No minimum age for endorsed (non-intoxicating) hemp products was located in the Alaska materials — the absence of an age gate was part of the State’s rationale for the 2023 ban on intoxicating consumables. Verify whether any current 11 AAC 40 provision or separate Alaska law (e.g. AS 17.21 / tobacco-style age law) imposes an age limit on hemp/CBD sales; do not assume 21+ applies to hemp. By contrast, marijuana sales are strictly 21+ (3 AAC 306.310(a)(1)).

1.5.4 The “sold through AMCO licensees” claim — flagged as Verify

Two secondary sources in this run state that intoxicating hemp products “must be sold through AMCO-licensed marijuana retailers” / that THCA flower or delta-8 “intended for human consumption must be sold through AMCO-licensed marijuana retailers under the 3 AAC 306 framework adopted in 2023.” This claim is NOT confirmed by any regulation text reproduced in this run. What the primary materials confirm is the negative proposition: such products may not be sold as hemp products to Alaska consumers. Whether a product manufactured from hemp-derived cannabinoids can enter the AMCO marijuana stream as a “marijuana product” depends on AS 17.38 / 3 AAC 306 definitions of “marijuana” and “marijuana product,” sourcing/testing/manufacturing rules, and the AS 11.71.900 exclusion of THC compounds from the “marijuana” definition — none of which was resolved in the materials located. Do not publish the “AMCO channel” proposition as Alaska law without checking current 3 AAC 306.990 definitions and any AMCO/Division joint guidance. The safer sourced statement is: intoxicating hemp products are barred from the hemp consumer channel; their status in the marijuana channel is Verify.

1.5.5 Testing and labelling (hemp)

  • Endorsement precondition: before a processed consumable hemp product is offered (with or without compensation) to a consumer, it must be endorsed by the Division (11 AAC 40.400(a)). Endorsement requires testing — the Division’s endorsed-products list shows approval/expiry dates per product (e.g. products approved Oct 2025 / Jan 2026 / Apr 2026 / Sept 2026 with 1-year expiries in the list reproduced in this run).
  • Retailer precondition: legal consumable products “may only be offered to consumers by retailers that are registered with the Division” (Division FAQ). Under 11 AAC 40.500, no one may receive compensation for processed industrial hemp from a consumer in the state without a Division retailer registration, and each product must be endorsed (per secondary summary — Verify current 40.500 text).
  • Labelling: the Division’s programme materials include label examples; the FAQ for professional licensees notes products may be labelled “broad spectrum” / “full spectrum” without clearly informing the user of delta-9 content, which is why the Division warns users cannot be certain from labelling alone without endorsement + COA. Full hemp labelling contents (contents, warnings, COA disclosure) were not reproduced in this run — Verify against current 11 AAC 40.400 / programme label guidance. Do not import marijuana labelling rules (3 AAC 306.570) into hemp products.
  • Grower/processor/retailer registration classes: grower, processor, retailer (three classes, 11 AAC 40) — full fees/requirements are Part 7, not Part 1, except to note the classes exist.
  • SB 208 testing changes (2026, Verify effective date): tiered testing frequency by risk category (crop history + past compliance); harvest window ≤30 days after sampling (extensions for weather/good cause); hemp at >0.3% but ≤1% delta-9 THC may be reconditioned/remediated/converted to non-intoxicating industrial use instead of immediate destruction; >1% subject to destruction. These are SB 208 statutory directions to DNR to make regulations — the implementing regulations themselves were not located in this run.

1.5.6 THC beverages — hemp vs marijuana

  • Hemp THC beverages: a beverage containing delta-9 THC (or any listed intoxicating cannabinoid) and intended for human consumption may not be sold to an Alaska consumer as a hemp product, for the same reasons as any other consumable (§1.5.3). A CBD sparkling water with delta-9 THC removed and Division endorsement appears on the endorsed-products list (e.g. Alaska Natural Extracts sparkling waters in the older list) — endorsement + zero delta-9 is the test, not the beverage form.
  • Marijuana THC beverages: allowed as marijuana products through AMCO retailers, subject to manufacturing/testing/packaging rules and the 10 mg/serving edible framework to the extent it applies to drinks — Verify whether drinks are classed as “edible marijuana products” (eaten/swallowed) under 3 AAC 306.560 or under a separate beverage rule; no beverage-specific rule was located.
  • Federal 0.4 mg/container cap (given, after 11 Dec 2026): if applied to Alaska hemp beverages, it would be stricter than the old federal 0.3% standard but less strict than Alaska’s current zero-delta-9 consumable bar. Alaska’s bar would therefore already satisfy the federal cap for consumables, but crop/production definitions and non-consumable products would still need conformity review — Verify with DNR after the federal dates pass.

1.5.7 Alaska’s response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates

No Alaska response was located in this run. No DNR/Division notice, AMCO guidance, bill, or regulation project addressing PL 119-37 §781, PL 119-103 §2019, the total-THC definition, or the 0.4 mg container cap was found in the searches performed. SB 208 (Aug 2026) predates the federal dates and is written in delta-9 terms (0.3% delta-9 THC, 1% remediation threshold). Whether Alaska will (a) leave 11 AAC 40 / AS 03.05.076 unchanged on the theory that its consumable bar already exceeds the federal cap, (b) amend the crop definition from delta-9 to total THC, or (c) seek USDA plan re-approval, is Verify / Unavailable at 1 Nov 2026 — before either federal date has taken effect. This dossier must not state that Alaska “has responded” or “will comply” in any particular way. Recommended follow-up (for Part 7 / the Run 2 notes): check DNR Industrial Hemp Program notices and the USDA State Plan page after 11 Dec 2026, and check whether SB 208’s regulation mandate is used to adopt total-THC testing.

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, onsite consumption, zoning/buffers

1.6.1 Packaging and labelling — current law (3 AAC 306)

  • Retail assurance rule (3 AAC 306.345(a)): a retail store shall assure (1) marijuana sold is packaged/labelled per 3 AAC 306.470/306.475 (cultivation), (2) marijuana products per 3 AAC 306.565/306.570 (manufacturing), and (3) marijuana/marijuana product sold is packaged in opaque, resealable, child-resistant packaging when the purchaser leaves the retail section of the licensed premises.
  • Exit packaging in practice: operating-plan certifications (MJ-03) require the licensee/general manager to inspect shipments, record them in Metrc, and ensure each product is in fully opaque, child-resistant packaging before the customer exits.
  • Edible packaging specifics (secondary summary of 3 AAC 306.565/306.570 and AMCO training materials): each edible unit individually wrapped or divided into serving sizes; child-resistant packaging unless a single-serving product ≤10 mg THC; reported THC content within 20% of the manufacturer’s target (e.g. a 25 mg package with five servings: each serving 4–6 mg). Verify these specifics against current 306.565/306.570 text — they rest on training/secondary materials in this run.
