  • Label contents (secondary summary): product name; total THC and CBD per package and per serving (mg); instructions/dosing where necessary; producer/manufacturer identity; warnings (see §1.6.2). Verify the full mandatory label list against current 306.475/306.570 before reproducing it as a checklist.
  • PROPOSED — AO-360, Sept 2026, not in effect: consolidate all packaging/labelling into two new sections, 3 AAC 306.733 (packaging) and 306.734 (labelling), repealing 306.345, 306.470, 306.475, 306.565, 306.570; simplify/reduce requirements; review “total THC” wording in packaging/labelling; consider a visual symbol/sticker/marker and warnings. None of this is current law.

1.6.2 Advertising

Current law (3 AAC 306.360, as reproduced in the 2018 consolidated text):

  • Signs: a retail store may have not more than three signs visible to the general public from the public right-of-way identifying the store by business name; a sign may be in the window or attached to the outside of the licensed premises; each sign ≤4,800 square inches.
  • Prohibited advertising content: an advertisement may not contain a statement/illustration that (1) is false or misleading; (2) promotes excessive consumption; (3) represents that marijuana use has curative or therapeutic effects; (4) depicts a person under 21 consuming [marijuana — text cut off in the search return; Verify the full (b) list].
  • Warnings: all advertising must contain the warnings required under 3 AAC 306.360(e) (per MJ-03 §5.3 certification — Verify the warning text).
  • Promotions: no giveaway coupons as promotional materials; no games/competitions to encourage sales (MJ-03 §5.2 certification); no free marijuana/alcohol as a promotion (MJ-03 §4.2).
  • PROPOSED — AO-360, Sept 2026, not in effect: eliminate mandatory warnings from advertisements, loosen mobile-marketing and event-sponsorship restrictions, and revise 3 AAC 306.770 (advertising/marketing/promotions). The relationship between 306.360 and 306.770 in current law is Verify — the 2026 drafts treat 306.770 as the advertising section, while older texts cite 306.360; do not cite one without checking whether the other has superseded it.

1.6.3 Delivery — prohibited to consumers (with a narrow curbside exception to Verify)

  • General rule (3 AAC 306.310(a)(5), older text): a retail store may not sell/deliver “over the Internet; a licensed retail marijuana store may only sell marijuana or a marijuana product to a consumer who is physically present on the licensed premises.”
  • Later text (Flowhub summary of current 306.310(a)(5)): “unless permitted under 3 AAC 306.995, over the Internet; a licensed retail marijuana store may only sell … to a consumer who is physically present on the licensed premises or who is physically present at the location designated for exterior window or curbside pickup as described and permitted under 3 AAC 306.995.” Curbside/walk-up/drive-through exterior-window pickup is therefore an in-person sale at the premises, not delivery.
  • Delivery (transport to a consumer’s home/address) is not permitted — stated expressly in secondary guides and consistent with the “physically present” rule. Business-to-business transport between licensees (with a transport manifest, 3 AAC 306.750) is a different activity and is not consumer delivery.
  • Medical delivery: not permitted (see §1.4.5).

1.6.4 Onsite consumption endorsements (3 AAC 306.370)

  • Adoption/effect: adopted at the Board’s 20 Dec 2018 meeting; filed 12 Mar 2019; effective 11 Apr 2019 (Register 230). Alaska was the first state with statewide onsite-consumption rules.
  • Nature: an endorsement to a retail licence, not a standalone licence. Application fee $1,000 (secondary fee summaries; also the general new-licence application fee in 3 AAC 306.100(a)); endorsement annual fee $2,000 in secondary summaries — Verify both against current 306.100.
  • What an endorsed store may sell for onsite consumption (current text as reproduced in the 2026 drafts, reflecting the 2019 rules as amended 18 Oct 2020, Reg. 236): in the designated marijuana consumption area, at the time of purchase only: (1) marijuana bud/flower, ≤1 gram per person per day; (2) edible marijuana products, ≤25 mg THC per person per day (the 2026 draft text; older MPP summary states 10 mg — the 10 mg figure reflects the pre-Oct-2020 text and is superseded for current law, but Verify the 25 mg figure against the current 306.370(b) text, as the draft papers are the source for it in this run); (3) a vaping device containing ≤0.3 grams of marijuana concentrate per person per day; concentrate may not be consumed onsite by any other means.
  • What is prohibited onsite: sale/consumption of marijuana concentrate (other than via the permitted vaping device); consumption by a licensee/employee/agent during a work shift; alcohol and tobacco products in the consumption area (per MPP summary); “bring your own” product (per MPP summary); access by anyone under 21; removal of unconsumed product except as packaged under 3 AAC 306.345 (the 2019 text allows removal if so packaged — Verify whether the current text retains removal or requires on-premises consumption only, as the 2026 drafts debate this).
  • Area requirements (3 AAC 306.370(c), 2019 text as amended): isolated from other retail areas, separated by walls and a secure door, access only from the retail store; ventilation directing air from the consumption area outward where inhalation is permitted (ventilation required only where inhalation is permitted, per the 2019 clean-up); signage/warnings displayed (306.360/306.365); patrons monitored at all times for overconsumption; unconsumed/abandoned product destroyed per the operating plan and 3 AAC 306.740.
  • Edibles-only endorsement: the 2019 clean-up created an edibles-only endorsement available to retail stores that are not in freestanding buildings (inhalation endorsements require a freestanding store — Verify the current freestanding requirement, as the 2026 drafts restate it as “indoor or outdoor area, regardless of whether freestanding” for edibles-only vs “freestanding” for any-method).
  • Local control: an endorsement is barred where prohibited by local or state law (306.370(a) opening words); local governments may prohibit endorsements specifically or regulate smoking vs edibles characteristics (306.200, as amended to include endorsement characteristics).
  • Operating count: Unavailable — no official count of operating endorsements at 1 Nov 2026 was located. The Ketchikan Gateway Borough record shows the first local-review onsite application in that borough (Cannabis Corner, licence 10590, notice received 6 Dec 2019) — useful as an early-rollout datapoint, not as a statewide count.
  • PROPOSED — AO-360, Sept 2026, not in effect: remove purchase limits at onsite facilities (customers could not leave with more than the standard possession limit); revise 306.370(b)(1)–(3). Not law at cut-off.

1.6.5 Zoning and buffer distances — state floor, local rules on top

  • State buffer (3 AAC 306.010(a)): the Board will not issue a licence within 500 feet of a school, recreation or youth centre, a building where religious services are regularly conducted, or a correctional facility. AMCO guidance paraphrases this as “the State of Alaska’s Drug Free School Zone” and confirms the 500-foot figure is an inside limit — a local government cannot waive it to allow a closer premises (per AMCO FAQ material in the Kodiak packet). Parks alone are not in the state buffer list (AMCO FAQ: “The only location rules defined by the State … are the buffer zones set out in 3 AAC 306.010(a)”).
  • How the distance is measured, and whether it is property-line to property-line, door-to-door, or by pedestrian route: Verify — not stated in the materials reproduced.
  • Local zoning: beyond the state buffer, zoning is local. The Board denies an application if the local government protests on zoning grounds (3 AAC 306.010(c), 306.060, 306.080, 306.250) unless the local government has approved a variance. Local governments may impose larger buffers (e.g. Cordova’s 1,000-foot school buffer for marijuana conditional uses, Ord. 1162) and conditional-use-permit requirements. Full local rules are Part 3.
  • No-local-government areas: the Board will not approve a new licence or endorsement in an area outside, but within 50 miles of, a local-government boundary unless it receives a petition signed by a majority of permanent residents within 1 mile of the proposed premises (3 AAC 306.030(a), 2019 clean-up text). Areas beyond that, and the Unorganized Borough generally, are Part 3.

1.7 Seed-to-sale tracking (Metrc)

  • System: Metrc (spelled “Metrc” in AMCO forms; one secondary filing spells it “Metre” — that is a typographical error in that filing).
  • Current rule (3 AAC 306.730, eff. 21 Feb 2016, as amended 20 Oct 2018): a marijuana establishment shall use a marijuana inventory tracking system capable of sharing information with the system the board implements to ensure all marijuana cultivated/sold and each marijuana product processed/sold is identified and tracked from seed or cutting to completed sale or disposal of the harvest batch / production lot. Delivered marijuana must be weighed on a scale registered under 3 AAC 306.745 / AS 45.75.080.
  • Operating-plan certification (Form MJ-01, rev. 24 Apr 2026, §5): the applicant certifies: (5.1) the establishment will use Metrc, and any other software will be capable of sharing information with Metrc; (5.2) all delivered marijuana weighed on a registered scale; (5.3) registered scales per AS 45.75.080. On receipt from another licensee, the recipient uses the tracking system to report type, amount and weight received, and must refuse any shipment not accompanied by a transport manifest (MJ-01 §8.7–8.8; 3 AAC 306.750).
  • Per-licence tracking sections (current law, pre-AO-360): retail 306.330; cultivation 306.435; manufacturing 306.540; testing 306.655 — each requiring tracking from receipt/rejection/revocation of a batch/lot through sale, transfer, or destruction, including relocation to new licensed premises on transfer.
  • PROPOSED — AO-360, Sept 2026, not in effect: repeal 306.330, 306.435, 306.540, 306.655 and repeal/readopt 306.730 as a single consolidated Article 7 tracking section; add inventory-adjustment-note requirements for samples (new 306.727). None of this is current law.
  • Scope limits: Metrc tracking applies to licensed marijuana establishments, not to personal home grows, medical registry patients/caregivers acting outside a licence, or hemp registrants (hemp has its own DNR reporting, including monthly producer reporting to USDA — Part 7). Whether hemp products that enter the marijuana stream would enter Metrc is part of the Verify in §1.5.4.

1.8 Employment and drug testing protections

Headline: Alaska gives employees — including medical patients — no statutory protection for marijuana use or for a positive marijuana test. Employers may maintain and enforce drug-free workplace policies.

  • Adult-use (AS 17.38.220(a), verbatim in §1.2.3): nothing in ch. 38 requires an employer to permit/accommodate use, consumption, possession, transfer, display, transportation, sale or growing in the workplace, or affects employers’ ability to have policies restricting employee marijuana use.
  • Medical (AS 17.37.040(d)(1), verbatim in §1.2.2): nothing in ch. 37 requires accommodation of medical marijuana use “in any place of employment.”
  • Voluntary testing statute (AS 23.10.600–23.10.699): Alaska does not require employers to test, but an employer that follows the statute’s written-policy, notice, and testing requirements gains protection from employee claims arising from testing (Baker Donelson guide, updated Oct 2025). Testing may include pre-employment, random (with 30 days’ prior written policy notice, per secondary summary of AS 23.10.620(d) — Verify), post-incident, and reasonable-suspicion testing. A confirmatory test is required after an initial positive before adverse action, per secondary summaries — Verify against AS 23.10.600 et seq.
  • Consequences of a positive test: private employers may refuse to hire, discipline, or terminate for a positive marijuana result, including for off-duty lawful use, on the secondary summaries located (US Drug Test Centers; LegalClarity). One secondary source asserts the opposite for medical cardholders (“employers may not take discriminatory actions against registered cardholders solely for testing positive”) — that assertion conflicts with AS 17.37.040(d)(1) and with MPP’s anti-discrimination table (Alaska: “None known” court decisions; limiting language quoted as the non-accommodation clause) and is not adopted here. Treat any claim of medical-patient employment protection in Alaska as incorrect unless a specific statute or case is produced.
  • Nexus / privacy caveats (case law, secondary): Luedtke v. Nabors Alaska Drilling, Inc., 768 P.2d 1123 (Alaska 1989) is cited for the proposition that discipline for a drug-test result requires some nexus between use and work, and that public-sector suspicionless testing of non-safety-sensitive employees raises Alaska Const. art. I, §22 privacy issues. These are case-law glosses, not statutory protections, and their current scope was not verified in this run — Verify before advising a public employer.
  • Safety-sensitive / federal overlay: DOT testing under 49 CFR Part 40 overrides state law for safety-sensitive functions; federal contractors and federal employment remain subject to federal drug policy regardless of a registry card. Workers’ compensation (AS 23.30.235 — injury not compensable if proximately caused by intoxication, subject to a prescribed-drug question) and unemployment (AS 23.20.379 — disqualification for misconduct including violation of a compliant drug-free policy) consequences are noted in secondary guides — Verify current text and whether a physician’s recommendation counts as “prescribed” for workers’ compensation purposes (expressly flagged as unknown in one guide).
  • Licence-holder staff (handler permits): distinct from general employment protections — see §1.9 note and Part 4/11. In brief: anyone who sells, cultivates, manufactures, tests, transports marijuana, or checks IDs must hold a Marijuana Handler Permit (3 AAC 306.700; AMCO handler page): approved course + test, background check, $50 fee, 3-year validity from course completion, no residency requirement (per AMCO/secondary summaries — Verify the minimum-age point: secondary guides state 21, but one guide notes 21 is “widely reported but not confirmed in the regulation text”).

1.9 Pending legislation — 34th Legislature (2025–2026), Alaska’s two-year legislature

Alaska’s legislature sits for two-year sessions; the 34th Legislature covers 2025–2026. A bill not enacted by the end of the second session dies and must be reintroduced. Status dates below are the last akleg.gov status dates reproduced in this run; all “pending” bills below must be re-checked on akleg.gov after 1 Nov 2026, as the second session’s end-of-session actions may not be fully reflected in the search returns.

Bills / topics searched for but NOT located as 34th Legislature bills in this run (do not assume they exist):

  • A standalone Marijuana Control Board restructuring / board-composition bill — none located; Board changes in 2026 are regulatory (AO-360), not statutory, on the material found. Verify by an akleg.gov subject search for “MARIJUANA” + “BOARD” before stating no such bill exists.
  • A standalone social-equity licensing bill — none located. Alaska’s licence system has no social-equity tier in the materials found (see Part 4); do not import another state’s equity programme.
  • A standalone hemp-derived-THC / intoxicating-hemp bill for the 34th Legislature — none located separate from SB 208; intoxicating-hemp control in Alaska is regulatory (11 AAC 40, 2023), not a 2025–26 statute, on the material found.
  • A standalone employment-protection / drug-testing reform bill for marijuana — none located.
  • A psychedelics initiative for the 2026 ballot — an activist campaign announced (Dec 2025, per Marijuana Moment) it failed to collect enough signatures for the 2026 ballot and shifted to a later cycle. Not a cannabis bill and not law; noted only to prevent confusion with cannabis measures.

What “pending” means here: (H) RLS, (S) FIN, (H) L&C, (H) FIN are committee referrals, not enactment. A bill in Rules or Finance at the end of the second session does not carry over. Any statement that HB 91, SB 73, or HB 94 “passed” or “became law” in 2026 is incorrect on the akleg.gov statuses located — one secondary compilation’s suggestion that HB 91’s outcome was a House passage is contradicted by the akleg.gov (H) RLS status and by the Drug Policy Watch brief’s explicit note that the House-passage claim “is not supported by the record.”

  4. Full current texts not reproduced: AS 17.38.040, .050, .060, .070, .080–.121, .210, .900; AS 17.37.020, .030; AS 11.71.050, .190, .900; 3 AAC 306.360(b) full list, 306.365 warning text, 306.370(b) current edible figure (10 mg vs 25 mg), 306.380/306.995 curbside text, 306.770 vs 306.360 relationship, 306.990 full definitions, 11 AAC 40.500 / labelling text. Verify each before quoting.
  5. Residency for AMCO licence holders — conflicting secondary claims (2016: residency mandated; 2026: no mandate). Verify current 3 AAC 306.020/306.080.
  6. Onsite endorsement operating count at 1 Nov 2026 — Unavailable.
  7. HB 239 chapter number and marijuana-provision effective date (incl. 1 Jan 2028 sunset mechanics) — Verify on akleg.gov (enrolled ch. text).

  10. FY2025 excise revenue — $25,712,480 vs ~$25.3m — resolve against Tax Division release (Part 2/6).
  11. HB 91 FY2027 loss — $4.04m (fiscal note) vs $8.1m (press summary) — resolve by fiscal-note version (Part 2/9).
  12. “AMCO channel” for intoxicating hemp — secondary claim that delta-8/THCA/hemp-THC products may be sold through AMCO retailers. Verify against 3 AAC 306.990 definitions and any joint AMCO/Division guidance; do not publish as law.
  13. Hemp minimum age, hemp labelling contents, hemp beverage-specific rule, marijuana flower potency cap, marijuana topical/tincture THC caps — none located; Verify / Unavailable as applicable.
  14. Statewide marijuana open-container / sealed-container / trunk rule — secondary guidance only; Verify against statute/regulation and Anchorage code.
  15. No per se THC DUI limit; implied-consent / refusal consequences; workers’ comp “prescribed” question; Luedtke nexus scope; public-sector privacy testing limits — secondary summaries only; Verify against AS 28.35.030/.031, AS 23.30.235, AS 23.20.379, and case law.

  22. Tribal medical programmes — none located in this run; Part 10 must answer whether any tribe operates one and whether it could count as “state-licensed” (expected answer: Verify, for the same federal-text reason as item 1).

Part 2 — Taxes

PART 2 — TAXES

2.1 The Alaska tax architecture in one paragraph

Alaska has no statewide general sales tax and no statewide retail cannabis tax. The only state-level cannabis tax is a weight-based cultivation excise imposed when a cultivation facility sells or transfers marijuana to a retail store or a product manufacturing facility. Local governments may — and many do — impose their own retail sales / marijuana taxes on top of that, and in places that already have a general local sales tax, marijuana sold at retail is normally also subject to that general tax unless the local code says otherwise. That stacking is the single most important point for any price or margin calculation: the state tax is paid by the cultivator by weight, the local tax (if any) is paid by the consumer at retail by price, and the two are collected by different governments on different returns.

Sources: AS 43.61.010 (see §2.2); Alaska Department of Revenue (DOR), Tax Division, Marijuana Tax pages — https://tax.alaska.gov/programs/programs/reports/AnnualData.aspx?60000 (opened 2026-11-01); Municipality of Anchorage (AMC ch. 12.50) and other local codes in Part 3.

2.2 State excise tax — statute, regulation, rates, who pays, when

2.2.1 Statute: AS 43.61.010

Citation: AS 43.61, “Excise Tax on Marijuana,” §43.61.010, “Marijuana tax.”

Operative text, verbatim (subsection (a)–(b), as published):

“(a) An excise tax is imposed on the sale or transfer of marijuana from a marijuana cultivation facility to a retail marijuana store or marijuana product manufacturing facility. Every marijuana cultivation facility shall pay an excise tax at the rate of $50 per ounce, or proportionate part thereof, on marijuana that is sold or transferred from a marijuana cultivation facility to a retail marijuana store or marijuana product manufacturing facility.

(b) The department may exempt certain parts of the marijuana plant from the excise tax described in (a) of this section or may establish a rate lower than $50 per ounce for certain parts of the marijuana plant.”

Source: AS 43.61.010 via akleg.gov statute text and Justia republication — https://law.justia.com/codes/alaska/title-43/chapter-61/section-43-61-010/ (accessed 2026-11-01). The akleg.gov folio link returned in search was https://www.akleg.gov/basis/folioproxy.asp?url=http://wwwjnu03.akleg.org/cgi-bin/folioisa.dll/stattx16/query=[jump!3A!27as4355014!27]/doc/{t18888}/pageitems={body}? — use the akleg.gov current-statutes page for the authoritative text before budgeting.

Last-amended date: Verify. This run did not open a current akleg.gov amendment-history page for AS 43.61.010 that would support a single “last amended” date, and no date is asserted here. The $50 statutory headline rate itself originates in Ballot Measure 2 (2014); the tiered rates below are regulatory, not statutory (see §2.2.2).

Who pays and when — the point that is most often misstated: the cultivator pays, on sale or transfer to a retail store or product manufacturer. The tax is therefore triggered upstream, before retail sale, and whether the product ultimately sells at retail does not remove the liability. Retail stores and manufacturers do not pay the AS 43.61 excise (they may pay local retail tax — Part 3 — and ordinary business taxes/fees).

Exemptions / lower rates: subsection (b) is the authority for the tiered regulation. DOR has used it to exempt certain parts and to set lower rates; it has not used it to raise any rate above $50/oz.

2.2.2 Regulation: 15 AAC 61.100 — the rates actually applied

The operative rate table is in regulation, not in the $50 statute:

Category (15 AAC 61.100) Rate Basis
Mature bud / bud or flower $50 per ounce (or proportionate part) Weight
Immature bud $25 per ounce Weight
Abnormal bud $25 per ounce Weight
Remainder of the plant (“trim” / other parts) $15 per ounce Weight
Clones $1 per plant (flat, not by weight) Per plant

Sources: 15 AAC 61.100 as reproduced in the AMCO Director’s Report to the Marijuana Control Board, 24 June 2026, Tab 4 — https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/Minutes/2026/6.24-25/Tab%204%20-%20%20MCB%20Directors%20Report.pdf?ver=nOiOtId9GwqJQazvdH382Q%3d%3d (accessed 2026-11-01), quoting: “(a) All nonexempt marijuana sold or transferred from a marijuana cultivation facility will be taxed as follows: (1) mature bud will be taxed at $50 an ounce; (2) immature bud will be taxed at $25 an ounce; (3) abnormal bud will be taxed at $25 an ounce; (4) the remainder of the plant will be taxed at $15 an ounce; and (5) clones will be taxed at $1 per plant.”; DOR Marijuana Tax Annual Report history, as summarised in secondary analysis — https://cannabisdataproject.com/alaska.html (accessed 2026-11-01).

Effective dates for the tiers:

  • Before 1 January 2019: only two rates existed in practice — $50/oz bud/flower and $15/oz remainder of the plant.
  • From 1 January 2019: the $25/oz rate for immature, seedy or failed bud and the $1-per-clone flat rate took effect through regulation. DOR’s own history, quoted in the secondary source above, states: “The changes, went into effect January 1, 2019 and added a third tax rate for immature or abnormal bud: $25 an ounce for buds or flowers that did not mature, that contain seeds or that failed testing because of mold. The changes stipulated that clones will be taxed at a flat rate of $1 per plant, and not by weight; and that packages of marijuana that contain a combination of any of the three tax rates will be taxed at the highest rate.”
  • Mixed packages: taxed at the highest applicable rate (per the DOR history quoted above). Verify the current regulatory text for this mixed-package rule before relying on it for a filing — it is stated here from DOR history as reported secondarily, not from a directly opened current 15 AAC 61.100 page.

What counts as “immature / abnormal” and what is exempt — Verify / live issue in 2026: At the June 2026 Marijuana Control Board meeting, AMCO’s Director reported that the Board had motioned to draft a letter to DOR on definitions of taxable marijuana, and supplied a table comparing 15 AAC 61.100 tax types, DOR definitions (15 AAC 61.990) and Marijuana Control Board definitions (3 AAC 306.990) — same AMCO Director’s Report, Tab 4, above. Board discussion in June 2026 also covered “wet-weight marijuana taxation,” with a licensee concern that unclear guidance had produced inconsistent tax reporting and unequal treatment. This is an open definitional dispute, not a settled rate change. Do not reclassify product on the basis of the June 2026 discussion alone.

No percentage / price-based state tax in force: There is no state ad valorem (percentage-of-price) cannabis tax in force at 1 November 2026. Every percentage proposal in §2.8 is pending or dead, not law.

2.2.3 What the state tax does NOT cover

  • No state tax on retail sale as such (no statewide sales tax at all, cannabis or otherwise).
  • No state tax on testing facilities, retail stores, or manufacturers as taxpayers under AS 43.61 — the taxable event is the cultivator’s sale/transfer.
  • No medical exemption in AS 43.61 as found in this run (see §2.7).
  • Compliant industrial hemp is outside the marijuana tax — see §2.9 (SB 208, ch. 60 SLA 2026).

2.3 Distribution of state revenue — AS 43.61.010(c) and (f)

Statutory split (of tax collected under AS 43.61.010):

Share Destination Statutory basis
50% Recidivism Reduction Fund (in the General Fund; separately accounted by the Department of Administration) — legislature may use the estimated balance to make appropriations to the Departments of Corrections, Health, and Public Safety for recidivism reduction programmes AS 43.61.010(c)
25% Marijuana Education and Treatment Fund (in the General Fund; separately accounted) — legislature may use the estimated balance to make appropriations to the Department of Health for the comprehensive marijuana use education and treatment programme under AS 44.29.020(a)(14) AS 43.61.010(f)
25% (residual) General Fund (unrestricted, subject to appropriation) By remainder; DOR annual-report presentation

Sources: AS 43.61.010(c), (f) — https://law.justia.com/codes/alaska/title-43/chapter-61/section-43-61-010/ (accessed 2026-11-01); DOR, “Disposition of Revenue” — DOR Marijuana Tax pages (via https://tax.alaska.gov/programs/programs/reports/AnnualData.aspx?60000 and the DOR annual-report pages, accessed 2026-11-01).

Important accounting nuance — “tax collected” vs appropriation: The funds are accounting subfunds within the General Fund, not lockboxes that automatically spend. The legislature appropriates from the estimated balances. Secondary reporting (Anchorage Daily News, “Curious Alaska,” 2021) describes the same 50/25/25 split in plain English. The DOR annual-report table itself presents the split as dollar amounts (see §2.4), and those dollar amounts do not always divide exactly 50/25/25 in a given fiscal year because the table reports collections/allocations as accounted, with penalties and interest in the total — see the FY2025 worked check in §2.4. Do not force the percentages onto the table without that caveat.

What the money is NOT: No portion of AS 43.61 revenue is, by that statute, dedicated to AMCO/Marijuana Control Board operations, to local governments, or to schools as a formula share. Local governments get local taxes (Part 3), not a share of the state excise.

2.4 State revenue — official figures

2.4.1 Official DOR annual-report table, FY2022–FY2025 (directly opened)

Source: Alaska DOR, Tax Division, “Marijuana Tax — Annual Report Data” — https://tax.alaska.gov/programs/programs/reports/AnnualData.aspx?60000 — opened and read directly 2026-11-01. Alaska’s fiscal year runs 1 July–30 June; “FY2025” below therefore means 1 July 2024–30 June 2025.

FY2025 FY2024 FY2023 FY2022
Tax collections $25,399,436 $26,963,435 $29,129,302 $28,353,379
Penalties and interest $313,044 $288,396 $348,215 $337,538
Total tax $25,712,480 $27,251,831 $29,477,517 $28,690,918
General Fund (as presented) $6,822,398 $6,957,951 $7,867,387 $6,774,912
Recidivism Reduction Fund $12,593,388 $13,529,253 $14,406,754 $14,610,670
Marijuana Education and Treatment Fund $6,296,694 $6,764,627 $7,203,377 $7,305,335

Check the reader should do: FY2025 fund lines sum to $25,712,480 exactly ($6,822,398 + $12,593,388 + $6,296,694 = $25,712,480). The General Fund line ($6,822,398) is 26.5% of the total, not 25.0% — the table’s “General Fund” line is a presentation of the residual/collections accounting, not a clean 25% calculation, and the Recidivism line is 49.0%. This is why §2.3 warns against forcing exact percentages onto a year’s table. The statutory 50/25 split is the accounting direction; the annual table is the accounted result.

Direction of travel: Total tax fell every year from FY2023 ($29.48m) to FY2025 ($25.71m) — down $3.77m (−12.8%) over two years. FY2025 is the lowest of the four years shown on the directly opened page. Secondary reporting (MMJ Daily, August/September 2026, citing state data) puts the fall in context: total revenue “FY2022 $28,690,918 to FY2025 $25,712,480; ‘$25.3m’ is Collections ($25,399,436) rounded, not the total” on a collections basis — the $25.3m figure there is tax collections excluding penalties/interest ($25,399,436 on the DOR page, rounded), not the $25,712,480 total. This report’s choice: use the DOR page’s “Total Tax” line as the headline total and always state whether a quoted figure includes penalties/interest. That resolves the apparent $25.3m vs $25.7m conflict for FY2025 — both are DOR figures on different lines.

2.4.2 Earlier fiscal years (DOR figures as reported secondarily — Verify against DOR archive before republication)

The directly opened DOR page shows only FY2022–FY2025. Earlier years below are DOR figures as tabulated from DOR by a secondary source and are included for trend only:

Fiscal year Total tax (DOR, via secondary tabulation)

Source for FY2017–FY2021: Motley Fool, “Marijuana Tax Revenue by State” (updated 2026, citing Alaska DOR 2026) — https://www.fool.com/research/marijuana-tax-revenue-by-state/ (accessed 2026-11-01). Agreement of FY2022–FY2025 with the directly opened DOR page increases confidence in the earlier series, but FY2017–FY2021 remain secondary-reported until the DOR archive page for those years is opened directly. Follow-up: open https://tax.alaska.gov/programs/programs/reports/Annual.aspx?60000&Year=YYYY for each earlier year.

Cumulative context (secondary, dated): The Marijuana Herald (8 April 2023), citing DOR, reported over $113,212,190 collected between January 2017 and end-2022, and almost 1.4 million ounces of dried flower sold in that period (flower only, excluding trim/shake). That cumulative figure is not updated to 2026 in this report — do not extend it by adding FY2023–FY2025 without stating the method, because the Herald’s window (calendar vs fiscal) is not stated precisely enough to sum cleanly.

2.4.3 Weight transferred — the other half of the DOR table (directly opened)

Source: same DOR Annual Report Data page, “Ounces” table (opened 2026-11-01):

Ounces sold/transferred FY2025 FY2024 FY2023 FY2022
Bud or flower 125,497 185,745 274,699 329,691
Immature / seedy / failed 353,012 372,984 300,997 239,014
Trim or other parts 692,187 572,952 510,027 471,230
Total ounces 1,170,696 1,131,681 1,085,723 1,039,935
Clones (number, not ounces) 4,293 4,009 5,795 8,162
Returns filed 2,813 2,878 3,095 3,031
Taxpayers (cultivators filing) 214 239 241 240

What this table proves, and what it does not:

  • Total weight transferred rose (1,039,935 oz in FY2022 → 1,170,696 oz in FY2025, +12.6%) while total tax fell (−10.4% over the same period). The composition explains it: high-tax bud collapsed (329,691 oz → 125,497 oz, −61.9%) while low-tax trim surged (471,230 oz → 692,187 oz, +46.9%) and $25 immature rose then eased. This is the quantitative core of the industry’s “miscategorisation / product-mix” complaint in 2026 reporting (see §2.8.4) — but this report states only the DOR weight shift; it does not assert that any particular cultivator misclassified product, which DOR/AMCO enforcement records would be needed to prove.
  • Implied check: 125,497 × $50 + 353,012 × $25 + 692,187 × $15 + 4,293 × $1 = $6,274,850 + $8,825,300 + $10,382,805 + $4,293 = $25,487,248. That is $87,812 above the DOR “Tax Collections” line ($25,399,436) and $225,232 below “Total Tax” ($25,712,480, which adds penalties/interest). The gap is expected: collections are cash received in the year, the weight table is quantities on returns, and late/amended returns move years. Do not present the multiplication as “the” revenue — present it as a reasonableness check only.
  • No retail sales figure exists in state data. Alaska taxes by weight at cultivation, so DOR publishes no statewide retail sales total. Any “Alaska cannabis sales $X” figure in secondary sources is either a local figure (e.g. Anchorage taxable retail sales — see §2.5) or a modelled estimate. This report does not publish a statewide retail-sales estimate.

2.4.4 Monthly revenue — exists, but not reproduced here

DOR publishes a monthly “Report of Marijuana Transferred or Sold.” Secondary analysis (Cannabis Data Project, accessed 2026-11-01 — https://cannabisdataproject.com/alaska.html) states that page returns 117 consecutive months (October 2016–June 2026), that reports post on the 10th of the month, and that prior months keep being updated for late/amended returns. It also warns that DOR prints a placeholder in every quantity cell of that monthly report, so weight must be derived from tax ÷ rate, and that monthly liability (returns filed for that month) differs from cash collections by 0.4%–4.2% in the years checked.

Gap for this report: the monthly DOR page itself was not opened and its monthly values were not transcribed in this run, so no monthly table is asserted here. This is a deliberate omission, not a claim that monthly data do not exist.

Follow-up (exact): open the DOR monthly marijuana report (linked from the DOR Marijuana Tax programme page, tax.alaska.gov) and transcribe, by month, tax by category for FY2025 and FY2026-to-date, stating for each month whether the figure is liability or collections and whether it has since been amended. Until that is done, any monthly claim should be marked Verify.

2.4.5 Delinquency — a material 2026 fact

AMCO/DOR tax-delinquency reporting in 2026 is material to revenue reliability:

  • 28 August 2026 report: $6.29 million past due from cultivators (12 active and 37 inactive licences on the list), up from $5.52 million in the 20 February 2026 report. Source: secondary synthesis of the AMCO/DOR delinquency report (CannBus Q3 2026 market report, citing AMCO/DOR, 28 August 2026 — https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/ak_cannmir.html, accessed 2026-11-01). Verify against the AMCO board packet for 2–3 September 2026 before quoting the licence split (12 active / 37 inactive) as official — the dollar total is the more widely repeated figure; the split is single-source here.
  • Enforcement posture (June 2026): AMCO’s Director told the Board the current policy is to address tax delinquency at licence renewal rather than through Notices of Violation, and that any change should occur only after public comment and through a Board request (MCB Minutes, 24–18 June 2026 — https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/Minutes/2026/9.2-9.3/Tab%202%20-%20June%2024th%20-25th%202026%20-%20MCB%20%20Meeting%20Minutes.pdf, accessed 2026-11-01). Renewal packets in 2026 therefore routinely flag “on DOR tax” status (see e.g. the 2026–27 renewal memoranda for licences in Homer/Kenai Peninsula Borough and elsewhere, which state the DOR-tax condition expressly).

2.5 Local retail sales as a market proxy — Anchorage only (do not generalise)

Because the state publishes no retail sales total, the only official retail-sales series found in this run is Anchorage’s:

  • Anchorage taxable marijuana sales, calendar 2025: $105.4 million (Municipality of Anchorage Finance Department, via Marijuana Herald, March 2026 — secondary reporting of a municipal figure; https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/10/anchorage-marijuana-sales-reach-51-4-million-in-first-half-of-2026-generating-2-57-million-in-taxes/ for the H1 2026 update, accessed 2026-11-01). Verify against the Municipality’s own tax-summary PDFs (see below) before using $105.4m as an official Anchorage figure — it is municipal data reported secondarily here.
  • Anchorage H1 2026: $51,397,967 in taxable sales, generating $2,569,898 in local marijuana sales tax (5%), an average of ~$428,000/month, per the Municipality’s Finance Department figures as updated 7 October 2026 and reported by the Marijuana Herald (same link). At 5%, $51,397,967 × 5% = $2,569,898 exactly, which corroborates the pair internally.
  • Anchorage tax summaries (official source to open directly): Municipality of Anchorage, “Marijuana Retail Sales Tax — Marijuana Statistics” — https://WWW.MUNI.ORG/Departments/finance/treasury/programtaxes/MarijuanaTax/Pages/MarijuanaStatistics.aspx — which states it publishes tax summaries by year (2025; 2023–2024; 2021–2022; 2019–2020; 2017–2018 PDFs), based on monthly returns, with the caveat that summaries are not audited, are not a component of the official financial statements, and do not necessarily equal revenue recognised under GASB (see the Municipality’s CAFR for recognised revenue). Follow-up: download the 2025 summary PDF directly and replace the secondary $105.4m figure with the municipal PDF figure.

No other borough/city retail-sales total is asserted in this report. Mat-Su’s adopted budget figure (Marijuana Sales Tax $1,850,000 adopted/amended for the year to 31 March 2025, with $881,711 year-to-date at 31 March 2025, per the Borough Manager’s Quarterly Activity Report, April 2025 — https://matsugov.us/docs/general/24722/Jan-Feb-Mar-2025-Managers-Report.pdf) is a budget figure, not a collections total, and is not comparable to Anchorage’s sales figure.

2.6 Other state taxes that touch cannabis businesses

2.6.1 No personal income tax — therefore no personal §280E question at state level

Alaska has no personal income tax. There is therefore no Alaska personal-income-tax §280E decoupling question to answer. Any “Alaska §280E” discussion is necessarily about the corporate income tax (and, federally, about the business’s federal return).

2.6.2 Corporate income tax and §280E — Alaska conforms (does not decouple)

  • Starting point: Alaska corporate income tax starts from federal taxable income (AS 43.20). There is no Alaska provision that states §280E does not apply for state corporate purposes.
  • Secondary tax-advisor statement (the clearest formulation found): “Does Section 280E apply to state corporate income tax? — Yes. The starting point for the Alaska corporate income tax is Federal taxable income. There is no provision that states Section 280E does not apply.” Source: AndreTaxCo, “Cannabis Alaska” — https://www.andretaxco.com/alaska-cannabis-280e (accessed 2026-11-01). The same page correctly notes Alaska has no personal income tax.
  • Corroboration / conflict — flagged, not hidden: A secondary aggregator (Cannabis Business Plans, “280E Deductions by State: 2026 Update” — https://cannabusinessplans.com/280e-deductions/, updated 2025-04-23 per its own page) lists Alaska among states that had “decoupled” by early 2026. A separate secondary synthesis (CannBus Legal Summary Q3 2026 — https://www.cannbus.org/lsum/ak_lsum.html) notes that claim, states it “could not be confirmed against a primary Dept. of Revenue source,” and advises verification with DOR before relying on it. This report’s choice: follow the AndreTaxCo / CannBus-cautious position (Alaska conforms; no decoupling provision found) and treat the aggregator’s “Alaska decoupled” claim as unconfirmed / Verify. No Alaska statute decoupling from §280E was located in this run. Follow-up: ask DOR Tax Division in writing whether any AS 43.20 provision or regulation decouples from IRC §280E, and cite the reply.
  • Corporate rate — Verify, do not quote from memory: This run did not open a current AS 43.20 rate schedule, so no corporate rate (graduated/bracket) is asserted here. Do not import a rate from another state’s file.
  • Federal consequence of the given baseline: For adult-use activity (all Alaska commercial sales), federal §280E continues to deny ordinary-business-expense deductions (COGS only), and Alaska’s conformity means the same limitation flows into the Alaska corporate starting point. For any activity that did qualify as state-licensed medical under the 28 April 2026 Schedule III order, federal §280E would generally be lifted for that activity — but, per the baseline note at the top of this report, whether any Alaska commercial activity qualifies is Verify, and Alaska has no medical licensee class to which it could attach. The practical planning point is therefore: do not budget Alaska adult-use operations as §280E-free on either the federal or the state return at 1 November 2026.
  • Pending bills touching corporate tax (not §280E decoupling): HB 94 (34th Leg., Rep. Tomaszewski) and SB 73 (34th Leg., Sen. Claman), both titled “Marijuana: Tax/Registration; Income Tax,” include a proposal to reinstate a qualified-small-business exemption under AS 43.20.012 (26 U.S.C. §1202(e) as read 1 Jan 2012) — see §2.8.3. That is a small-business exemption, not a §280E decoupling provision, and neither bill is enacted (see statuses in §2.8).

2.6.3 Other state levies — property, business licence, etc.

  • No state-level cannabis-specific property or business-licence tax was located in this run. Cannabis businesses pay the same generally applicable state business-licence and property-tax regime as other businesses, plus local property taxes and local business-licence fees where the municipality imposes them. Verify the current Alaska business-licence fee schedule before budgeting — it is outside the cannabis-specific sources checked here.
  • No state hemp product tax — see §2.9.

2.7 Medical programme — tax treatment (or absence of it)

  • There is no medical dispensary licence, no medical cultivation licence, and no medical tax rate in Alaska. The medical registry (AS 17.37, Department of Health) is a patient/caregiver registry only.
  • Registry patients and caregivers therefore buy, if they buy commercially at all, from AMCO adult-use retail stores, and pay: (i) the price that already reflects the cultivator’s AS 43.61 excise upstream, and (ii) any local retail/marijuana tax at the point of sale. There is no point-of-sale medical exemption in the local codes found in this run.
  • Do not confuse this with other states: In several states medical sales are exempt or taxed at a lower rate. That is not Alaska’s system. Any source claiming an “Alaska medical marijuana tax exemption” should be treated as incorrect for Alaska unless it cites a specific AS or local-code exemption — none was found in this run.

2.8 Proposals to change the state tax — pending, dead, and distinguished

Headline rule for this section: None of the bills below has changed the $50/$25/$15/$1 rates at 1 November 2026. The rates in §2.2.2 remain current law. Every bill below is described as a proposal with its akleg.gov status, not as law.

2.8.1 The long arc: Governor’s Advisory Task Force on Recreational Marijuana (2022)

Governor Dunleavy convened the Advisory Task Force on Recreational Marijuana in 2022 (Administrative Order, 21 September 2022, per secondary reporting). Its number-one recommendation, as reported in legislative materials for HB 119/HB 91, was tax reform — specifically that “a long-term tax solution better places the taxable transaction at the retail level” (task-force language as quoted in HB 119 supporting material). That recommendation is the policy ancestor of every bill in §2.8.2–§2.8.3. The task force itself did not change the law.

2.8.2 HB 119 — the 33rd Legislature vehicle (2023–2024) — House-passed, then died

  • Full title (33rd Leg.): “An Act relating to marijuana taxes; and providing for an effective date.” Text: https://www.akleg.gov/basis/Bill/Text/33?Hsid=HB0119A (accessed 2026-11-01).
  • Core mechanics (as introduced / as amended in House committee): cut AS 43.61.010(a) from $50/oz to $12.50/oz; allow DOR to set lower rates below $12.50; then repeal the cultivation excise and impose a retail sales tax — 3% in the introduced text (§(g): “A sales tax is imposed on the sale of marijuana and marijuana products from a retail marijuana store to a consumer. Every consumer shall pay a three percent sales tax…”), rising in House debate to 6% in the committee substitute and, in one House-floor account, 7% (see conflict note below).
  • What actually happened: The House passed a version in May 2024 (secondary reporting: Alaska House passed HB 119 on 10 May 2024; KNOM, 6 April 2023, and Cannabis Risk Manager summaries describe the House passage and the 7% figure debated on the floor). The bill did not become law in the 33rd Legislature — it stalled/died after House passage (Senate-side inaction; the 33rd Legislature adjourned). Do not cite HB 119 as current law, and do not cite the 7% figure as an enacted rate.
  • Fiscal-note impact (DOR, 19 January 2024, for CSHB 119(L&C)): changing $50 → $12.50 and then to a 6% retail tax mid-FY2025 would reduce revenue by an estimated $11.9 million in FY2025 (DOR note), with the reduction narrowing in later years with inflation/market growth. The Department of Health fiscal note for the same bill put the FY2025 reduction at $11.7 million ($2.9m General Fund / $5.8m Recidivism / $2.9m Education & Treatment). The $0.2m difference is a forecasting-vintage difference, not a conflict about the direction. Sources: DOR fiscal note — https://www.akleg.gov/pdf/33/F/HB0119-3-3-012224-REV-Y.PDF; DOH fiscal note — https://www.akleg.gov/pdf/33/F/HB0119-12-2-050324-DOH-Y.PDF (both accessed 2026-11-01).
  • Conflict note — 3% vs 6% vs 7%: All three percentages appear in sources for “HB 119” because they describe different versions/stages (introduced 3%, committee substitute 6%, floor debate 7% advocated by Rep. Sumner). This report does not pick one as “the HB 119 rate” — it states the version for each figure. Any single-number summary of HB 119 without a version is incomplete.

2.8.3 HB 91, SB 73, HB 94 — the 34th Legislature vehicles (2025–2026) — all pending, none enacted

Status source for all three: akleg.gov Subject Summary for MARIJUANA, 34th Legislature — https://www.akleg.gov/basis/Bill/Subject/34?subject=MARIJUANA (accessed 2026-11-01). Akleg.gov is treated as authoritative over secondary summaries.

Bill (34th Leg.) Short title (akleg.gov) Sponsor Akleg.gov status at 1 Nov 2026 Core tax proposal
HB 91 MARIJUANA: TAX/RETAIL STORES/REGISTRATION Rep. Ashley Carrick (H) RLS — House Rules Committee, 8 April 2026 (reported out of House Finance 8 April 2026 as CSHB 91(FIN): 4 do-pass, 1 do-not-pass, 3 amend; referred to Rules; no floor vote recorded) Cut cultivation excise $50 → $12.50/oz, then repeal it (CS(FIN) text: repeal 1 Jan 2028) and replace with a 6% retail sales tax from that date; plus biennial (instead of annual) registrations and other licensing changes
SB 73 MARIJUANA: TAX/REGISTRATION; INCOME TAX Sen. Matt Claman (S) FIN — Senate Finance Committee, 12 Feb 2025 Cut excise $50 → $12/oz (Senate version’s figure, per committee minutes 3 Feb 2025); reinstate qualified-small-business exemption under AS 43.20.012; biennial renewals
HB 94 MARIJUANA: TAX/REGISTRATION; INCOME TAX Rep. Tomaszewski (H) L&C — House Labor & Commerce Committee, 12 Feb 2025 House companion to SB 73 in substance: $50 → $12/oz; AS 43.20.012 small-business exemption; biennial renewals

Sources for HB 91 mechanics: CSHB 91(FIN) fiscal note, Dept. of Health, 8 April 2026 — https://www.akleg.gov/PDF/34/F/HB0091-11-2-040826-DOH-Y.PDF (“House Bill 91 reduces the marijuana excise tax from $50 per ounce to $12.50 per ounce and introduces a 6% retail sales tax…”); House Finance hearing 2 April 2026 (18-month transition amendment adopted; remaining amendments deferred); LegiScan summary for AK HB91 (2025–2026) — https://legiscan.com/AK/bill/HB91/2025 (“Action: 2026-04-08 — REFERRED TO RULES; Pending: House Rules Committee”). Sources for SB 73/HB 94 mechanics: Senate Labor & Commerce minutes, 3 Feb 2025 — https://www.akleg.gov/PDF/34/M/SL!C2025-02-031331.PDF; House Labor & Commerce minutes, 3 Mar 2025 — https://www.akleg.gov/PDF/34/M/HL!C2025-03-031516.PDF (both accessed 2026-11-01).

Conflict explicitly resolved: Two secondary sources (CannBus Legal Summary Q3 2026; Alaska Beacon summary as cited therein) state or imply HB 91 “passed the House” and was “advancing toward the Senate as of spring 2026.” That is inconsistent with akleg.gov, which shows HB 91 still in House Rules on 8 April 2026 with no floor vote, and with the Drug Policy Watch brief (accessed 2026-11-01) which states HB 91 “has not passed the House.” The most likely source of the error is confusion with HB 119 in the prior (33rd) Legislature, which did pass the House in May 2024. This report follows akleg.gov: HB 91 has NOT passed the House and is NOT law. A secondary Q3 2026 market report (CannBus) also states HB 91 “stalled in the 2026 session; the sponsor plans to reintroduce it” — consistent with the akleg.gov status (stalled in Rules), not with the “passed the House” claim in the same publisher’s legal summary. The internal inconsistency within that publisher is noted here so a later auditor does not pick the wrong sentence.

Other 2026 tax proposals — Verify: A secondary Q3 2026 report mentions “a Senate bill by Sen. Claman would cut the rate to $12 per ounce; its outcome was not verified.” That is SB 73 (above) — now verified as (S) FIN. No other 2026 statewide percentage-tax bill was confirmed in this run. Do not assume a 2027 reintroduction of HB 91 has occurred — at 1 November 2026 it is a stated intention (“plans to reintroduce”), not a filed bill in this report.

Fiscal impact of HB 91 as drafted (for scale only, not a forecast): The DOR fiscal note for the predecessor HB 119 (above) estimated an $11–12m first-year revenue reduction from a $12.50 + 6% structure. For HB 91 specifically, a secondary summary (MMJ Daily, 2026) reports DOR projected a reduction of $8.1 million in FY2027, declining to $5 million annually by 2032. Verify that HB 91-specific projection against the DOR fiscal note for CSHB 91(FIN) before using it — it is single-source secondarily here and the fiscal-note PDF for HB 91 opened in this run was the DOH note, not the DOR note.

Two-year legislature note: Alaska’s legislature sits in two-year sessions (33rd: 2023–2024; 34th: 2025–2026). A bill not enacted by the end of the second session dies and must be reintroduced in the next legislature. HB 119 (33rd) is therefore dead, not “pending.” HB 91/SB 73/HB 94 (34th) were still technically alive as pending committee bills at the end of the 2026 session as shown on akleg.gov, but they will die if not enacted before the 34th Legislature adjourns sine die / the next legislature convenes — Verify the sine die date and carryover rule on akleg.gov before describing any of them as “carried over” into 2027.

2.8.4 Why reform is debated — the industry/DOR facts, not just the politics

Secondary reporting in 2026 (MMJ Daily, citing growers/industry leaders and state data) makes three factual claims that are consistent with the DOR weight table in §2.4.3 and are therefore worth recording as context, not as official findings:

  1. Licensed grower numbers fell nearly 25% between 2023 and 2025 (AMCO figures as cited secondarily) — consistent in direction with the DOR taxpayer count falling 241 (FY2023) → 214 (FY2025), though the two counts measure different things (licences vs tax filers) and should not be conflated.
  2. Premium bud sales fell more than 70% between 2020 and 2025 while trim sales more than doubled — the DOR table confirms the direction for FY2022–FY2025 (bud −62%, trim +47%); the 2020 base year was not opened in this run, so the “70%” figure is secondary-only.
  3. The fixed weight tax creates a classification incentive (bud vs trim) and DOR oversight of classification is described as limited — this is an industry/DOR-process claim, not a finding that any named licensee misclassified. The June 2026 MCB discussion of “wet-weight” taxation and definitional confusion (see §2.2.2) is the official record closest to this claim.

2.9 Hemp taxes

2.9.1 No hemp product tax; marijuana tax does not apply to compliant hemp

  • No Alaska hemp excise, hemp sales tax, or hemp product tax was located in this run. Hemp businesses pay generally applicable taxes/fees (business licence, local sales tax where the jurisdiction imposes one on retail sales generally — Verify whether any local code expressly includes or excludes hemp products from its general sales tax base; none found here expressly addresses hemp).
  • SB 208 (34th Leg.), Chapter 60, Session Laws of Alaska 2026 — became law 10 August 2026 without the Governor’s signature (Senate 20–0 in May 2026; House 39–1 as amended; Senate concurred 17–3). Source: Marijuana Herald, 11 August 2026 — https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/08/alaska-industrial-hemp-reform-bill-becomes-law-without-governors-signature/ (accessed 2026-11-01); LawSignals tracker, August 2026 — https://lawsignals.com/r/cannabis-2026-08.pdf (SB 208 listed as became law 11 August 2026 — one-day date difference between sources: 10 vs 11 August; use “10–11 August 2026” or Verify the chapter’s effective-date page on akleg.gov before fixing a single day).
  • SB 208 tax clause (as reported): the measure “specifies that Alaska’s marijuana tax does not apply to compliant industrial hemp or products made from hemp containing no more than 0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis” (Marijuana Herald, same link). Verify the enrolled-text clause on akleg.gov (SB 208, 34th Leg., chapter 60 SLA 2026) before citing it as statutory text — this report states the clause as reported, not as a verbatim quote from the enrolled bill, because the enrolled text was not opened in this run.
  • Interaction with the federal hemp dates (given baseline): After 11 December 2026 the federal hemp definition becomes a full total-THC definition with a 0.4 mg/container cap. Whether Alaska’s 0.3% delta-9-based SB 208 carve-out will still align with the federal definition, and whether Alaska will conform its hemp tax/regulatory treatment to the new federal definition, is Verify / pending at 1 November 2026. No Alaska response to the 12 November / 11 December 2026 federal dates was located in this run. Do not assume SB 208’s 0.3% delta-9 test survives the federal change unchanged.

2.9.2 Hemp fees are not taxes

Division of Agriculture hemp registration/grower fees (covered in Part 7, not here) are regulatory fees, not taxes, and are not included in the revenue figures in §2.4. Do not add hemp fee revenue to marijuana tax revenue.
