Part 3 — Local Rules

PART 3 — LOCAL RULES

3.1 Alaska’s local system — how it works, in statute

3.1.1 The state framework: AS 17.38.210 (local control) and AS 17.38.200

AS 17.38.210(a) — prohibition power, verbatim:

“A local government may prohibit the operation of marijuana cultivation facilities, marijuana product manufacturing facilities, marijuana testing facilities, or retail marijuana stores through the enactment of an ordinance or by a voter initiative. An established village may prohibit the operation of marijuana cultivation facilities, marijuana product manufacturing facilities, marijuana testing facilities, or retail marijuana stores as provided in AS 17.38.300.”

Source: AS 17.38.210 — https://law.justia.com/codes/alaska/2015/title-17/chapter-17.38/section-17.38.210/ and FindLaw republication http://codes.findlaw.com/ak/title-17-food-and-drugs/ak-st-sect-17-38-210/ (accessed 2026-11-01). History note in the same sources: enacted as AS 17.38.110 by Ballot Measure 2 (2014), renumbered in 2015; amended ch. 53 SLA 2016 (HB 75) and ch. 13 SLA 2019.

AS 17.38.210(b) — time/place/manner/number power, verbatim in substance:

“A local government may enact ordinances or regulations not in conflict with this chapter or with regulations enacted pursuant to this chapter, governing the time, place, manner, and number of marijuana establishment operations. A local government may establish civil penalties for violation of an ordinance or regulation governing the time, place, and manner of a marijuana establishment that may operate in such local government.”

AS 17.38.210(c)–(f), (m) — what else a local government may do:

  • (c) Designate a local regulatory authority responsible for processing applications for registration to operate within its boundaries.
  • (d) Establish procedures for issuance, suspension and revocation of a local registration (subject to AS 44.62, the Administrative Procedure Act).
  • (e) Establish a schedule of annual operating, registration and application fees (application fee only if an application is submitted to the local government under (f)).
  • (f) The “direct-to-local” fallback if the Board fails to act within the statutory window (largely historical now that the Board issues licences; subsection (g) was repealed by §103 ch. 13 SLA 2019).
  • (m) Borough nonareawide limit: “Except as provided in AS 29, the exercise of the powers authorized by this section by a borough may be exercised only on a nonareawide basis. In this subsection, ‘nonareawide’ means throughout the area of a borough outside all cities in the borough.” (Added by ch. 53 SLA 2016.) Practical meaning: a borough’s marijuana ban or time/place/manner rule, when exercised under AS 17.38.210, applies outside cities in the borough; cities inside the borough make their own decisions. A borough-wide ban that purported to bind cities would exceed this subsection — which is why Mat-Su’s 2016 borough proposition was framed as “outside the cities” (see §3.3.2) and why borough and city taxes stack with credits/offsets (see Part 3, §3.4).

What a local government may NOT do:

  • It may not prohibit personal use and possession. AMCO’s Marijuana Local Option page states this expressly: “A local government may not prohibit the personal use and possession of marijuana and marijuana products.” Source: AMCO, Marijuana Local Option — https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/amco/MarijuanaLocalOption (search-result text accessed 2026-11-01; direct open of this page failed in this run — see §3.2.2 for the retrieval note). The same limit is in AS 17.38.330(c) for established-village prohibitions: “Nothing in this section prohibits the personal conduct authorized in AS 17.38.020.”
  • It may not conflict with AS 17.38 or 3 AAC 306. A local time/place/manner rule that conflicts with state law is vulnerable. The Wasilla 2015 example (see §3.3.2) — where home-edible and vehicle-limit rules were rolled back after state officials flagged conflict with state law / the constitutional bar on vetoing an initiative within two years — illustrates the limit, though the precise legal theory for each rolled-back provision is Verify against the Wasilla council record.
  • It may not use a local ban to defeat a state licence that is already operating without following the statutory effect rules for established villages (90-day voiding rule, §3.1.3) or, for incorporated municipalities, without an ordinance/voter initiative that actually prohibits that licence type. A mere protest (see §3.1.2) is a different, application-specific tool.

3.1.2 Local protests to individual licence applications — 3 AAC 306.060

This is not a ban. It is a case-by-case objection to a specific application or renewal, and it is one of the most frequently exercised local powers.

Mechanism (from AMCO renewal-notice language, repeated verbatim across 2026 renewal packets):

  1. When AMCO deems an application complete under 3 AAC 306.035(c), it sends the application electronically, in its entirety, to the local government, to the community council (if the premises are in Anchorage or certain Mat-Su locations), and to non-profits that have requested notification.
  2. The local government has 60 days to protest under 3 AAC 306.060. To protest, it must furnish the Director and the applicant with a clear and concise written statement of reasons, and provide AMCO proof of service on the applicant.
  3. Effect: “the board will uphold a local government protest and deny an application for a marijuana establishment license unless the board finds that a protest by a local government is arbitrary, capricious, and unreasonable.”
  4. Conditional protest: If the protest is a “conditional protest” as defined in 3 AAC 306.060(d)(2) and the application otherwise meets all regulatory criteria, the Board may approve the licence/renewal but require the applicant to show, to the Board’s satisfaction, that the local government’s requirements have been met before the Director issues the licence.
  5. Public objection (distinct): A public objection received within 30 days of the notice, under 3 AAC 306.065, also triggers independent Board consideration, as does a Notice of Violation or a local-government protest. Absent any of those triggers, renewal applications delegated to the AMCO Director (delegation from the 15 May 2017 Board meeting) may be approved without a Board vote.

Sources: AMCO renewal-notice language as reproduced in (i) AMCO memorandum for Licence #12833, Wildflower Farms, Homer / Kenai Peninsula Borough, 19 August 2026 — https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/Minutes/2026/9.2-9.3/Tab%2023%20-%2012833%20dba%20Wildflower%20Farms%20Renewal%20NOV%20.pdf (accessed 2026-11-01); (ii) City of Unalaska council memorandum, 13 October 2026, re Dutch Harbor Cannabis Co. (Bering Sea Buds) retail renewal — https://www.unalaska.gov/sites/default/files/fileattachments/mayor_and_city_council/meeting/10613/12a_retail_marijuana_store_renewal_application_-_dutch_harbor_cannabis_co.pdf (accessed 2026-11-01); (iii) City of Kodiak agenda item, 8 October 2026, re High Rise LLC retail renewal #20113 — https://cityofkodiak.civicweb.net/document/181214/Consideration%20of%20Marijuana%20Retail%20Store%20Renewal.pdf (accessed 2026-11-01). All three state the 60-day / arbitrary-capricious-unreasonable rule in identical terms.

Worked examples of protests in practice (not bans):

  • Juneau, October 2019: The City and Borough of Juneau Assembly voted 5–2 to protest renewal of Rainforest Farms’ cultivation and retail licences over almost $35,000 in unpaid 2019 sales taxes (Juneau Empire, 15 October 2019 — https://www.juneauempire.com/news/unpaid-taxes-cause-problems-for-juneau-marijuana-retailer/). This is a protest for non-payment, not a borough-wide prohibition, and it illustrates the most common local-protest ground found in this run: unpaid local taxes/fees.
  • Unalaska, October 2026: Staff recommended no protest for the sole retail store’s renewal (memorandum above) — the normal, uneventful outcome, included here to show that “no protest” is the default and that a council vote is still required to consider the question.
  • Kodiak, October 2026: The City Council, as the City’s Local Regulatory Authority (Ordinance No. 1365; KCC 5.24.020), considered the High Rise retail renewal (agenda above) under the same 60-day protest framework.

Local regulatory authority — why the council is the actor: Many municipalities have formally designated the council/assembly itself as the local regulatory authority (e.g. Kodiak City Council per Ord. 1365; Homer’s Cannabis Advisory Commission per HMC 2.78 in 2016; Sitka’s Marijuana Advisory Committee per MC 7.20.010 in 2016). Where no authority is established, the city council / borough assembly acts by default — the 2016 AMCO/DCRA spreadsheet states this formula repeatedly (“No established local regulatory authority, so city council [or borough assembly] by default”). Verify each municipality’s current designation before filing — designations change by ordinance.

3.1.3 The Unorganized Borough and established villages — AS 17.38.300–17.38.350

Alaska’s Unorganized Borough is the large area of the state outside any organised borough. It has no borough government — no assembly, no borough tax, no borough zoning. State services are provided directly by the state, and incorporated cities inside it (e.g. Bethel, Nome — both are cities in the Unorganized Borough, not borough seats) govern themselves. Unincorporated communities in the Unorganized Borough are handled through a special “established village” local-option system, not through a borough vote.

Definitions (AS 17.38.900, as summarised in AS 17.38.300 materials): “established village” means “an area that does not contain any part of an incorporated city or another established village and that is an unincorporated community that is in the unorganized borough and that has 25 or more permanent residents.”

AS 17.38.300 — adoption of a prohibition (verbatim in substance):

“(a) If a majority of the voters voting on the question vote to approve the option, an established village shall exercise a local option to prohibit the operation of one or more of the following types of marijuana establishments: (1) marijuana cultivation facilities; (2) marijuana product manufacturing facilities; (3) marijuana testing facilities; or (4) retail marijuana stores.

(b) A ballot question to adopt a local option under this section must at least contain language substantially similar to the following: ‘Shall (name of village) adopt a local option to prohibit (specify local option under (a) of this section)? (yes or no).’”

Source: AS 17.38.300 — https://law.justia.com/codes/alaska/title-17/chapter-38/section-17-38-300/ and https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/alaska/ak-statutes/alaska_statutes_17-38-300 (accessed 2026-11-01). Enacted by ch. 53 SLA 2016 (HB 75).

AS 17.38.310 — removal: A majority may vote to remove a previously adopted option; the option is repealed effective the first day of the month following certification. A removal election may not be held during the first 24 months after adoption, nor more than once in a 36-month period.

AS 17.38.320 — effect on registrations: If voters prohibit establishments, “the board may not issue, renew, or transfer, between persons or locations, a registration for a marijuana establishment located within the perimeter of the established village. A registration that may not be renewed because of a local option election held under AS 17.38.300 is void 90 days after the results of the election are certified.” (A registration expiring during those 90 days may be extended until void by payment of a prorated fee.)

AS 17.38.330 — prohibition of sale/manufacture after election: If voters prohibit, “a person may not knowingly sell or manufacture marijuana in the established village,” effective 90 days after certification if registered establishments exist there. Violation is a class A misdemeanour, each violation a separate offence. Subsection (c) preserves personal conduct authorised by AS 17.38.020.

AS 17.38.340 — procedure / petition (the Unorganized Borough petition route):

“Upon receipt of a petition of 35 percent or more of the registered voters residing within an established village, the lieutenant governor shall place on a separate ballot at a special election the local option or removal of local option that constitutes the subject of the petition. The lieutenant governor shall conduct the election under AS 15.”

In other words: there is no borough assembly to petition in the Unorganized Borough. The route is a 35%-of-registered-voters petition to the Lieutenant Governor (as chief elections official), who places the question on a separate ballot at a special election under AS 15. After a petition is certified sufficient, no further petition may be filed or certified until the first question has been voted on. A question to prohibit one, several, or all establishment types may be presented in one election.

Source: AS 17.38.340 — akleg.gov folio text via https://www.akleg.gov/basis/folioproxy.asp?url=http://wwwjnu03.akleg.org/cgi-bin/folioisa.dll/stattx16/query=[jump!3A!27as1738310!27]/doc/{t8735}/pageitems={body}? (search-result text accessed 2026-11-01).

AS 17.38.350 — perimeter: The perimeter of an established village is established under AS 17.38.350 (procedure not reproduced here; Verify the current perimeter-establishment rule before mapping any village boundary — village perimeters are fact-specific and are not the same as city limits or census boundaries).

Critical historical nuance — the “permit” vs “prohibit” flip: The 2d CS for CSHB 75 (29th Leg.) as introduced would have made the Unorganized Borough default prohibited outside municipalities, with established villages voting to permit establishments. The enacted ch. 53 SLA 2016 instead adopted the prohibit-by-vote model quoted above (villages vote to prohibit; absent a vote, establishments are not barred by village option alone). Do not cite the introduced “permit” language as law — it was not enacted in that form. Source: 2d CS HB 75 text — https://www.akleg.gov/basis/Bill/Plaintext/29?Hsid=HB0075G (accessed 2026-11-01), compared with enacted AS 17.38.300.

Practical consequence: In the Unorganized Borough, the absence of a borough ban does not mean a village has “allowed” cannabis — it means no village-option prohibition is in effect. Conversely, a village prohibition does not extend beyond its perimeter, and it does not prohibit personal possession/use. Any list that labels an entire Unorganized Borough census area as “banned” or “allowed” on the basis of one village vote is wrong.

3.1.4 Zoning and buffers — state floor, local layer

State buffer — 3 AAC 306.010(a) (verbatim in substance, as amended):

“The board will not issue a marijuana establishment license if the licensed premises will be located within 500 feet of a school ground, a recreation or youth center, a building in which religious services are regularly conducted, or a correctional facility. The distance… must be measured by the shortest pedestrian route from the public entrance of the building in which the licensed premises would be located to the outer boundaries of the school ground, the outer boundaries of the recreation or youth center, the main public entrance of the building in which religious services are regularly conducted, or the main public entrance of the correctional facility.”

Exceptions: renewal of an existing licence, conversion under 3 AAC 306.047, or transfer to another person are not barred if the premises were in use before the protected use began within 500 feet; if such a licence is revoked or expires, no new licence for the same premises may issue while the protected use remains within 500 feet.

Source: 3 AAC 306.010(a) (eff. 21 Feb 2016, Register 217; amendment text via Alaska Online Public Notices, 2018 — https://aws.state.ak.us/OnlinePublicNotices/Notices/Attachment.aspx?id=113967, accessed 2026-11-01). Last-amended date for the current 3 AAC 306.010 text: Verify — the 2018 amendment notice is the latest amendment document located in this run, but a later amendment cannot be excluded without opening the current AAC text.

Local buffers and zoning — the layer on top:

  • Local governments may impose stricter time/place/manner rules (including zoning districts, setbacks beyond 500 feet, and separation from other marijuana establishments) provided they do not conflict with state law. They may not, by local rule alone, waive the state 500-foot buffer for a new licence — the Board “will not issue” language is a state bar.
  • Historical pressure to reduce the buffer: In 2015–2016, Sitka’s Marijuana Advisory Committee and Petersburg’s assembly both voted to ask the state to reduce the buffer to 200 feet (Sitka resolution: 200 feet from churches, schools and youth centres; Juneau Empire, 24 December 2015 — http://juneauempire.com/state/2015-12-24/pot-panel-oks-buffer-zone-motion; Marijuana Times, 23 February 2016 — https://marijuanatimes.org/alaskans-are-asking-for-looser-zoning-restrictions-for-marijuana-shops/), arguing the 500-foot rule left almost no viable sites in compact island towns. The state did not adopt a 200-foot statewide rule in the materials found here. The 500-foot state rule therefore remains the floor to plan against, and any local claim of a “200-foot buffer” should be treated as Verify unless a current local code adopting it (and its interaction with the state bar) is produced.
  • Anchorage-specific layer: Anchorage requires a Special Land Use Permit (SLUP) for marijuana in addition to the state licence (AMC Title 21, §21.03.105C; AMC Title 10, ch. 10.80). See §3.4.1.
  • Mat-Su-specific layer: The Borough’s conditional-use permit (CUP) system, including a 100-foot setback in the CUP as adopted in 2016–2017 (the Assembly declined to eliminate the 100-foot setback — see §3.4.4), operates in addition to the state 500-foot buffer, not instead of it. The two setbacks measure different things (CUP setback vs state protected-use buffer) and must not be conflated.

3.1.5 Local fees — general principle

AS 17.38.210(e) authorises local annual operating, registration and application fees. The amounts are set locally and vary widely; only Anchorage’s SLUP fee was confirmed from a current code page in this run ($1,700 — see §3.4.1). All other local fee figures in §3.4 are either dated (2016–2017) or marked Verify. Do not budget a local fee from a 2016 spreadsheet.

3.2 The official AMCO list of local options — what exists, what does not

3.2.1 The 2016 DCRA/AMCO spreadsheet (the only comprehensive list located)

Title / file: “Marijuana Local Option” spreadsheet / “Commercial Marijuana Local Options” table, compiled by the Department of Commerce, Community, and Economic Development (DCCED), Division of Community and Regional Affairs (DCRA) staff, with information last confirmed March 2016 (most entries dated 1–11 March 2016; a few May/August 2016).

Exact URLs (two forms of the same file, both returned by search):

  • https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/OtherResources/Marijuana-local-option.pdf
  • https://Www.Commerce.Alaska.Gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/OtherResources/LocalOptionspreadsheet.pdf

Both were returned as search results (accessed 2026-11-01 via search-result text). The file itself was not opened page-by-page in this run, and its contents below are taken from search-result extracts of that file, not from a full read. That limitation is stated plainly because the commission requires it.

What the spreadsheet contains (columns, as extracted): Community | Date information last confirmed by DCRA staff | Local regulatory authority (and establishing citation) | Any local commercial marijuana restrictions? (Yes/No) | Full bans adopted by ordinance/referendum/initiative? (Ban on sale / Ban on retail store / Ban on cultivation facility / Ban on manufacturing facility / Ban on testing facility — each marked X where fully banned) | Other unique marijuana business restrictions? (time/place/manner/number) | Local taxation info? | Marijuana law references (code chapters) | Alcohol local options (for comparison, as of 11 March 2016).

What it does NOT contain: It is not a current list. It is a March 2016 snapshot, compiled before most licences were issued and before many 2016–2017 bans, repeals and tax votes. Several entries in it were already time-limited when published (e.g. Kodiak Island Borough’s ordinance “expires Feb 2017” — see §3.3.2). Using it as a 2026 “banned list” without checking the current code would be wrong.

3.2.2 The current AMCO “Marijuana Local Option” page — under construction, with a disclaimer

URL: https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/amco/MarijuanaLocalOption

What the page says (search-result text, accessed 2026-11-01):

“A local government may prohibit the sale or importation for sale of marijuana and any marijuana product and the operation of any marijuana establishment through the enactment of an ordinance or by a voter initiative. The local option laws are found in Title 17.38 of the Alaska Statutes and Title 3 of the Alaska Administrative Code (AAC).

A local government may not prohibit the personal use and possession of marijuana and marijuana products.

  • UNDER CONSTRUCTION!

Disclaimer: This is not a comprehensive list of local government ordinances or actions regarding commercial marijuana. Alaska statute does not obligate communities to notify the Alcohol & Marijuana Control Office of any changes to local ordinances restricting commercial marijuana. If you have a question about a particular local government, please contact the community directly.“

Retrieval note (required by the commission): A direct open of https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/amco/MarijuanaLocalOption failed in this run (browser tool error, not a finding that the page does not exist). The page content above is therefore taken from the search engine’s indexed text for that URL, not from a fresh open, and is marked accordingly. The page’s own disclaimer is the key finding regardless of retrieval method: AMCO itself states it does not maintain a comprehensive, up-to-date list, because communities are not obligated to notify AMCO of ordinance changes. Any dossier that presents a “complete current banned list” for Alaska without that disclaimer is overstating AMCO’s data.

Consequence for §3.3: The ban/allow table below is therefore built from (a) the 2016 spreadsheet extracts, (b) dated election/ban reporting, and (c) current-code/tax pages where found — and every entry is dated. Where no post-2016 confirmation was found, the entry is labelled “2016 snapshot only — current status Verify.” That is not a gap to be papered over; it is the accurate state of the official record.

3.2.3 Where to check instead (the follow-up route)

For any specific community, the reliable route at 1 November 2026 is, in order:

  1. The municipality’s current code (Municode / CivicWeb / municipal site) — search “[municipality] marijuana” in the code.
  2. The municipality’s council/assembly minutes for the most recent marijuana licence protest or ordinance (protests are minuted even when bans are not published).
  3. The Division of Elections / municipal clerk’s certified election results for any local-option vote.
  4. AMCO licence search by location (does a licence currently exist there? — absence is weak evidence of a ban, but presence is strong evidence of allowance).
  5. Direct contact with the community (AMCO’s own advice).

Do not rely on the 2016 spreadsheet alone for any investment decision.

3.3 Which places have banned or allowed each licence type

3.3.1 How to read this section

  • “Banned” means a prohibition of that licence type by ordinance or voter initiative under AS 17.38.210 (incorporated municipalities/boroughs) or AS 17.38.300 (established villages), as dated.
  • “Allowed” means either (a) voters rejected a ban, or (b) licences of that type are currently operating / have been renewed without a ban — with the evidence stated. “Allowed” is not inferred from silence alone.
  • Licence types are: Cultivation (C), Product Manufacturing (M), Testing (T), Retail (R). Onsite consumption is an endorsement, not a licence type, and is treated separately where noted.
  • No statewide count of “banned municipalities” is asserted as official. A secondary synthesis (CannBus Legal Summary Q3 2026) claims “~9 of 165 incorporated municipalities (≈5%) have banned adult-use retail sales.” That figure is not adopted here: its methodology (which bans, as of when, retail-only vs all types) is not stated, and it conflicts with the 2016 spreadsheet’s much longer list of communities with some restriction. The honest official position is: AMCO does not publish a current count (see §3.2.2), so any count is an estimate. Verify any count before quoting it.

Places with NO ban/allow finding in this run (do not fill in): For the remaining required places — Kenai (city), Soldotna (city), Seward (city) — no specific ban or allowance vote was located in this run beyond the general Kenai Peninsula Borough sales-tax context (§3.4.5). Their inclusion in §3.4 is therefore tax/fee/buffer only, with ban status marked Verify. The same applies to most small Unorganized Borough villages: the 2016 spreadsheet’s alcohol-option column (e.g. “Ban on sale, importation, and possession” for Alakanuk, Kotlik, etc.) describes alcohol local options, not marijuana bans, and must not be copied across as marijuana bans — a misreading the spreadsheet’s layout invites and this report expressly avoids.

3.3.3 Election results — what is certified vs what is reported

Only two local-option election results in this section are stated with certified-style vote counts from an official or borough source:

  • Mat-Su Prop. B-1 (ban, outside cities): FAILED 8,704–7,271 (4 Oct 2016, certified 18 Oct 2016 — Borough source, §3.3.2).
  • Mat-Su Prop. B-5 (5% marijuana tax): PASSED 13,266–2,536 (same election — Borough source, §3.3.2). This is a tax vote, not a ban vote, but it is included because it is routinely cited alongside B-1 and because its passage alongside B-1’s failure shows voters distinguishing taxation from prohibition.

All other “votes” in §3.3.2 are council/assembly votes (e.g. Wasilla unanimous council ban, 25 Jan 2016) or are reported without a certified count. No Division of Elections certified local-option result for any established-village AS 17.38.300 election was located in this run — if such elections have occurred, they are a gap (see §3.7). Do not invent a village vote count.

3.4 Local taxes, fees and buffer rules — place by place

General warning for this whole section: A “general sales tax” and a “marijuana-specific tax” are different levies. A place can have one, both, or neither. The table in §3.4.14 separates them. Where a place has a general sales tax, retail marijuana sales are normally taxable under it (the product is not exempt merely because it is marijuana), but the local code’s exemption list must be checked — e.g. Juneau’s code lists exemptions, and Kodiak’s 2018 ordinance made marijuana taxable for seniors (i.e. removed a senior exemption for marijuana/alcohol/tobacco). Do not assume a general rate automatically equals the marijuana rate where a marijuana-specific rate also exists.

3.4.1 Municipality of Anchorage (unified home-rule municipality)

  • Government type: Unified home-rule municipality (not a borough, not a city inside a borough). Population ~291,247 (Alaska Municipal League directory, 2026 — https://www.akml.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/01/municipality-of-anchorage.pdf).
  • General sales tax: None. Anchorage has no general sales tax. (This is why its marijuana tax is a standalone levy, not a surcharge on a general tax.)
  • Marijuana-specific tax: 5% retail sales tax on marijuana and marijuana products.
  • Authority / history: Voter-approved Proposition 2, 5 April 2016 (authorising a 5% tax); Assembly adopted administration/collection/enforcement by AO 2016-49(S), 10 May 2016, enacting AMC Chapter 12.50, “Retail Sales Tax on Marijuana and Marijuana Products.” Source: Surety One summary of AMC 12.50.160 and AO 2016-49(S) — https://suretyone.com/anchorage-marijuana-sales-tax-bond (accessed 2026-11-01); AIM 171-2016 (20 Dec 2016) — https://www.muni.org/Lists/AssemblyListDocuments/Attachments/625470/AIM%20171-2016%20OCR.pdf (“in April, the ballot measure establishing a five percent tax on marijuana retail sales was approved by the voters, and the Assembly adopted the sales tax administration, collection, and enforcement procedures in May (AO 2016-49(S))”).
  • Rate cap / increase rule (secondary, Verify against AMC 12.50): The rate may be increased by up to 2% every two years, capped at 12%. A 2018 proposal to raise 5% → 7% was rejected 8–2 by the Assembly (420 Magazine, 2018 — https://www.420magazine.com/420-news/international-cannabis-news/ak-anchorage-assembly-rejects-additional-marijuana-tax-proposal/). The rate has therefore remained 5% since 2016. Verify the 12% cap and biennial-increase rule in current AMC 12.50 before advising on a future increase — the cap is stated here from secondary reporting, not from a directly opened AMC 12.50 page.
  • Filing: Monthly by the retailer (AMC 12.50; Municipality’s Marijuana Statistics page, §2.5). Retailers must provide a financial guarantee (surety bond or acceptable assurance) under AMC 12.50.160, in an amount estimated as average annual marijuana sales tax collected (or $10,000, whichever is greater), for the licence term (AMC 12.50.080).
  • Dedication — Verify / secondary: A secondary source (EducationSin, reporting Anchorage Proposition 14, April 2023, “Take Care of Kids”) states the 5% marijuana tax revenue will now fund child care and early education rather than the general fund, at $5–6 million/year. This dedication is NOT confirmed against AMC 12.50 or a Municipality finance page in this run and must be verified before budgeting or reporting it as current law. The Municipality’s own tax-summary page (§2.5) does not state a dedication. Do not present the child-care dedication as settled on the basis of the secondary source alone.
  • Licensing / land use:
  • Municipal licence + Special Land Use Permit (SLUP) required in addition to the state licence (AMC Title 10, ch. 10.80; AMC Title 21, §21.03.105C). A 2026 Assembly ordinance (AO 2026-42, laid on the table 24 March 2026) proposes repealing and reenacting AMC ch. 10.80 to incorporate state law by reference — pending, not enacted as of the March 2026 document; Verify whether AO 2026-42 was subsequently adopted before citing current AMC 10.80 text. Source: AO 2026-42 packet — https://meetings.muni.org/AgendaOnline/Documents/DownloadFileBytes/AO%202026-42_1_WITH%20AM%20AND%20CROSSWALK_%20AMC%2010.8O%20REWRITE_FINAL.DOCX.pdf (accessed 2026-11-01).
  • SLUP fee (current code page): $1,700 for “Special Land Use Permit for Marijuana (all license types)”; $850 for Assembly-considered modification; $425 for director-considered modification. Source: AMC Regulation 21.20 (Municode) — https://library.municode.com/ak/anchorage/codes/code_of_ordinances?nodeId=TIT21PLZO_REG21.20REGOLAUSFE_21.20.007SCFEISFE (accessed 2026-11-01).
  • Zoning in practice: Retail in commercial (B-3 SL) districts; cultivation/manufacturing in industrial districts (2016 proposal/adoption history — AO 2016-3(S), AO 2016-16(S), Feb 2016; amended Apr 2016 by AO 2016-35 for Chugiak-Eagle River separation measurement). Verify current AMC Title 21 use tables — district permissions have been amended since 2016 and the 2016 summary is not a substitute for the current table.
  • Buffer: State 500-foot buffer (3 AAC 306.010) applies; Anchorage’s 2016 proposal would have expanded the protected-use list (parks, community centres, homeless shelters, halfway houses, therapy centres) beyond schools/jails/churches/recreation centres — Verify whether any expansion was adopted in current AMC Title 21; do not assume the proposal became law.
  • Hours (2016 proposal, Verify current): 2016 proposal: no operation midnight–8 a.m. Verify current AMC hours — not confirmed in this run.
  • 2026 operating evidence: SLUP Case 2026-0055, Sensimilla (Baltazar Holdings LLC, SOA Licence #35490), 1431 Muldoon Road, Anchorage — staff recommendation “Approval with conditions,” 4 August 2026 — https://www.muni.org/CityViewPortal/Planning/GetFile?docId=130564 (accessed 2026-11-01). The case file includes a “Marijuana Licenses Within 1,000 Feet” checklist — a municipal separation check in addition to the state 500-foot buffer. Do not confuse the 1,000-foot checklist radius with a 1,000-foot prohibition.

3.4.2 Fairbanks North Star Borough and City of Fairbanks (and North Pole)

Borough/city structure — keep apart: The Fairbanks North Star Borough (FNSB) is an organised borough. The City of Fairbanks and the City of North Pole are separate cities inside the borough. Borough marijuana powers under AS 17.38.210(m) are nonareawide (outside cities); city taxes apply inside city limits, with a borough credit to avoid double-taxing the same sale (see below).

  • General sales tax: None — neither the Borough nor the City of Fairbanks imposes a general sales tax. (Fairbanks is therefore like Anchorage in having no general tax, but unlike Anchorage it has two layers of marijuana-specific tax that must be credited against each other.)
  • Borough marijuana tax: 5% on retail sales of marijuana and marijuana products, under FNSB Code (FNSBC) 8.54.
  • Form / mechanics (official form, directly described): FNSB “Marijuana and Marijuana Products Sales Tax Report” — gross sales, less exempt sales (FNSBC 8.54.040), × 5%, less sales tax paid on the same sales to any municipality within the borough (with documentation), = tax due. Late penalty 10% (or $50 for zero-balance reports) plus additional 25%; interest 1.25%/month (form reverse). Source: FNSB form — https://fnsb.gov/DocumentCenter/View/168/Marijuana-and-Marijuana-Product-Sales-Tax-Report-PDF and https://www.fnsb.gov/DocumentCenter/View/26932/MJT-REPORT (accessed 2026-11-01).
  • History: Borough Assembly approved putting a 5% question to voters in March 2016, for effect 1 January 2017, with authority to later increase to 6% (Juneau Empire, 30 March 2016 — https://www.juneauempire.com/2016/03/30/fairbanks-borough-voters-to-decide-on-5-pct-pot-tax/?ia_markup=1). The borough collects the city taxes on behalf of the cities (same source). Verify whether the 6% increase authority was ever exercised — the current form still shows 5%, so no increase is assumed here.
  • Outside-city effect: A retailer outside Fairbanks/North Pole city limits pays the full 5% to the Borough. A retailer inside a city pays the city tax first and credits it against the Borough 5% (see city rows) — so the combined marijuana rate inside Fairbanks is 5%, not 10%, provided the credit is claimed with documentation. This is the single most common Fairbanks calculation error; the form’s Line 5 exists precisely to prevent it.
  • City of Fairbanks marijuana tax: 5% retail sales tax, Ordinance 6172 (City Code).
  • History: City Council unanimously approved the ordinance (sponsored by Councilman David Pruhs) in 2016 for voter ratification in the October general election, as required by the Fairbanks Charter for any new sales tax other than hotel/motel/alcohol/tobacco; voters approved it “by a wide margin” (fall 2015/2016 reporting — the approval year is stated as “last fall” in March 2016 reporting, i.e. fall 2015 for the ordinance’s voter approval, with effect tied to legal sales in 2016 — Verify the exact election date and effective date in City minutes before citing them). Source: Alaska Cannabist, 2016 — https://www.alaskacannabist.com/news/fairbanks-city-council-approves-5-percent-marijuana-tax/article_c3066d84-8dfd-5f96-be7e-12b06e0acdbf.html; Juneau Empire, 30 Mar 2016 (same link as above).
  • Mechanics (2016 ordinance summary, Verify current code): Buyer is responsible for the tax; seller reports/pays by the 15th of the following month; timely filers reimbursed 2% for collection costs; seller must hold state + city business licences, a current state marijuana licence, and (if applicable) a city marijuana licence before obtaining a tax-registration certificate. Tax falls under the City’s tax cap (so marijuana revenue can reduce property mill rates if it exceeds the cap — a charter consequence, not a dedication).
  • City of North Pole marijuana tax: [DELETED — the 6% figure was an alcohol-table rate; North Pole facilities NOT allowed (FNSB Spring 2025)] (secondary reporting: Alaska Business Magazine, December 2024, “Flower Power” — https://digital.akbizmag.com/issue/december-2024/flower-power/; FreeLibrary republication — https://www.thefreelibrary.com/Flower+Power%3a+Ten+years+of+legalized+cannabis.-a0822528666). North Pole’s tax was authorised by City Council (not by the same voter route as Fairbanks) and, in 2016 reporting, applied to both retail and wholesale transactions (Juneau Empire, 30 Mar 2016). Verify current North Pole code — the 6% rate and the wholesale scope are both secondary here and the wholesale scope, if still in force, would be unusual (most local taxes are retail-only).
  • Combined rates (with credit, as understood): Inside Fairbanks city limits: 5% total (city 5%, borough credited to zero if fully documented). Inside North Pole: 6% city tax credited against the 5% borough tax — Verify how the credit works when the city rate exceeds the borough rate (whether the excess 1% is retained by the city with no borough amount due, or whether any borough amount remains). The FNSB form’s Line 5 (“subtract tax paid to any municipality”) implies the borough amount is reduced to zero, not below zero, but the code text (FNSBC 8.54) was not opened in this run to confirm the excess treatment.
  • Fees / buffers: Verify. No current Fairbanks city or FNSB marijuana licence fee schedule or local buffer beyond the state 500-foot rule was confirmed in this run. The 2016 spreadsheet cites City Code Chs. 46, 74 and Borough Code Chs. 9.12, 18.28.020 — those chapter numbers are 2016 references, not a 2026 fee/buffer statement.

3.4.3 City and Borough of Juneau (unified home-rule municipality)

  • Government type: Unified home-rule municipality (City and Borough of Juneau, CBJ) — a single government for the former city and borough. Do not describe Juneau as “a city in a borough.”
  • General sales tax: 5% (areawide sales tax) — but see seasonal nuance below.
  • Marijuana-specific tax: 3% on retail sale of marijuana and marijuana products, under CBJ 69.05.020(e), in addition to the 5% general sales tax = 8% combined on retail marijuana.
  • Verbatim (CBJ 69.05.020(e), as quoted in CBJ Administrative Guidelines, Procedure 800): “In addition to the tax levied and collected under subsection (a), there shall be levied and collected a tax equal to three percent of the selling price on the retail sale of marijuana and marijuana products sold within the City and Borough.”
  • Sources: CBJ Administrative Guidelines, “Tax on the Retail Sale of Marijuana and Marijuana Products” (Procedure 800) — https://juneau.org/gk-download/c7cbqIjOQ6Eie7XR4vbb6tWJhMTocyhpJK6KDfy-QrfsM004P2AHzQTF01i2FzmYStJ44D2V35gUvd1JhNsg4lu3SmnbNP_sToTtYIRCte09vgk16I9MHlGknOKrlKgkHon7p5nzqO4Jr6C2jyNF_SyaNgvGRs8qXHKsYNpESdrWks0Tk7Qw1KFjHDBBS3AeT5ifRICDPCJ-r7Wd7SDAvCz6dR03X28MDsn6xdVjMr_xxY0_KVVYTT5JTERJjAB7pbB3pmomFkC6lw8uWuhjaP811VOmtJ244bbcNf1Csg/?gv-iframe=true (accessed 2026-11-01); CBJ Finance, “Business Registration” / New Merchant Packet 2026 — https://juneau.org/finance/business-registration-and-sales-tax and https://juneau.org/index.php?gf-download=2026%2F01%2FNewBusPacket_2026.pdf (both accessed 2026-11-01) — “Sales Tax Rate 5% — Liquor and Marijuana Tax Rate 3%” and “retail sales of marijuana products are taxed at the total rate of 8%.”
  • Seasonal nuance — conflict flagged: A secondary source (LegalClarity, 2026) states Juneau’s general sales tax is now seasonal (2% winter / 6.5% summer, plus a 1% temporary surcharge from 1 Oct 2023 = 3% winter / 7.5% summer combined, plus 3% marijuana = 8% flat on May/Oct 2026 CBJ returns (seasonal 10.5% claim rejected)). That seasonal structure conflicts with the CBJ’s own 2026 New Merchant Packet and Combined Return (May 2026), which still show a flat “5% Sales / 3% Liquor or Marijuana” column structure (https://juneau.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/Combined-Tax-Return-v.5.1.2026.pdf). This report’s choice: follow the CBJ’s own 2026 documents (5% + 3% = 8%) as the headline, and flag the seasonal claim as Verify against current CBJ 69.05.020 — the seasonal claim may reflect a different (or proposed) rate structure, a misreading of the temporary surcharge, or a post-May-2026 change not yet in the packet. Do not quote a Juneau marijuana rate of 10.5% without opening current CBJ 69.05.
  • Scope: Flowers/buds, clones, seeds, prerolls, extracts, concentrates, tinctures, terpenes, topicals, capsules, edibles, and any other marijuana product intended for consumption/use on the body (CBJ Guidelines list, non-exhaustive). Sales for resale are not subject to either tax (Procedure 432, resale).
  • Filing: Under CBJ 69.05.170 (return each period regardless of sales level; successor liability on sale of business — final return within 15 days, CBJ 69.05.130).
  • Fees / buffers: Verify. No current Juneau marijuana licence fee or local buffer beyond the state 500-foot rule was confirmed in this run. Juneau’s protest practice (2019 Rainforest Farms example, §3.1.2) shows unpaid local sales tax is treated as a protest ground, which is operationally more important than a fee schedule for renewals.

3.4.4 Matanuska-Susitna Borough (Mat-Su) — Wasilla, Palmer, Houston

  • Government type: Organised borough. Wasilla, Palmer and Houston are separate cities inside it. Borough marijuana powers are nonareawide (outside cities) under AS 17.38.210(m).
  • Borough marijuana tax: 5% areawide retail sales tax on marijuana and marijuana products, with a city credit of up to 3% (see below).
  • History / vote: Assembly approved putting the question on the 4 Oct 2016 ballot; voters passed Prop. B-5 13,266–2,536 (Borough source, §3.3.2). The proposition as described by the Borough: “add a 5 percent Borough sales tax to the retail sales of marijuana with a sales tax exemption for cities of up to three percent” (Borough news, 2016). The Assembly’s earlier description (2016): “Cities are partially exempted up to the level of their levied sales tax” — Wasilla 2%, Houston 2%, Palmer 3% at that time (Borough news, 2016 — https://matsugov.us/news/arss/17010-public-safety-building-pot-more).
  • Current rates (Borough’s own tax page, accessed 2026-11-01): Mat-Su Borough, “Marijuana Tax” — https://matsu.gov/services/marijuana-tax (and mirror https://Matsugov.us/s/s-2/property-taxes/marijuana-sales-tax):
    • Outside city limits: 5%
    • Houston: 3% (borough rate — i.e. 5% less a 2% city credit, on the 2016 logic)
    • City of Palmer: 2% (borough rate — i.e. 5% less a 3% city credit)
    • Wasilla is not listed on the current Borough page’s rate table. That omission is itself a finding: either Wasilla’s city marijuana tax is now 0% (so the borough rate would be 5% and the table would not need a Wasilla row), or Wasilla is omitted for another reason. Verify Wasilla’s current city marijuana tax and the borough rate inside Wasilla before calculating a Wasilla total. Do not assume Wasilla = 5% borough + 0% city, and do not assume Wasilla = 3% borough (the old 2% city credit) without a current source.
  • Filing: Quarterly, self-reported Marijuana Tax Quarterly Return, payment due 30 days after the end of the quarter (Borough page, above). Contrast Anchorage/Fairbanks monthly filing — the filing cadence is local, not state.
  • Budget figure (not a tax rate): Borough Manager’s Report, April 2025: Marijuana Sales Tax adopted/amended $1,850,000 for the year, $881,711 year-to-date at 31 March 2025 (unaudited) — https://matsugov.us/docs/general/24722/Jan-Feb-Mar-2025-Managers-Report.pdf. This is a budget line, not a collections total.
  • Borough land use — Conditional Use Permit (CUP):
  • The Borough adopted a CUP system for marijuana businesses in late 2016 / early 2017 (Assembly vote, December 2016–January 2017 reporting). Key features as reported: limited cultivation facilities exempt from the CUP requirement (defined by the state in part by size — the 2016 reporting says “500 feet or less,” which appears to be a reporting error for 500 square feet of canopy; Verify the current Borough code definition before quoting it); 100-foot setback retained in the CUP (Assembly declined to eliminate it); grandfathering amendment for businesses inside a Special Use District (SPUD) or Residential Land-Use District whose state application had already been accepted before the local regulation date (notably affecting a Talkeetna retailer application).
  • Sources: 420 Magazine reporting of Borough Assembly action, Dec 2016–Jan 2017 — https://www.420magazine.com/community/threads/ak-pre-christmas-permits-for-mat-su-marijuana-vaporize.303567/ and https://www.420magazine.com/community/threads/ak-getting-the-green-light-mat-su-pot-ready-to-go.318265/ (both accessed 2026-11-01). Both are secondary; Verify the current Mat-Su Borough Code CUP chapter (and any SPUD overlay) before advising a site — the CUP rules have been amended since 2017 and the SPUD interaction is fact-specific to Talkeetna/Sutton and similar communities.
  • Community-council notification: AMCO renewal notices state that applications are sent to community councils “if your proposed premises is in Anchorage or certain locations in the Mat-Su Borough” — Mat-Su is therefore one of only two jurisdictions with a formal community-council notification layer in AMCO’s process. Verify which Mat-Su locations trigger council notification.
  • City taxes inside Mat-Su (current, as far as confirmed):
  • Palmer: City marijuana/sales tax rate implied by the Borough credit is 3% (5% − 2% borough rate = 3% city rate, on the Borough page’s logic). Verify Palmer’s own code — the Borough page does not state Palmer’s city rate directly; it states the Borough rate in Palmer.
  • Houston: City rate implied is 2% (5% − 3% borough rate). Verify Houston code on the same basis.
  • Wasilla: City rate Verify (see above). Historical note: Wasilla had a 2% general sales tax in 2016 (Borough Assembly description, 2016) — whether that general tax applies to marijuana sales today, and whether Wasilla has a separate marijuana tax, are both Verify.
  • City bans inside Mat-Su: See §3.3.2 (Wasilla 2016 ban, Palmer 2016 “nixed,” Houston embraced). All three city-ban statuses are Verify in 2026 — the Borough tax page’s Palmer/Houston rates suggest those two cities now allow (taxed) retail sales, which would be inconsistent with a continuing Palmer ban, but a tax page alone does not prove a ban was repealed. Open Palmer and Wasilla city codes.

3.4.5 Kenai Peninsula Borough — Kenai, Soldotna, Homer, Seward

  • Government type: Organised borough. Kenai, Soldotna, Homer, Seward and Seldovia are separate cities inside it. The borough seat is Soldotna.
  • Borough general sales tax: 3% (borough-wide). Source: Sales-Taxes.com / Sales Tax Handbook, October 2026, citing Alaska DOR — https://www.sales-taxes.com/ak/kenai-peninsula-borough (“county-level sales tax rate is 3%”) and https://world.salestaxhandbook.com/alaska/rates/kenai-peninsula-borough (both accessed 2026-11-01). The borough’s 3% is a general sales tax, not a marijuana-specific tax.
  • No borough marijuana-specific tax was confirmed in this run. The 2016 AMCO spreadsheet’s Kenai Peninsula Borough entry (9 May 2016, Ordinance 2016-03, “YES” restrictions) does not state a marijuana-specific rate in the extract. Verify whether the Borough has since adopted a marijuana-specific levy — none was found, but absence from the sources checked is not proof of absence from the code.
  • Combined general sales-tax totals (October 2026, secondary, citing DOR) — for marijuana sold at retail, these are the general-tax totals that would normally apply unless marijuana is exempted (no exemption found):
Place Combined general sales tax (Oct 2026) Composition (as reported)
Kenai (city) 6% Borough 3% + City 3%
Soldotna (city) 6% Borough 3% + City 3%
Homer (city) 7.85% Borough 3% + City 4.85% (highest in Alaska)
Seward (city) 7% Borough 3% + City 4%
Outside cities (borough only) 3% Borough 3%
Seldovia (city, for context) 5% Borough 3% + City 2%

Sources: https://www.salestaxhandbook.com/alaska/rates and https://www.sales-taxes.com/ak/kenai-peninsula-borough (both accessed 2026-11-01; “last updated October 2026” / “January 2026” respectively — the January vs October vintage difference is noted, but the rates agree across both). These are general sales-tax rates, not marijuana-specific rates, and are secondary (handbook) presentations of DOR data — Verify each city’s current code (Kenai, Soldotna, Homer, Seward) for (a) whether marijuana is included in the general base, (b) any marijuana-specific surcharge, and (c) any cap (e.g. single-item cap) that affects marijuana sales, before quoting a Homer/Seward marijuana total.

  • Homer-specific regulation (confirmed 2016, operating 2026): Cannabis Advisory Commission per HMC 2.78; Code Title 21 as amended by Ord. 16-04(A-2)(S-3) (2016 spreadsheet). Operating cultivation in Homer confirmed 2026 (Wildflower Farms renewal, §3.1.2). Verify current Homer Municipal Code Title 21 zoning districts, any Homer marijuana fee, and any Homer buffer beyond the state 500 feet.
  • Kenai / Soldotna / Seward fees, buffers, bans: Verify / not located. No current fee schedule, local buffer, or ban/allow ordinance for any of the three was confirmed in this run. Do not infer a ban from the absence of a finding.

3.4.6 Ketchikan Gateway Borough and City of Ketchikan (and Saxman)

  • Government type: Organised borough (Ketchikan Gateway Borough) with the City of Ketchikan and the City of Saxman inside it.
  • General sales tax:
  • Borough (outside city limits): 2.5%
  • City of Saxman: 6.5% (combined, as stated on the Borough’s sales-tax guide)
  • City of Ketchikan (non-residential sales): 8.0% April 1–September 30; 5.5% October 1–March 31 (seasonal city rate; residential rent in the city is 5.5% year-round)
  • Source: Ketchikan Gateway Borough, “Sales Taxes” — https://www.borough.ketchikan.ak.us/449/Sales-Taxes (and mirror https://www.kgbak.us/449/Sales-Taxes) (accessed 2026-11-01).
  • Marijuana-specific tax — Borough: 5% special sales tax on the retail price of marijuana and marijuana products sold in the borough for retail, under Borough Code 4.50(b).
  • History / vote: Borough Assembly adopted Ordinance 1839-Substitute-Amended, 21 August 2017, establishing the 5% areawide special tax; voters approved Proposition 1, 3 October 2017 (ballot question for the 5% tax, effective 1 January 2018, with a five-year sunset unless extended — Verify whether the tax was extended beyond its original five-year term, i.e. beyond 2022/2023, because the Borough’s current sales-tax page still lists the 5% tax in 2026, implying extension or re-adoption, but the extension instrument was not located in this run). Source: SitNews, 1 October 2017 — http://www.sitnews.us/1017News/100117/100117_ketchikan_regular_election.html; Borough Attorney memorandum, 30 March 2023 — https://public.destinyhosted.com/ketchdocs/2023/ASSY/20230403_1332/8264_MA_Proposal_to_Impose_Per_Unit_Sales_Tax_on_Fuel_FINAL_3.30.23.pdf (“the Borough adopted a special sales tax on marijuana and marijuana products effective January 1, 2018 with a levy of 5%, which is deposited in the General Fund”).
  • City credit: The Borough tax is credited for City of Ketchikan special marijuana tax paid, up to 5% (Borough Code 4.50(b); City Code 3.04.020(b)). The Borough collects the marijuana tax on behalf of the City (Borough sales-tax page, above).
  • Marijuana-specific tax — City of Ketchikan: 5% special sales tax, under Ketchikan Municipal Code (KMC) 3.04.020(b) (and Ord. 16-1821, 2016 — 2016 spreadsheet: “5% in addition to current sales tax”).
  • Combined effect (2017 voter-packet arithmetic, still the structure on the Borough’s current page): Outside the City: general 2.5% + marijuana 5% = 7.5% total on retail marijuana. Inside the City: general (seasonal 5.5%/8.0% as above, which already includes the borough 2.5%) + city marijuana 5% + borough marijuana 5% less city credit (up to 5%) = the 2017 packet’s stated total of 11.5% [2017 historical total only — superseded; current inside-city 10.5% winter / 13.0% summer] inside the City under the rates then in force (city general 4% + city marijuana 5% + borough general 2.5% = 11.5% [2017 historical total only — superseded; current inside-city 10.5% winter / 13.0% summer], with the borough marijuana tax fully credited). Do not quote 11.5% [2017 historical total only — superseded; current inside-city 10.5% winter / 13.0% summer] as the 2026 combined rate without recalculating from the current seasonal general rates — the city general rate has changed seasonally since 2017, so the 2026 combined inside-city total will differ from 11.5% [2017 historical total only — superseded; current inside-city 10.5% winter / 13.0% summer] even though the marijuana-specific structure (5% + 5% with credit) is unchanged. Verify the current combined calculation on the Borough’s Marijuana Sales Tax Form (Excel, linked from the Borough sales-tax page).
  • Deposit: Borough marijuana tax proceeds go to the General Fund (Borough Attorney memo, 2023, above). No dedication to a special fund was found.
  • Fees / buffers: Verify. No current Ketchikan city or borough marijuana licence fee or local buffer beyond the state 500-foot rule was confirmed in this run. The 2016 spreadsheet cites City Code Ch. 5.20 and Borough Code Chs. 2.25, 8.10 — 2016 references only.

3.4.7 Sitka (City and Borough of Sitka — unified municipality)

  • Government type: Unified municipality (City and Borough of Sitka).
  • General sales tax: Seasonal, 5%–6% (secondary reporting, 2026 — Sitka “already charges a sales tax which alternates seasonally between 5- and 6-percent” — Cannabis Law Report, 2026, on a Sitka ballot proposition — https://cannabislaw.report/alaska-sitka-school-board-endorses-plan-to-tax-cannabis-to-support-student-activities/?_page=2). Verify current Sitka code for the exact seasonal rates and whether marijuana is included in the general base.
  • Marijuana-specific tax — proposed, not confirmed as enacted: A Sitka Assembly draft ballot proposition (2025–2026 reporting) would eliminate the municipal (general) sales tax on cannabis and replace it with a slightly higher consumer (marijuana-specific) tax, capped at 8% (after deleting a proposed 10% top rate), with proceeds intended to support student participation in sports/activities outside the state school-funding formula (the draft’s “included in the calculation of local support” language was to be deleted after School Board concern). The School Board gave informal support in June 2026 (same Cannabis Law Report source). This is a proposal, not a confirmed enacted tax. Do not quote an 8% Sitka marijuana tax as current law without opening the Sitka Assembly’s adopted ordinance / certified ballot result. The DCCED Alaska Taxable 2023 comparison table lists “Sitka borough 5%–6% [general] / 6% [marijuana?]” — the column alignment in that secondary presentation is ambiguous and is not relied on here for a marijuana-specific rate.
  • Buffer history (see §3.1.4): Sitka’s 2015–2016 push for a 200-foot buffer (vs the state’s 500 feet) was not adopted statewide. Verify whether Sitka’s current code contains any local buffer or zoning overlay — none was confirmed in this run beyond the state rule.
  • Onsite consumption — in progress (2025 reporting): Sitka had not previously aligned its code with the state’s onsite-consumption endorsement (2019 state rules), so Sitka retailers could not apply for AMCO endorsements. In April 2025 the Planning Commission approved a zoning change requested by a Sitka shop, and an ordinance to permit onsite consumption passed first reading 6–1 (Deputy Mayor Tim Pike opposed). Source: WeedMan summary of Sitka reporting, 17 May 2025 — https://weedman.ai/news/policy/2025/05/17/sitka-plans-to-permit-on-site-cannabis-consumption-in-stores/ (accessed 2026-11-01). Verify whether the ordinance passed second/final reading and is now in Sitka code — first reading is not enactment.

3.4.8 Kodiak — City of Kodiak and Kodiak Island Borough (keep apart)

Borough vs city — critical: The Kodiak Island Borough has no general sales tax and, at 1 November 2026, no borough marijuana tax. The City of Kodiak (inside the borough) does have a general sales tax and taxes marijuana under it. Conflating the two is the most common Kodiak error.

  • Kodiak Island Borough:
  • General sales tax: None.
  • Borough marijuana tax: None in force. A 2026 proposal — Ordinance No. FY2027-05, “Levying An Areawide Marijuana Retail Sales Tax, Establishing The Community Well-Being Fund, And Providing For Voter Approval Of The Tax” — would have levied a 3% areawide retail sales tax, with proceeds tracked for health, education and economic programmes (Community Well-Being Fund), raising an estimated $135,000–$150,000/year from the borough’s three dispensaries (two in the City, one in Bell’s Flats). The Assembly passed first reading 5–1 on 23 July 2026 (Gardner opposed) and held a public hearing 6 August 2026, but the motion to send the question to voters failed 4–2 (Citizen Portal summary of the 6–7 August 2026 Assembly action — https://citizenportal.ai/articles/9146393/alaska/kodiak-island-borough/assembly-declines-to-send-3-marijuana-retail-tax-to-ballot-after-amendment-and-close-votes; Borough minutes, 6 Aug 2026 — https://kodiakak.civicweb.net/document/313350/20260806%20RM%20Minutes.pdf). The 3% borough tax is therefore NOT law and was NOT on the October 2026 ballot on that motion. Do not budget a Kodiak borough marijuana tax.
  • Historical note: In 2016 the Borough considered following the state’s cultivation-point model rather than a sales tax, precisely because it had no sales-tax mechanism (420 Magazine, 2016 — https://www.420magazine.com/community/threads/ak-in-local-marijuana-regulations-borough-looks-to-state.315419/). That 2016 discussion did not produce a borough tax either.
  • Borough ban: Temporary ban expired Feb 2017 (see §3.3.2). No current borough ban.
  • City of Kodiak:
  • General sales tax: 7% on paper (City work session, 11 Aug 2026: “On paper, the City of Kodiak collects seven percent sales tax; however, in practice it only collects about 2.8 percent” after exemptions; FY2025 gross $496.2m, $13.9m remitted — https://cityofkodiak.civicweb.net/document/178828/City%20Sales%20Tax%20and%20Revenue%20Discussion.pdf). Marijuana is taxable under the city sales tax — Ordinance No. 1377 (2018) made tobacco, marijuana and alcohol taxable for seniors (i.e. removed the senior exemption for those products), and Ordinance No. 1367 (2017) raised the maximum taxable sale from $750 to $3,000 (with a preserved $750 cap for long-term rentals under Ord. 1368). Verify whether the $3,000 single-item cap applies to marijuana sales and how it interacts with bulk/wholesale transactions — the cap is a general sales-tax feature, not a marijuana-specific rule, but it affects the effective tax on large sales.
  • Marijuana-specific tax: No separate city marijuana percentage was confirmed beyond the general 7% (on paper). A January 2026 City Council work session discussed raising the City’s marijuana tax (agenda item: “Discussion Regarding Raising the City’s Marijuana Tax,” 20 Jan 2026 — https://cityofkodiak.civicweb.net/document/171774/), with a 5-year marijuana sales-tax collection memo attached. Verify whether any increase ordinance was subsequently adopted — discussion is not enactment, and no adopted increase was located in this run.
  • Local regulatory authority: City Council, per Ordinance No. 1365; licence review under KCC 5.24.020 (see §3.1.2, Kodiak renewal example).
  • Fees / buffers: Verify. No current City marijuana licence fee or local buffer beyond the state 500-foot rule was confirmed in this run.

3.4.9 Bethel, Nome, Valdez, Haines, Skagway — smaller places, thinner record

These five are grouped because the official record for each is thinner than for the larger municipalities, and inventing parallel detail for them would be worse than stating the thinness. Each entry states what was found and marks the rest Verify.

Bethel (city, Bethel Census Area, Unorganized Borough):

  • Marijuana tax: 15% — the highest local marijuana rate found in this run. Source: Alaska Department of Labor, Alaska Economic Trends, December 2022, table “Marijuana taxes collected from consumers” (2021 collections, source: DCCED, Alaska Taxable 2021): “Bethel 15% $803,522” — https://akdolphp.ayera.net/sites/default/files/trends-mag-file/dec22.pdf (accessed 2026-11-01). The same 15% figure is repeated in the CannBus Q3 2026 market report (“Bethel’s is 15%”). Verify current Bethel code for (a) whether 15% is a marijuana-specific rate, a general rate, or a combined rate, (b) its base (retail sales vs something else), and (c) whether it is still 15% in 2026 — the primary evidence is 2021 collections, not a 2026 code opening. Bethel’s general sales tax is 6% (Sales Tax Handbook, Oct 2026 — https://www.salestaxhandbook.com/alaska/rates/bethel-census-area), which suggests the 15% is not the general rate alone — but whether 15% is in addition to 6% (total 21%) or includes it cannot be determined from the sources checked and is Verify.
  • Ban/allow: No ban found; taxation implies retail allowance (see §3.3.2). Verify Bethel code.
  • Fees/buffers: Verify / not located.

Nome (city, Nome Census Area, Unorganized Borough):

  • General sales tax: Verify. No current Nome general or marijuana-specific rate was confirmed in this run. A Nome City Council work session in January 2020 discussed a possible excise tax on alcohol and marijuana sales (including a THC-content-based option raised by a student in 2019) — KNOM, 30 Jan 2020 — https://knom.org/2020/01/30/city-council-doles-out-nsedc-funds-discusses-possible-excise-tax-and-budget-amendments/. Discussion is not enactment; no enacted Nome marijuana tax was located. Do not quote a Nome marijuana rate.
  • Cultivation allowed (2023 evidence): Nome Grown LLC, described as the only licensed grow in Western Alaska (KNOM, Apr 2023, §3.3.2). Verify current licence status.

Valdez (city, Chugach Census Area, Unorganized Borough — formerly Valdez-Cordova Census Area):

  • General sales tax: Verify. No current Valdez general or marijuana-specific rate was confirmed in this run.

Do not list Valdez as banned or as taxed in 2026 without opening Valdez Municipal Code (VMC 5.06.020 in 2016) and current Valdez finance pages.
  • Fees/buffers: Verify / not located.

Haines (Haines Borough — organised borough, not a city inside a borough):

  • General sales tax: 5.5% (DCCED Alaska Taxable 2023 comparison table, as reproduced in the Lake and Peninsula Borough presentation, May 2024: “Haines Borough 5.5% … 2% [marijuana?]” — https://lakeandpen.civicweb.net/document/35306/Loeffler%20Partial%202024%20Tax%20Presentation.pdf). The table’s column alignment is ambiguous (the “2%” may be a different levy), so the 5.5% general rate is stated as secondary, and any Haines marijuana-specific rate is Verify. No Haines marijuana-specific tax was confirmed from a Haines Borough code or finance page in this run.
  • Regulation: Borough Code §2.82.040 (2016 spreadsheet, “NO” full bans). Operating manufacturer confirmed 2026 (Happy Valley LLC, §3.1.2).
  • Fees/buffers: Verify / not located.

Skagway (Municipality of Skagway — organised borough/municipality):

  • General / marijuana tax: Verify. No current Skagway sales-tax or marijuana-tax rate was confirmed from a Skagway code or finance page in this run. The 2016 spreadsheet cites Code Titles 5, 9, 19 (“YES” restrictions) without a rate in the extract.
  • Buffer history: Skagway’s borough clerk was quoted in 2016 reporting (alongside Petersburg and Sitka) on the difficulty of the 500-foot buffer in a compact town (Marijuana Times, Feb 2016, §3.1.4) — context, not a current buffer rule.
  • Fees/buffers/ban: Verify / not located.

3.4.10 Other places with a marijuana tax found in the DCCED/Alaska Taxable comparison (for completeness, not for the required list)

The DCCED Alaska Taxable 2023 comparison table (via the Lake and Peninsula presentation, May 2024) also flags marijuana taxes (Yes/rate) for, among others: Denali Borough 5% (and DOL Dec 2022: Denali 5%, $55,795 collected in 2021); Petersburg Borough $25/oz (a weight-based local tax, distinct from the percentage taxes elsewhere — Verify current Petersburg code, as a $/oz local tax alongside the state $/oz tax would be unusual and the table’s column for Petersburg is the only weight-based entry); Yakutat 8% (column ambiguous — may be a general rate, not marijuana-specific). These are listed here only so a later auditor does not mistake the required-list table (§3.4.14) for a statewide list. None of these three rates is adopted as a confirmed 2026 marijuana rate in this report.

3.4.11 Local taxes where there is NO general sales tax — the point for operators

Anchorage, Fairbanks/FNSB, and (on current evidence) Kodiak Island Borough illustrate the same lesson from opposite directions:

  • Where there is no general sales tax, a marijuana tax must be created as a standalone levy (Anchorage 5%, FNSB 5%, Fairbanks 5%, North Pole 6%). The absence of a general tax does not mean marijuana is untaxed locally.
  • Where there is a general sales tax, marijuana is normally taxed under it even without a marijuana-specific levy (Kenai Peninsula cities, Kodiak city, Juneau’s 5% layer). A marijuana-specific levy, where it exists, stacks on top (Juneau +3%, Ketchikan +5% with credit, Mat-Su +5% with credit).
  • The credit/offset mechanism (FNSB, Mat-Su, Ketchikan) exists precisely because borough and city taxes would otherwise double-tax the same retail sale. Always calculate the combined rate with the credit, not by adding borough + city headline rates.

3.4.12 Local fees — what was actually confirmed

Place Fee confirmed in this run Source
Anchorage SLUP $1,700 (all licence types); modification $850 (Assembly) / $425 (director) AMC Reg. 21.20 (Municode) — §3.4.1
All other required places No current fee confirmed — (Verify each municipal code / fee schedule)

This thinness is itself a finding: local marijuana fees are set by ordinance and change without AMCO notification (see §3.2.2 disclaimer), so a 2026 fee table built from 2016 sources would be misleading. The follow-up for each place in §3.4 is to open its current fee schedule, not to carry forward a 2016 figure.

3.4.13 Buffers — summary

Layer Distance Applies to Source
State (3 AAC 306.010) 500 feet (shortest pedestrian route, public entrance → protected-use boundary/entrance) New licences vs school ground, recreation/youth centre, religious-services building, correctional facility §3.1.4

3.5 Business concentration — ONLY what official sources say

Rule applied: This section does not estimate, rank, or cluster licensees itself. It reports only concentration statements found in official (or officially reported) sources, with dates. Anything else is a gap.

3.5.1 What official sources actually say

  1. Cultivators concentrated in Southcentral (2017, DOR/DOL). Alaska Department of Labor and Workforce Development, Alaska Economic Trends, May 2018, “Marijuana” article (sources: Alaska DOR; DOL Research and Analysis), headed “Cultivators Concentrated in Southcentral” with a map of taxpaying cultivators by borough/census area in 2017. The article’s text (as extracted) notes the industry grew rapidly once it had regulatory footing, to 73 businesses in 2017, and discusses Anchorage’s 5% marijuana tax vs Fairbanks’ lack of a general sales tax. Source: http://laborstats.alaska.gov/trends/may18art1.pdf (accessed 2026-11-01). This is a 2017–2018 statement. It must not be presented as a 2026 concentration finding without a current DOR/AMCO location table.
  2. Taxpaying farms by location, March 2018 (DOR via contemporaneous reporting). Contemporaneous DOR reporting (March 2018, via 420 Magazine summary of DOR figures) stated: “Fairbanks has the most taxpaying marijuana farms (20), with Anchorage second at 17. Juneau has four taxpaying farms.” Total taxpaying farms in March 2018: 92, rising every month since October 2016. Source: https://www.420magazine.com/420-news/international-cannabis-news/alaska-marijuana-tax-collections-set-another-record/ (accessed 2026-11-01). This is a March 2018 snapshot, not a 2026 ranking. Do not quote “Fairbanks has the most farms” as current.
  3. Mat-Su as cultivation hotspot (secondary, 2016–2026, not an official count). Secondary sources repeatedly describe Mat-Su as Alaska’s “biggest marijuana cultivation hotspot” / “cultivation capital” (Ganjapreneur, 2016, on the Mat-Su ban vote; CannBus summaries). No official AMCO/DOR location count for Mat-Su in 2026 was opened in this run to support a current “most cultivators” claim. The description is therefore recorded as secondary characterisation, not an official concentration finding, and the 2016 ban-vote context (Mat-Su voters rejected a ban while being described as a hotspot) is the reason it is mentioned at all.
  4. Anchorage as the largest retail market by sales (municipal data, 2025–2026). Anchorage’s taxable retail sales ($105.4m in 2025, $51.4m in H1 2026 — §2.5, municipal-via-secondary) are larger than any other single municipality’s retail figure found in this run, but no comparative municipal sales table was opened that would support a formal “Anchorage is #1” ranking across all municipalities in 2026. The Anchorage figures are stated as Anchorage-only; the ranking is not asserted.
  5. Single-store / few-store communities (2026 renewal evidence). Unalaska’s council memorandum states there is one retail store in Unalaska (749 E Broadway Ave) in 2026 (§3.1.2). Kodiak Island Borough’s 2026 tax-proposal materials state there are three dispensaries in the borough (two in the City of Kodiak, one in Bell’s Flats) (§3.4.8). These are the only current (2026) location counts stated as fact in this report, and they are counts for those places only.

3.5.2 What official sources do NOT say (gaps)

  • No current (2025–2026) AMCO/DOR table of licensees by borough/city was opened in this run, so no current concentration ranking, cluster map, or “top borough” claim is made.
  • No official “retail outlets per capita” calculation is reproduced here. Part 6 (Market Data) is the proper place for any per-capita calculation, and it must state its licence-count source and population source explicitly. This Part 3 section deliberately does not pre-empt it.
  • The 2016 AMCO spreadsheet’s community list is not a concentration dataset — it lists communities contacted, not licences operating.

3.6 Cross-cutting local issues

3.6.1 Double taxation and credits — the three credit systems

System Borough rate City rate Credit Result
FNSB / Fairbanks / North Pole 5% 5% (Fairbanks) / 6% (North Pole) City tax credited against borough tax (FNSBC 8.54 form, Line 5) 5% (Fairbanks) / 6% with excess treatment Verify (North Pole)
Mat-Su / Palmer / Houston / Wasilla 5% Up to 3% credit (Palmer 3%, Houston 2% in 2016) Borough rate reduced by city tax, up to 3% 5% total (where credit fully applies)
Ketchikan Gateway / Ketchikan 5% marijuana + 2.5% general 5% marijuana + seasonal general Borough marijuana tax credited for city marijuana tax, up to 5% General + one layer of marijuana tax (not two)

In all three systems, adding borough + city headline marijuana rates without the credit overstates the consumer’s tax. Any price comparison that adds them is wrong.

3.6.2 Local taxes in places WITHOUT a general sales tax — why they matter for the state debate

Anchorage, Fairbanks/FNSB and Mat-Su all levy marijuana taxes despite having no general sales tax (Anchorage and FNSB) or no borough general tax (Mat-Su). This matters for the statewide percentage-tax debate (§2.8): a future statewide retail sales tax would stack on top of these local taxes unless the bill provides a credit or pre-emption — and HB 91/HB 119 as described in this run do not contain a local-credit provision that was confirmed in the bill texts opened here. Verify the current CSHB 91(FIN) text for any local-tax interaction clause before modelling a combined post-reform rate.

3.6.3 The “no notification” problem — why local law is the hardest part of Alaska cannabis research to keep current

AMCO’s disclaimer (§3.2.2) is not boilerplate. Because communities need not notify AMCO of ordinance changes, and because borough/city codes are amended by ordinance without a state register entry, any Alaska local table has a short shelf life. The practical research rule that follows is: for a transaction (site selection, licence application, tax calculation), always re-open the current municipal code and the most recent council/assembly minutes for that place. This report’s dated entries are designed to make that re-check possible — each entry states its evidence date so the reader can see what is stale.
