Sources: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/10/anchorage-marijuana-sales-reach-51-4-million-in-first-half-of-2026-generating-2-57-million-in-taxes/ and https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/ak_cannmir.html — accessed 2026-11-01. Anchorage is ~40% of Alaska’s population (nearly 300,000 residents, per CannBus) — do NOT gross Anchorage sales up to a statewide total by population share, because Anchorage’s share of cannabis sales is not established as equal to its population share (one secondary article claims Anchorage accounts for 70–80% of statewide sales, but that claim is unsourced to a board dataset and is NOT adopted here).

6.4 Licence counts over time, retail outlets per capita, and official price data

  • Licence counts over time: the only official time-series points verified are the renewal-cycle points in §4.6 (472 up for renewal 2026–27; 30 surrendered/not renewed) and the trade-press claim that “the number of licensed growers fell nearly 25% between 2023 and 2025, according to [AMCO]” (https://www.mmjdaily.com/article/9867533/alaska-cannabis-weight-based-tax-drives-growers-out-feeds-black-market/ — accessed 2026-11-01). A full year-by-year licence-count series by type is Unavailable from the sources opened — it would require extracting successive MCB Licensing Statistics tabs (§5.3).
  • Retail outlets per capita — CONFLICTING secondary figures (NO official per-capita figure was located; do not cite any one of these as official):
  • 36 per 100,000 residents — Cross River Therapy 2024 national comparison, as relayed by a 2025 Alaska Story article (https://thealaskastory.com/anchorage-is-a-top-city-for-cbd-usage-reports/ — accessed 2026-11-01). This is the highest figure in circulation and is a third-party comparison, not a regulator count.
  • 15.95 per 100,000 (117 Google Maps “cannabis_store” listings, live 27 August 2026) — Crawlora mapping of 11,469 US dispensaries (https://crawlora.net/blog/cannabis-dispensary-density-2026 — accessed 2026-11-01). The same table ranks Alaska 4th among recreational states on this method (behind Montana 36.36 and Maine 36.24). Google Maps listings include hemp shops and closed listings — the source itself flags contamination issues for other states.
  • 12.7 per 100,000 — Verilife/ValueWalk analysis of 600+ US cities (2019-era data, https://www.valuewalk.com/cities-states-most-marijuana-dispensaries/ — accessed 2026-11-01). Ranked Alaska 5th at the time. Stale; included only to show the series has moved.
  • Anchorage density datapoint (operator estimate, not a count): Catalyst Cannabis founder estimate reported by Alaska Business Magazine (July 2025): “approximately one retailer for every 6,000 residents” in Anchorage, “about one shop for every 2,000” potential customers. Source: https://www.akbizmag.com/featured/grand-opening-catalyzes-cannabis-industry-community-gathering-space/ — accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Treatment adopted: Alaska is consistently in the top tier of states for retail density on every method, but the exact per-capita rate is Verify — it cannot be stated as a single number without first establishing the numerator (operating retail licences from AK-ACCIS/MCB statistics) and the denominator (current Census population estimate). The task premise (“one of the highest rates”) is supported directionally by all methods; the precise rate is not.
  • Official price data: Unavailable. Alaska publishes no official retail or wholesale price series (no price-per-ounce, per-gram or per-product publication was located from AMCO, the Tax Division or any municipality). Secondary price signals (all weak, none adopted as a market price):
  • Anchorage H1 2026 sales fell 1.4% while the Herald attributes the fall “primarily to an average drop in prices of around 5%” (municipal data relay, October 2026); CannBus attributes the 2025 Anchorage dip to “down about 10%” in average price (Herald explanation, March 2026). The 5% vs 10% figures refer to different periods and neither is a published price index.
  • A peer-reviewed study (Clinical Therapeutics, 2023, covering data through June 2022) is the strongest price-adjacent source: it reports Alaska pre-tax sales per resident of $382.97 for July 2021–June 2022 (highest of the five early-adopting states studied), on total pre-retail-tax sales of $256M for that year. Source: https://www.clinicaltherapeutics.com/article/S0149-2918(23)00221-7/fulltext — accessed 2026-11-01. This is a modelled academic figure from AMCO communications, not a current official price.
  • Price-of-Weed crowdsourced Alaska prices are cited in MPP footnotes but are NOT used here (crowdsourced, undated, unverifiable).
  • Early-market sales datapoint (academic, via AMCO communication): total Alaska sales $28,201,518 from market opening (29 October 2016) through the early period ending June 2017, with monthly splits unavailable (same Clinical Therapeutics study). Included for history; not comparable to later fiscal-year tax data.

6.5 Registry patient counts (counts only, no patient data)

  • Most recent secondary-reported official figure: 97 active registered patients as of 31 December 2025, sourced to “Alaska Department of Health via email 2/10/2026” as reported by NORML. Source: https://norml.org/laws/medical-laws/alaska-medical-marijuana-law/ — accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Prior figure: 113 patients, December 2024, per Marijuana Policy Project patient-numbers table (population base 737,270–740,133 depending on vintage; 0.02% of population). Source: https://www.mpp.org/issues/medical-marijuana/state-by-state-medical-marijuana-laws/medical-marijuana-patient-numbers/ — accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Older/conflicting figures NOT adopted: 393 patients (Americans for Safe Access patient-population table, undated vintage, with 93 “medical retail locations” — a figure that cannot be medical-specific in Alaska since no medical dispensary licence exists, and is therefore treated as an error or as counting adult-use stores); 391 cards issued in 2019 (Department yearly statistics as relayed by a secondary site). Both are listed here only so a future auditor can see they were considered and set aside.
  • Treatment adopted: active patients ≈ 97 (31 Dec 2025) is the most recent figure, from a secondary relay of a Department email — NOT from a published official table. The Department publishes no patient-count table (see §5.8). Medical sales are NOT reported separately: cardholders buy from adult-use stores and pay the same taxes. Caregiver counts: Unavailable.

6.6 Tourism-related sales (officially reported)

  • No officially reported tourism-specific cannabis sales figure was located. No Tax Division, AMCO or municipality publication splits sales by resident vs visitor, cruise passenger, or tourist. The following context is included so the gap is explicit, not filled:
  • Cruise visitors CAN purchase from licensed retail stores on the same terms as residents (21+, within purchase limits) — the visitor-purchase rules themselves belong to Part 12 of the full dossier; no visitor sales volume is published.
  • Anchorage’s ~$105M (2025) includes whatever visitor purchases occurred in Anchorage, but the visitor share is not separated in the municipal data as relayed.
  • Good Titrations (Fairbanks) is described by its architect as a “show-grow” attraction for “Alaskan cannabis tourism” (https://harkahq.com/good-titrations — accessed 2026-11-01) — a qualitative claim, not a sales figure.
  • Treatment: tourism-related sales = Unavailable as an official figure. Do not estimate a tourism share from Anchorage totals or cruise-passenger volumes.

6.7 Hemp licence/registration counts and hemp product market figures

  • Programme: Alaska runs its own USDA-approved state plan (effective 1 January 2022; see §5.10). Hemp registrations are DNR Division of Agriculture registrations (grower / processor / retailer / product endorsement), NOT AMCO licences. Full hemp licence-type detail belongs to Part 7 of the full dossier; counts and market figures for Parts 5–6 purposes are:
  • Hemp fee schedule (current, 11 AAC 40.100 as amended 3 November 2023, Register 248 — via akleg AAC text): non-refundable application fee $50 per annual registration; annual grower registration $100; processor not for human/animal consumption $50; processor for human/animal consumption $250; retailer $50; endorsement fee $25 per product; sampling fee $100; seed testing (wild/landrace/unknown origin) $1,200. Source: https://www.akleg.gov/basis/folioproxy.asp?url=http://wwwjnu03.akleg.org/cgi-bin/folioisa.dll/aac/query=[jump!3A!2711aac40!2E205!27]/doc/{t106321}/pageitems={body}? — accessed 2026-11-01 (via search result). Note: pre-November 2023 fees were roughly double (grower $200, processor $250/$750, retailer $300, endorsement $100) — do not use the pre-2023 schedule. SB 208 (2026, hemp reform, Ch. 60 SLA 2026, became law without signature 10 August 2026) adds a micro-grower tier (<¼ acre or <200 plants, reduced/waived fees, scaled reporting/testing/inspection) and risk-based testing, 30-day harvest window after sampling, and 0.3%–1% reconditioning/remediation (above 1% destruction) — but SB 208’s provisions take effect in stages (parts 1 July 2026, remainder 1 January 2027 per secondary summaries) and its fee/registration consequences for the 5 October 2026 DNR lists are Verify against the enrolled SB 208 text. Source for SB 208 becoming law: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/08/alaska-industrial-hemp-reform-bill-becomes-law-without-governors-signature/ — accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Hemp registrant counts (secondary, from the DNR registrant lists — the 5 October 2026 lists themselves were NOT counted in this run, see §5.7):
  • 2023: 215 registered hemp establishments — 4 growers, 4 processors, 207 retailers.
  • November 2024: 170 active — 1 grower, 1 processor, 168 retailers.
  • April 2025: 148 registered — ALL retailers (0 growers, 0 processors on that list). Source (secondary synthesis citing the DNR registrant lists): https://hempcbdbusinessplans.com/alaska-hemp-industry/ — accessed 2026-11-01. Treatment: these counts are adopted as the best available time series BUT flagged as secondary — the underlying DNR lists must be opened to verify the April 2025 “zero growers/processors” claim, which, if correct, means Alaska’s hemp production had effectively paused while hemp retail (largely CBD/topical retail) continued. The 5 October 2026 registrant list may show a different picture post-SB 208 — Verify by opening it.
  • Hemp product market figures: No official Alaska hemp product sales/revenue figure was located. No DNR, Tax Division or USDA publication gives Alaska hemp product sales, acreage planted/harvested, or farm-gate value for 2024–2026 in the sources opened. USDA NASS Alaska agriculture statistics may contain hemp acreage in some years — Verify via NASS (not opened for this report). Secondary qualitative notes (not market figures): Alaska Mill and Feed (hemp bedding, topicals, animal chews) and Denali Hemp Company (hemp-seed-oil topicals) are cited as examples of hemp product retail in Alaska (same secondary source as above) — examples only, not sales data.
  • Hemp tax: compliant industrial hemp (≤0.3% delta-9 THC) is outside Alaska’s marijuana tax (SB 208, August 2026, per CannBus). No separate hemp product tax was located — Verify whether any hemp-specific tax/fee beyond registration/endorsement fees exists (none is stated in 11 AAC 40.100 as reproduced above).

6.8 Cannabis-friendly banks and credit unions

No official list exists. No Alaska or federal regulator publishes a list of financial institutions serving Alaska marijuana businesses, and there is no federal cannabis banking law (given baseline). The institutions below are identified ONLY from their own marketing / secondary reporting. Inclusion here is NOT an endorsement and NOT a verification that the institution currently onboards Alaska marijuana businesses — onboarding policies change without public notice and must be confirmed directly with the institution. No account terms, fees or eligibility criteria are stated because none were verified from a primary institutional source in this run.

Institution (as marketed) What is claimed (secondary/marketing) Caveat
Credit Union 1 (Alaska-based credit union) Listed in a 2023-era secondary roundup as an Alaska-based credit union serving the cannabis industry (checking, loans, merchant services). Source: https://www.420-packaging.com/Blogs/Will-Silicon-Valley-Bank-Failure-Affect-Cannabis-Business.html — accessed 2026-11-01 Dated, secondary, single-source. Current onboarding status Verify directly.
Salal Credit Union (Washington-based, member-owned) States it serves cannabis businesses/employees nationally including Alaska; an Alaska-expansion announcement states Salal had “no current business members in Alaska” at announcement and that “very limited financial institutions” served Alaska cannabis businesses. Sources: https://www.salalcu.org/articles/financial-services-for-the-cannabis-industry-a-comprehensive-guide/ and https://www.salalcu.org/articles/growing-into-alaska-with-our-members/ — accessed 2026-11-01 Announcement is undated in the crawl and predates 2026; whether Salal now holds Alaska cannabis business members is Verify. Salal’s own page confirms some services (e.g. international wires) are unavailable due to federal uncertainty.
Safe Harbor Financial (SH Financial, Colorado-based) Markets Alaska cannabis banking/financial services (banking, lending, digital payments, vendor pay, payroll, tax support) and claims operation since 2015. Source: http://shfinancial.org/locations/alaska — accessed 2026-11-01 Marketing page only; Alaska-specific onboarding, partner-bank identity and current status Verify.
Herring Bank (Texas-based) Markets an “Alaska Cannabis Bank” programme (checking/savings, 24/7 online banking, cash management, armoured courier connection, ACH/wire, advisory). Source: https://www.herringbank.com/?p=7904 — accessed 2026-11-01 Marketing page only (last updated ~517 days before crawl); Alaska onboarding status Verify. The page’s claim that Alaska hosts “the most retail cannabis shops per capita in the U.S.” is marketing copy, not a verified ranking (see §6.4 conflicts).
Abaca (fintech) Named in the same 2023-era roundup as providing banking/payment/compliance solutions for cannabis. Source as for Credit Union 1 above Dated, secondary; Alaska-specific availability Verify.
  • Cash/crime context (why banking matters here, secondary): Alaska’s cash-heavy, weight-taxed cultivation sector with $6.29M past-due tax (§6.2) and a large rural/off-road geography faces acute cash-transport and deposit problems; no official survey of Alaska licensees’ banking access was located. Do not present the marketing list above as evidence that banking access is adequate.
  • Explicit negative: First National Bank Alaska, Alaska USA / Global Credit Union, Northrim Bank and other large Alaska institutions were NOT found in the sources opened to market cannabis business banking, and no source in this run states they serve marijuana businesses. Their absence from the marketing list above is NOT evidence they refuse such business — it is evidence only that no marketing/secondary source in this run claims they serve it.

VERIFY / UNAVAILABLE / CONFLICTING ITEMS (Parts 4–6)

Verify (must be checked in an official source before reliance)

  1. Current 3 AAC 306.100(d) renewal fees — pre-2025 higher renewal schedule vs AMCO application-page $5,000/$1,000 renewal column vs 2025 proposal to cut renewals (§4.2). Also $600 renewal application fee and $1,000 late-renewal fee.
  2. Current 3 AAC 306.015 residency rule — regulation text (PFD eligibility for every financial-interest holder, last amendment shown Jan 2023) vs CannBus “no residency mandate” summary vs third claim of non-resident stakes in up to two establishments (§4.4).
  3. Current onsite-consumption endorsement count and whether AK-ACCIS exposes an endorsement search field (§4.3.7). At least Good Titrations (Fairbanks) + Cannabis Corner (Ketchikan) reported operating in 2026; “5” and “only one” counts both circulate and neither is official.
  4. Current 3 AAC 306.370 consumption-area purchase limits (1 g / 25 mg / 0.3 g as summarised) and whether the September 2026 package removing those limits was adopted.
  5. Whether the September 2026 MCB package (sampling, equivalency 1 oz = 14 g = 11,200 mg, advertising) and the AO-360 package (hours, under-21 access) were adopted, and their effective dates — both were proposals in the sources opened.
  6. Testing-facility operating count (Cannatest + Land and Seas per one secondary page vs older 4-lab lists) and the December 2025 total-THC reporting advisory text.
  7. Handler-permit 3-year term in the regulation text; full disqualifying-history list in 3 AAC 306.700.
  8. Whether any statewide ownership cap exists in 3 AAC 306 (none located, but absence in sources opened ≠ absence in regulation).
  9. AK-ACCIS search fields, record fields, and whether any per-record export/print exists; DNR hemp PDF direct URLs/file names and the counts in the 5 October 2026 registrant + endorsed-product lists.
  10. MCB Licensing Statistics Tab 5 (27 Aug 2026) by-type breakdown, if any; a full year-by-year licence-count series by type.
  11. Tax Division monthly “Total Collected Tax” values by month (July 2026–September 2026 posting status) and the Collections Summary component split (collections vs total tax).
  12. Current statutory distribution of marijuana tax revenue (50/25/25 split as reported in 2022 — confirm current law).
  13. Anchorage Finance Department marijuana-tax report direct URL/file; Fairbanks/Juneau/Bethel local tax rates and report URLs beyond Anchorage’s 5%.
  14. Whether any Alaska tribe holds a USDA-approved hemp plan or operates a cannabis/hemp programme with public records (none located).
  15. USDA HeMP public search coverage/fields/export for Alaska (restricted law-enforcement tool vs public tool — confirm which is citable).
  16. Banking: current onboarding status at Credit Union 1, Salal, Safe Harbor, Herring Bank and Abaca for Alaska marijuana businesses (all marketing/secondary as of crawl).
  17. Events: whether NWCC Anchorage or Legacy Cup ran in 2025–2026 (dates/venues unverified).
  18. SB 208 (Ch. 60 SLA 2026) effective-date staging and its effect on hemp registration fees/counts after 1 January 2027.
  19. Hemp acreage/production and hemp product sales for Alaska (NASS/USDA — not opened).
  20. Fingerprint cost: whether the $50.00 combined check fee from 1 October 2026 is the total applicant fingerprint cost.
  21. Whether the 30-day objection period on AMCO’s current home page (3 AAC 306.065) vs the 60-day local protest period in secondary sources reflects two different periods (state objection vs local protest) or a discrepancy — confirm both periods and which applies to whom.
  22. Anchorage local licensing period (two-year local licences per secondary report) — ordinance number/date.

Unavailable (no public source located in this run)

  • Official by-type operating licence census (AMCO publishes renewal-cycle totals only, §4.6).
  • Official statewide retail sales total; official retail/wholesale price series; official tourism/visitor sales split; official hemp product sales/revenue.
  • Official Department of Health registry count table (only secondary relays of Department emails exist).
  • Bulk downloadable AMCO licence register (CSV/XLSX) with a verified direct link + file name — none verified (§5.12).
  • Consolidated searchable enforcement/disciplinary database.
  • Public tribal licence-holder register.
  • Credible official startup-cost estimate (only weak secondary retail ranges, §4.10).
  • Caregiver counts; cultivation/manufacturing startup-cost estimates.

Conflicting figures (choice made in this report + alternatives)

  • Renewal fees: pre-2025 text ($7,000/$1,400/$2,000/$5,000) stated with conflict flagged; AMCO page column ($5,000/$1,000) treated as consistent with the pending proposal, NOT as proof of adoption (§4.2).
  • Residency: regulation text (PFD rule) preferred over CannBus “no mandate” summary (§4.4).
  • Fingerprint fee: $50.00 (1 Oct 2026, AMCO home) adopted; $48.25 (superseded)/$47.00 (superseded) treated as superseded (§4.2).
  • Onsite consumption operating count: “at least 2 reported” adopted; “5” (directory) and “only one” (older press/architect) listed as alternatives, neither adopted (§4.3.7).
  • Retail per capita: no single rate adopted; 36 (2024 comparison), 15.95 (Aug 2026 Maps listings), 12.7 (2019-era) all listed with methods (§6.4).
  • Patient counts: 97 (31 Dec 2025, NORML/DOH email) adopted as most recent; 113 (Dec 2024, MPP) as prior; 393 (ASA, undated) set aside as inconsistent with the no-medical-dispensary structure (§6.5).
  • Tax totals: fiscal-year Total Tax adopted (FY2025 $25,712,480); press calendar-rounded $25.3M treated as the same series rounded, with basis flagged; General Fund forecast lines ($6.8M/$6.7M/$6.4M) explicitly NOT total tax (§6.2).
  • Anchorage 2025 sales: $105,427,131 (Herald/Municipality) adopted; “~$108M” for 2024 treated as rounded, not exact (§6.3).
  • Hemp registrants: 2023 (215) → Nov 2024 (170) → Apr 2025 (148, all retailers) adopted as a secondary series pending verification against the 5 Oct 2026 DNR lists (§6.7).
  • Anchorage price change: H1 2026 “~5% decline” (Herald Oct 2026) and 2025 “~10% decline” (Herald Mar 2026 via CannBus) refer to different periods; neither is a price index (§6.4).
  • Testing labs: 2 active (secondary lab page) vs 4 historical names — count left as Verify (§4.3.5).

Recommended follow-ups (in priority order)

  1. Open the current 3 AAC 306 text (Register citation + effective date) and settle §§4.2/4.4/4.3.7 in one pass.
  2. Open MCB Licensing Statistics Tab 5 (27 Aug 2026) and the AK-ACCIS public search in a live browser session; tabulate operating licences by type/status and test the endorsement filter.
  3. Open the DNR hemp lists dated 5 October 2026 (copy PDF URLs/file names) and count registrants by type + endorsed products.

  6. Ask the Department of Health (Health Analytics and Vital Records) for the current active registry count.

Part 7 — Hemp Licences

PART 7 — HEMP LICENCES

7.1 Answer in one paragraph: registration, not licence — and Alaska’s own USDA-approved plan

Alaska does not issue “hemp licences.” It issues registrations through the Department of Natural Resources (DNR), Division of Agriculture, Plant Materials Center (PMC), under AS 03.05.076 and 11 AAC 40. There are three registration classes — grower, processor, and retailer — plus a separate product endorsement for each consumable hemp product. A person may hold a single class or any combination of classes (processor application form, DNR).

Alaska runs its own USDA-approved state plan; it does not use the USDA direct-licensing programme. The USDA Alaska State Plan was submitted 28 December 2021 and is effective 1 January 2022 (USDA plan PDF, opened directly). The plan history in that PDF states: SB 6 (2018, ch. 5 SLA 2018) created the pilot programme, implemented 4 April 2020 under §7606 of the 2014 Farm Bill; SB 27 (2021) established the permanent programme, aligned state statutes with the 2018 Farm Bill, and authorised DNR to develop a programme complying with federal requirements and submit the plan to USDA for approval. The permanent-programme regulations in 11 AAC 40 became effective 3 November 2023 (DNR programme page, opened directly).

That distinction matters for Part 5’s USDA point: USDA’s HeMP system is the restricted law-enforcement / regulator reporting tool, not a public Alaska licence register. The public Alaska lists are the DNR lists in §7.8.

Programme home page (opened directly, 2026-11-01): https://dnr.alaska.gov/ag/akpmc/industrialhemp.htm — Division of Agriculture, Plant Materials Center, 5310 S Bodenburg Spur, Palmer, AK 99645; programme contact email published on that page is the Division’s public programme address (industrialhemp@alaska.gov). Do not confuse Palmer, AK with Palmer, MA.

7.2 Statute: AS 03.05.076 and AS 03.05.100(5) — verbatim operative text

Source: Alaska Statutes, Title 3, Chapter 5, via akleg.gov (statute text as reproduced in search/open results and in the enrolled SB 208 text, which amends the same sections — see §7.7 for the amendment history). Last amendment before SB 208: SB 27, ch. 32 SLA 2021 (per Run 1; SB 208, ch. 60 SLA 2026, is the latest amendment — see §7.7).

Definition — AS 03.05.100(5) (pre-SB 208 text, as reproduced on akleg.gov):

“(5) ‘industrial hemp’ means the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including its seeds and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry-weight basis.”

Source: https://www.akleg.gov/basis/folioproxy.asp?url=http://wwwjnu03.akleg.org/cgi-bin/folioisa.dll/stattx21/query=[jump!3A!2706!2E60!2E014!27]/doc/{t407}/pageitems={body}? — accessed 2026-11-01. Verify whether SB 208 amended this definition section: the enrolled SB 208 text read in this run amends AS 03.05.010, AS 03.05.076, AS 03.05.079 and adds AS 03.05.100(6) (“micro-grower”), but no amendment to the AS 03.05.100(5) definition itself was seen in §§1–28 of the enrolled text. The definition above is therefore carried forward as the current definition, flagged Verify against the post-SB 208 consolidated statute.

Registration duty — AS 03.05.076(a), as amended by SB 208 §4 (enrolled text, opened directly):

“(a) Industrial hemp is an agricultural crop in the state. An individual who produces industrial hemp shall apply to the department for registration under this section. Registration is valid for one year but may be renewed. An application for registration or renewal must be on a form prescribed by the department that includes (1) the name and address of the applicant; (2) the address and global positioning system coordinates of the area to be used for the production of industrial hemp; and (3) the applicant’s acreage or estimated plant count sufficient to determine the applicant’s tiered grower category under AS 03.05.010(a)(7)(I)…”

Source: enrolled SB 208 (HCS CSSB 208(RES) am H), LegiScan reproduction of the enrolled PDF: https://legiscan.com/AK/text/SB208/id/3444060/Alaska-2025-SB208-Enrolled.pdf — accessed 2026-11-01. The bracketed text deleted by §4 is the former signed perjury statement about controlled-substance felony convictions — see §7.7 on what that deletion does and does not prove.

What a registered producer may do — AS 03.05.076(b), as amended by SB 208 §5:

“(b) An individual registered under this section may (1) produce industrial hemp, including growing, harvesting, possessing, transporting, processing, selling, or buying industrial hemp; (2) use any propagation method, including planting seeds or starts or using clones or cuttings to produce industrial hemp; (3) retain industrial hemp seeds for the purpose of propagating industrial hemp in future growing seasons; (4) retain and recondition, remediate, or convert any industrial hemp that tests between 0.3 percent and one percent delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol on a dry-weight basis.”

Source: same enrolled SB 208 PDF — accessed 2026-11-01. Note the interaction with §7.5: the 0.3%–1% retention/reconditioning right is a producer right for plant material; it does not authorise sale to a consumer of a consumable product containing delta-9 THC, which remains unendorsable under 11 AAC 40.400(d).

Testing / stop-order framework — AS 03.05.010(c), as amended by SB 208 §2:

“Industrial hemp shall be tested using a federally compliant testing method that accounts for measurement uncertainty. Noncompliant industrial hemp is industrial hemp that tests above 0.3 percent delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol on a dry-weight basis…”

and, in substance, the commissioner shall issue a stop order to a registered producer found producing a plant over 1% delta-9 THC, and may allow a registered producer at 0.3%–1% to retain and recondition, remediate or convert the plant to a nonintoxicating industrial use before issuing a stop order requiring destruction.

Source: same enrolled SB 208 PDF — accessed 2026-11-01.

Marijuana-tax exclusion — new AS 43.61.010(g), added by SB 208 §22:

“(g) The tax imposed by (a) of this section does not apply to industrial hemp produced in compliance with AS 03.05.076 or to a product made from industrial hemp if the product contains not more than 0.3 percent delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol content on a dry-weight basis as determined by testing that complies with the requirements of AS 03.05.010(c).”

Source: same enrolled SB 208 PDF — accessed 2026-11-01. This is consistent with Run 1 (no hemp product tax; marijuana tax does not apply to compliant hemp) and does not create a hemp tax.

Unregistered production: Producing industrial hemp without a registration under AS 03.05.076 is a violation punishable by a fine of $500 (AS 03.05.076, pre-SB 208 text as reproduced in secondary statute compilations — Verify the fine provision survives SB 208 in the consolidated statute; it was not seen in the enrolled SB 208 sections read here).

7.3 Regulation: 11 AAC 40 structure

Citation: 11 AAC 40, Alaska Administrative Code, Division of Agriculture. Pilot rules effective 4 April 2020 (Register 234); permanent-programme rules amended effective 3 November 2023 (Register 248). Source for the 3 Nov 2023 date: DNR programme page (opened directly) and the fee-schedule history line “(Eff. 4/4/2020, Register 234; am 11/3/2023, Register 248)” in 11 AAC 40.100.

Articles (from the DNR programme page, opened directly):

Article Sections Subject
1 (general) 11 AAC 40.010–40.100 Programme, definitions, fee schedule (§7.4)
2 11 AAC 40.200–40.295 Grower registration
3 11 AAC 40.300–40.335 Processor registration
4 11 AAC 40.400–40.420 Hemp product endorsement and labelling
5 11 AAC 40.500–40.525 Retailer registration
6 11 AAC 40.600–40.665 Testing (and related product requirements)
7 11 AAC 40.700+ Transport (incl. 40.710 permits — see §7.7)
8–9 11 AAC 40.800–40.910 Enforcement, definitions (incl. 40.900, 40.910)

No separate article exists for a hemp distributor, wholesaler, transporter, testing laboratory, or seed dealer as a standalone registration class in the article list on the DNR page. Testing is performed by the Division or an authorised/accredited facility under the plan and Article 6; SB 208 now expressly contemplates independently accredited laboratories and samplers (AS 03.05.010(a)(7)(B) as amended) — whether a new lab-registration class follows by regulation is Verify.

Registration term: The DNR programme page and the USDA plan agree on the shape but state it differently, and both are carried here so the difference is visible: the USDA plan (2021) says approved registrations are good for the remainder of the calendar year, renewal applications must be received before 15 December and, on approval, are valid for the following calendar year, with a prior-year registration remaining valid until a timely subsequent application is acted on. The current DNR application forms (2025 revisions) state registration is valid for 12 consecutive months from the date issued and must be renewed annually. Reconciliation: the 12-month-from-issue formulation on the current forms is the one a new applicant will actually be given; the calendar-year formulation in the 2021 USDA plan may be superseded in practice. Verify which formulation governs a 2026–27 registration — do not quote one without the other.

7.4 Every registration / endorsement type — what it allows, fees, requirements

Summary table (fees from 11 AAC 40.100, eff. 4/4/2020, am 11/3/2023 — opened directly via akleg.gov)

Processor — product intended for human/animal consumption 11 AAC 40.300–40.335 $50 $250 $300 Process raw hemp into consumable form (product still needs endorsement, §7.4.4)

Source for every fee line: 11 AAC 40.100, opened directly via akleg.gov folioproxy: https://www.akleg.gov/basis/folioproxy.asp?url=http://wwwjnu03.akleg.org/cgi-bin/folioisa.dll/aac/query=[jump!3A!2711aac40!2E205!27]/doc/{t106321}/pageitems={body}? — accessed 2026-11-01. The same fees are confirmed on the DNR 2025 application forms (retailer: “$50 NON-REFUNDABLE APPLICATION FEE AND $50 REGISTRATION FEE. $100 total for new registrant or renewal”; endorsement: “$25 PER PRODUCT”).

No cap, no social-equity discount in the regulation: No statewide cap on the number of hemp registrations was located in AS 03.05.076 or 11 AAC 40, and no social-equity programme for hemp registrations was located. SB 208 adds a different kind of relief — reduced or waived fees for micro-growers (see §7.7) — which is size-based, not equity-based, and no implementing fee figure was located at cut-off.

7.4.1 Grower registration (11 AAC 40.200–40.295)

Duty — 11 AAC 40.200 (verbatim, opened directly):

“A person may not grow industrial hemp unless the person has obtained a grower registration from the division in compliance with this chapter or is operating under a registration subject to 11 AAC 40.200 – 11 AAC 40.295.”

(Eff. 4/4/2020, Register 234.)

What it allows: Production as defined in AS 03.05.076(b) (§7.2): growing, harvesting, possessing, transporting, processing, selling or buying industrial hemp; any propagation method including seeds, starts, clones or cuttings; retaining seed for future seasons; retaining/reconditioning 0.3%–1% material.

Requirements (from the regulation article list, the USDA plan, and the DNR forms): prescribed application form with applicant name/address, grow-area address and GPS coordinates and a map (USDA plan: “address, global positioning system coordinates, and a map of the area to be used to produce” industrial hemp); acreage or estimated plant count sufficient to determine tiered grower category (SB 208 §4); criminal history report (USDA plan: “each applicant to complete an industrial hemp grower registration and a Criminal History Report”); planting reports; pre-harvest report at least 30 days before projected harvest (USDA plan, citing 11 AAC 40.265); Division/agent sampling of every lot and growing location — producers may not sample their own crop (USDA plan); post-harvest reports within 60 days of sampling results (USDA plan); records retained at least 3 years after sale/transfer (AS 03.05.076(c) as amended by SB 208 §6); land-use restrictions, signage, seed/propagule source documentation, pesticide rules (article headings 40.210–40.255).

Age: The DNR processor form states: “A person under the age of eighteen (18) years of age shall not apply for or hold a grower, processor, or retailer registration.” That sentence is form text (DNR form, via search return). The regulation section imposing the age bar was not separately opened in this run — Verify the cited section before quoting it as regulation text. No separate hemp minimum purchase age for consumers was located in 11 AAC 40 in this run — Verify; do not import the 21+ marijuana age into hemp.

Felony history — flagged, not resolved (see §7.7): The 2021 USDA plan states a registrant is not eligible if convicted of a felony involving a controlled substance within the last 10 years. SB 208 §4 deletes the application attestation to that effect. Whether a substantive 10-year bar survives elsewhere, or the USDA plan must be amended, is Verify.

Info link: DNR programme page (above) → “Grower Registration Application” and “Grower Reporting Package” under 11 AAC 40.200–40.295. Direct form URLs are linked from that page; the page itself is the stable citation because form file names change by revision date.

7.4.2 Processor registration (11 AAC 40.300–40.335)

Duty — 11 AAC 40.300 (verbatim, via akleg.gov search/open):

“A person may not process industrial hemp from its raw form into any other form, unless the person has obtained a processor registration from the division in compliance with this chapter or is operating under a registration subject to 11 AAC 40.300 – 11 AAC 40.335.”

(Eff. 4/4/2020, Register 234.)

Privileges — 11 AAC 40.305(a) (verbatim in substance, via akleg.gov): A registered processor is authorised to (1) purchase and store raw industrial hemp for processing at the locations identified in the processor registration; (2) process industrial hemp; (3) receive compensation for raw industrial hemp as permitted for registered growers under 11 AAC 40.205(a)(4); (4)–(5) provide samples to the Division for sampling/testing and recondition/reextract a failing batch only as described at 11 AAC 40.325(b); (6) store processed hemp or hemp products at the locations identified in the registration; (7) transport industrial hemp or industrial hemp products in compliance with 11 AAC 40.710; and (8) receive compensation for processed industrial hemp or industrial hemp products from a processor or retailer registered under 11 AAC 40.300–40.335 or 11 AAC 40.500–40.525.

Prohibited acts — 11 AAC 40.305(b)–(c) (summary, full text not reproduced here): A processor must comply with the chapter’s processing, record and reporting duties, and may not (among other things) receive compensation for processed hemp/products from an unregistered person, or process/store hemp in a structure primarily used for residential purposes. The full prohibited-acts list was not opened in full in this run — Verify against 11 AAC 40.305 before advising a processor.

“Processing” scope (DNR 2025 processor form, opened via search return): “all means of converting, extracting, refining, altering, manufacturing, or turning industrial hemp into finished industrial hemp or an industrial hemp product ready for market,” including grain, fibre and floral-material processing (form checkboxes). A consumable product made by a processor still needs its own product endorsement (§7.4.4) — holding a processor registration alone does not authorise consumer sale of a consumable product.

Form: DNR 2025 processor application (rev. 2025.07.02): https://plants.alaska.gov/hemp/pdf/forms/2025/New_Processor_Registration_Application_2025.07.02.pdf — accessed 2026-11-01 (via search return; form content quoted above).

7.4.3 Retailer registration (11 AAC 40.500–40.525)

Duty — from the DNR retailer application (2025 revision, via search return):

“A person may not receive compensation for processed industrial hemp or processed industrial hemp product from a consumer in the state, unless the person has obtained a retailer registration from the Division.”

Key mechanics:

  • Separate registration per facility/location: “A separate application and fee are required for each facility or location where industrial hemp products are offered for sale.”
  • Term: “Registration is valid for 12 consecutive months from the date it is issued and must be renewed annually” (retailer form).
  • Endorsement still required: “All products offered for sale must also carry a product endorsement from the division as stated at 11 AAC 40.400 and 11 AAC 40.515. Only after an individual product intended for human or animal consumption carries an endorsement from the Division can it then be offered for sale to consumers.” Products not endorsed cannot be offered for sale even if the seller holds a retailer registration.
  • Distribution channels (endorsement form checkboxes, via search return): self-distribution to retailer stores — NO retailer registration required for that step alone; e-commerce / direct-to-consumer website sales — retailer registration REQUIRED (review 11 AAC 40.500); retail store sales — retailer registration required. In other words, the retailer registration is a consumer-facing registration, not a wholesale registration.
  • Out-of-state / online sellers: The registrant list (§7.8) contains many out-of-state retailer registrations (registration numbers with an “OR” element, e.g. 20-OR-xxxx), confirming that a non-Alaska manufacturer/brand selling consumable hemp products to Alaska consumers must register as a retailer and endorse its products — it cannot rely on registration in its home state alone. Verify the exact “OR” coding rule against the Division; the coding is inferred from the list pattern, not from a published coding key in the sources opened.
  • AMCO licensees are not exempt: A separate DNR retailer application exists specifically for marijuana product manufacturing facilities licensed through AMCO (rev. 2025.04.18): https://plants.alaska.gov/hemp/pdf/forms/2025/MPMF_Retailer Application_2025.04.18.pdf — accessed 2026-11-01. It applies to an AMCO licensee that wants to make hemp products (CBD) or incorporate an endorsed hemp product into an already-approved AMCO marijuana product. Holding an AMCO licence does not substitute for DNR retailer registration or endorsement.

Form (general retailer): linked from the DNR programme page under 11 AAC 40.500–40.525 (“Retailer Registration Application” and “Retailer Records”).

7.4.4 Product endorsement / product registration (11 AAC 40.400–40.420)

This is the fourth, product-level authorisation. It is not a business licence; it attaches to a single product SKU.

Duty — 11 AAC 40.400(a) (verbatim, via DNR Endorsement Guide):

“Before being offered with or without compensation to a consumer, any industrial hemp product processed beyond its raw form and intended for human, or animal consumption must be endorsed by the division.”

Fee and term: $25 per product SKU; endorsement valid for 12 consecutive months from the date issued; a new registration is required every year for every registered product (Endorsement Guide). A certificate of analysis (COA) is required for each product in its final form; COAs for batches are not accepted (Guide).

Who applies: The product manufacturer or brand owner — not the retailer (secondary guide summary, consistent with the DNR forms). If a brand owner uses a contract manufacturer, the brand owner still files the endorsement application.

What it covers: Ingestible products (gummy, tincture, capsule, beverage) and dermally applied topicals, and pet products (Guide). The January 2024 DNR advisory (via AMCO, §7.5) confirms the same scope: “any ingestible product like a gummy, tincture, capsule, or beverage, and any dermally applied topical product. It also applies to pet products.”

Exemptions stated in the Guide (flagged): “Hemp fiber products and hemp seed oils are exempt from the registration requirements.” The Guide places this in the “Who is Required to Register?” section. Whether “registration requirements” there means product endorsement only, or also retailer registration for seed-oil sellers, was not verified against regulation text — Verify before relying on the exemption. The Division’s own FAQ (May 2025, §7.5) separately states: “Products derived from the seeds of the hemp plant may be offered to consumers without an endorsement. These products contain no cannabinoids like CBD or THC and the seeds themselves do not naturally contain tetrahydrocannabinol (THC).”

AMCO-manufacturer endorsement: A separate endorsement application exists for marijuana product manufacturing facilities (rev. 2025.04.18): https://dnr.alaska.gov/ag/akpmc/hemp/pdf/forms/2025/MPMF_Endorsement Application_2025.04.18.pdf — accessed 2026-11-01. Fee on that form: $25 per product; “Hold off on payment until you receive an invoice.”

Endorsed-products list: see §7.8.2.

7.4.5 Other / related authorisations — what does NOT exist as a hemp class

Sought class Status in Alaska hemp programme
Hemp distributor / wholesaler No standalone class located. Processor-to-processor and processor-to-retailer compensation is covered by the processor privilege (40.305(a)(8)); self-distribution to retailers needs no retailer registration (form checkbox, §7.4.3).
Hemp transporter No standalone class located. Transport is a privilege of grower/processor registrations, subject to 11 AAC 40.710 documentation/permits (§7.7).
Hemp testing laboratory No standalone DNR hemp-lab registration class located. Testing is by the Division or an authorised/accredited facility (USDA plan; Article 6). SB 208 contemplates independently accredited labs/samplers — implementation Verify.
Hemp seed dealer / nursery No standalone class located. Seed/propagule rules sit inside grower registration (40.210–40.230), including the $1,200 testing fee for wild/landrace/unknown-origin seed (40.100(12)) and the Division’s accepted/prohibited varieties list (DNR programme page, “Alaska Accepted and Prohibited Hemp Varieties”).
Medical hemp / medical marijuana licence None. Hemp registrations are agricultural, not medical. Alaska has no medical dispensary or medical business licences at all (Run 1).
Hemp “licence” under USDA direct programme Not used. Alaska has its own USDA-approved plan (§7.1). A USDA producer licence number appearing in the DNR list (e.g. one entry annotated “(USDA license #)”) is an annotation in a cumulative list, not evidence of a parallel Alaska USDA-licence class — Verify that entry’s meaning; do not count it as a separate class.

7.5 The endorsement ban — the rule that defines Alaska hemp consumables

11 AAC 40.400(d) (verbatim, via DNR Endorsement Guide and January 2024 advisory):

“The division may not endorse a product that contains delta-9-THC or a nonnaturally occurring cannabinoid, including a cannabinoid made from an ingredient extracted from industrial hemp and modified beyond its original form.”

11 AAC 40.520(b)(3) (verbatim, via January 2024 AMCO/DNR advisory):

“[T]he Division may issue a notice of violation and a stop order for any processed hemp product intended for human or animal consumption from a registered retailer if the hemp product contains any delta-9-THC.”

What is therefore prohibited for consumer sale in Alaska (DNR endorsed-products page header, opened via search return; DNR FAQ, May 2025; January 2024 advisory): delta-9 THC (any amount, in a consumable product — the ban is not “up to 0.3% is fine”); delta-8 THC and delta-8 THC-O-acetate; delta-10 THC and delta-10 THC-O-acetate; delta-6 THC and delta-6 THC-O-acetate; THCA; THCV; THCP; HHC; HHCP; and synthetic or lab-created cannabinoids. The January 2024 advisory states flatly: “It is unlawful to sell to a consumer in Alaska an industrial hemp product that is intended for human or animal consumption that contains delta-9-THC or is otherwise not endorsed by the Division of Agriculture. Hemp-derived products containing delta-9-THC may not be sold or given away to a consumer in Alaska.”

What CAN be endorsed and sold: Broad-spectrum and isolate-based products with delta-9 THC removed, as shown on the final-product COA, carrying a Division endorsement (January 2024 advisory). The DNR FAQ (May 2025) adds that legal products “may only be offered to consumers by retailers that are registered with the Division.”

THC beverages: No separate THC-beverage class, limit or authorisation was located. A beverage is an ingestible consumable product under 40.400(a) and therefore needs endorsement, and cannot be endorsed if it contains delta-9 THC or a nonnaturally occurring cannabinoid. A delta-9-removed CBD beverage could in principle be endorsed like any other ingestible — but no endorsed beverage was specifically verified in the endorsed-products list excerpts opened here, so that application is stated as a reading of the rule, not as a verified endorsed product. Verify against the current endorsed-products list before advising a beverage maker.

Sources for this section (all accessed 2026-11-01):

  • DNR Endorsement Guide: https://dnr.alaska.gov/ag/akpmc/hemp/pdf/forms/Industrial%20Hemp%20Product%20Endorsement%20Guide.pdf
  • DNR endorsed-products page (header prohibitions): https://dnr.alaska.gov/ag/akpmc/hemp/pdf/resources/AIH Endorsed Products _TODAY.pdf
  • DNR FAQ via Dept. of Commerce, Community, and Economic Development packet, May 2025: https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/5/pub/Archive26/BoardMeetings/PHYpacket_2025_05_16.pdf
  • January 2024 DNR/AMCO advisory: https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/HomePage/ProhibitedUseSaleUnendorsedHemp.pdf and https://Www.Commerce.Alaska.Gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/Minutes/2024/02.01/Tab4.pdf

AMCO interaction (2022 advisory, still cited in 2024 materials): It is a violation of a marijuana establishment licence to incorporate into a product and/or dispense directly to consumers hemp products not endorsed by DNR. AMCO may inspect, seize/destroy non-compliant hemp products, and suspend/revoke/deny registrations or endorsements under 11 AAC 40 or licences under 3 AAC 306. This is an enforcement notice, not a separate hemp licence class.

7.6 USDA plan — detail beyond §7.1

Plan document (opened directly): https://www.ams.usda.gov/sites/default/files/media/AlaskaStateIndustrialHempPlan.pdf — “Alaska State Plan, Date of Submission: December 28, 2021, Effective Date: January 1, 2022, Submitted by: David W. Schade, MPA, Director” — accessed 2026-11-01. (Mirror: https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/AlaskaStateIndustrialHempPlan.pdf.)

Key plan commitments (from the plan text, opened directly):

  • Producer/land information: grower registration + criminal history report; application must give grow-area address, GPS coordinates and map; legal description forwarded to FSA; producer status/numbers reported to USDA via HeMP, monthly (first of each month).
  • Sampling/testing: every lot and every growing location sampled and tested; sampling by the Division or its authorised agents only; producer must give projected harvest date at least 30 calendar days prior; samples from flowering tops (top 5–8 inches of main stem / terminal bud / central cola in the plan’s sampling section); testing by post-decarboxylation or similarly reliable method, counting conversion of THCA into THC (plan definition: decarboxylated value = delta-9 THC + 87.7% of THCA); acceptable hemp THC level at or below 0.3% delta-9 THC (plan, citing 11 AAC 40.630).
  • Over-limit handling in the plan (pre-SB 208 formulation): product >0.3% but ≤1.0% delta-9 THC → notice of violation; >1.0% → notice of violation and destruction order (plan, citing 11 AAC 40.325). SB 208 now frames the 0.3%–1% band as retain/recondition/remediate/convert (§7.2) — the plan text has not been shown to have been re-issued to match SB 208, so the plan’s over-limit wording is Verify as current.
  • The plan states Alaska’s programme “will follow or exceed the minimum requirements of the USDA Industrial Hemp Regulations” and that a plan “may include requirements more stringent than this part or the Act” — which is the federal hook for Alaska’s stricter consumable endorsement ban (§7.5), which goes beyond the federal production definition.
  • No tribal hemp plan for Alaska in this plan document: The plan is a state plan. Whether any Alaska tribe holds a separate USDA-approved tribal hemp plan is Part 10, not Part 7, and was not located in this run — Verify in Part 10 against the USDA tribal plan list.

Does the plan need amendment after SB 208 and after the federal 2026 hemp changes? Verify. SB 208 changes testing frequency (tiered/risk-based), harvest window (≤30 days after sampling), transport permits, enforcement (corrective action plans), grower tiers and fees — all matters the 2021 plan describes. No amended Alaska plan was located in this run. Likewise, no Alaska plan amendment responding to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 was located.

7.7 SB 208 (2026) — what it actually changes for hemp licensing, and what is still Verify

Status (confirmed on akleg.gov, accessed 2026-11-01): SB 208, “AGRICULTURAL LAND; HEMP; STATE VEGETABLE,” prime sponsor Senator Bjorkman et al., 34th Legislature (2025–2026), current status CHAPTER 60 SLA 26, status date 08/10/2026. Bill history page: http://www.akleg.gov/Basis/Bill/Detail/34?Root=SB 208. Senate Journal, 11 Aug 2026, records the Governor’s message dated 10 Aug 2026 letting the bill become law without signature and transmitting engrossed/enrolled copies for permanent filing: https://www.akleg.gov/pdf/34/J/S2026-08-11.PDF. Votes (secondary reporting, consistent with the bill history): Senate final passage 20–0 (13 May 2026); House as amended 39–1; Senate concurrence in House amendments 17–3 (18 May 2026).

Enrolled text verification: The enrolled text was read directly in this run (LegiScan reproduction of the enrolled PDF, SB0208Z, 16 pages): https://legiscan.com/AK/text/SB208/id/3444060/Alaska-2025-SB208-Enrolled.pdf — accessed 2026-11-01. Verification status: enrolled text confirmed; the akleg.gov enrolled PDF itself (SB0208Z, listed on the bill history page dated 05/18/2026) was not separately opened in this run, so the LegiScan reproduction is the text actually read. For publication-grade citation, open the akleg.gov SB0208Z PDF and compare section numbers — Verify (formality, not substance: the section numbers and effective-date clauses below are quoted from the text read).

Effective dates — enrolled §§26–28 (verbatim in substance):

  • “Sections 23 and 25 of this Act take effect immediately under AS 01.10.070(c).” (§26) — §23 is the state-vegetable designation (AS 44.09.055); §25 is the regulations-transition section.
  • “Sections 1 – 10, 22, and 24 of this Act take effect July 1, 2026.” (§27) — this covers ALL the hemp sections (§§1–10), the marijuana-tax exclusion (§22), and the registrant-reinstatement transition (§24).
  • “Except as provided in secs. 26 and 27 of this Act, this Act takes effect January 1, 2027.” (§28) — this covers the agricultural-land sale/lease sections (§§11–21).

Consequence for the Run 1 date flag: At the 1 Nov 2026 cut-off, the hemp sections are therefore in effect on their face (their stated 1 July 2026 effective date had passed, and the Act became law 10 Aug 2026), even though the Act’s filing post-dates 1 July. The “Nov 2026 vs Jan 2027” conflict flagged in Run 1 does not survive the enrolled text for the hemp sections: the Jan 2027 date applies to the land sections, not the hemp sections. What remains genuinely Verify is implementation: whether DNR has adopted the new regulations, tiered fee schedule, or amended USDA plan, and whether the 11 AAC 40 text on the books has been conformed. No such post-SB 208 regulation was located in this run, and the DNR programme page still describes the 3 Nov 2023 regulations without mentioning SB 208 implementation.

Hemp changes, section by section (enrolled text):

SB 208 section Amends Change for licensing/registration
§1 AS 03.05.010(a)(7) Commissioner must adopt hemp regulations that, among other things: (A) specify approved seed/propagation sources incl. federally approved programmes / certified seed schemes / third-party certifiers, without requiring department grow-out trials; (B) require registrant-paid delta-9 testing after harvest, allowing independently accredited labs and samplers; (D) set ≥1,000 m isolation distance for certified/seed-purity production and recommend distances otherwise; (E) permit manufacturing and retail sale of hemp and hemp products; (F) establish a registration and renewal procedure; (G) establish tiered testing frequency by risk categories (crop history, past compliance); (H) allow a harvest window of not more than 30 days after sample collection, with extensions for weather/good cause; (I) establish tiered grower categories including a micro-grower category with scaled reporting, inspection, testing and fee requirements; (J) eliminate permits for in-state transport between registered parties when accompanied by a COA or other approved shipping documentation; (K) establish corrective-action-plan enforcement (written notice, opportunity to cure, objective escalation criteria incl. civil penalties, suspension, revocation).
§2 AS 03.05.010(c) Federally compliant testing accounting for measurement uncertainty; stop-order regime at >1% (shall) and 0.3%–1% (retain/recondition before destruction order) — §7.2.
§3 AS 03.05.010 (new (e)) Commissioner may issue enforcement actions using the corrective action plan under (a)(7)(K).
§4 AS 03.05.076(a) Application now requires name/address, grow-area address + GPS, and acreage/estimated plant count sufficient to determine tiered grower category. The former perjury statement on controlled-substance felony convictions is deleted from the application contents.
§5 AS 03.05.076(b) Producer privileges, incl. retain/recondition/remediate/convert 0.3%–1% material — §7.2.
§6 AS 03.05.076(c) Record duties: maintain ≥3 years after sale/transfer records of recipient name/address and amount transferred; make available on ≥10 days’ notice (reduced from 3 days in the bracketed prior text — the enrolled text shows “[THREE]” replaced by “10”).
§7 AS 03.05.076(d) Department shall set fee levels so total fees approximately equal regulatory costs, with reduced or waived fees for micro-growers; annually review fee levels; notify MCB and DPS of repeated/knowing violations; require destruction (in full form, not for reconditioning/gifting/transfer except for destruction) for >1% material; issue violation notices for repeated/knowing violations.
§8 AS 03.05.076(e) Department may issue stop-sale orders / violation notices for unregistered production; may adopt shipping-documentation regulations and eliminate permits for in-state transport between registered parties; may conduct random tests/inspections, using risk-based tiers with reduced frequency for low-risk growers/products/production or department-approved testing methods.
§9 AS 03.05.079 0.3%–1% retention/reconditioning is expressly not a violation under AS 11.71.040–11.71.060; failing to recondition/remediate/convert retained material is a violation.
§10 AS 03.05.100 (new (6)) “micro-grower” means an individual registered under AS 03.05.076 to produce industrial hemp whose product is cultivated on less than one-quarter of an acre or who has fewer than 200 plants, including indoor or greenhouse production. (Note the disjunctive “or” — either limb qualifies.)
§22 AS 43.61.010 (new (g)) Marijuana tax does not apply to compliant hemp / hemp products ≤0.3% delta-9 THC — §7.2.
§24 Uncodified transition: registrants An individual who held a valid AS 03.05.076 registration at any time 1 Jan 2021–31 Dec 2025 and whose registration lapsed solely as a result of DNR regulatory action 1 Jan 2024–31 Dec 2025 is eligible for reinstatement on application without paying a renewal of registration fee. DNR may not require destruction of lawfully held hemp/product of such a registrant if it satisfies AS 03.05.076(c) testing standards as amended by §6.
§25 Uncodified transition: regulations DNR may adopt regulations to implement §§1–10, 22 and 24; regulations take effect under AS 44.62 but not before the effective date of the law implemented.

What SB 208 does NOT do (do not over-read it):

  • It does not change the 0.3% delta-9 definition of industrial hemp in AS 03.05.100(5) (no amendment to that paragraph was seen in the enrolled text).
  • It does not authorise sale of delta-9-containing consumable hemp products, and does not amend 11 AAC 40.400(d). The endorsement ban (§7.5) stands unless and until 11 AAC 40 is amended.
  • It does not set a dollar fee for micro-growers or any other class. The $50/$100/$250/$50/$25 schedule in 11 AAC 40.100 (§7.4) remains the only published fee schedule located at cut-off. A micro-grower fee reduction/waiver is a statutory direction to DNR, not a fee in force on the face of the Act.
  • It does not, on its face, create a new hemp licence class. Grower tiers (including micro-grower) are categories within grower registration.
  • Its transport-permit elimination is statutory (§§1(J), 8), but 11 AAC 40.100(8) still lists a $25 transport permit and 11 AAC 40.710 still exists in the regulation article list. Until 11 AAC 40 is conformed, a transporter faces a statute/regulation mismatch — Verify with DNR which document a transporter should carry on 1 Nov 2026. The safe statement is: the statute eliminates the permit for in-state transport between registered parties accompanied by a COA/approved shipping documentation; the regulation fee line has not been shown to have been repealed.
  • Its deletion of the felony attestation (§4) does not, without more, prove the substantive 10-year felony ineligibility in the 2021 USDA plan is gone. Verify (see §7.4.1).

Secondary summary checked: Marijuana Herald, 11 Aug 2026 (“Alaska Industrial Hemp Reform Bill Becomes Law Without Governor’s Signature”) correctly summarises the micro-grower threshold (<¼ acre or <200 plants), risk-based testing, 30-day harvest window, transport elimination, 0.3%–1% retention and >1% destruction, corrective-action enforcement, and the marijuana-tax exclusion. It is used here only as a cross-check; every proposition above is sourced to the enrolled text itself. Source: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/08/alaska-industrial-hemp-reform-bill-becomes-law-without-governors-signature/ — accessed 2026-11-01.

7.8 Registries — Part 5 detail, extended for Part 7

Run 1 Part 5 inventoried the sources; this section gives the Part 7 working detail (URLs, format, dates, fields, limitations) and states what was actually seen in this run. No individual contact emails or phone numbers from these lists are reproduced in this report, per the commissioning instruction; business names and premises cities are public business information and are used only where needed to illustrate a pattern.

7.8.1 Registrant list (the master list)

  • URL (opened directly, 2026-11-01): https://dnr.alaska.gov/ag/akpmc/hemp/pdf/resources/AIH%20Registrants_TODAY.pdf
  • Linked from: DNR programme page as “Current list of Registrants as of 10/5/2026” (link label on the page opened 2026-11-01).
  • Format: PDF, 986 extracted lines in the open. No CSV/XLSX download was located; no search/filter interface — the PDF must be read or text-searched as a whole.
  • How to read the registration numbers: The PDF header explains: “The three types of registrations can be differentiated by the letter in the registration number” — Retailer: 20-3R-0000; Processor: 20-3P-0000; Grower: 20-3G-0000 (the leading element appears to encode region/year in the Division’s internal scheme; the letter is the class key). Out-of-state retailer entries use an “OR” element (e.g. 20-OR-xxxx) — coding rule Verify (§7.4.3).
  • Fields per row: Business name, DBA, contact name, premises address, city/state/ZIP, registration number, issue date, expiration date, contact email, phone number. (Field names are listed here; the values are not reproduced.)
  • Critical limitation — cumulative, not current-only: The PDF contains expired entries back to 2020–2021 alongside entries expiring in 2026–2027. It is therefore NOT a count of current registrants, and no total may be read off its length. Any count derived from it must filter by expiration date ≥ the count date AND by class letter, and must de-duplicate businesses holding multiple classes/locations. No such systematic count was performed in this run (see below).
  • What was actually seen in this run: In the sections read (lines 0–652 of 986), the overwhelming majority of entries with 2026–2027 expirations are retailer (R) registrations, many of them held by businesses that are also AMCO marijuana retailers/manufacturers (identified by DBA). Historical grower (G) and processor (P) entries were seen with expirations in 2020–2024 (e.g. early Palmer/Willow/Trapper Creek entries). No unexpired grower or processor registration was spotted in the sections read. That is an observation about the sections read, NOT a finding that zero growers/processors are currently registered — the tail of the PDF (lines 652–986) was not read in this run, and a filtered count is still required. Verify by filtering the full PDF.
  • Relation to Run 1 counts: Run 1’s secondary series (215 registrants in 2023: 4 growers, 4 processors, 207 retailers; 170 in Nov 2024; 148 in Apr 2025, all retailers) is consistent with the pattern seen here (retailer-dominated, grower/processor attrition), but the 2025–2026 figures in that series were not independently re-derived from this PDF in this run. Do not present 148 as a 2026 figure. A current (Oct 2026) active-registrant count by class is Unavailable in this run pending the filtered count above.

7.8.2 Endorsed-products list

  • URL (via search return; PDF file name as labelled on the DNR page): https://dnr.alaska.gov/ag/akpmc/hemp/pdf/resources/AIH Endorsed Products _TODAY.pdf — linked from the DNR programme page as “Current list of Endorsed Products as of 10/5/2026.”
  • Format: PDF list. Fields (from the page header and excerpts): product name, company name, phone number, website, date registration approved, date registration expires.
  • Header prohibition (verbatim in substance, §7.5): “It is unlawful to sell to a consumer in Alaska an industrial hemp product that is intended for human or animal consumption that contains delta-9-THC or is otherwise not endorsed by the Division of Agriculture.”
  • Limitation: The list excerpts opened show approved/expires dates in 2025–2027 for the entries sampled, and at least one entry pair shows an apparent typographical inversion (approved 5 Oct 2026, expires 5 Oct 2007 — plainly a data-entry error for 2027, flagged here rather than silently corrected). No total endorsed-product count was derived in this run — Unavailable pending a full count. The list is the only authoritative check for whether a specific consumable product is endorsed; a product not on it may not be sold to a consumer, even if the seller is registered (§7.4.3).

7.9 Part 7 — what a new hemp entrant actually needs (checklist)

  1. Decide the class(es): grower (to cultivate), processor (to convert raw hemp — choose consumable vs non-consumable fee tier honestly, because the tier follows the intended product), retailer (to sell to a consumer — one per location), plus endorsement per consumable SKU.
  2. Grower: prescribed form + GPS/mapped grow area + acreage/plant count (for tiering) + criminal history report (per 2021 plan — Verify current form requirements post-SB 208) + fees ($50 application + $100 registration, before any micro-grower reduction — none published yet).
  3. Processor: prescribed form + processing/storage locations + safety measures + waste disposal per 11 AAC 40.665 + fees ($50 + $50 or $250).
  4. Retailer: prescribed form per location + fees ($50 + $50); then endorsement for each consumable SKU ($25 each, final-form COA, manufacturer/brand-owner application).
  5. AMCO licensee route: use the separate MPMF retailer and MPMF endorsement forms; do not assume the AMCO licence covers DNR registration.
  6. Before selling any consumable: check the product is on the endorsed-products list (§7.8.2) and contains no delta-9 THC and no nonnaturally occurring cannabinoid (§7.5). Broad-spectrum/isolate CBD with delta-9 removed (final-product COA) is the only consumable pattern the Division endorses on the sources opened.
  7. Before transporting in-state: carry a COA or approved shipping documentation (SB 208 §1(J)); confirm with DNR whether a 40.710 permit is still being issued, given the statute/regulation mismatch (§7.7).
  8. Before relying on the 2021 USDA plan’s calendar-year term, felony bar, or over-limit wording: confirm the current DNR position post-SB 208 (§§7.3, 7.6, 7.7).

Part 8 — Cannabis-Zoned Properties

PART 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

8.0 Strict verified standard — and the headline result

Strict verified standard applied in this part (all five must be met on the listing itself, as opened 2026-11-01):

  1. A specific, openable listing page for an Alaska property (not a category page, not a directory of operating stores).
  2. The listing itself shows it is active and recent (an ACTIVE badge on a page opened 2026-11-01, or a published/updated date in 2026).
  3. The listing explicitly markets the property for cannabis use (cultivation, manufacturing, retail) or states an operating cannabis licence/business is included or tied to the premises — not merely that the zoning could allow cannabis.
  4. A zoning type is stated on the listing (or in a directly linked official zoning record for that address).
  5. A price is stated on the listing (sale price, or lease rate for a lease listing).

Result: ZERO properties meet the strict verified standard.

That is a finding about the listings actually opened, not a claim that no cannabis-suitable property exists in Alaska. Alaska has no “cannabis zoning” district as such: cannabis establishments are allowed through ordinary commercial/industrial zoning plus a state licence and, in Anchorage, a special land use permit for marijuana (SLUP) — see §8.5. A property can therefore be cannabis-eligible without ever being marketed as cannabis property, and a cannabis business can operate in a building that is listed (or not listed) as ordinary industrial space. The closest candidates are listed separately in §8.2 with the exact reason each fails the standard. Every other property discussed in §8.3 fails on marketing, recency, or both.

Searches tried (2026-11-01): 420 Property Alaska index and Wasilla pages; CannaMLS Alaska catalogue; LoopNet image/PDF documents for Anchorage industrial property; general web searches for Alaska cannabis dispensary/cultivation property for sale (Anchorage, Fairbanks, Wasilla, Juneau, Kenai); Alaska hemp farm/land for sale. No Alaska-specific hemp property listing was found at all (§8.4). No Fairbanks, Juneau, Kenai, Ketchikan, Sitka, Kodiak, Bethel, Nome, Valdez, Haines or Skagway cannabis-property listing with an address and price was found in the searches tried — that absence is reported, not filled by inference from operating-store directories (a store directory is not a property listing).

Same-name warning applied: All properties below were checked to be in Alaska (Anchorage AK, Wasilla AK). Listings for other states that appear on the same index pages (e.g. New York, New Jersey, California, Florida, Michigan) were excluded and are not counted.

8.1 Why “cannabis-zoned” needs care in Alaska (read before the table)

  • Alaska does not have a statewide “cannabis zone.” State law (AS 17.38; 3 AAC 306) licenses establishments; local governments zone them and may prohibit types by local option (Run 1 Part 3). The 500 ft state buffer (3 AAC 306.010(a), from a school ground, recreation/youth centre, religious-services building, or correctional facility) is a siting rule, not a zone.
  • Anchorage: commercial marijuana establishments require a municipal licence and a special land use permit for marijuana (SLUP) under AMC Title 10 and Title 21 (Anchorage SLUP application form, via muni.org search return). Anchorage zoning materials state, in substance, that marijuana retail sales establishments are permitted in the I-2 district when collocated with and attached to a marijuana cultivation or manufacturing facility, and marijuana cultivation facilities are permitted in the B-3 district when collocated with and attached to a retail sales establishment (AO 2017-175(S) materials; 2026 TSDO drafts). Verify the current AMC 21.05 table before advising on a specific parcel — the drafts and the 2018 ordinance are not a substitute for the current code table.
  • Fairbanks North Star Borough: the borough’s marijuana information packet (FNSB, via fnsb.gov) sets facility zoning by district and a 500 ft buffer from K-12 schools and residential zones for most establishments, with a detailed type-by-district table (OR, RA, RF, RE, RR, SF, TF, MF, MFO, LC, GC, CBD, LI, HI, ML, GU). That packet is a borough guidance document, not a property listing, and is cited here only to show that “cannabis-eligible” in Fairbanks is district- and type-specific.
  • Consequence: A listing that says only “I-1” or “I-2” does NOT prove a cannabis establishment can operate there. It proves the industrial zoning class; the SLUP/local-option/buffer/licence questions remain. Conversely, a listing that says “active cannabis cultivation facility” but states no zoning does NOT prove zoning — it proves current use, which may be licensed at that address but does not travel with the land to a new operator (AMCO licences are premises-tied; see the Anchorage listing’s own statement that the tenant’s licence “is tied to the building and non-transferable to another location”).

8.2 Closest candidates — each fails the strict standard for a stated reason

Candidate A — 3253 West Coghlan Circle, Wasilla, AK (Mat-Su) — CLOSEST, but fails on zoning + price conflict

  • Listing (opened directly, 2026-11-01): https://www.420property.com/listing/cannabis-cultivation-facility-mixed-use-investment-and-cultivation-business-wasilla-alaska-13700sf/
  • Also shown ACTIVE on: https://www.420property.com/alaska/ (opened directly, 2026-11-01) and https://www.420property.com/alaska/wasilla-real-estate/ (search return, crawled 8 days before access).
  • What the listing itself states: “3253 W Coghlan Circle offers a rare, diversified investment opportunity in Wasilla, Alaska — a 13,700 SF modern building (built 2017) on 0.92 acres directly on Jacobsen Lake, with mountain views and Parks Highway visibility. The ground level houses an active, seller-owned cannabis cultivation operation alongside flexible commercial/warehouse space, while the upper level features two large 3BR/2BA residential units overlooking the lake.” Detail: 13,700 SF total (6,960 SF ground level, 6,740 SF second floor); three oversized bay doors; 20+ surface parking spaces; seller open to (a) combined sale (real estate + cultivation operation), (b) real estate only with cultivation space vacated, or (c) leaseback with seller remaining as tenant. “Asking price: $1,950,000. Full offering memorandum available upon request.”
  • Published / updated on the listing itself: Published 2026-08-17, Updated 2026-09-09. This is the only candidate with a 2026 published/updated date on the detail page itself.
  • Why it FAILS the strict standard: 1. No zoning type is stated anywhere in the detail page as opened. The Wasilla index page’s tagline (“with zoning notes where available”) does not supply a zoning for this parcel. Without a zoning type, criterion 4 fails. (Wasilla is in the Matanuska-Susitna Borough, not in the City of Wasilla’s zoning jurisdiction unless the parcel is inside city limits — the listing does not state which jurisdiction’s zoning applies. Verify parcel zoning with Mat-Su Borough / City of Wasilla before any reliance.) 2. Price conflict: The detail page states $1,950,000. The Wasilla index search return states “Price Reduced $1,995,000” for the same listing. The $45,000 difference is unresolved from the sources opened (it may reflect a price change between crawl and detail-page update, or an index caching error). The price is therefore reported as $1,950,000 (detail page) / $1,995,000 (index snippet) — conflict, Verify with the broker/offering memorandum. 3. Business + real estate, not land zoning alone: The “cultivation operation” is seller-owned and the deal structures include vacating the cultivation space or a leaseback — so the cannabis use is not shown to transfer automatically with the land. A buyer would still need AMCO licensing/transfer and local approval for the premises.
  • Listing status: ACTIVE on the index page opened 2026-11-01, and recent (published/updated 2026) — this part of the status is confirmed on the listing itself, unlike the other candidates. It is the closest candidate in the state on the searches tried, but it is NOT counted as strict-verified because of (1) and (2).

Candidate B — 7031 Arctic Boulevard, Anchorage, AK 99518 — fails on recency / active status / price

  • Listing (opened directly, 2026-11-01): https://cannamls.com/listing/alaska/cultivation/13512 — “Cannabis Cultivation & Manufacturing Facility for Sale in Anchorage, Alaska — 16,954 Sq. Ft. Warehouse with Active Licenses.” Text: “16,954 Sq. Ft office warehouse, situated on .61 acres of I1 (light industrial) zoned land, for sale in midtown Anchorage. Sale price of $2,950,000 ($174/Sq. Ft.). … The property is being leased by a well-established, long-term tenant that is currently operating multiple locations throughout Anchorage. The existing lease goes until 2030. … Tenants state issued operating license is tied to the building and non-transferable to another location…”
  • Listing page metadata (as opened): “Published: 2024-07-08” and “Price: 2950000 USD.” No ACTIVE badge and no 2025–2026 update date appear on the page as opened.
  • Corroboration that this is a real cannabis premises (not a generic warehouse): AMCO Marijuana Control Board agendas list Great Northern Manufacturing, Inc. (marijuana product manufacturing facility) at 7031 Arctic Boulevard, Suite 100, Anchorage, and Great Northern Cultivation, Inc. (standard marijuana cultivation facility) at 7031 Arctic Boulevard, Suite 1, Anchorage (MCB agendas, June 2020 / June 2021, via commerce.alaska.gov). A retail-locations page also lists Great Northern Manufacturing at 7031 Arctic Boulevard, Anchorage. This corroborates past/current cannabis use at the address; it does not prove the sale listing is still active.
  • Why it FAILS the strict standard: 1. Stale: Published July 2024, with no update shown. A 2024 listing cannot be treated as active/recent at 1 Nov 2026 without a fresh confirmation. 2. Price has moved in other syndication: The same property appears at $2,795,000 ($165/sq ft, last updated 01/09/2025, Brevitas) and at $2,695,000 (AlaskaRealEstate.com, MLS# 25-3521, via search returns), against the CannaMLS $2,950,000. Three prices for one property, none confirmed as the current asking price on 1 Nov 2026. Price: Verify — $2,950,000 (CannaMLS, 2024) vs $2,795,000 (Brevitas, Jan 2025) vs $2,695,000 (AK MLS, undated in excerpt). 3. Zoning is stated (I-1), marketing is explicit (cultivation/manufacturing, active licences), address is exact — so this candidate passes criteria 1, 3 and 4, and fails only on 2 (active/recent) and on price certainty (5, as a single confirmed price). It is the second-closest candidate.
  • Listing status: Verify — do not treat as active without contacting the listing broker.

Candidate C — 5740 B Street, Anchorage, AK 99518 — fails on recency and on cannabis marketing strength

  • Listing document (LoopNet PDF, via search return): https://images4.loopnet.com/d2/VExric19FRPAUiPaQEOT8Pd1_2FG0RvTde7d-ElvHAQ/document.pdf
  • What it states: 5740 B Street, Anchorage, AK 99518; Lot 7, B Street Industrial Center Subdivision; 4,326 sq ft building on a 9,259 sq ft lot; Zoning I-1 (Light Industrial); $4,500 per month ($1.04/sq ft in one rendering, $1.13/sq ft in another passage of the same document — internal inconsistency, flagged); “Landlord is open to conventional uses AND cannabis”; “Cannabis Potential: Landlord is open to cannabis operation, terms to be negotiated.”
  • Why it FAILS: (a) It is a lease, not a sale, and the document carries MLS 18-7470, indicating a 2018-era listing — stale, with no 2026 active confirmation; (b) “open to cannabis” is landlord willingness, not a cannabis licence, SLUP, or cannabis-specific zoning — it is cannabis-eligible industrial space at most, not a cannabis property in the strict sense; (c) the rent-per-sq-ft inconsistency inside the document itself undermines price certainty.
  • Listing status: Verify (stale).
