8.4 Hemp properties — none found

No property in Alaska marketed specifically for hemp use (hemp farm, hemp processing site, hemp-zoned land) with an address and price was found in the searches tried (420 Property hemp/real-estate pages, general hemp farm/land searches). The Wasilla and Anchorage candidates above are marijuana (cannabis) properties, not hemp properties. State agricultural land that could be used for hemp exists under AS 38.05.059 / AS 38.05.084 as amended by SB 208 §§12–18 (sale/lease of state land for agricultural uses, with per-acre price schedules and agricultural development plans), but no specific parcel offered for hemp use with a price was located — Unavailable as a listing, and SB 208’s land sections do not take effect until 1 Jan 2027 (§7.7), so no SB 208 land offering is in effect at the 1 Nov 2026 cut-off in any event.

8.5 What “zoned for cannabis” would actually require a buyer to check (Alaska-specific)

A buyer of any candidate above would still need, in order, none of which is shown by a listing alone:

  1. State licence: AMCO marijuana establishment licence for the specific type (cultivation, manufacturing, retail, testing) at that premises, or a transfer/relocation approved by the Marijuana Control Board. Licences are premises-tied (see Candidate B’s own statement).
  2. Local option: That the local government has not prohibited that licence type at that location (Run 1 Part 3; AS 17.38.210). Wasilla’s 2016 council ban and its 2026 status are both flagged Verify in Run 1 — a Wasilla buyer must confirm current local-option status, not rely on the listing’s “active cultivation” statement.
  3. Local zoning + SLUP (Anchorage) / borough zoning + permits (elsewhere): Current AMC Title 21 table / SLUP, or the borough’s facility zoning table and any conditional-use / SLUP equivalent, plus the 500 ft state buffer (3 AAC 306.010(a)) and any stricter local buffer.
  4. Hemp overlay (if hemp use is intended): A hemp grower registration is tied to a mapped grow area with GPS coordinates (§7.4.1); buying land does not transfer a hemp registration, and a new registration for that area is required. Hemp processing/retail at a cannabis premises additionally needs the DNR registrations/endorsements in §7.4, not just the AMCO licence.
  5. Federal overlay: Federal land, federal airspace/waters, and interstate transport issues are Part 10, not Part 8, and are not resolved by a state zoning or listing.

  16. Richardson Highway / North Pole photograph: Locate a broker listing with address and price before treating it as a property listing at all.

Part 9 — Regulators and History

PART 9 — REGULATORS AND HISTORY

9.1 Every state agency regulating cannabis and hemp

The table below is the complete state-agency map for Parts 9–10. Federal agencies that touch Alaska cannabis in transport or on federal land (TSA, U.S. Coast Guard, National Park Service, Bureau of Indian Affairs, USDA, U.S. Department of Defense) are not state agencies and are dealt with separately in Part 10, where their role actually arises. Within the state, no single agency regulates everything: licensing and enforcement sit with AMCO and the Marijuana Control Board, the medical registry sits with the Department of Health, hemp sits with the Department of Natural Resources’ Division of Agriculture, tax sits with the Department of Revenue’s Tax Division, criminal enforcement sits with the Department of Public Safety (Alaska State Troopers) and local police, and legal advice to state agencies sits with the Department of Law.

The Department states on that page that it “cannot provide legal advice to private citizens or organizations, but only to agencies and employees of the State of Alaska” — cited from the search return reproducing the FAQ page, Verify against the live page.

9.1.1 AMCO and the Marijuana Control Board — what each does, and what it does not do

AMCO is a staff office, not the decision-maker. The Board is, in the words of the HB 123 text reproduced on akleg.gov:

“The Marijuana Control Board is established in the Department of Commerce, Community, and Economic Development as a regulatory and quasi-judicial agency. The board is in the Department of Commerce, Community, and Economic Development for administrative purposes only.” — CSHB 123(L&C), § 2, repealing and reenacting AS 17.38.080(a), via https://www.akleg.gov/basis/Bill/Text/29?Hsid=HB0123B, accessed 2026-11-01 (search return reproducing the bill text).

The Board’s five seats are set out in the same text (see §9.2.5). AMCO’s own “About Us” page, directly opened in this run, states that AMCO’s headquarters are in Anchorage “with two enforcement outposts in Fairbanks and Juneau” and that its management team consists of the Director, the Program Coordinator, the Chief Investigator and the Administrative Officer. It also carries AMCO’s mission statement verbatim:

“Enforce alcohol and marijuana commerce laws and provide clear, consistent standards for licensure to protect the public from harm.” — AMCO “About Us” page, https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/amco/AboutUs, directly opened 2026-11-01.

Two limits matter for anyone using this dossier. First, AMCO’s home page carries its own warning, reproduced in Run 1: “During the database transition, not all license information may be current.” Any licence figure taken from the AK-ACCIS search inherits that caveat. Second, AMCO does not regulate hemp, does not run the medical registry, and does not collect the excise tax — those are DNR, DOH and DOR respectively (see table). Conflating AMCO with “the Alaska cannabis regulator” in full is therefore wrong; it is the marijuana-establishment regulator.

9.1.2 Department of Health — the medical registry, and only the registry

The Department of Health page, directly opened in this run, is short. In full, its operative text is:

“The Alaska Medical Marijuana Registry is a private program for people with certain medical conditions. It gives patients a special ID card that allows them to legally use medical marijuana. The card is only available to Alaska residents and is only valid in Alaska.” — DOH, “Medical Marijuana”, https://health.alaska.gov/en/education/drugs-and-alcohol/marijuana/medical-marijuana/, directly opened 2026-11-01.

“Alaska residents can apply for a Medical Marijuana Registry card using the Medical Marijuana Application Packet. Be sure to read all instructions carefully, as missing steps may result in a denial, and you won’t be able to reapply for at least 6 months. For more information, contact the Bureau of Vital Statistics, Registration Unit at (907) 465-5423.” — same page, directly opened 2026-11-01.

The registry statute itself, AS 17.37.010(a), as reproduced from akleg.gov in search returns, provides in operative part:

“(a) The department shall create and maintain a confidential registry of patients who have applied for and are entitled to receive a registry identification card according to the criteria set out in this chapter. … The registry and the information contained within it are not a public record under AS 40.25.100 - 40.25.220.” — AS 17.37.010(a), via https://www.akleg.gov/basis/folioproxy.asp?url=http://wwwjnu03.akleg.org/cgi-bin/folioisa.dll/stattx01/query=[jump!3A!27as1807051!27]/doc/{t6961}/pageitems={body}?, accessed 2026-11-01 (search return reproducing statute text; Verify against the live akleg.gov page for the current amended text).

What the Department does not do is equally important and is a Run 1 headline retained here: it does not license medical dispensaries, does not license medical cultivators or manufacturers, and does not administer a medical tax exemption. There is no medical business licence in Alaska. A registry card is therefore not a licence to sell, and a sale to a cardholder at an adult-use retail store is a sale by an adult-use licensee (see §10.1.6 on Schedule III).

A live regulatory footnote, dated after the dossier’s nominal cut-off window but visible in this run’s search returns, is that the Department of Health proposed changes to 7 AAC 34 (medical-use programme requirements) — including removing the Social Security number from reporting requirements — with written comments due by 5:00 p.m. on 6 November 2026 (Alaska Online Public Notices, https://aws.state.ak.us/OnlinePublicNotices/Notices/View.aspx?id=225117, accessed 2026-11-01). That proposal is not law at 1 November 2026 and is recorded here only so that a Run 2 or audit reader does not mistake a November 2026 proposal for a November 2026 rule.

9.1.3 Division of Agriculture — the hemp regulator

The Division of Agriculture (DNR) runs the industrial hemp programme under the USDA-approved Alaska State Plan (effective 1 January 2022, Run 1). Its statutory spine is AS 03.05.076–03.05.078 and the hemp definition in AS 03.05.100(5), which defines industrial hemp as “all parts and varieties of the plant Cannabis sativa L. containing not more than 0.3 percent of delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol (THC)” — quoted from the Division’s own 2019 presentation reproduced in search returns, and from SB 6 materials on akleg.gov (see §9.2.9). The Division’s operational home is the Plant Materials Center in Palmer, which is why hemp contact addresses in this dossier are Palmer addresses, not Anchorage or Juneau addresses. Caveat: the Division’s home page direct open failed with a 429 error in this run and was not retried; the Palmer addresses and phone numbers above are therefore cited from place records returned by search and are marked Verify against the live DNR page.

9.1.4 Department of Revenue, Tax Division — the tax collector and delinquency reporter

The Tax Division does not license anyone. Its cannabis role is threefold: (1) it administers the AS 43.61 excise tax and its regulations (15 AAC 61); (2) it publishes the annual marijuana-tax reports that are the only official statewide revenue series (Run 1, Part 6); and (3) it reports delinquent cultivators to the Marijuana Control Board. The 28 August 2026 delinquency letter reproduced in MCB meeting documents states the mechanism and the then-current position in the Division’s own words:

“As authorized by Alaska Statutes and Regulations, AS 43.05.230(e), AS 43.61.030 and 15 AAC 61.020, the Department of Revenue will inform the Marijuana Control Board (MCB) of licensed cultivators that have failed to pay tax due or file a return as required by law.” — DOR Tax Division letter, 28 August 2026, reproduced in MCB Tab 3 (Tax Delinquency Reports), https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/Minutes/2026/9.2-9.3/Tab%203%20-%20Tax%20Deliquency%20Reports%20last%2018th%20months.pdf, accessed 2026-11-01 (search return reproducing the letter).

“As of August 27th, 2026, there were 12 cultivation facilities with active licenses that failed to pay marijuana excise taxes as required under AS 43.61.010 for tax periods through June 30, 2026, and did not have an approved payment plan in place.” — same letter, same source.

That 12-active figure is consistent with, but narrower than, Run 1’s headline of $6.29 million past due across 12 active plus 37 inactive licences; the two figures measure different populations and are not in conflict.

9.1.5 Alaska State Troopers and the Department of Law — enforcement and legal advice

The Troopers are the state’s general criminal-enforcement arm for controlled substances outside licensed activity. The DPS contact page, directly opened in this run, lists the Statewide Drug Enforcement Unit at 5500 East Tudor Road, Anchorage, AK 99507, phone (907) 269-5511, with anonymous tips about drug trafficking directed to that unit, and the Troopers’ Director’s Office at 5700 East Tudor Road, Anchorage, AK 99507, phone (907) 269-5511, email dps.ast.directors.office@alaska.gov. Handler-permit background checks are processed through DPS offices, not through AMCO itself — a point that belongs in Part 11 but is noted here because it explains why DPS appears in a cannabis dossier at all.

The Department of Law’s role is advisory and prosecutorial, not regulatory. Its Commercial and Fair Business section “provides representation, defense, and counsel to 17 different divisions, commissions, and corporations” and, in the FY2019 budget component summary reproduced in search returns, “spends substantial time every year researching, analyzing, and advising the agencies on statutory licensing and enforcement requirements” for marijuana, with many issues “of first impression”. Two formal Attorney General opinions are the ones a researcher will actually cite (see §9.2.10): the 31 August 2016 opinion that unlicensed marijuana social clubs are illegal, and the 21 July 2005 press-release advice that Gonzales v. Raich, 545 U.S. 1 (2005), did not prohibit the state from continuing to register medical marijuana users — while warning that “under Raich the federal government can criminally prosecute Alaskans for the possession, distribution, or use of marijuana under federal law even if the person has registered with the state.”

9.1.6 Other bodies that appear in cannabis files but are not state cannabis regulators

  • Division of Elections (Office of the Lieutenant Governor) — runs ballot-measure and local-option elections and publishes certified results. It does not regulate cannabis; it is the source for §9.3. See https://www.elections.alaska.gov/ — Verify the current results-portal URL, as no Division of Elections results page was directly opened in this run.
  • Alaska Court System — decides Ravin, Noy and John v. Baker (see §9.2). It is not a regulator.
  • Department of Transportation and Public Facilities (DOT&PF), Alaska Marine Highway System (AMHS) — a transport operator whose published marijuana policy is a federal-law policy, not a state licensing rule (see §10.2.2). AMHS reservations: 1-800-642-0066, as published on the AMHS travel-policies page directly opened in this run.
  • Local governments — boroughs, unified municipalities and cities exercise the AS 17.38.210 local-option and zoning powers detailed in Run 1, Part 3. They are not state agencies.

The timeline below gives, for each step, the date, what happened, and whether the step comes from Ravin (1975), a ballot measure, a later bill (with number), a court ruling, or a Board regulation. Dates that are given only by year in the sources are given only by year here; no day or month has been invented.

9.2.1 Ravin v. State (1975) — what it actually held, and what it did not hold

Ravin is the foundation of everything distinctive about Alaska cannabis law, and it is also the most overstated case in popular summaries. The holding, as the Alaska Court of Appeals restated it in Noy, is narrow and home-bound:

“In Ravin v. State, 537 P.2d 494 (Alaska 1975), the Alaska Supreme Court held that the privacy clause of the Alaska Constitution (Article I, Section 22) protects the possession of marijuana in one’s home for personal use.” — Noy v. State rehearing opinion, as reproduced at https://www.casemine.com/judgement/us/5914786dadd7b049343e565e, accessed 2026-11-01.

Three limits, all stated in the August 2026 Connecticut Cannabis Reform Project brief that collects the Alaska cases (a secondary source, used here only to flag limits that the primary opinions themselves contain), must be kept with the holding:

  1. Ravin protected possession and ingestion in the home; it did not protect cultivation as such, and popular summaries that describe it as a cultivation right overstate it — Verify the cultivation point against the full Ravin opinion before relying on it, as the brief itself notes the point is contested in popular retellings.
  2. Ravin expressly refused to protect buying, selling or transfer — the commercial market created in 2014 therefore does not rest on Ravin at all; it rests on Ballot Measure 2 and AS 17.38.
  3. Every other state supreme court asked to follow Ravin has declined. Ravin is, in the words of the Cannabis in Alaska summary, “the first – and only – U.S. state or federal court to announce a constitutional right to privacy that protects some level of marijuana use and possession” — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_in_Alaska, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary; the “only” claim is Verify against a current 50-state survey, but no contrary state supreme court holding was located in this run).

The constitutional text behind Ravin is Alaska Constitution art. I, § 22: “The right of the people to privacy is recognized and shall not be infringed.” That text was added in 1972 and is the reason an initiative or a statute cannot simply override Ravin by majority vote — a point the courts returned to in Noy and in the 2006 litigation.

9.2.2 The 1990 recriminalisation and the 1992 Superior Court response

Ballot Measure 2 (November 1990) purported to recriminalise possession of any amount of marijuana, with penalties of up to 90 days in jail and a fine of up to $1,000 for simple possession (secondary summary, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis_in_Alaska, accessed 2026-11-01). It passed (see §9.3). It did not, however, erase Ravin in the courts. In 1992, Superior Court Judge Michael A. Thompson dismissed possession charges against Patrick McNeil (0.21 grams in the home), ruling that the initiative’s provisions conflicting with Ravin were “essentially legally irrelevant in light of stare decisis” — as summarised in “The continuing vitality of Ravin v. State”, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary legal commentary; the Superior Court record itself was not opened in this run — Verify).

The deeper point, made in the Noy rehearing opinion, is that the 1982 legislature had already tried to give Ravin a workable line:

“In Ravin v. State, this court held that the state could not bar the personal use and possession of marijuana in the home. In view of the relative harmlessness of the drug, the individual’s right to privacy under the Alaska Constitution was found to outweigh the state interest in regulation.” — Noy rehearing opinion, quoting the 1982 Commentary and Sectional Analysis, as reproduced at https://www.casemine.com/judgement/us/5914786dadd7b049343e565e, accessed 2026-11-01.

“The approach taken in this Act is to define, for purposes of the decision in Ravin, an amount which is indicative of personal use, and to provide a clear line of demarcation of four ounces, so that citizens of this state will know precisely what conduct is prohibited.” — same source, same page.

That 4-ounce line is the line Noy later enforced.

9.2.3 Noy v. State (2003) — the 4-ounce rule

Noy v. State, 83 P.3d 538 (Alaska App. 2003), with rehearing denied at 83 P.3d 545, is the case that actually decided the fate of the 1990 initiative. David S. Noy was convicted by a jury of possessing less than 8 ounces. The Court of Appeals reversed and, in the sentence most often quoted, held:

“To make AS 11.71.060(a)(1) consistent with article I, section 22 of the Alaska Constitution as interpreted in Ravin, we must limit the scope of the statute. As currently written, the statute prohibits possession of any amount of marijuana. But with regard to possession of marijuana by adults in their home for personal use, AS 11.71.060(a)(1) must be interpreted to prohibit only the possession of four ounces or more of marijuana.” — Noy, 83 P.3d 538, as quoted at https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noy_v._State, accessed 2026-11-01.

And in the rehearing opinion, the court rejected the State’s argument that Ravin created only an affirmative defence rather than a limit on legislative power, noting that in 1978, in State v. Erickson, 543 P.2d 206, 207 (Alaska 1975) [sic — the year as reproduced in the search return; Verify the Erickson citation], “the supreme court again declared that Ravin represented a restriction on the state’s power to legislate” — https://www.casemine.com/judgement/us/5914786dadd7b049343e565e, accessed 2026-11-01.

Practical consequence, retained from Run 1 and still true at 1 November 2026: the statute (AS 11.71.060) and the constitution (as interpreted in Ravin/Noy) point in different directions for in-home possession between 1 and 4 ounces. AS 17.38 now authorises 1 ounce in public and the home-grow regime, but the Ravin/Noy 4-ounce in-home protection is a separate constitutional floor that a statute cannot remove. No post-2014 case located in this run holds that Ballot Measure 2 repealed or narrowed Ravin; the 2014 measure is better read as superseding the statutory fight by creating a licensed market, while Ravin remains the constitutional backstop. That reading is an interpretation, not a quoted holding — Verify against a current Alaska appellate decision before advising on it.

9.2.4 The 2006 legislature, the Superior Court, and State v. ACLU (2009)

In June 2006 the legislature amended AS 11.71.060(a) to prohibit possession of less than 1 ounce (HB 149, ch. 53 SLA 2006). The ACLU of Alaska and two anonymous plaintiffs sued. Superior Court Judge Patricia Collins granted summary judgment for the ACLU in July 2006, holding that a lower court “cannot reverse the State Supreme Court’s 1975 decision in Ravin v. State” and that “[u]nless and until the Supreme Court directs otherwise, Ravin is the law in this state” — as reported by NORML, 13 July 2006, https://norml.org/news/2006/07/13/alaska-judge-rejects-pot-recriminalization-measure/, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary; court record not opened — Verify).

The Alaska Supreme Court did not affirm that judgment on the merits. In State v. American Civil Liberties Union of Alaska, 204 P.3d 364 (Alaska 2009), the court held the pre-enforcement challenge was not ripe and dismissed the appeal, reasoning that the plaintiffs “will remain subject to prosecution under federal law regardless of how we might rule” and that “any challenge to the statute must await an actual prosecution” — https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/state-v-american-civil-893509815, accessed 2026-11-01 (reproducing the opinion). The dissent (Carpeneti, J.) called it a “high-profile” case. The net effect, correctly stated in Run 1 and retained here, is: Ravin was not overruled in 2009; it was left intact because the court declined to decide the abstract challenge. Anyone who writes that “the Alaska Supreme Court upheld the 2006 recriminalisation in 2009” is wrong; anyone who writes that “the Supreme Court struck down the 2006 law in 2009” is also wrong. The 2009 decision decided ripeness, not the merits.

9.2.5 Ballot Measure 8 (1998) and the medical registry’s statutory afterlife

Ballot Measure 8 (3 November 1998) created the medical-use regime now codified at AS 17.37. Its ballot title, as reproduced in the 1998 election summary, was “BILL ALLOWING MEDICAL USE OF MARIJUANA” (Initiative No. 97PSDM), and it provided that “patients [may] use marijuana for certain medical purposes” on a doctor’s finding — Erowid 1998 election summary, https://www.erowid.org/plants/cannabis/98_vote/, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary; the official ballot pamphlet was not opened — Verify).

The effective-date question for Measure 8 is genuinely messy in the secondary literature and is flagged rather than resolved here:

  • One secondary summary (Connecticut OLR Research Report 98-R-1390, 10 December 1998) gives Alaska’s effective date as 1 December 1998 — https://cga.ct.gov/PS98/rpt/olr/htm/98-R-1390.htm, accessed 2026-11-01.
  • Another secondary summary (the long-running medical-programme summary reproduced by 420 Magazine and others) gives the law’s effective date as 4 March 1999, and states that SB 94, effective 2 June 1999, then mandated registry enrolment and a card — https://www.420magazine.com/community/threads/summary-of-the-active-medical-marijuana-programs-in-the-united-states.96818/, accessed 2026-11-01.

Both dates may be describing different legal events (initiative effective date vs. the date the registry regime as amended by SB 94 became the operative protection). Verify against the 1998 session law and the SB 94 enrolled text on akleg.gov before publishing a single effective date. What is not in doubt is the structure that resulted: a confidential registry (AS 17.37.010), physician certification, primary and alternate caregivers, and — crucially — no dispensary system. That absence is not an oversight in the 1998 text that was later filled; it has never been filled. The medical programme has remained a registry-only programme for 28 years.

9.2.6 Ballot Measure 2 (2014) and the first retail sales (October 2016)

Ballot Measure 2 (4 November 2014), “An Act to tax and regulate the production, sale, and use of marijuana”, passed 149,021 to 130,924 (53.23% — see §9.3) and took effect 24 February 2015. It created AS 17.38, the $50/oz excise tax (AS 43.61.010), the local-option system (AS 17.38.210 and AS 17.38.300–.350), and the authority for a Marijuana Control Board. The Board itself was created by the legislature, not by the initiative: HB 123 (2015) repealed and reenacted AS 17.38.080 to establish a five-member board (see §9.1.1). The Governor’s transmittal and the bill’s sectional analysis describe the seats identically: public safety, public health, rural, marijuana industry, and general public or industry — https://www.akleg.gov/basis/get_documents.asp?session=29&docid=3741 (HB 123 sectional analysis), accessed 2026-11-01 (search return).

First retail sales, October 2016 — the clarification promised in §9.0. Two events in two days are routinely collapsed into one, and they must be kept apart:

Date (2016) Event Source (accessed 2026-11-01)
28 October, evening Pakalolo Supply Co., Fairbanks, makes a ceremonial first legal sale (Facebook Live video); the store was not yet open to the public. It had passed final state inspection that morning and received its first flower test results that afternoon. Leafly, 31 October 2016, https://www.leafly.ca/news/industry/alaska-celebrates-first-retail-sales-two-years-legalization
29 October, noon Herbal Outfitters, Valdez, opens to the public — the first retail store to open. More than 250 people lined up; the first customer bought five strains totalling 8 grams for $145.65. Alaska Dispatch News, 29 October 2016, as reproduced at https://bandarlampung.imigrasi.go.id/atp/api/crawl-website.php?url=https%3A%2F%2Fweb.archive.org%2Fweb%2F20161116020638%2Fhttps%3A%2F%2Fwww.adn.com%2Falaska-marijuana%2F2016%2F10%2F29%2Fanticipation-builds-as-alaskas-first-marijuana-store-set-to-open-to-the-public%2F ; Leafly, same article as above.
31 October (Monday after) CannTest, Anchorage, the state’s first testing lab, opens after clearing final regulatory hurdles. Leafly, same article as above.

Run 1’s headline — “first retail store opened 29 October 2016, Herbal Outfitters, Valdez” — is therefore retained as the correct statement about the first store opening to the public. The 28 October Pakalolo sale is the correct statement about the first legal sale. Neither date contradicts the other once the distinction is drawn.

9.2.7 Onsite consumption: rules (2019) and first openings (2020–2021)

Alaska was the first state to adopt statewide onsite-consumption rules. The sequence, with the Run 1 effective date retained, is:

  1. December 2018 — Marijuana Control Board adopts onsite-consumption regulations (3 AAC 306.370 and related rules). Source: ADN visitor guide (December 2018 vote) and MPP Alaska page, https://www.mpp.org/states/alaska/?state=AK, accessed 2026-11-01.
  2. 11 April 2019 — rules effective (Run 1 headline, retained). Licensed retailers may apply for a special onsite-use endorsement, an add-on to a retail licence, not a standalone licence type. One secondary source (500 Nations, summarising the same event) gives “Beginning April 11, 2019 Alaska’s licensed marijuana retailers can apply for a ‘special onsite use endorsement’” — https://www.500nations.com/Alaska-Cannabis.asp, accessed 2026-11-01 — which is consistent with the 11 April 2019 effective date. A different secondary date, 14 March 2019, appears in some summaries for the same rules; it is treated here as the adoption/publication step, not the effective date, and is flagged Verify against the Alaska Administrative Code register entry (Register 230, per Run 1).
  3. January 2020 — Board approves the first two onsite-consumption licences: Cannabis Corner (Ketchikan) and GoodSinse (Fairbanks) — Alaska Cannabist, 2021, cited in §9.2 table.
  4. 2020 (days only, then closed) — Cannabis Corner in Ketchikan briefly opens for onsite consumption but closes after a few days because of the COVID-19 pandemic — Alaska Cannabist, 2021 (“A business in Ketchikan did briefly open for on-site consumption, but the pandemic forced it to close after just a few days”).
  5. 20 April 2021, 4:20 p.m. — Good Titrations, Fairbanks (506 Merhar Ave., in a former Chili’s restaurant off the Johansen Expressway), opens the first sustained onsite-consumption café. The build-out cost is reported at approximately $375,000, including ventilation — Alaska Cannabist, 2021, and the Alaska Leaf / Saving the Plants summaries, https://savingtheplants.com/what-ever-happened-to-the-promise-of-pot-cafes-in-alaska/, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary; the $375,000 figure is a business-reported figure, not an official figure).

The operating count at 1 November 2026 remains Unavailable as an official figure (Run 1, Part 4). MPP’s Alaska page, accessed 2026-11-01, states that “[a]t least two retailers — Good Titrations in Fairbanks and Cannabis Corner in Ketchikan — allow on-site consumption” — https://www.mpp.org/states/alaska/?state=AK — which is consistent with Run 1’s “at least two reported operating in 2026” but is not an official AMCO count and is not adopted as one.

9.2.8 Tax changes — the only tax timeline that matters for Part 9

The excise tax was created by Ballot Measure 2 itself (AS 43.61.010, $50/oz). Its subsequent history, as published by the Tax Division itself, is:

“2014 – Ballot Measure 2, the act to tax and regulate the production, sale, and use of marijuana, was passed on Nov. 4, 2014. Pursuant to the ballot measure, Alaska will levy a tax on the marijuana sold in Alaska. 2016 – The Legislature passed Senate Bill 91, a comprehensive criminal reform bill, which included a provision that diverted half of the state’s marijuana excise taxes to programs aimed at reducing repeat criminal offenders, under a newly created Recidivism Reduction Fund. – The Department of Revenue adopted a regulation that set the tax as $50 for any part of a marijuana bud or flower, and $15 for the remainder of the plant. 2018 – The Legislature passed Senate Bill 104, an education curriculum bill, which includes a provision that deposits 25% of the state’s marijuana excise taxes into the Marijuana Education and Treatment Fund, effective Oct. 28, 2018. – The department adopted changes to marijuana tax regulations in Title 15, Chapter 43 [sic — the Tax Division history page as reproduced says Chapter 43; the current citation in Run 1 is 15 AAC 61, and the discrepancy is flagged Verify] of the Alaska Administrative Code. The changes, which go into effect Jan. 1, 2019, add a third tax rate for immature or abnormal bud: $25 an ounce for buds or flowers that did not mature, that contain seeds or that failed testing because of mold. The changes stipulate that clones will be taxed at a flat rate of $1 per plant, and not by weight.” — DOR marijuana-tax history, https://tax.alaska.gov/programs/programs/reports/Annual.aspx?60000&Year=2018, accessed 2026-11-01 (search return reproducing the DOR page; the direct open of the Tax Division index page failed with a 429 error in this run and was not retried).

The resulting distribution, retained from Run 1, Part 2, is: 50% Recidivism Reduction Fund (SB 91, 2016), 25% Marijuana Education and Treatment Fund (SB 104, 2018, effective 28 October 2018), 25% residual General Fund — with Run 1’s caveat that the DOR FY2025 presentation does not divide exactly 50/25/25. No rate change has been enacted since 1 January 2019. The reform arc after 2019 is a history of proposals, not law: the Governor’s Advisory Task Force on Recreational Marijuana (administrative order, 21 September 2022, per secondary reporting); HB 119 (33rd Legislature), which passed the House in May 2024 ($12.50/oz, then a retail sales tax in successive versions) and died; and HB 91, SB 73 and HB 94 (34th Legislature, 2025–2026), all still in committee at their last akleg.gov status dates (Run 1). None of those bills is law, and none is presented as law here.

9.2.10 Attorney General opinions and other legal landmarks that belong in Part 9

  • 31 August 2016 — Attorney General Jahna Lindemuth opinion: unlicensed marijuana social clubs are illegal; consumption at such clubs is public consumption, which AS 17.38 prohibits, and offering samples to paying patrons may violate the criminal distribution law. Source: opinions index, https://LAW.ALASKA.GOV/doclibrary/opinions-index/opinions2016.html, and AP report via SitNews, http://www.sitnews.us/0916News/090116/090116_social_clubs.html, accessed 2026-11-01. Quoted rationale: “When Alaskans voted in 2014 to liberalize personal use of marijuana and to allow a commercial marijuana industry, they also voted to prohibit public consumption of marijuana.” This opinion is the reason the 2019 onsite-consumption endorsement, rather than a social-club model, is the only lawful public-consumption route.
  • 2001 — Attorney General opinion index entry, 9 November 2001, “Initiative Petition Legalizing Hemp and Marijuana” — https://law.alaska.gov/doclibrary/opinions-index/opinions2001.html, accessed 2026-11-01. The opinion itself was not opened in this run; its holding is Unavailable here and is listed only so that an auditor does not mistake the index entry for a holding.

Historical interest only; it predates SB 6 by 25 years and is not current law.

If a later statewide measure exists, it was not located — Verify against the Division of Elections measure list before asserting that none exists. No statewide banned-count and no consolidated local-option election-results table is asserted here, for the same reason as in Run 1: the AMCO local-option page is UNDER CONSTRUCTION and the only comprehensive list located is the March 2016 spreadsheet.

A note on the 1990 measure’s description in secondary sources: one secondary index (Initiative and Referendum Institute, https://www.initiativeandreferenduminstitute.org/alaska, accessed 2026-11-01) describes the 1990 measure as one that “reduced penalties for marijuana possession” and says it “was approved”. That description is inconsistent with the recriminalisation account given by every other source in this run (including the ballot-measures list, which records it as “a measure criminalizing marijuana possession” passing 105,263 to 88,644, and the Noy history, which treats the 1990 measure as the statute that “purports to criminalize the possession of any amount of marijuana”). The IRI description is treated here as an error or as a description of a different 1990 proposal, and is not adopted. Verify against the 1990 Official Election Pamphlet if the distinction matters.

Polling is secondary evidence, and in Alaska it is unusually thin and unusually volatile. The polls below are the ones with a named pollster, a date and (where published) a sample. Polls without a sample are flagged as such. No Alaska-specific poll from 2024, 2025 or 2026 was located in this run; the most recent Alaska-specific polls located are from 2019, and the 2025 national figures below are not Alaska figures and must not be presented as such.
