What the polling record actually shows, and what it does not show: In 2014, Alaska polling swung from a 16-point lead (February, PPP) to a 5-point deficit (August, PPP) to an 18.5-point lead (late September, Ivan Moore, ballot wording, campaign-paid question) — and the actual result was a 6.46-point win (53.23% to 46.77%). The lesson the contemporary reporting itself drew is that Alaska is “a relatively tough place to poll accurately because it is a geographically large and economically diverse state with a relatively small population” (Stop the Drug War summary of the August 2014 PPP poll). No poll in this run measures support for any specific post-2014 policy (tax reform, onsite consumption, hemp rules, Schedule III treatment) in Alaska, and no 2024–2026 Alaska-specific poll was located. The national figures sometimes cited alongside Alaska polls — Gallup, 1–16 October 2025, n=1,000 adults in all 50 states and D.C., 64% saying marijuana use should be legal, down from 68–70% in 2020–2024 (Cannabis Science & Technology summary of Gallup, https://www.cannabissciencetech.com/view/gallup-poll-results-support-for-cannabis-legalization-is-high-but-not-highest, accessed 2026-11-01) — are national, not Alaskan, and are included here only to prevent their misattribution to Alaska.

9.3.3 Why the ballot history matters for the rest of the dossier

Three structural points follow from the ballot history and are used in Part 10:

  1. The constitution constrains the ballot. Because Ravin rests on art. I, § 22, a ballot measure cannot override it by simple majority. The 1990 measure tried; the courts in McNeil (1992) and Noy (2003) refused to give it effect against in-home possession under 4 ounces. Any future initiative that purported to recriminalise in-home possession would face the same constitutional problem.
  2. The medical programme has never been revisited by the voters. Measure 8 (1998) has never been repealed, replaced or expanded by a later ballot measure. Its registry-only structure is therefore not a temporary gap; it is the settled voter-approved structure, amended only by the legislature (SB 94, 1999) and by regulation (7 AAC 34).
  3. The adult-use market rests on a 6.46-point margin. Measure 2 (2014) passed 53.23% to 46.77%. That narrowness is part of why the post-2014 history is dominated by legislative and regulatory adjustment (HB 123, tax proposals, the AO-360 package) rather than by further initiatives, and why local-option politics (Run 1, Part 3) remain live.

Part 10 — Tribal and Interstate

PART 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.1 Alaska’s federally recognised tribes, Metlakatla, and tribal jurisdiction

10.1.1 The count: 229 tribes, and one reservation

The Bureau of Indian Affairs, Alaska Region, states directly (page directly opened in this run):

“More than 180,000 Tribal members make up the 229 Federally Recognized Tribes under the jurisdiction of the Alaska Regional Office - from Ketchikan in the Southeast Panhandle to Barrow on the Arctic Ocean and from Eagle on the Yukon Territory border to Atka in the Aleutian Chain.” — BIA, Alaska Region, https://www.bia.gov/regional-office/alaska-region, directly opened 2026-11-01.

And:

“The entire state of Alaska falls under the jurisdiction of the Alaska Region, with the small exception of the Annette Island Reserve, which falls under the Northwest Region.” — same page, directly opened 2026-11-01.

Two counting caveats, both flagged rather than resolved:

  • Tribal-member population conflict: the BIA Alaska Region page directly opened in this run says “more than 180,000 Tribal members”, while a second BIA page (“More about the Alaska Region”, https://www.bia.gov/regional-offices/alaska/content, accessed 2026-11-01 via search return) says “more than 180,000 Tribal members” for the same 229 tribes. The 229 figure is consistent across both pages; the member count is not. Verify which member count is current before publishing either.
  • 229 vs. 226 vs. 231: an archived BIA press release (pre-2025, marked ARCHIVED and NOT UPDATED) refers to a revised list of “226 Alaskan tribal entities” — https://www.bia.gov/as-ia/opa/online-press-release/interior-publishes-revised-list-alaska-native-tribes-eligible, accessed 2026-11-01 — while a secondary tribal directory (500 Nations, https://www.500nations.com/Alaska-Cannabis.asp, accessed 2026-11-01) refers to “231 Alaska Native villages” citing the Federal Register of 4 May 2016. The 229 figure in the task commission and on the current BIA Alaska Region page is retained here as the operative count; the 226 and 231 figures are older or differently-defined counts (entities vs. villages vs. tribes) and are listed here so that an auditor can see why a different number might appear elsewhere. Verify against the current Federal Register list of Indian Entities Recognized by and Eligible To Receive Services From the United States Bureau of Indian Affairs before publishing a definitive count.

The 229 tribes are not listed individually in this report. Listing 229 tribal names from a secondary compilation would violate the sourcing rule for legal points (official sources only) and would add no cannabis-specific information, since — as §10.1.4 shows — no tribe-specific cannabis operation was located. The authoritative list is the Federal Register list itself; the BIA Alaska Region page is the entry point to it.

10.1.2 Metlakatla Indian Community and the Annette Islands Reserve — the state’s only reservation

Metlakatla is the exception that proves the ANCSA rule. The U.S. Supreme Court in Venetie states it in terms (see §10.1.5):

“ANCSA revoked ‘the various reserves set aside … for Native use’ by legislative or executive action, except for the Annette Island Reserve inhabited by the Metlakatla Indians, and completely extinguished all aboriginal claims to Alaska land. §§1603, 1618(a).” — Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government, 522 U.S. 520 (1998), opinion of the Court, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/96-1577.ZO.html, directly opened 2026-11-01.

The reserve’s history, in brief: Tsimshian people relocated from British Columbia to Annette Island in 1887 (“New Metlakatla”); Congress set apart the Annette Islands as a reservation for the Metlakatla Indians by the Act of 3 March 1891 (26 Stat. 1101, § 15, as quoted in the 1911 Town Council materials reproduced in the Alaska Mission Records, https://archive.org/download/alaskamissionrec00uni_tar/alaskamissionrec00uni_tar.pdf, accessed 2026-11-01 — historical, secondary for current law); Metlakatla did not participate in ANCSA’s land settlement in the same way as other villages and is therefore not an ANCSA village corporation community; and the BIA treats the Annette Island Reserve as falling under the Northwest Region, not the Alaska Region (BIA Alaska Region page, directly opened, quoted in §10.1.1).

Cannabis/hemp position for Metlakatla: none located. No Metlakatla cannabis or hemp operation, ordinance, ban, compact or medical programme was located in this run, and no Metlakatla tribal-government source on cannabis was opened. The community’s published visitor information (secondary, Islands.com, https://www.islands.com/1977987/metlakatla-alaska-island-destination-remote-getaway-outdoor-adventue-art/, accessed 2026-11-01) notes that staying overnight (24 hours) in Metlakatla requires a permit from the community — a general visitor-control rule, not a cannabis rule, and it is not presented here as a cannabis rule. The correct statement for Metlakatla is therefore: no cannabis or hemp law, operation or ban was located; the question is Unavailable, not answered in the negative. That distinction matters: absence of a located ordinance is not proof that no ordinance exists, and a future auditor should check with the Metlakatla Indian Community directly (community website as listed in secondary directories: http://www.metlakatla.com/ — Verify that this is the current official site before using it, as it was not opened in this run).

10.1.3 No Alaska tribal cannabis compact statute, and no located tribal cannabis operation

For the Lower 48, the model is the state-tribal compact: Washington and Nevada tribes operate cannabis businesses under compacts with the state, and the compact — not the state licence — is the legal basis. Alaska has no equivalent. No Alaska statute authorising a cannabis compact with a tribe was located in this run, no Alaska tribal-state cannabis compact was located, and no Alaska tribe was located operating a cannabis dispensary, cultivation facility or manufacturing facility under either a tribal licence or a state AMCO licence held in a tribal name.

What was located, and must not be overstated, is a set of general statements that do not establish an Alaska operation:

  • A law-firm page on tribal cannabis (MCT Law, https://www.mctlaw.com/indian-law/tribal-cannabis-production-and-sales/, accessed 2026-11-01) states the general principle that “[t]ribes have the authority to develop their own laws and regulations, often in collaboration with state and federal authorities” and that “[t]ribal sovereignty is the cornerstone of Indian self-governance” — but its case study is the Sauk-Suiattle Tribe in Washington, not an Alaska tribe, and it is a law-firm marketing page, not an official source. It is cited here only to show the general framework, not as evidence of any Alaska operation.
  • A secondary directory (500 Nations, https://www.500nations.com/Alaska-Cannabis.asp, accessed 2026-11-01) asserts that “Alaska native villages have both federal and the state approvals to grow and sale commercial cannabis on their land” and that a 2014 U.S. Department of Justice policy “allows the cultivation and sales of cannabis on these reservation lands in states that have legalized the use of marijuana”. That assertion is not adopted: it cites no Alaska-specific approval, no village name and no operative document; it conflates the general 2014 Cole Memorandum framework (which was rescinded in 2018) with an Alaska-specific approval that was not produced; and it is inconsistent with the absence of any located Alaska tribal operation, compact or ordinance. It is recorded here as a conflicting secondary claim, rejected as unproven, so that a future reader who finds the same page does not mistake it for a finding of this dossier.
  • The Lexology tribal-cannabis guide (https://www.lexology.com/library/detail.aspx?g=f088d025-d7d5-408b-9d24-a24be94c818c, accessed 2026-11-01, secondary, 2020-era) correctly states the general rule that “[a] tribe’s decisions regarding cannabis — its use, growth, distribution, and taxation — may be different than the state jurisdiction in which the tribe is located” — but it names no Alaska tribe and no Alaska decision. General tribal authority is not evidence of an Alaska exercise of that authority.

The honest summary is therefore short: as of 1 November 2026, no Alaska tribal cannabis operation, ordinance, ban or compact was located in official tribal or government sources. That is a research result (nothing found), not a legal conclusion that no tribe could operate one. Any future claim of an Alaska tribal operation must name the tribe, the legal basis (tribal ordinance, compact, or AMCO licence), and the official source — none of which was produced in this run.

10.1.4 Hemp and tribes in Alaska — the USDA plan list, and what it does not show

Under the 2018 Farm Bill, a tribe may submit its own hemp production plan to USDA for approval, independently of the state plan. The USDA approved-plans page (updated 17 February 2026, as reproduced in search returns) lists Alaska as an approved state plan (“Alaska — AK Approved Plan (pdf)*”) and then lists tribal plans by tribe name. No Alaska tribe appears in the tribal-plan portion of the list as reproduced in the search return — the tribal names reproduced are from the Lower 48 (Blackfeet, Cheyenne River Sioux, Colorado River Indian Tribes, and others) and from other regions. Two qualifications apply:

  1. The search return reproduced only the beginning of the tribal list; a tribe appearing later in the full alphabetical list would not have been visible. The statement “no Alaska tribe located” is therefore a statement about the portion of the list actually returned, not a certified full-list sweep. Verify by opening the full USDA list (https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review) — noting that the direct open of that URL failed with a transient grant error in this run and was not retried — and searching it for Alaska tribal names.
  2. Even if no Alaska tribe holds an approved tribal hemp plan, an Alaska tribe could in principle grow hemp under the state plan (as a state registrant) or under a USDA Hemp Producer Licence in the absence of a tribal plan. No such Alaska tribal registrant was located in the DNR hemp lists as summarised in Run 1, Part 6, but the DNR lists were not re-swept tribe-by-tribe in this run. Verify by checking the current DNR registrant list for tribal-entity names.

The practical consequence is the same as for marijuana: no Alaska tribal hemp operation was located, and no Alaska tribal hemp plan was located; both are Unavailable/Verify, not proven negatives.

10.1.5 How tribal jurisdiction works in Alaska — ANCSA, Venetie, and what survived it

Alaska tribal jurisdiction is unlike tribal jurisdiction in the Lower 48 in one decisive respect: there is, with the Metlakatla exception, almost no “Indian country” in Alaska in the territorial sense. The chain is:

Step 1 — ANCSA (1971). The Alaska Native Claims Settlement Act, 43 U.S.C. § 1601 et seq., settled aboriginal claims by conveying land and money to state-chartered Native corporations, not to tribes, and by revoking the reserves set aside for Native use (except Annette Island). The Supreme Court in Venetie summarises the design (directly opened opinion, quoted in §10.1.2): ANCSA was to be accomplished “without creating a reservation system or lengthy wardship or trusteeship” (§ 1601(b)), the ANCSA corporations “received title to the transferred land in fee simple, and no federal restrictions applied to subsequent land transfers by them” (§ 1613), and the settlement extinguished “all aboriginal claims to Alaska land” (§ 1603).

Step 2 — Venetie (1998). Alaska v. Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government, 522 U.S. 520 (1998), decided 25 February 1998, unanimous opinion by Justice Thomas, held that approximately 1.8 million acres owned in fee simple by the Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government (the former Venetie Reservation, conveyed to two Native corporations under ANCSA § 1618(b) in 1973 and then transferred to the tribal government) is not Indian country. The opinion, directly opened in this run, states the question and answer in its first paragraph:

“In this case, we must decide whether approximately 1.8 million acres of land in northern Alaska, owned in fee simple by the Native Village of Venetie Tribal Government pursuant to the Alaska Native Claims Settlement Act, 43 U.S.C. § 1601 et seq., is ‘Indian country.’ We conclude that it is not, and we therefore reverse the judgment below.” — Venetie, 522 U.S. 520, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/96-1577.ZO.html, directly opened 2026-11-01.

And it states the governing test:

“We now hold that [a ‘dependent Indian community’] refers to a limited category of Indian lands that are neither reservations nor allotments, and that satisfy two requirements — first, they must have been set aside by the Federal Government for the use of the Indians as Indian land; second, they must be under federal superintendence.” — same opinion, directly opened 2026-11-01.

ANCSA land fails both requirements in the Court’s analysis: it was not set aside by the federal government for Indians as Indian land (it was conveyed in fee simple to state-chartered corporations with no federal restrictions on alienation), and it is not under federal superintendence. The Court therefore reversed the Ninth Circuit, which had applied a six-factor balancing test and found Indian country. The practical holding for cannabis purposes is: a tribe’s ANCSA-conveyed fee-simple land is not Indian country, so the tribe cannot rely on Indian-country territorial jurisdiction to exclude state marijuana law from that land. That is a statement about territorial jurisdiction, not about tribal sovereignty as such (see Step 3).

Step 3 — What Venetie did not decide: inherent sovereignty without territory. Venetie decided a tax-jurisdiction question (whether the tribe could tax a non-member contractor doing business on tribal land). It did not hold that Alaska tribes are not sovereign, and it did not address tribal authority over tribal members in internal matters. The Alaska Supreme Court addressed that gap the next year.

Step 4 — John v. Baker (1999). John v. Baker, 982 P.2d 738 (Alaska 1999), held that Northway Village, a federally recognised tribe, possessed inherent sovereign authority to adjudicate an internal child-custody dispute between tribal members outside Indian country, and that state courts should generally recognise tribal court decisions under comity. The summary of the holding, from the case summary reproduced in search returns, is:

“The Alaska Supreme Court held that Northway Village possessed inherent sovereign authority to adjudicate internal child custody disputes between its members outside of Indian country, and it reversed and remanded so the superior court could determine whether the tribal custody order should be recognized under comity, with the recognition to depend in part on the children’s tribal membership status and due-process considerations.” — Studicata summary of John v. Baker, https://www.studicata.com/summaries/supreme-court-of-alaska/john-v-baker-1999-7i7np9/, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary; the opinion itself was not opened in this run — Verify against the official opinion).

The University of Oklahoma tribal-court guide states the principle more broadly, and it is the best secondary statement of the post-Venetie position located in this run:

“Even without territory, tribes in Alaska still have a great deal of jurisdiction. The Alaska Supreme Court basically held in John v Baker (1999) that tribal courts in Alaska have sovereign powers to regulate internal domestic affairs even if tribes do not have Indian country.” — Thorpe (University of Oklahoma College of Law), “Tribal Court Development: Alaska Tribes”, Chapter Two, https://thorpe.law.ou.edu/AKtribalct/chapter_two.html, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary, academic; Verify against John v. Baker itself for any proposition beyond custody).

Step 5 — Public Law 280. Alaska is a Public Law 280 state: Congress in 1953 mandated that certain states, including Alaska upon statehood, assume criminal jurisdiction (and limited civil jurisdiction) over Indian country. Because there is almost no Indian country in Alaska after Venetie, PL 280’s practical effect in Alaska is that the state exercises criminal jurisdiction over tribal members and villages for most purposes, while tribal courts retain civil jurisdiction over internal matters and, under John v. Baker, over member disputes. The Thorpe guide states the working rule: “Because PL-280 granted the state jurisdiction over most criminal cases and some civil cases, tribal jurisdiction in Alaska is generally limited to civil cases. Civil tribal ordinances can be enforced through tribal law enforcement and a tribal court that uses civil penalties, including fines, community service, destruction of contraband, and other forms of restitution.” — same Thorpe guide, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary; Verify the PL 280 civil-jurisdiction limits against 28 U.S.C. § 1360 and current Alaska case law before advising on a specific ordinance).

Step 6 — Land into trust (the partial, later reversal of the territorial problem). For 30 years, Interior regulations expressly excluded Alaska tribes (other than Metlakatla) from the land-into-trust process. In 2013, the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia held in Akiachak Native Community v. Salazar that the exclusion was unlawful; Interior then amended 25 C.F.R. § 151.1 to remove the “Alaska exception” and permit all of Alaska’s 229 tribal governments to participate in the land-into-trust process; and in 2017 the Craig Tribal Association became the first Alaska Native tribe to have land placed into trust within its village — all as recited in the Wrangell Cooperative Association resolution (No. 12-05-18-01, 5 December 2018) reproduced on the BIA site, http://bia.gov/sites/default/files/dup/assets/as-ia/raca/pdf/07-FTT_Wrangell-Coop-Assoc_508.pdf, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary — a tribal resolution reciting federal history, not a federal register notice; Verify the trust-land status of any specific parcel against the BIA’s trust records). In 2018, Interior withdrew a Solicitor’s opinion supporting the Alaska trust-lands programme and announced a review — also recited in the same resolution. The net position at 1 November 2026 is therefore: trust land in Alaska exists, parcel by parcel, but it is not the general condition of village land, and no trust parcel has been identified in this run as a cannabis site. Whether land taken into trust after Venetie would count as Indian country for cannabis purposes is a question of federal law that has not been decided in an Alaska cannabis case located in this run — Verify before advising on any trust-land cannabis proposal.

Step 7 — Later rulings, and what they do not change. McGirt v. Oklahoma, 591 U.S. 894 (2020), held that Congress must clearly state an intent to disestablish a reservation, and that the Creek Reservation had not been disestablished. McGirt does not restore ANCSA land to Indian-country status: ANCSA did not “disestablish” a reservation by ambiguous language; it revoked the reserves by express statutory text (§ 1618(a)), and Venetie already construed that text. No post-McGirt Alaska case located in this run holds that Venetie is overruled or narrowed for cannabis purposes. That is an analytical conclusion, not a quoted holding, and is flagged Verify against current federal Indian-law commentary before it is relied on for a specific proposal. Similarly, the 2000 State–tribes agreement recognising village sovereignty and In re C.R.H. (Alaska 2001, concurrent tribal jurisdiction in Indian Child Welfare Act cases), both noted in the ICWA history summary (https://fileserver-az.core.ac.uk/download/pdf/162576341.pdf, accessed 2026-11-01, secondary), concern child-welfare jurisdiction, not cannabis, and are not extended to cannabis here.

Synthesis for cannabis: A tribe in Alaska that wished to run a cannabis business would face three distinct questions, and they must not be collapsed:

  1. Territorial question: Is the site Indian country? For ANCSA fee land, Venetie says no. For the Annette Islands Reserve (Metlakatla), the answer is yes (reservation under 18 U.S.C. § 1151(a)). For trust land, the answer depends on the parcel’s trust status — Verify parcel by parcel. For an individual Native allotment whose title has not been extinguished, § 1151(c) may apply — Verify parcel by parcel. The Thorpe guide’s summary is the right caution: “Native allotments and all or some lands within Alaska Native townsites likely are Indian country. It will take more court cases or an act of Congress to sort out the final picture of where Indian country exists in Alaska.”
  2. Member-regulation question: Can the tribe regulate its own members’ cannabis conduct as an internal matter under John v. Baker? That case concerned custody, not a commercial regulatory regime, and it does not by itself authorise a tribe to license commercial sales to non-members or to displace state licensing for a retail store. Verify against a cannabis-specific tribal-sovereignty opinion before asserting it.
  3. Non-member / commercial question: Can the tribe tax or regulate non-member commercial activity on non-Indian-country land? Venetie itself was a tax-on-non-member case, and the tribe lost on territorial grounds. The Montana v. United States, 450 U.S. 544 (1981), exceptions (consensual relationships; conduct threatening the tribe’s political integrity, economic security, or health and welfare) are the framework a Lower 48 court would apply, but no Alaska cannabis case applying Montana was located — Verify before advising.

10.1.6 Tribal programmes and Schedule III — the “state-licensed” question, stated precisely

The given federal baseline moves “state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana” to Schedule III. The question for a tribal programme is whether a tribal licence counts as “state-licensed”. The answer in this dossier is Verify, for three independent reasons, any one of which is sufficient:

  1. No tribal medical programme was located. There is nothing to test the question against. A programme that does not exist in the record cannot be classified.
  2. The federal rule’s text was given, not re-researched, and it does not on its face settle the tribal question. “State-licensed” in ordinary federal usage means licensed by a state, not by a tribe; but tribes are not states, and the rule’s treatment of tribal programmes (if any) was not in the baseline as given. Whether the rule contains a tribal provision, a compact provision, or silence is Verify against 91 FR 22714 itself.
  3. Alaska’s own structure cuts against a tribal-medical reading. Alaska has no state medical business licence at all (Run 1). A tribal programme could not be “state-licensed” by piggybacking on an Alaska medical licence that does not exist; it would need either its own federal recognition as medical licensing or a compact deeming it state-licensed, neither of which was located.

The safe, sourced position — identical to Run 1’s position for the state market — is: adult-use activity, tribal or not, remains Schedule I and fully subject to 280E on the given baseline; any claim that a tribal programme is Schedule III / 280E-exempt is Verify and must not be advised on the basis of this dossier.

10.1.7 Local-option bans in Alaska Native villages — the established-village mechanism, and the absence of a located list

Alaska Native villages that are not incorporated cities and that sit in the Unorganized Borough can ban marijuana establishments, but only through the established-village local-option mechanism in AS 17.38.300–17.38.350, not through a tribal ordinance alone and not through the alcohol local-option system. The mechanism, as reproduced from akleg.gov in search returns, is:

“(a) If a majority of the voters voting on the question vote to approve the option, an established village shall exercise a local option to prohibit the operation of one or more of the following types of marijuana establishments: (1) marijuana cultivation facilities; (2) marijuana product manufacturing facilities; (3) marijuana testing facilities; or (4) retail marijuana stores.” — AS 17.38.300(a), via https://www.akleg.gov/basis/folioproxy.asp?url=http://wwwjnu03.akleg.org/cgi-bin/folioisa.dll/stattx16/query=[jump!3A!27as1738310!27]/doc/{t8735}/pageitems={body}?, accessed 2026-11-01 (search return reproducing statute text; Verify the current amended text on akleg.gov).

“(b) Upon receipt of a petition of 35 percent or more of the registered voters residing within an established village, the lieutenant governor shall place on a separate ballot at a special election the local option or removal of local option that constitutes the subject of the petition. The lieutenant governor shall conduct the election under AS 15.” — AS 17.38.340(b) (as renumbered in the current code; cited in the search return as AS 17.38.340), same source.

And the effect on existing registrations:

“If a majority of voters vote to prohibit the operation of marijuana establishments under AS 17.38.300, the board may not issue, renew, or transfer, between persons or locations, a registration for a marijuana establishment located within the perimeter of the established village. A registration that may not be renewed because of a local option election held under AS 17.38.300 is void 90 days after the results of the election are certified.” — AS 17.38.320, same source.

“(c) Nothing in this section prohibits the personal conduct authorized in AS 17.38.020.” — AS 17.38.330(c), same source. A village ban therefore bans establishments, not personal possession or use by adults.

“Established village” is defined in AS 17.38.900, as reproduced in the same search returns, as “an area that does not contain any part of an incorporated city or another established village and that is an unincorporated community that is in the unorganized borough and that has 25 or more permanent residents.”

No named established village was located in this run as having adopted a marijuana local-option ban. That is not surprising: the AMCO local-option page is UNDER CONSTRUCTION and disclaims comprehensiveness (Run 1), and the Division of Elections does not publish a consolidated marijuana-local-option list that was opened in this run. The correct statement is therefore: the mechanism exists and is quoted above; the list of villages that have used it for marijuana is Unavailable in this run. Two confusions must be avoided:

  • Alcohol local-option (“dry/damp”) villages are not marijuana bans. Many Alaska Native villages have voted under AS 04.11.491 to prohibit or restrict alcohol. That vote says nothing about marijuana establishments unless a separate AS 17.38.300 vote has also been held. Any source that lists “dry villages” as “cannabis-banned villages” is conflating two systems and must not be relied on.
  • A tribal council resolution is not a local-option election. A tribal government may adopt a resolution opposing cannabis, or a tribal court may enforce a tribal civil ordinance against members, but neither act, without an AS 17.38.300 election conducted by the Lieutenant Governor, triggers the Board’s duty to refuse, void or decline to renew a state establishment registration in that village. Whether a tribal civil ordinance could independently prohibit a state-licensed establishment on tribal-owned fee land is a Venetie/Montana question (see §10.1.5) and is Verify for any specific village.

10.2 Interstate and travel — air, ferries, Canada, cruises, federal land, hemp shipments

The unifying rule for this section is short and should be stated before the detail: Alaska state law legalises possession and licensed sale inside Alaska; it does not legalise transport across any federal boundary — an international border, a state line, federal airspace, federal waters, or a federal enclave. Every subsection below is an application of that rule. Where older practice reporting suggests local discretion, it is labelled as dated practice and is not presented as a current rule.

10.2.1 By air — airports, federal airspace, and the TSA position

The federal rule. Marijuana remains a Schedule I controlled substance for adult-use purposes (Schedule III only for state-licensed medical activity, which Alaska does not license commercially — see the federal baseline and §10.1.6). Carrying it on an aircraft — interstate or intrastate — is therefore a federal offence, and airport security screening is a federal process. TSA’s published position, as reproduced in secondary reporting of the live TSA page, is:

“TSA security officers do not search for marijuana or other illegal drugs, but if any illegal substance or evidence of criminal activity is discovered during security screening, TSA will refer the matter to a law enforcement officer.” — TSA, “What Can I Bring? — Medical Marijuana”, as quoted in NORML, “TSA Marijuana Policy FAQ”, https://norml.org/news/2026/07/15/faq-tsa-updates-its-website-with-new-marijuana-policies/, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary reproduction; Verify the live TSA page text at https://www.tsa.gov/travel/security-screening/whatcanibring/items/medical-marijuana before citing the wording, as the TSA page was not directly opened in this run).

The final decision on whether an item is permitted through the checkpoint rests with the TSA officer on duty. That is a discretionary checkpoint decision, not a permission to carry marijuana on a plane.

The April 2026 TSA wording change — not a policy change. In April 2026, TSA changed the “Medical Marijuana” entry on its “What Can I Bring?” page from “Yes (Special Instructions)” language that had been read as permissive to a clarified entry, and TSA confirmed to NORML that the change was “for clarity and conciseness” and did not reflect a policy change — TSA still does not search for marijuana, still refers discovered marijuana to law enforcement, and the decision still rests with the officer. Source: NORML FAQ, 15 July 2026, https://norml.org/news/2026/07/15/faq-tsa-updates-its-website-with-new-marijuana-policies/, and CannaMD update 2026, https://www.cannamd.com/blog/tsa-marijuana-policy-2026/, both accessed 2026-11-01 (both secondary). Do not state that TSA “allows” medical or adult-use marijuana on planes. The hemp exception in TSA’s older wording — products containing no more than 0.3% THC on a dry-weight basis, or FDA-approved products, are not treated as illegal marijuana for screening purposes — is also Verify after 11 December 2026, when the federal hemp definition switches to total THC with a 0.4 mg per container cap (see §10.2.6); no updated TSA wording for the post-11 December 2026 definition was located.

Alaska airport practice — dated, not current policy. The only Alaska-specific airport practice reporting located is from 2016–2019, and it must be read as history:

  • The Anchorage Daily News visitor guide (2019, via ADN CDN, https://www.adn.com/alaska-news/2019/06/28/visitors-guide-to-marijuana-in-alaska/, accessed 2026-11-01 via search return) reported that Anchorage and Fairbanks airport police enforced state law and generally allowed a traveller carrying up to 1 ounce through after a field interview and a record, while Juneau Police took a hands-off approach leaving discretion to TSA. TSA would notify local law enforcement of any discovered marijuana, and federal law enforcement could request records and consider charges.
  • The Fairbanks case that illustrates the discretion: Frank Berardi, whose 8 grams were found by TSA on 27 July 2016 on a Fairbanks–Anchorage flight, had the marijuana turned over to airport police, and obtained a court order for its return (Juneau Empire, 24 November 2016, https://www.juneauempire.com/news/local-news/state-news/alaska-man-wins-back-marijuana-confiscated-at-airport/, and Marijuana Times, 24 November 2016, https://www.marijuanatimes.org/alaska-man-wins-back-marijuana-confiscated-at-airport/, both accessed 2026-11-01). That is a 2016 state-court outcome about return of seized property, not a rule that airport carriage is lawful.
  • Older reporting (Merry Jane / ADN, 2016–2019, secondary) described licensed Alaska distributors flying product between Alaska communities with airport-police knowledge and MCB documentation, and noted that Alaska Airlines had a no-marijuana policy and that an airline knowingly carrying marijuana risked FAA action. No 2026 AMCO, FAA or airline source confirming that intrastate commercial air transport of marijuana is currently authorised was located. Do not present intrastate commercial air transport as currently authorised; the position is Verify against AMCO and the FAA, and the default federal rule (prohibited) applies until a contrary authorisation is produced.

Practical rule for a traveller, stated plainly: Do not carry marijuana through an Alaska airport, on any flight, or into any other state or country, on the basis of Alaska law. The 2016–2019 Alaska airport-police practice described above is not a safe harbour, it is not current, and it does not bind TSA, federal law enforcement, or the airline. The same applies to bush planes: they operate in federal airspace and are subject to the same federal prohibition, even when they fly only within Alaska.

10.2.2 Alaska Marine Highway ferries and other boats — federal waters

The current published policy, directly opened. The Alaska Marine Highway System (AMHS) travel-policies page was directly opened in this run and states the rule in terms:

“Please note that regardless of recent measures in the states of Alaska and Washington that decriminalize recreational use of marijuana, it remains a prohibited substance under federal law. Federal law prohibits possession and transportation of marijuana. Furthermore, taking cannabis, and products containing it, across international borders, including the Canada – United States border, is illegal and can result in serious criminal penalties.” — AMHS, “Travel Policies”, https://dot.alaska.gov/amhs/policies.shtml, directly opened 2026-11-01.

AMHS reservations: 1-800-642-0066 (same page, directly opened).

Distinguish policy from older crew practice. The ADN visitor guide (2019) and a 2014 Coast Guard statement, both secondary, reported that the U.S. Coast Guard is the law-enforcement entity on federal waterways including AMHS routes and cruise routes, that the Coast Guard “can seize marijuana on federal waterways, issue a civil penalty, and/or pursue criminal action under federal law”, and that AMHS crew in 2019 typically told a person with less than 1 ounce to put it away and reported more than 1 ounce to law enforcement, while AMHS itself did not actively seek out marijuana (Shannon McCarthy, DOT&PF, 2019, via the ADN guide). A Coast Guard spokesman was quoted in 2014 as saying: “Wherever Coast Guard personnel are, we are going to continue to enforce federal laws in all navigable waters.” Those are 2014–2019 practice reports, not the current published policy. The current published policy is the AMHS quotation above: prohibited under federal law. Do not present the 2019 crew-practice account as the current rule, and do not advise a traveller to rely on crew discretion.

Private boats are in the same position. A private vessel on navigable waters is in federal waters for Coast Guard enforcement purposes. The Coast Guard’s authority does not depend on whether the vessel is a state ferry, a cruise ship, or a private boat, and Alaska state law does not create a federal-waters exception. The same applies to the Inside Passage, Prince William Sound, and the Gulf of Alaska routes: the water is federal for this purpose even when both ports are in Alaska.

10.2.3 Through Canada by road — the Alaska Highway problem

The rule, in one sentence: Cannabis is illegal to transport across the Canada–United States border in either direction, even though cannabis is legal for adults in Canada and in Alaska, and even for medical purposes. That single rule determines the whole Alaska road question, because there is no road from Alaska to the contiguous United States that does not cross Canada.

The Canada Border Services Agency notice, as reproduced in search returns, states:

“Despite the fact that cannabis (marijuana) is legal and regulated in Canada, it remains illegal to take cannabis across Canada’s national borders. If you are found to be in possession of cannabis and it has not been declared to the CBSA, you will be arrested and charged.” — CBSA, “Cannabis and international travel”, https://www.cbsa-asfc.gc.ca/travel-voyage/nc-ame-eng.pdf, accessed 2026-11-01 via search return (the CBSA PDF itself was not directly opened in this run — Verify the live PDF text before quoting it as a direct quotation).

The Government of Canada’s travel guidance (https://travel.gc.ca/travelling/cannabis-and-international-travel, accessed 2026-11-01 via secondary summaries — Verify the live page) is the official route to cite for the Canadian side, and the AMHS quotation in §10.2.2 covers the U.S. side for the Canada–U.S. border generally.

Practical consequences for Alaska road travel:

  • The Alaska Highway (Alaska Route 2 / Yukon Highway 1) crosses at Alcan / Beaver Creek. The Top of the World Highway crosses at Poker Creek / Little Gold Creek (near Chicken, Alaska, and Dawson City, Yukon). Both are international crossings for cannabis purposes. Carrying marijuana across either, in either direction, is a border offence in the country entered, regardless of the law at the point of departure.
  • There is no lawful “transit” exception for driving through Canada with marijuana in the vehicle, even if the traveller does not intend to stop, consume, or sell in Canada. Declaring it does not make it lawful; the CBSA notice quoted above says an undeclared possession leads to arrest and charge, and a declared possession is still a prohibited import.
  • Crossing hours, seasonal opening dates, and border-station details for Alcan, Beaver Creek, Poker Creek and Little Gold Creek are not stated here from secondary travel guides, because they change seasonally and were not verified against CBSA or U.S. Customs and Border Protection sources in this run. Verify current hours on the official CBSA and CBP sites before any travel advice is given.
  • The same border rule applies to hemp, with a complication: hemp that is lawful in Canada under Canadian law and lawful in Alaska under the Alaska State Plan is still a cross-border movement of a controlled-plant product, and after 11 December 2026 the U.S. federal hemp definition (total THC, 0.4 mg per container cap) will differ from the Canadian definition. Whether a particular hemp shipment may lawfully cross the Canada–U.S. border is Verify against CBSA, CBP, and USDA guidance for the specific product and date — it is not answered by the Alaska State Plan alone.

10.2.4 Cruise ship visitors — buying ashore, not carrying on board

The shipboard rule. Marijuana is prohibited on board cruise ships under U.S. federal maritime law and under every major cruise line’s guest contract, regardless of the law at the port of call. The line policies located (secondary reproductions, Verify against the current guest contract of the specific line before advising a specific passenger) are:

  • Carnival: Carnival “follows U.S. federal law, which strictly prohibits possession and use of recreational/medicinal marijuana”; a violation results in a $500 charge per violation and may result in disembarkation of all guests in the stateroom; the policy applies to all forms of smoking including marijuana, in staterooms, on balconies, and ship-wide (except seated-and-playing casino areas for tobacco). Source: Alaska Cruises reproduction of Carnival policy, https://www.alaskacruises.com/cruise-lines/carnival-cruise-line/carnival-marijuana-policy/, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary; Verify against Carnival’s current guest contract).
  • Norwegian: Norwegian similarly prohibits medical marijuana on board, in transfers, in terminals, and on shore excursions. Source: Cruzely summary, https://www.cruzely.com/norwegian-cruise-line-marijuana-policy/, accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary; Verify against Norwegian’s current guest contract).

The distinction between medical and recreational does not help on board: both lines prohibit medical marijuana on board as well, and the federal maritime rule does not recognise a state medical card.

Ashore in Alaska — what a cruise passenger may and may not do. A cruise passenger who is 21 or older may buy at a licensed Alaska retail store in a port town (Juneau, Ketchikan, Skagway, Sitka, and others) and may consume only where Alaska law allows: on private property with the owner’s permission, or in a licensed onsite-consumption area. The passenger may not consume on streets, parks, waterfronts, docks, or other public places, and may not take product bought ashore back on board the ship. The Sitka rule is stated in a business FAQ that summarises the Sitka code (secondary, Verify against the Sitka Municipal Code):

“Marijuana consumption is prohibited in public places in Sitka, including streets, parks, and waterfront areas. Consumption is only permitted on private property with the owner’s permission or in licensed consumption areas.” — The Joint Sitka FAQ, https://thejointsitka.com/faq.html, accessed 2026-11-01 (business source summarising Sitka law; Verify against Sitka Municipal Code Title 8 / Chapter 8.20 or the relevant consumption ordinance before citing as law).

Sitka onsite consumption for cruise visitors — Verify, not operating fact. A secondary cruise-guide claim (accessed 2026-11-01) states that the Sitka Assembly approved in 2025 a code change allowing licensed retailers to offer onsite consumption, driven in part by cruise-visitor demand, with a facility near the Sitka Cruise Terminal expected in summer 2026. That claim is not adopted as an operating fact: no Sitka ordinance text was opened in this run, no opening date was verified, and the claim’s “expected summer 2026” timing means it would in any case need a post-summer-2026 check. Verify the final ordinance and any licensed onsite area before stating that cruise visitors can consume onsite in Sitka.

Skagway note, same caution. Secondary cruise guides cite the Remedy Shoppe in Skagway as a dispensary a few blocks from the cruise port whose products cannot be taken back on board. That is a business/secondary claim, not an official source, and is included here only to illustrate the port-town pattern (buy ashore, cannot re-board with product). Verify the store’s current licence and location against AMCO before naming it in any passenger-facing advice.

10.2.5 Federal land — national parks, forests, refuges, BLM land, and military bases

Alaska is unusual in the sheer extent of federal land within the state, and the federal-land rule is therefore not a footnote for Alaska — it is a routine trap for residents and visitors who assume that state law follows them everywhere in the state. The rule is: on federal land, federal law applies, and marijuana is a federal controlled substance. State law does not authorise possession on federal land.

National Park Service — directly opened. The Denali National Park and Preserve laws-and-policies page was directly opened in this run and states:

“Possession or use inside a National Park Service unit (parks, preserves, rivers and monuments) is prohibited. While Alaska provides for regulated possession and use of marijuana, it remains an illegal drug under federal law and enforced within the park units.” — NPS, Denali, “Laws & Policies”, https://www.nps.gov/dena/learn/management/lawsandpolicies.htm, directly opened 2026-11-01.

The same wording appears on the Sitka National Historical Park page (updated 6 February 2026), the Lake Clark National Park and Preserve page, and the Yukon-Charley Rivers National Preserve page, as reproduced in search returns (accessed 2026-11-01 — Verify each park page directly before quoting it as a direct quotation, as only the Denali page was directly opened in this run).

Enforcement scale — dated secondary figure. The ADN visitor guide (2019) reported 54 million acres of federal parks and preserves in Alaska and, for 2018, five marijuana-possession citations, all within Denali National Park and Preserve (NPS spokesman Peter Christian, via the guide). That is a 2018 figure, not a 2026 enforcement rate, and it is not evidence that possession on federal land is tolerated elsewhere; it is evidence only that citations were rare in that year in that park system. Verify current NPS citation practice before characterising enforcement risk.

Penalty — Verify against the regulation. NPS regulation 36 CFR 2.35 prohibits possessing a controlled substance on park land unless obtained through a valid prescription (or, in the regulation’s terms, except as permitted by a valid prescription or as otherwise authorised). Secondary summaries describe a violation as a petty offence (up to 6 months’ imprisonment and/or a $5,000 fine). The penalty figure is not stated here as directly verified from the CFR text in this run — Verify 36 CFR 2.35 and 18 U.S.C. § 1865 / 36 CFR 1.3 (penalties) before advising on consequences. The same Verify caveat applies to U.S. Forest Service (36 CFR 261), U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service refuge (50 CFR 27), and BLM (43 CFR 8365) controlled-substance rules, none of which were opened in this run; the general federal-land rule is stated, the specific regulation-by-regulation penalty table is not asserted.

Military bases — Joint Base Elmendorf-Richardson (JBER), Anchorage. JBER official articles, accessed 2026-11-01 via search returns (Verify the live articles before quoting as direct quotations, as they were not directly opened in this run), state:

“It’s illegal to use, possess, grow, manufacture, and distribute marijuana on Joint Base Elmendorf-Richardson. The new Alaska state law doesn’t change that.” — JBER, “Marijuana remains illegal according to federal law”, https://www.jber.jb.mil/News/News-Articles/Article/771269/marijuana-remains-illegal-according-to-federal-law/, accessed 2026-11-01 via search return.

And the UCMJ position for service members:

“UCMJ Article 112a prohibits service members from using, possessing, manufacturing, or distributing marijuana ‘under any circumstances, in any location, at any time, regardless of state or local laws,’ and prohibits introduction of marijuana into an installation, vessel, vehicle, or aircraft under Armed Forces control.” — JBER drug-recognition-expert summary, https://www.jber.jb.mil/News/News-Articles/Article/1041039/, accessed 2026-11-01 via search return (official article, secondary for the UCMJ text itself — Verify 10 U.S.C. § 912a (Article 112a) for the statutory text).

Dependents, employees, contractors and visitors on base are subject to prosecution for on-base possession (21 U.S.C. § 844 cited in JBER material; a first-offence fine of up to $1,000 and/or up to 1 year, per the JBER summary — Verify the statute). The JBER article also states a narrow allowance that must be quoted narrowly and not generalised: a dependent or visitor who used marijuana off base and comes on base with marijuana in their system but not in possession and not driving is described as allowable. That is a statement about presence of metabolites without possession or impairment, not a permission to possess or use on base, and it does not apply to service members, who are prohibited “under any circumstances, in any location, at any time”.

A dated command-policy note, included only as history: U.S. Army Alaska policy in 2016 forbade Alaska-based soldiers from attending marijuana fairs, festivals and conventions (Army Times / Associated Press, 23 September 2016, secondary, accessed 2026-11-01). Current command policy was not verified in this run — Verify before advising any service member.

Federal-land share — do not publish a single figure. Secondary sources give conflicting shares for the proportion of Alaska that is federally owned (roughly 61% to roughly two-thirds, depending on whether state, Native corporation and private land are counted differently and whether the denominator is total land area or land area excluding water). No single figure is adopted here. Verify against an official land-status source (BLM Alaska Public Land Survey System / Alaska Department of Natural Resources land-status data) before publishing any percentage. The qualitative point — that federal land is a large fraction of Alaska and that a person travelling in Alaska will routinely cross it — does not depend on the exact percentage.

10.2.6 Hemp shipments — the federal rule, the Alaska gap, and the 11 December 2026 change

The federal rule. The 2018 Farm Bill, § 10114 (7 U.S.C. § 1621 note), provides that no state or Indian tribe may prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp produced in accordance with an approved state or tribal plan (or, in the absence of a plan, under a USDA licence). That is a federal pre-emption of state shipment bans for compliant hemp. It is not a permission to ship non-compliant hemp, and it is not a permission to ship marijuana disguised as hemp.

What “compliant” documentation looks like in practice — secondary best practice, not an Alaska rule. Secondary best-practice summaries (accessed 2026-11-01) describe the documentation a shipper should carry: a certificate of analysis from an approved or DEA-registered laboratory tying the THC result to the specific batch or lot, the grower’s or handler’s licence or registration, and a bill of lading or manifest describing the cargo as hemp. The U.S. Postal Service, per secondary summaries citing USPS rules, requires that mailed hemp comply with federal, state and local hemp laws and with USDA-approved plans, and that compliance records be retained for at least 2 years after mailing. Verify USPS Publication 52 (Hazardous, Restricted, and Perishable Mail), current text, before advising any mailer — the USPS rule was not opened in this run, and the 2-year retention figure is a secondary summary, not a verified quotation.

The Alaska-specific gap — stated plainly. No Alaska-specific hemp-shipment documentation rule, transit permit, road/air/ferry hemp-transport rule, or DNR guidance on shipping hemp out of Alaska was located in this run. In particular, the following questions are Unavailable / Verify and must not be answered by inference from the general federal rule:

  • Whether Alaska requires any state documentation beyond the federal best practice for hemp shipped out of Alaska by road, air or ferry.
  • Whether AMHS, Alaska Airlines, or any other Alaska carrier has a published hemp-carriage policy, and whether any such policy distinguishes compliant hemp from marijuana for screening purposes.
  • Whether Alaska’s stricter consumable-endorsement rule (no delta-9 consumable endorsed; no delta-8/THCA/THCV/THCP/HHC or synthetic product endorsed — 11 AAC 40.400(d), 40.520(b)(3)) affects the shipment of non-consumable hemp products (fibre, grain, CBD isolate with no delta-9) out of Alaska, or the transit of such products through Alaska. On the sources located, the endorsement rule governs sale in Alaska, not shipment through Alaska, but no DNR or AMCO source was located stating that distinction — Verify before advising a shipper.
  • What Alaska guidance, if any, will be issued for the post-11 December 2026 federal hemp definition (total THC, 0.4 mg per container cap). No Alaska response to the 12 November / 11 December 2026 federal hemp dates was located in this run. A hemp product that is compliant under the current delta-9-only definition may become non-compliant under the total-THC definition, and a shipper relying on a pre-December certificate of analysis does so at their own risk until Alaska and USDA guidance for the new definition is published.

Marijuana shipments — the contrasting rule. Shipping marijuana across state lines, including by mail through USPS or by private carrier, is a federal crime even between two states where marijuana is legal, and even if the product was lawfully grown and sold in Alaska. Alaska-grown product sold in Alaska was grown and manufactured in-state precisely because it cannot lawfully cross a state line. That rule is stated from the federal baseline, not from a shipment-volume figure, and no Alaska marijuana export volume is asserted (none exists lawfully, so none should be expected in official data).

Part 11 — Operations Rules

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

11.1 Testing: required tests, sampling rules, failed tests and recalls, licensed testing facilities

11.1.1 The testing framework in one paragraph

Alaska does not have a state-run testing laboratory. Testing is done by independent, board-licensed marijuana testing facilities, and no marijuana or marijuana product may be sold or transported (with the narrow untested-to-extraction exception in §11.1.3) until the required testing under 3 AAC 306.645 is complete — for cultivators under 3 AAC 306.455(a), for manufacturers under 3 AAC 306.550(a), and for retailers under the former 3 AAC 306.340 principle carried in the 2025 text. The 2026 Laboratory Testing Working Group (AMCO/Marijuana Control Board, meeting Jan–Aug 2026) was still working, at cut-off, on the known gaps in this framework: whether contaminant testing (pesticides, heavy metals, mycotoxins) is mandatory for clearance, sampling protocols, lab-to-lab transfers, remediation/retest rules, and “loopholes to pass unsafe product” (e.g. presenting a clean sample). Those working-group discussions are not law and are reported here only to show what is unsettled.

11.1.2 Required tests — 3 AAC 306.645 (29 May 2025 text; last amended 1 Sept 2021, Register 239)

Verbatim operative text:

“(a) A marijuana testing facility shall use the general body of required laboratory tests as set out in this section for marijuana plant material, an extract or concentrate of marijuana, and a marijuana product. Required tests may include potency analysis, moisture content, foreign matter inspection, microbial screening, pesticide, other chemical residue, and metals screening, and residual solvents levels.”

“(b) The tests required for each marijuana type or marijuana product, are as follows: (1) potency testing is required on marijuana bud and flower, marijuana concentrate, and a marijuana product… (A) the required cannabinoid potency test must at least determine the concentration of THC, THCA, CBD, CBDA, and CBN cannabinoids…”

Potency reporting (306.645(b)(1)(B)): for marijuana and concentrate, a single percentage concentration per required cannabinoid, based on dry weight, representing an average of all samples in the test batch, plus total THC and total CBD. For a marijuana product, total milligrams of each cannabinoid per single retail unit. For multi-serving edibles, each serving must be within 20% of the manufacturer’s target (the regulation’s example: in a 100 mg total THC package with 10 servings, each serving must contain between 8 and 12 mg of THC). An edible fails potency testing if an individually packaged 10-serving product in a test lot has more than 120 mg of THC in it, or if its THC content is not homogeneous (306.645(b)(1)(C)).

Total THC definition (3 AAC 306.990(b)(48), as applied in the AMCO advisory on laboratory reporting): “total THC” means the sum of THC and (0.877)×(THCA). AMCO’s advisory directs facilities to report total THC as delta-9 THC + (0.877)×THCA and not to include other cannabinoids such as delta-8 or delta-10 in the total-THC calculation.

Microbial testing (306.645(b)(2)) — acceptable limit in every case is less than 1 colony-forming unit per gram (CFU/g):

Substance Products tested
Shiga-toxin producing Escherichia coli (STEC) — bacteria Marijuana; retail marijuana products; water- and food-based concentrates
Salmonella species — bacteria Same as above
Aspergillus fumigatus, Aspergillus flavus, Aspergillus niger — fungus Marijuana; retail marijuana products; water- and food-based concentrates

Residual solvents and metals (306.645(b)(3)) — solvent-based concentrates only:

Substance Acceptable limit
Butanes Less than 800 parts per million (PPM)
Heptanes Less than 500 PPM
Benzene Less than 1 PPM
Toluene Less than 1 PPM
Hexane Less than 10 PPM
Total xylenes (meta-, para- or ortho-xylenes) Less than 1 PPM

What is NOT in the mandatory clearance list in the 29 May 2025 text — a central gap: the text says required tests “may include” pesticide, other chemical residue and metals screening, but the (b) tables set numeric limits only for potency, the three microbial groups above, and the six residual solvents above. There is no numeric pesticide limit table and no numeric heavy-metals limit table in 3 AAC 306.645 as opened. That is consistent with the secondary reporting in this run that Alaska does not require its legal products to be tested for pesticides at clearance, and instead relies on cultivator disclosure of chemicals used (see §11.6.2) plus the board’s supplemental-testing power (§11.1.5) and the 2026 working group’s pesticide-limits work (173 pesticides on a working list at July 2026, not adopted — Verify whether any of that work was adopted after Aug 2026). Do not state that Alaska requires pesticide or heavy-metals clearance testing as current law. Terpene testing and moisture-content testing are likewise listed only as tests that “may” be included, not as numeric clearance requirements in (b).

Source: 3 AAC 306 (AMCO copy, updated 29 May 2025), §§306.645, 306.990(b)(48) — https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/Archive26/MCB/StatutesAndRegulations/3%20AAC%20306%20-%20Marijuana%20Regulations%20(Effective%205.29.2025).pdf — accessed 2026-11-01. AMCO advisory, “Laboratory Reporting Requirements for Total THC by Marijuana Testing Facilities” — https://commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/StatutesAndRegulations/BoardGuidance/MCB%20Advisories/Laboratory%20Reporting%20Requirements%20for%20total%20THC%20-%20182026.pdf — accessed 2026-11-01.

11.1.3 Sampling rules — cultivators and manufacturers

Cultivators — 3 AAC 306.455 (am. 16 Apr 2023, Register 246):

“(a) A marijuana cultivation facility shall provide samples from each harvest batch package of marijuana produced at the facility to a marijuana testing facility and may not sell or transport any marijuana, except as provided for in (c) of this section, until all laboratory testing required under 3 AAC 306.645 has been completed.”

“(b) To comply with (a)… (1) collect a representative sample for testing from each harvest batch package that has been uniformly dried and cured, in an amount as set out in the following table… (2) designate an individual responsible for collecting each sample; that individual shall (A) prepare a signed statement showing that each sample is representative of the harvest batch package; (B) provide the signed statement to the marijuana testing facility; and (C) maintain a copy as a business record under 3 AAC 306.755; and (3) transport the samples to the marijuana testing facility’s licensed premises in compliance with 3 AAC 306.750.”

Sample-size table (number of 1 g sub-samples making up the required sample, by harvest-batch package size in pounds): 1 lb → 4; 2 lb → 4; 3 lb → 5; 4 lb → 6; 5 lb → 8; 6 lb → 10; 7 lb → 11; 8 lb → 13; 9 lb → 14; 10 lb → 16. Packages may not exceed 10 pounds (3 AAC 306.435(a)(2)(B), 306.445(c)).

Segregation pending results (306.455(c)): the harvest-batch package must be segregated and kept in a secure, cool, dry location, and may not be sold or transported from it until the testing facility has completed testing and provided results in writing — except that untested marijuana may be transferred to a licensed concentrate or standard product manufacturing facility to be used to make carbon-dioxide- or solvent-based extract before written results arrive, provided the resulting extract then passes all required tests.

Manufacturers — 3 AAC 306.550(a) (same principle): a product manufacturing facility shall provide a sample of each marijuana product manufactured to a licensed testing facility and may not sell or transport a marijuana product until all laboratory testing required under 306.645 has been completed. The full text of 306.550’s sampling table was not reproduced in this run — Verify against the 29 May 2025 text before quoting a manufacturer sample size.

Chain of custody — 3 AAC 306.650: the testing facility must establish chain-of-custody and sample instructions covering minimum sample and storage requirements, condition of external packaging and integrity seals, condition/amount on receipt, every person handling original samples, aliquots and extracts, transfers to another testing facility, a current list of authorised persons and restricted entry, securing the facility and storage areas when not in use, a secured log-in/aliquot area, appropriate sample storage, and documented disposal of samples, aliquots and extracts.

Reporting — 3 AAC 306.670: results must be reported directly into the marijuana inventory tracking system not later than 24 hours after the test is completed; the final report goes to the submitting establishment in a timely manner and to the director not later than 72 hours after the facility determines results exceed allowable levels. The final report must include the facility name/location, unique sample identifier, submitter, submitter’s sample identifier, date received, and the other items in 306.670(b). A 2020 enforcement example (AMCO Notice of Violation, Jan 2020, Alaskabuds/CannTest) shows the failure mode this rule polices: a sample initially recorded in Metrc as passed was in fact a visual-inspection failure for molds/mildew/filth, and product made from the batch had already been sold before the correction — cited under 306.645, 306.660 and 306.670.

Random/supplemental testing — 3 AAC 306.465 (random sampling of cultivators) and 3 AAC 306.665 (supplemental marijuana quality testing):

306.665(a): “The board or director may at any time determine that the interests of the public require random supplemental testing of marijuana or a marijuana product. When the board or director requires random supplemental testing, the board will or director shall direct the marijuana cultivation facility that produced the marijuana, or the marijuana product manufacturing facility that manufactured the product, to submit a specified sample, batch, or packaged product to a designated marijuana testing facility.”

The testing facility bills all costs directly to the facility that provided the samples (306.665(c)). Under 306.465, the board/director may require a cultivation facility to provide samples of flower, growing medium, soil amendments, fertilisers, crop-production aids, pesticides or water for random compliance checks, screened for pesticides, chemical residues and unsafe metals; the cultivation facility bears all costs. Whether the 2026 working group’s “testing for cause” and “supplemental surveillance screening” proposals were adopted is Verify — they were drafts at the Jan–Aug 2026 meetings, not law at cut-off.

11.1.4 Failed tests and recalls

Failed materials — 3 AAC 306.660 (repealed and readopted 13 Mar 2020, Register 233):

“(a) If a sample tested by a marijuana testing facility does not pass the required tests based on the standards set out in 3 AAC 306.645, including a visual foreign matter inspection, the marijuana establishment that provided the sample shall dispose of the harvest batch package or production lot from which the sample was taken, as required under 3 AAC 306.740.”

“(b) If a marijuana sample fails a required test, any marijuana plant trim, leaf, and other usable material from the same harvest batch package fail the required test. The board or director may approve a written request, on a form prescribed by the board, to allow a batch of marijuana that fails a required test to be used to make carbon dioxide or solvent-based extract. After processing, the carbon dioxide or solvent-based extract must pass all required tests.”

“(c) If a marijuana cultivation facility or a marijuana product manufacturing facility submits a written request, on a form prescribed by the board, for a retest of marijuana or a marijuana product that failed a required test, the board or director may authorize a retest to validate the test results. The marijuana cultivation facility or a marijuana product manufacturing facility shall pay all costs of a retest.”

“(d) When requested by a marijuana product manufacturing facility, the director may authorize a retest of a marijuana concentrate that passed a required test when the licensee wishes to reprocess a marijuana product to further reduce residual solvent levels. The marijuana product manufacturing facility shall pay all costs of a retest.”

There is no standalone “recall” regulation in 3 AAC 306 as opened. The functional equivalents are: (i) the disposal duty above; (ii) seizure / administrative hold under 3 AAC 306.830 (see §11.2.6), which can be used where product poses an immediate threat to worker or public health, safety or welfare, is not properly logged, packaged or labelled, or is evidence of a violation; (iii) summary suspension under 3 AAC 306.825 where continued activity is an immediate threat to public health, safety or welfare (hearing not later than five days after notice unless the licensee requests delay); and (iv) AMCO licensee-wide notices, as in the documented Calm N Collective case (Houston, Alaska — not Houston, Texas): on 1 Nov (year not stated in the secondary report relied on; the underlying AMCO notice year is Verify), AMCO suspended all shipments from that cultivator and ordered retailers to remove and quarantine products containing its marijuana, on credible information that Eagle 20 (myclobutanil, which releases hydrogen cyanide when combusted) had been used. That case is secondary-reported (Merry Jane, undated in the search return) and is included as an example of the mechanism, not as a verified current recall. No current (2025–2026) Alaska marijuana recall list was located — Unavailable. Do not present the Calm N Collective action as a 2026 recall without verifying its date.

2026 testing-reform status (proposed, not law): the Laboratory Testing Working Group’s 2026 agenda items included number of samples tested, financial responsibility for testing, loopholes to pass unsafe product, testing for cause, sample size (6 g for flower, 2 g for concentrate in working papers — different from the 306.455 table above, which is the law), lab-to-lab transfers, R&D contaminant testing, remediation and retest of failed products, CBD-to-THC conversion identification, labelling, residual-solvent limits, mycotoxins, pesticides and random sampling. None of these was shown to be adopted at 1 Nov 2026 — Verify against the post-August 2026 board minutes before relying on any of them. The AO-360 package of 22 Sept 2026 (Run 1) is likewise proposed only.

11.1.5 Licensed testing facilities — list

Only two testing facilities with active operating licences were identified in this run, in both a secondary directory and the 2026 working-group membership (where both labs were represented):

Facility Location (public business address, secondary directory) Status at cut-off
CannTest LLC (spelled “Cannatest LLC” in one secondary source — spelling Verify against AK-ACCIS) 620 E Whitney Rd B, Anchorage, AK Active operating licence, per secondary directory; represented on the 2026 Laboratory Testing Working Group
Land and Seas Laboratory (also rendered “Land & Seas Laboratory” / “Land & Sea Labs”) 3516 W Coghlan Cir, Wasilla, AK (Matanuska-Susitna Borough — not Wasilla in any other state) Active operating licence, per secondary directory; represented on the 2026 Laboratory Testing Working Group

Sources: AlaskaStateCannabis.org, “Alaska Cannabis Testing Labs” (undated secondary directory, accessed 2026-11-01) — https://alaskastatecannabis.org/testing-lab ; AMCO Laboratory Testing Working Group minutes, 16 July 2026 and 18 Aug 2026 — https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/Minutes/2026/8.18.26%20Lab%20Meeting/Meeting_Minutes_7.16.2026_Laboratory_Testing_Working_Group.pdf — accessed 2026-11-01. Caveat: no official AMCO testing-facility list was opened in this run; the two-facility count is secondary, and Run 1 already recorded that by-type operating census figures are Unavailable. Verify both licences and addresses in AK-ACCIS before publication. A testing facility must hold no interest in any other licence type, and no other licensee may hold an interest in a testing facility (3 AAC 306.605(c) and the parallel prohibitions in 306.305/306.400/306.500) — see §11.2.5.

Testing-facility approval (3 AAC 306.620): AMCO approves only laboratories found in compliance with good laboratory practices; the facility must have a scientific director, a written manual of standard operating procedures covering sample preparation, reagent/reference-standard preparation, instrument setup, standardisation, data acquisition, calculation, identification criteria, QC frequency and acceptance criteria, and corrective-action protocol, with each SOP and revision approved, signed and dated by the scientific director and provided to the board not later than 10 days after approval, and not implemented until approved by the board or its contractor (306.640, am. 25 Mar 2020).

11.2 Licence rules: transfers, ownership changes, renewal, ownership caps, relocation, surrender/revocation, insurance/bonding

11.2.1 Transfers to another person — 3 AAC 306.045

“(a) A person may not receive or transfer a marijuana establishment license or a controlling interest in a marijuana establishment license issued to a partnership, including a limited partnership, a limited liability company, a corporation, or a local government, without applying for and receiving the written consent of the board. Transfer of a license includes a sale of all or part of the interest of an individual owner.”

The transfer application (Form MJ-17c, rev. 3/21/2024 in the example packet opened) must be filed under 3 AAC 306.025, name the current holder and the proposed transferee (including all persons listed in 306.020 if the transferee is an entity), contain the same information about each transferee as a new-licence applicant, and include a sworn statement by the current holder listing all debts of the business, all taxes owed, current contact information for each creditor, and an affirmation that a copy of the transfer application was submitted to all creditors. On receipt of a complete application the director must immediately send written notice to each listed creditor (with the amount owed), each local government with jurisdiction, any community council, and any nonprofit community organisation that requested notification. A renewal application must be submitted before or at the same time as a transfer application submitted after 30 April and before 31 August (306.045(d)).

AMCO advisory warning (2026, “Marijuana License Transfer & Ownership Applications”): business entities holding a licence “may not change ownership or the controlling interest of the marijuana license without regulatory approval,” and transferring shares/membership interests to a transferee before obtaining board approval “is a violation of 3 AAC 306.045” and may result in disciplinary action. This is the single most common transfer trap in the 2026 board packets reviewed: applicants who have already closed the entity-level deal must still obtain board consent, and the board treats the pre-approval transfer as a violation.

Security interests — 3 AAC 306.049, 306.051, 306.052 (added 22 Mar 2024): a licensee may grant a security interest in a licence only through the board’s process; transfer packets in 2026 routinely include a “transfer of ownership with security interest” application. The full text of 306.049–306.052 was not reproduced in this run — Verify before advising on perfection, foreclosure or lender step-in rights. No lender step-in or foreclosure sale bypassing board consent was located.

Fees (306.100, 29 May 2025 text): transfer application (to another person or to another location) $1,000 non-refundable; ownership-change approval request $250 where the licensee owns one licence, $500 where it owns two or more (306.100(i), am. 19 Jan 2024); change of business name, licensed-premises diagram, operating plan or proposed new product $250 (306.100(c)), which does not apply to a transfer, transfer of controlling interest, or transfer to another location.

11.2.2 Ownership changes that are not transfers — 3 AAC 306.040

“(b) If any change required to be submitted under this section will result in a change in controlling interest of the marijuana establishment license, the marijuana establishment must file an application for transfer of license to another person under 3 AAC 306.045.”

“Ownership change” means, for a partnership, any change in partners or ownership percentages; for an LLC, any change in members/managers or ownership percentages; for a corporation, any change in corporate officers, any sale of stock to a person not currently an owner, any change in an existing shareholder’s percentage, or any change in directors (306.040(f)). New persons identified must submit fingerprints and DPS fees under AS 12.62.160, and must be a resident of the state as required under 306.015(b)(4) and 306.015(f)(2) (see §11.2.5 on the residency question). The director approves an ownership-change application when the new owner is not disqualified under 306.010(d), 306.015(b), 306.300(b), 306.400(c), 306.500(c) or 306.605(c), and is a resident (306.040(e)).

11.2.3 Renewal — 3 AAC 306.035 (am. 30 July 2022; 22 Jan 2023; 11 Mar 2023)

Timeline (verbatim substance):

  • On or before 1 May each year, the director sends notice that an establishment with a licence in active and operating status must file a renewal application not later than 30 June of the current year. Failure to receive the notice does not excuse filing.
  • 30 June (or 4:30 p.m. on the first business day after 30 June if it falls on a Saturday/Sunday; mailed applications must be postmarked by the deadline) is the renewal deadline, with the renewal fee.
  • Late: a licensee that misses 30 June is delinquent and must pay the late renewal application fee ($1,000, 306.100(b)) with the renewal application, in addition to the $600 renewal application fee.
  • On or before 15 August, the director delivers a notice of expiration to each establishment that has not filed a complete renewal application (with affidavit and fee), unless it has notified the director it does not intend to renew.
  • 31 August, 12:00 midnight: if a complete renewal application and the required fees (including late fee) have not been delivered by 31 August, the licence expires. The holder must immediately surrender the licence to the board; to operate again it must file a complete new application under 306.020/306.025 with fees.
  • Reinstatement: a holder of an expired licence may apply for reinstatement not later than 30 September of the year of expiry, submitting a statement of reason on the board’s form, a complete renewal application with fees (including fingerprints/fees if required), an attestation that it has not been granted reinstatement under this section during the prior five years, and any additional board requirements, plus a non-refundable reinstatement fee equal to 100% of the applicable renewal licence fee in addition to the renewal fees (306.100(b)). Nothing in the reinstatement subsection gives any person a right to reinstatement, and reinstatement does not prevent additional disciplinary action (306.035(j)–(k)).

Renewal application contents (306.035(b)): licence number, type, establishment name and premises address; the new-licence information under 306.020(b)(1)–(2) and (4)–(8); for each licensee, any criminal charge on which convicted in the previous two calendar years and any civil violation of AS 04, AS 17.38 or 3 AAC 306 in the previous two calendar years; on request, adequate proof of right to possession of the licensed premises (entity documents and proof of possession are no longer required at every renewal — 306.035(b) as amended 11 Mar 2023); and a declaration under penalty of unsworn falsification. The director may require fingerprints/fees under 306.055(a) on renewal or reinstatement. Renewal where the licensee is no longer a state resident as defined in 306.015(f)(2) is possible only with documentation, to the board’s satisfaction, that its primary residence is in the state, it has good cause for not meeting the residency definition, and the cause is temporary (306.035(h)).
