Tax-delinquency overlay (306.100(g)): if a licensee pays delinquent tax after a local government protests renewal but before the board denies renewal, a $200 processing fee; after appealing a denial but before a hearing officer is appointed, $500; before the administrative hearing begins, $5,000; after a hearing resulting in a recommendation to deny renewal, $10,000. This is the fee ladder behind the $6.29m past-due figure in Run 1: delinquency is not just a debt, it is a renewal-blocking event with escalating cure costs.

11.2.4 Relocation — 3 AAC 306.046 (added 22 Jan 2023, Register 245)

Relocation is not a mere change of address: it requires a board-approved application for transfer of a marijuana establishment licence to another location, including to a different local government/jurisdiction. The application must identify the current holder, current location and local government, proposed location (including GPS coordinates), proposed local government if applicable, endorsements applicable to the current location and whether the holder intends to transfer them, plus the same information about each person as a new-licence applicant, a sworn statement of debts/taxes/creditors (where the location transfer is concurrent with a transfer to another person), and the proposed licensed premises as described under 306.020(b)(8), 306.705(d) and 306.710(d) if applicable. The older rule that “relocation of licensed premises [is] not allowed” (former 3 AAC 306.050, repealed in the 22 Jan 2023 package) no longer states the law — do not cite it. Inventory relocated to the new premises must be tracked in the marijuana inventory tracking system (306.435(a)(1)(A), 306.750(i), 306.755(a)(10)), and a licence may not be transferred to another location into a liquor-licensed premises, into a place within 500 ft of a school ground, recreation/youth centre, religious-services building or correctional facility (subject to the pre-existing-use proviso in 306.010), or over a local-government protest on zoning grounds (306.010(b)–(c) as summarised in the akleg.gov AAC index). The full text of 306.046 was not reproduced in this run — Verify the notice, protest and effective-date provisions before advising on a relocation timetable.

11.2.5 Ownership caps and cross-ownership bans

No general numeric ownership cap was located. No provision in 3 AAC 306 as opened caps the number of licences one person or entity may hold, and Run 1 likewise found no statewide cap (local caps still apply). What Alaska does impose is a set of cross-ownership bans designed to keep testing independent:

  • A licensee of a cultivation, product manufacturing or retail facility, or an employee/agent of such a facility, may not have an ownership or direct/indirect financial interest in a licensed marijuana testing facility (3 AAC 306.305(b) for retail; parallel provisions for cultivation at 306.400(c) and manufacturing at 306.500(c)).
  • Conversely, a testing-facility licensee may not have an interest in any other licence type (3 AAC 306.605(c)).
  • The same separation is sworn to on transfer packets (Anchorage transfer form, citing 3 AAC 306.020(d)).

Residency — the unresolved Run 1 conflict, narrowed but not closed: the 29 May 2025 text still requires new persons under an ownership change to be state residents under 306.015(b)(4) and 306.015(f)(2), requires a corporate officer and director to be state residents (306.015(b)(4) as amended 18 Oct 2020), and provides the temporary-absence renewal route in 306.035(h). At the same time, secondary sources in 2026 (CannBus Q3 2026 summary; AMCO handler-permit instructions, which state “There are no Alaska residency restrictions for marijuana handler permits”) say there is no residency mandate — but those statements concern handler permits or are secondary summaries, and do not repeal 306.015. The position taken here is: residency requirements in 306.015 remain in the regulation text as opened; whether the board still enforces them for owners in practice, or has liberalised them by a post-May-2025 amendment or practice, is Verify. Do not advise a non-resident buyer that no residency rule applies.

Disqualification (3 AAC 306.010(d) and 306.700(f)): the board will not issue a licence to a person convicted of a felony in the state within the past five years or currently on probation/parole for that felony, or convicted within the previous two years of a class A misdemeanour involving a controlled substance other than a Schedule VIA substance (AS 11.71.190) or relating to selling/furnishing/distributing marijuana, or currently under indictment for such an offence. The same lookbacks apply to handler permits (§11.8). Fingerprinting under AS 12.62.160 / 3 AAC 306.055 applies to licensees and to new persons on ownership change.

11.2.6 Surrender, suspension, revocation, seizure and probation

  • Expiry/surrender on non-renewal: see §11.2.3 — an expired licence must be immediately surrendered to the board (306.035(g)).
  • Suspension/revocation — 3 AAC 306.810 (am. 23 July 2023, Register 247): the board will suspend or revoke if any licensee is convicted of a felony or of a crime listed in 306.010(d)(2)–(3), or if it becomes aware of an undisclosed such conviction. The board may suspend or revoke, refuse renewal, order probation (306.836) or impose a civil fine (306.840) if the licensee misrepresented a material fact on an application/affidavit/report/statement; follows a practice contrary to the public interest (including using an unapproved extraction/manufacturing process or selling/distributing an unapproved concentrate or product); fails within a reasonable time to correct a defect that was the subject of an advisory notice or notice of violation (of AS 17.38, 3 AAC 306, a board condition/restriction, or other applicable law); knowingly allows an employee/agent to violate AS 17.38, 3 AAC 306 or a board condition; violates a board condition/restriction; fails to comply with any applicable public-health, fire, safety or tax statute, ordinance, regulation or other law; or uses the licensed premises for an illegal purpose including gambling, possession/use of narcotics other than marijuana, prostitution or sex trafficking.
  • Employee acts — 3 AAC 306.811: the board may find the licensee knowingly allowed an employee/agent act if the licensee was physically present, knew or should have known, and did not stop it; failed to adequately supervise; failed to adequately train on AS 17.38/3 AAC 306; or was reckless or careless in hiring.
  • Handler-permit discipline — 3 AAC 306.812: the board may suspend/revoke a handler permit, impose probation or a civil fine if the holder acted in violation of a statute, regulation or local law; the director may also suspend a permit where the permit fee cheque is returned unpaid until fees are paid. The director maintains a list of suspended, revoked, expired or otherwise disciplined permit holders, available to licensees and the public on request (contact in the regulation’s editor’s note: AMCO, 550 W 7th Ave., Ste. 1600, Anchorage, AK 99501).
  • Summary suspension — 3 AAC 306.825: where the director finds a licensee is acting and appears to be continuing to act in a way constituting an immediate threat to public health, safety or welfare, the director may immediately suspend the licence and order an immediate stop to the threatening activity, with an expedited board hearing not later than five days after notice unless the licensee requests delay.
  • Seizure / administrative hold — 3 AAC 306.830: the director, enforcement agent, board employee or peace officer may seize or place an administrative hold on marijuana/product that is not properly logged in the tracking system; is an adulterated food/drink product prohibited under 306.510(a)(4); is not properly packaged/labelled; is held by an establishment that has not renewed or whose renewal was denied; is reasonably believed to be evidence of a violation or an immediate threat to worker/public health, safety or welfare; or involves diversion or a crime on the premises. Held product must be physically separated in a secured restricted-access area and may not be sold, moved, transferred or otherwise commercially dealt with without the director’s express written consent after the investigation, fine payment, suspension lifting, and any informal conference, formal hearing or APA hearing conclude. The establishment may request an informal conference or formal hearing not later than 15 days after notice.
  • Enforcement ladder — 3 AAC 306.806 (23 July 2023): verbal warning (quickly correctable, no immediate public-safety/health risk); inspection report; advisory notice; notice of violation (more serious or repetitive violations). A licensee may contest a notice of violation by written response not later than 10 days after receipt, either as an appeal of the director’s decision (306.845, superior court under AS 44.62.560) or by requesting an informal conference with the director (306.844, to be scheduled not later than 10 days after the request). Accusation proceedings for suspension/revocation follow AS 44.62.360–44.62.630 (306.816).
  • Civil fines — 3 AAC 306.840: incorporates by reference the Fine Schedule dated 23 Mar 2021 (as amended 19 Jan 2022). The fine schedule itself was not opened in this run — the dollar amount for any specific violation is therefore Verify against the incorporated schedule, not stated here. A 2026 example from board packets illustrates scale: a standard cultivation facility (licence 10248) was recommended a fine of $8,200 with $8,200 suspended plus 12-month probation for a mould-remediation/licence-record matter (AMCO memo, 16 June 2026) — an example only, not a tariff.

11.2.7 Insurance and bonding — no statewide minimum found

No insurance minimum and no surety-bond minimum for marijuana establishments was located in AS 17.38 or 3 AAC 306 as opened. The operating-plan form (MJ-01, rev. 24 Apr 2026) requires applicants to describe security, transportation and other operational matters, but no section of 3 AAC 306 reviewed imposes a dollar insurance or bonding floor, and no such floor appears in the 306.100 fee schedule. This is a negative finding, not an oversight: do not invent a minimum. Practical consequences to flag: (i) landlords and lenders routinely impose their own insurance requirements by contract (see the 2026 Anchorage commercial lease in board transfer packets, where loss of state/municipal cannabis licences is an event of default); (ii) local governments may impose conditions by ordinance or licence condition; (iii) workers’ compensation and general business insurance obligations under other Alaska law still apply. Verify whether any local ordinance (Anchorage, Fairbanks, Juneau, Mat-Su) imposes a cannabis-specific insurance or bonding requirement — none was located in this run, which did not re-open those codes for this point.

11.3 Cultivation rules: canopy limits, indoor/outdoor/greenhouse rules, home grow rules

11.3.1 Commercial canopy — the only numeric limit is the limited tier

Tier Canopy / area limit Source
Standard cultivation facility No square-foot canopy cap in 3 AAC 306 as opened. The licence is not capped by area; the operating plan must state “the size of the space intended to be under cultivation” (306.420(2)(A)), and the premises, security, odour and local zoning rules are the practical constraints. 3 AAC 306.405, 306.420
Limited cultivation facility Fewer than 500 square feet under cultivation — “has the privileges set out in 3 AAC 306.405(a) and (b), except that it must have fewer than 500 square feet under cultivation” (306.410(1)). Note the 2025 text prints “3 AAC 305.405” in one place — an obvious typographical error for 306.405. 3 AAC 306.410

Do not convert the limited tier’s 500 sq ft into a standard-tier cap, and do not import another state’s canopy tiers (e.g. California’s) into Alaska. Plant-count rules for commercial facilities are not stated as a numeric cap in the sections opened; instead the facility must assign a tracking number to each mature or flowering plant and a batch name/number to each batch of immature plants propagated from seed, clones or cuttings (306.435(a)(2)–(3)), and packages may not exceed 10 pounds (306.435(a)(2)(B)). A 31 Dec 2023 amendment to 306.405(a) increased the number of clones and allowed any number of seeds to be introduced to a licensed premises — the numeric clone limit after that amendment was not reproduced in this run, Verify.

11.3.2 Indoor / outdoor / greenhouse — 3 AAC 306.430(b)

“(b) A marijuana cultivation facility shall conduct any marijuana growing operation within a fully enclosed secure indoor facility or greenhouse with rigid walls, a roof, and doors. Where not prohibited by local government, outdoor production may take place in non-rigid greenhouses, other structures, or an expanse of open or cleared ground fully enclosed by a physical barrier. To obscure public view of the premises, outdoor production must be enclosed by a sight-obscuring wall or fence at least six feet high.”

Additional cultivation-premises duties (306.430(c)–(d), 306.440, 306.445): marijuana at the facility must not be observable by the public from outside, and must not emit an odour detectable by the public from outside except as specifically allowed by a local government approval; the premises must have full video surveillance as required under 306.720, including any area where marijuana is grown, processed, packaged or stored or where waste is destroyed; staff in contact with marijuana must wear clean clothing and protective apparel as necessary and practise good sanitation; harvested marijuana must be segregated into harvest batches, uniformly dried and cured, then divided into packages not exceeding 10 pounds, with a sample of each harvest-batch package submitted for required testing before sale/transfer (see §11.1.3). Extraction on a standard cultivation premises is prohibited unless the licensee also holds a product manufacturing licence, and then only in a separate room posted with a restricted-access sign and in compliance with 306.500–306.570 (306.405(c)(6)).

11.3.3 Home grow — restated from Run 1, with the privacy overlay

Home grow is governed by AS 17.38.020 (see Run 1, Part 1) and the constitutional privacy right in Ravin v. State (1975), not by 3 AAC 306: 6 plants per adult (≤3 mature/flowering), 12 plants per single dwelling regardless of the number of adults residing there (≤6 mature/flowering), plus possession of the marijuana produced by those plants on the premises where grown. Plants must not be visible to the public without optical aid/aircraft (AS 17.38.020 as summarised in Run 1). The Ravin home-possession protection (up to 4 oz in the home for personal use, as summarised in Run 1) is a possession protection, not a grow licence, and does not authorise commercial sale. No home-grow registration, plant tagging or Metrc requirement applies to personal growers — Verify whether any local ordinance imposes a home-grow registration, as none was located in state law. Landlords may prohibit cultivation by lease term (see §12.6).

11.4 Security: cameras, alarms, storage, transport (including between communities off the road system)

11.4.1 Alarms, locks, lighting, badges — 3 AAC 306.715 (am. 19 Jan 2022, Register 241)

“(a) Each licensee, employee, or agent of a marijuana establishment shall have on display, either on the person or in a readily accessible location on the licensed premises, an identification badge issued by the marijuana establishment at all times when on the marijuana establishment’s licensed premises.”

“(b) The licensed premises of a marijuana establishment must have (1) exterior lighting to facilitate surveillance; (2) a security alarm system on all exterior doors and windows; and (3) continuous video monitoring as provided in 3 AAC 306.720.”

“(c) A marijuana establishment shall have policies and procedures that (1) are designed to prevent diversion of marijuana or marijuana product; (2) prevent loitering; (3) describe the use of any additional security device, such as a motion detector, pressure switch, and duress, panic, or hold-up alarm to enhance security of the licensed premises; and (4) describe the actions to be taken by a licensee, employee, or agent… when any automatic or electronic notification system alerts a local law enforcement agency of an unauthorized breach of security.”

“(d) A marijuana establishment shall use commercial grade, non-residential door locks on all exterior entry points to the licensed premises.”

“(e) A marijuana establishment shall notify the Department of Commerce, Community, and Economic Development, as reasonably practical and in any case not more than 24 hours after any unauthorized access to the premises or the establishment’s knowledge of evidence or circumstances that reasonably indicate theft, diversion or unexplained disappearance of marijuana, marijuana products, or money from the licensed premises.”

No backup-power, alarm-monitoring-company or failure-notification requirement was found in 306.715/306.720 as opened — Verify before advising that none exists, as the operating-plan form (MJ-01 §3.8) asks applicants to certify written policies for any additional devices used.

11.4.2 Video surveillance — 3 AAC 306.720 (repealed and readopted 10 Dec 2020, Register 236)

  • Coverage: each restricted access area and each entrance to a restricted access area within the licensed premises; each entrance to the exterior of the licensed premises; and each point-of-sale area (306.720(a)).
  • Placement: each required camera must produce “a clear view adequate to identify any individual inside the licensed premises, or within 20 feet of each entrance to the licensed premises. Both the interior and the exterior of each entrance to the facility must be recorded by a video camera” (306.720(b)). Any area where marijuana is grown, cured or manufactured, or where marijuana waste is destroyed, must have a camera in the room facing the primary entry door, in adequate fixed positions at a height giving a clear, unobstructed view of regular activity without sight blockage from lighting hoods, fixtures or other equipment (306.720(c)).
  • Storage: recording equipment and records must be housed in a locked and secure area (lock box, cabinet, closet or other secure area) accessible only to a licensee or authorised employee and to law enforcement (peace officer or board agent). Offsite monitoring and offsite storage are permitted if security at the offsite facility is at least as strict as onsite (306.720(d)–(e)).
  • Retention: each surveillance recording must be preserved for a minimum of 40 days, in a format easily accessed for viewing; all recorded images must clearly and accurately display the time and date and be archived in a format that does not permit alteration, so images can readily be authenticated. After 40 days the establishment may erase recordings unless the licensee knows or should know of any pending criminal, civil or administrative investigation for which the recording may contain relevant information (306.720(f)).
  • No pixel/resolution minimum is stated in 306.720 as opened — the standard is the “clear view adequate to identify” test in (b).

11.4.3 Restricted access areas and storage — 3 AAC 306.705, 306.710, 306.725

Restricted access areas (cultivation: 306.430(a) under 306.710; generally 306.710) are the areas where marijuana is grown, processed, packaged, stored or tested, or where waste is destroyed; access is limited to licensees, employees/agents holding handler permits, and visitors logged under 306.755(a)(6) (name, date and time of entry of each visitor permitted in a restricted access area). Licensed premises must be made available for inspection on request by the director, a board employee/agent, or an enforcement officer, including storage and overlapping premises, and inspection covers premises, facilities, personnel qualifications, methods of operation, business/financial records, the tracking system, policies and purposes (306.725). Overlapping premises (two licences sharing a restricted area) are permitted only if approved by the board under 306.705(d)/306.710(d). Marijuana and product must be stored in a manner preventing bacterial/microbial growth (306.735(b)(11)); product stored beyond its usable life or stored improperly (extremes of temperature, humidity, smoke, fumes, pressure or radiation due to disaster, fire, accident or equipment failure) may not be salvaged and returned to the marketplace, except under the narrow testing-facility salvage route in 306.735(d) (quality-assurance testing showing moisture, potency and contaminants meet standards, plus premises inspection showing the product was not adversely affected, plus a tracking-system record of name, lot number and final disposition).

11.4.4 Transport — 3 AAC 306.750 (am. 5 Jan 2024, Register 249)

“(a) Marijuana or a marijuana product may only be transported to a licensed marijuana establishment by a licensee or an agent or employee of a licensee.”

“(b) A marijuana establishment from which a shipment of marijuana or marijuana product originates is responsible for preparing, packaging, and securing the marijuana or marijuana product during shipment, for recording the transfer in the marijuana inventory tracking system, and for preparing the transport manifest. An individual transporting marijuana in compliance with this section shall have a marijuana handler permit required under 3 AAC 306.700.”

“(c) When marijuana or a marijuana product is transported, the marijuana establishment that originates the transport shall use the marijuana inventory tracking system to record the type, amount and weight of marijuana or marijuana product being transported, the name of the transporter, the time of departure and expected delivery, and the make, model, and license plate number of the transporting vehicle. A complete printed transport manifest on a form prescribed by the board must be kept with the marijuana or marijuana product at all times.”

“(d) During transport, the marijuana or marijuana product must be in a sealed package or container and in a locked, safe, and secure storage compartment in the vehicle transporting the marijuana or marijuana product. While the secure storage compartment may be opened to access or rearrange its contents, the sealed package containing the marijuana or marijuana product may not be opened during transport. A vehicle transporting marijuana or a marijuana product must travel directly from the shipping marijuana establishment to the receiving marijuana establishment, and may not make unnecessary stops in between except to deliver or pick up marijuana or a marijuana product at another licensed marijuana establishment.” “Unnecessary stops” do not include refuelling, basic vehicle maintenance, necessary rest/food breaks, or delivery/deposit of monetary receipts at a financial institution or payment of state excise taxes if not significantly off route.

“(e) When a marijuana establishment receives marijuana or a marijuana product transported in compliance with this section, the recipient… shall use the marijuana inventory tracking system to report the type, amount, and weight of marijuana or marijuana product received. The recipient shall refuse to accept any shipment of marijuana or marijuana product that is not accompanied by the transport manifest.”

Records of all shipments/receipts must be kept under 306.755, and records of inventory relocated on a board-approved transfer to another location must also be kept (306.750(i), 306.755(a)(10)). Trade-show transport is permitted only under 306.760 and 306.750 (306.750(g)).

Off the road system — the hard edge: 306.750 as opened is written for a vehicle with a make, model and licence plate number, a locked compartment in that vehicle, and direct travel between licensed establishments. It contains no air-transport, marine-transport or unaccompanied-cargo provision in the current text. The emergency commercial marine/air carrier regulation (former 306.750(h), adopted Apr 2020 for Covid-19) expired 14 Aug 2020 and was repealed 7 May 2020 in the change log. AMCO enforcement has stated the position expressly (Enforcement Supervisor’s Report, Apr 2025, for the 16–17 Apr 2025 board meeting): “marijuana and marijuana products may not be transported as unaccompanied luggage or cargo on airlines. We understand the unique challenges of operating in certain communities off the road system, however this activity is a clear violation of 3 AAC 306.750 and enforcement action may be taken against your license.” In practical terms, a licensee in a community off the road system (e.g. Bethel, Nome, Kodiak, Unalaska, and the many smaller villages — see Part 3) has no compliant unaccompanied-air-freight route in the current text; accompanied transport by a handler-permit holder in a vehicle is the only route the regulation describes, and whether a handler-permit holder may lawfully carry product as accompanied baggage on a commercial flight, or on a ferry in federal waters, is Verify — it is not answered by 306.750 and it implicates federal aviation/maritime law (see Part 10 in Run 2). Do not advise any operator that air freight of product is permitted.

Consumer transport is distinct and is covered in §12.2 (vehicle/open-container rules) and §12.5 (cruise/air travel).

11.5 Waste disposal rules — 3 AAC 306.740 (am. 16 Oct 2022, Register 244)

“(a) A marijuana establishment shall store, manage, and dispose of any solid or liquid waste, including wastewater generated during marijuana cultivation production, processing, testing, or retail sales, in compliance with applicable federal, state, and local statutes, ordinances, regulations, and other law.”

“(b) Marijuana waste must be rendered unusable for any purpose for which it was grown or produced before it leaves a marijuana establishment. Marijuana waste includes (1) marijuana plant waste, including stalks, leaves, and stems that have not been processed with solvent; (2) solid marijuana sample plant waste in the possession of a marijuana testing facility; (3) marijuana or marijuana product that has been found by the licensee unfit for sale or consumption; (4) expired marijuana products; and (5) other waste as determined by the board.”

Note: “roots” was removed from the plant-waste definition in the 2018 amendment (the 2018 draft struck “[ROOTS,]”), so roots are not listed in the current (b)(1).

“(c) A marijuana establishment shall (1) [repealed 10/16/2022]; (2) record the waste in the marijuana inventory tracking system required under 3 AAC 306.730; and (3) keep a record through the marijuana inventory tracking system of the final destination of marijuana waste made unusable.”

The repealed (c)(1) was the former three-day waiting period (board notice not later than three days before making waste unusable and disposing of it). That waiting period was removed effective 16 Oct 2022 (Lt. Governor filed 16 Sept 2022, Register 244, Jan 2023) — do not cite a three-day waste notice as current law. (Some older operating-plan supplements, e.g. a Kodiak onsite-consumption packet, still describe a three-day hold; that is stale form language, not the current regulation.)

“(d) Marijuana plant waste must be made unusable by grinding the marijuana plant waste and mixing it with at least an equal amount of other compostable or non-compostable materials. A marijuana establishment may use another method to make marijuana waste unusable if the board approves the method in advance.”

Permitted mixing materials: (1) compostable materials including food waste, yard waste, vegetable-based grease or oils, or other board-approved wastes where the mixed material can be used as compost feedstock or in another organic-waste method such as an anaerobic digester with approval of any applicable local government entity; or (2) non-compostable materials including paper waste, cardboard waste, plastic waste, oil, or other board-approved wastes where the mixed material may be delivered to a permitted solid waste facility, incinerator, or other facility with approval of any applicable local government entity.

Alaska Department of Environmental Conservation (DEC) guidance, “Cannabis Waste” (accessed 2026-11-01) — https://dec.alaska.gov/eh/solid-waste/how-do-i-dispose-of/cannabis-waste/ — adds the disposal-route rules that 306.740 incorporates by reference to other law: without prior ADEC approval, cannabis waste may only be disposed at a permitted Class I or Class II landfill willing to accept it; cannabis waste processed with solvent, and solvent waste itself, is hazardous waste and must be disposed out of state at an appropriate facility; and the generating facility is responsible for ensuring disposals are recorded in the tracking system. DEC’s page still describes a three-day board notification in its summary text — that summary is stale against the 16 Oct 2022 repeal and should not be relied on for the notice period; the landfill/hazardous-waste points are the live DEC content.

Law-enforcement disposal (306.740(e)): marijuana/product found by or surrendered to a law-enforcement officer, including a peace officer or airport security officer, may be disposed of as provided in 306.740 or by any method allowed under any applicable local ordinance. Onsite-consumption waste: unconsumed marijuana left abandoned or unclaimed in a consumption area must be destroyed in accordance with the operating plan and 306.740 (306.370(g)(1)).

11.6 Environmental rules: water, pesticide limits, energy (including energy costs in rural Alaska)

11.6.1 Water and wastewater

No marijuana-specific water-use, water-rights or irrigation-volume rule was located in 3 AAC 306. The provisions that do touch water are general: the cultivation operating plan must describe “the irrigation and waste water systems to be used” (306.420(2)(D)) and “waste disposal arrangements” (306.420(2)(E)); wastewater generated during cultivation, production, processing, testing or retail sales must be stored, managed and disposed of in compliance with applicable federal, state and local law (306.740(a)); and health-and-safety rules require adequate, readily accessible toilet facilities and convenient handwashing facilities with running water at a suitable temperature (306.735(b)(2)). Water rights, well/septic permits, discharge permits and municipal water/sewer connections are therefore governed by the general Alaska water and environmental codes (AS 46; 18 AAC), not by a cannabis-specific rule — Verify the applicable DEC water/wastewater permit for any specific facility, as none was located for a named facility in this run. No water-use volume cap for cannabis cultivation was located — Unavailable as a cannabis-specific figure; do not import another state’s gallons-per-plant figure.

11.6.2 Pesticides — the criteria-list system, and why there is no numeric limit table in 306.645

There are no pesticides specifically approved by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) for use on cannabis crops, except for some hemp fibre crops, and no pesticide product label currently lists cannabis as an allowable application (DEC, “Cannabis and Pesticides,” accessed 2026-11-01 — https://dec.alaska.gov/eh/pest/cannabis/). DEC’s published position is that pesticides meeting its criteria “can probably be used on cannabis crops without significant risk or violating the label, provided that all label instructions are followed,” and DEC maintains a partial list (“Pesticides that Meet Criteria to be Used on Cannabis Crops,” updated at minimum each June) of products meeting those criteria. Both state and federal law require pesticides to be applied according to label directions; application by a DEC-certified applicator is required for hemp under 11 AAC 40 (see Run 2, Part 7), and the general Alaska pesticide code is 18 AAC 90 (certification, permits for aircraft/water applications, and government-program applications under 18 AAC 90.500–90.540).

For marijuana specifically, the enforcement mechanism in the current framework is disclosure plus supplemental testing, not a clearance limit table: the cultivation operating plan must list fertilisers, chemicals, gases and delivery systems including carbon dioxide (306.420(2)(C)); the board/director may order random sampling of pesticides, fertilisers and other crop-production aids and screen for pesticide/chemical residues and unsafe metals, at the cultivator’s cost (306.465); and a failed-test batch may not be sold (306.660). The 2026 Laboratory Testing Working Group’s pesticide subgroup (July–Aug 2026 minutes) was vetting a list of 173 pesticides/analytes (some combined), comparing other jurisdictions (with a focus on the Pacific Northwest and Colorado), and discussing adopting a reference guidance document by reference in regulation, with a PhD-level review of a “pesticide recommendation” worksheet reported as ready for the board at 18 Aug 2026. Whether any pesticide list or numeric limit was adopted after 18 Aug 2026 is Verify — it was not law in the 29 May 2025 text and was not shown to be adopted at 1 Nov 2026. The Eagle 20/myclobutanil example in §11.1.4 is the enforcement illustration: the product was not caught by a clearance pesticide test, but by credible information and out-of-state laboratory analysis, followed by a shipment suspension and quarantine order.

Solvents and extraction: permitted extraction solvents are addressed in the manufacturing article (3 AAC 306.555, referenced in 306.405(c)(6)); the residual-solvent limits in 306.645(b)(3) (§11.1.2) are the numeric backstop. The full 306.555 solvent list was not reproduced in this run — Verify before advising which solvents a manufacturer may use.

11.6.3 Energy — no cannabis-specific energy rule; rural cost context

No energy-efficiency, lighting, carbon or renewable-energy requirement specific to marijuana establishments was located in 3 AAC 306. Energy enters the framework only through general building/fire/health-and-safety inspection (306.735(a): inspection by the local fire department, building inspector or code enforcement officer) and the cultivation operating plan’s description of growing systems. The economic context, however, is material to any rural-Alaska operation and is therefore stated here with secondary/official sources, clearly marked:

  • Alaska has three non-interconnected electrical grids, with the Railbelt (Fairbanks–Anchorage–Kenai Peninsula, ~600 miles, mainly natural gas) and more than 150 islanded rural microgrids, each serving a single village with a standalone diesel plant (secondary summary, ElectricChoice, Oct 2026 — Verify against Alaska Energy Authority (AEA) data).
  • The Power Cost Equalization (PCE) programme (AS 44.83, administered by AEA) subsidises residential electricity in ~188–193 rural communities (81 utilities, 81,000+ Alaskans, $46m disbursed in FY2025 per AEA’s 13 Aug 2026 overview presentation). The PCE base rate (the “floor”) was 19.92 cents/kWh in the prior year cited by Dermot Cole (Apr 2026) and 20.38 cents/kWh for FY2026 in the ElectricChoice summary; the subsidy covers the first 750 kWh/month for residential customers (70 kWh/month per resident for community facilities in the AEA presentation; 500 kWh for community facilities in one secondary summary — conflict, Verify). Crucially, schools, government agencies and commercial customers do not receive the PCE subsidy — a commercial cultivation facility in a PCE village therefore pays the utility’s full (often diesel-based) rate, not the subsidised residential floor. Unsubsidised rural rates of 55–80+ cents/kWh are cited secondarily, and a Nome example (KNOM, 2016, for illustration only) put a business rate at 34.6 cents/kWh against 7.2 cents in Seattle — dated, do not use as a current tariff.
  • Practical consequence: indoor cultivation’s lighting/HVAC load is priced at Railbelt rates (~22 cents/kWh secondarily) on the road system but at full diesel-microgrid rates off it, and PCE does not shelter a commercial grow. Any rural-Alaska cultivation business plan that assumes a residential PCE rate for a commercial load is wrong. No official cannabis-specific energy-cost figure was located — Unavailable.

Sources: AEA Overview Presentation to the Arctic Energy Office, 13 Aug 2026 — https://www.akenergyauthority.org/Portals/0/Who%20We%20Are/News%20Room/Presentations/2026.08.13%20AEA%20Overview%20Presentation%20to%20Arctic%20Energy%20Office%20(Final).pdf — accessed 2026-11-01; Dermot Cole, “Energy crisis faces Fairbanks as well as Anchorage,” 16 Apr 2026 — https://www.dermotcole.com/reportingfromalaska/2026/4/16/4jyions3bzh1xrryz0gyokxyk3jsb6 — accessed 2026-11-01 (secondary).

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts and promotions

11.7.1 Samples — three distinct regimes, do not conflate them

A. Cultivator samples to retailers/manufacturers — 3 AAC 306.460 (current text): a cultivation facility may provide a free sample to a retail store in a sample jar containing not more than 3.5 grams, protected by a plastic or metal mesh screen to allow smelling before purchase (306.460(a)); for negotiating a sale, a sample of not more than one ounce, and not more than one ounce per month free of charge to any one retail store or manufacturing facility for that purpose (306.460(b)). A retail store that receives a sample may not sell it to a customer; it must return it or destroy it after use and document the destruction in its inventory control system (306.460(c)). All samples provided for negotiating sales must be recorded in the tracking system, including amount, recipient, and disposal of any expired/outdated promotional sample returned (306.435(c)).

B. Employee quality-control samples — 3 AAC 306.460(d) (continuing) and 306.557 (manufacturers): a facility may provide samples to its own handler-permit-holding employees for quality control only, capped cumulatively at the 306.355 amounts in a 30-day period, tracked in the inventory system, with consumption not on the licensed premises, a board-approved QC form per sample kept under 306.755, and off-premises samples in opaque, resealable, child-resistant packaging marked “For Quality Control” and tested under 306.455/306.645 before provision. The 22 Sept 2026 AO-360 draft would repeal 306.460 and 306.557 and consolidate them into a new 306.727 (“Samples for the purpose of facilitating sales”), removing the MJ-26 form requirement — proposed only, not law.

C. Trade-show samples — 3 AAC 306.760: see §11.7.3. No sale or distribution at the event; display only, handled only by handler-permit holders.

11.7.2 Giveaways, discounts and promotions at retail — what changed in 2023

The current retail prohibitions (3 AAC 306.310, as amended 13 Aug 2023 and 8 Oct 2023) are narrower than the pre-2023 text that still circulates in older summaries:

Current 306.310(a): a retail store may not sell/give/distribute/deliver/offer marijuana or product (1) to a person under 21; (2) to a person under the influence of an alcoholic beverage, inhalant or controlled substance; (3) that is not labelled/packaged as required; (4) in a quantity exceeding 306.355; (5) [repealed 13 Aug 2023 — formerly the internet-sale ban, now addressed through the exterior-window rule at 306.380]; (6) after the label expiration date.

Current 306.310(b): a retail store may not (1) conduct business or allow consumer access between 5:00 a.m. and 8:00 a.m. daily; (2) allow consumption on the premises except as provided in 306.305(a)(4) (the onsite-consumption endorsement route); (3) offer, deliver or sell an alcoholic beverage to a consumer for any reason; (4) allow intoxicated/drunken persons to enter or remain.

The former ban on giving free marijuana/product as a marketing promotion (former 306.310(b)(3)(A)) and on selling below acquisition cost was removed in the 2023 amendments (see the Mar 2023 board memo: “currently prohibited activities are proposed to be removed/deleted,” and the Oct 2023 amendment log: “306.310(b) amends prohibited marketing promotions”). The MJ-03 retail operating-plan supplement (rev. 21 May 2024) still certifies that the store will not offer free marijuana/product “as a marketing promotion or for any other reason” — that form language lags the regulation and should not be quoted as the current rule without checking which version of 306.310 the board is currently enforcing. Practical position at cut-off: free-product giveaways to consumers and below-cost promotions are not prohibited by the current 306.310 text as opened; but a store’s approved operating plan, a local ordinance, and the onsite-consumption bans in 306.370(d) (no below-regular-price sales, no unlimited-amount-for-fixed-price sales, no one-day below-general-public prices, no consumption games/contests with marijuana prizes — see §12.4) may still prohibit them in a specific case. Verify the current MJ-03 form and any local rule before running a promotion.

Discounts generally: ordinary price reductions (as opposed to the onsite-consumption-specific bans above) are not prohibited by 306.310 as opened. Loyalty pricing, bulk pricing and sale pricing are therefore not banned at state level in the current text — but they remain subject to the per-transaction caps (306.355: 1 oz / 7 g / 5,600 mg), the advertising rules in §11.7.4, and any local rule. No “happy hour” or time-limited discount ban was located in the current text.

11.7.3 Trade shows and events — 3 AAC 306.760 (am. 5 Mar 2023, Register 245)

  • A licensed cultivation facility may bring one plant for display; removal and return must be tracked; any marijuana removed from the plant at the event must be retained by the licensee and returned to the licensed premises.
  • A licensed cultivation facility and a licensed retail facility may bring up to one ounce of each strain for display, tracked in and out, contained so the public cannot remove it from the display’s immediate vicinity.
  • A licensed product manufacturing facility and a licensed retail facility may bring one sample package of each marijuana product made or sold by the facility, tracked in and out, remaining in approved packaging throughout.
  • A licensed testing facility may not perform required tests on samples at a trade show or similar event.
  • No marijuana or marijuana product may be sold or distributed by a licensee at the event.
  • Displayed product must be handled only by a licensee or employee/agent holding a valid handler permit, and advertising/promotions at the event must comply with 3 AAC 306.

11.7.4 Advertising and promotions — 3 AAC 306.770 (am. 8 Oct 2023, Register 248)

Business cards and merchandise (t-shirts, hats, stickers) containing only business name/logo, licence name, location and contact information are not advertising/promotions (306.770(a)); a compliant sign is likewise not advertising/promotions, but a licensee within a local government must comply with local sign size/number/placement laws, and where there is no local government the sign may not violate AS 19.25.075–19.25.160 (306.770(b)). Every advertisement for a licensed establishment or product must include the business name and licence number (306.770(c)), except that an ad containing only name, logo, business type, contact information, location and hours, with no written information or depictions of marijuana/product (other than in the established name font/logo), is exempt from the warning statement (306.770(d)). A logo/advertisement may not be false or misleading, promote excessive consumption, represent that marijuana use has curative or therapeutic effects, depict a person under 21 consuming marijuana, or include any object/character (including a toy or cartoon character) appealing to a person under 21 (306.770(e)). Placement bans (306.770(f)): within 1,000 feet of the perimeter of any child-centred facility (school, childcare, playground/recreation centre, public park, library, game arcade open to under-21s) except in an established publication for general readership; on or in publicly owned/operated property; or within 1,000 feet of a substance-abuse/treatment facility. The five warnings in 306.770(g) must appear with visual contrast (print) or intelligible audio (audio ads): intoxicating/habit-forming/addictive; impairs concentration, coordination and judgment — do not operate a vehicle or machinery; health risks; adults 21+ only, keep out of reach of children; should not be used by women who are pregnant or breastfeeding. Web advertising must use appropriate age-gating measures (306.770(h)); location-based-device marketing is banned unless via a mobile app installed by a 21+ device owner with a permanent, easy opt-out (306.770(i)). Sponsorship is permitted where no more than 30% of participants/audience is reasonably expected to be under 21, for an industry trade show, charitable event, sports event/competition, concert, or other board-approved event (306.770(j)); but a licensee may not encourage sale by consumption games/competitions or by promotional materials/activities especially appealing to children (306.770(k)).

11.8 Drug testing, background checks and marijuana handler permit rules

11.8.1 Who must hold a handler permit — 3 AAC 306.700 (current text; AO-360 draft would simplify it — proposed only)

“(a) Each agent of the marijuana establishment who sells, cultivates, manufactures, tests, or transports marijuana or a marijuana product, or who checks the identification of a consumer or visitor, and each licensee and employee must obtain a marijuana handler permit from the board before being licensed or beginning employment at a marijuana establishment.”

The parallel per-type duties (retail 306.320, cultivation 306.425, manufacturing 306.530, testing 306.622) repeat the same requirement: the permit card must be in the person’s immediate possession, or a valid copy on file on the premises, at all times while on the licensed premises. A handler permit is not a licence to operate an establishment, and holding one does not authorise sale to consumers outside a licensed retail store.

11.8.2 How to obtain and renew a handler permit (AMCO instructions, rev. 1 Jan 2025)

  1. Complete a Marijuana Control Board-approved marijuana handler permit education course and pass the written test; obtain the course-completion certificate (3 AAC 306.700(b)–(c), 306.701).
  2. Obtain an Alaska name-based criminal-history background check through a local Department of Public Safety office (not through myAlaska), dated within 30 days of the submitted application.
  3. Submit the certificate, valid government-issued ID (clear photocopy if mailing), and the $50 fee by cheque or money order payable to the State of Alaska (no cash or credit card) to AMCO.
  4. The permit card is valid for three years from the date of course completion (306.700(c); AMCO instructions). Renewal requires retaking an approved course and paying the $50 renewal fee (306.100(e)).

Source: AMCO, “Marijuana Handler Permit” page — https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/amco/MarijuanaHandlerPermit.aspx — accessed 2026-11-01; AMCO, “Marijuana Handler Permit Application Instructions” (rev. 1 Jan 2025) — https://www.commerce.alaska.gov/web/Portals/9/pub/MCB/MJHandlerPermit/MJ%20Handler%20Permit%20Instructions%201.1.25.pdf — accessed 2026-11-01.

Proposed change (not law): the 22 Sept 2026 AO-360 handler-permit draft would repeal 306.320/306.425/306.530/306.622 (folding them into 306.700), repeal 306.701 to remove the course requirement, and change 306.700(f) from “will not issue” to “may not issue” for disqualified persons. Do not advise applicants that the course is abolished — at 1 Nov 2026 it is still required in the current text and instructions.

11.8.3 Disqualifying criminal history — 3 AAC 306.700(f) (Justia current through 30 May 2025; AMCO page summary agrees)

The board will not issue a handler permit to a person who:

  1. has been convicted of a felony in the state and either (A) less than five years have elapsed from the time of conviction, or (B) the person is currently on probation or parole for that felony;
  2. has, within the two-year period immediately preceding submission, been convicted of a class A misdemeanour in the state involving a controlled substance other than a Schedule VIA controlled substance under AS 11.71.190;
  3. has, within the same two-year period, been convicted of a class A misdemeanour in the state relating to selling, furnishing or distributing marijuana; or
  4. is currently under indictment for an offence listed in this section.

AMCO’s page summarises this as: “Felony conviction in Alaska within the last five years” (disqualifying). The April 2026 board minutes record a contested handler-permit renewal (permit #13690) in which the argument that 306.700(f) applies only to initial issuance and not renewal was put and not adopted (motion to overturn the director’s denial failed for want of a second) — a useful illustration that renewal is not a rubber stamp, but a single board-meeting outcome, not a rule change. Whether a handler-permit holder’s out-of-state conviction counts is Verify — the regulation text as opened says “in the state.”

11.8.4 Licensee/owner background checks — fingerprints

Licensees and new persons on an ownership change must submit fingerprints and pay the Department of Public Safety fees under AS 12.62.160 (3 AAC 306.055(a), 306.040(c)); the director may require fingerprints/fees on renewal or reinstatement (306.035(d)). A secondary source in 2026 put the fingerprint combined-check cost at $48.25 (superseded)/person and, in Run 1, at $50.00 per submission effective 1 Oct 2026 (AMCO home page) — conflict, Verify against the current DPS fee schedule; the $50 handler-permit fee in §11.8.2 is separate from any fingerprint fee. Handler-permit applicants, by contrast, use a name-based (not fingerprint) check through DPS (see §11.8.2) — do not conflate the two checks.

11.8.5 Workplace drug testing and employment protection — no protection for off-duty use

AS 17.38.120 (employment): the adult-use chapter “does not require an employer to permit or accommodate the use, consumption, possession, transfer, display, transportation, sale, or growing of marijuana in the workplace” and does not affect an employer’s ability to have a drug-free workplace policy. Secondary summaries of AS 17.38.120 agree, and no Alaska statute was located that protects an employee from adverse action for off-duty lawful marijuana use or for a positive THC test alone. The medical chapter is to the same effect: AS 17.37.040(d) provides that nothing in the medical chapter requires accommodation of medical use in any place of employment (or in a correctional/medical/department-monitored facility, on or within 500 feet of school grounds, at or within 500 feet of a recreation/youth centre, or on a school bus).

Voluntary employer testing — AS 23.10.600–23.10.699: Alaska does not require private employers to test. It offers a voluntary framework: an employer that adopts a written policy (distributed to employees before testing begins, stating who is subject, when testing occurs, substances screened, consequences of a positive result or refusal, and the employee’s right to explain a positive result), uses a qualified laboratory, and provides confirmation testing for an initial positive, receives protection from certain civil claims arising from the testing programme (including defamation, invasion of privacy and negligence claims tied to the test itself) and may qualify for workers’ compensation premium discounts. Testing circumstances in compliant programmes include pre-employment (conditional offer), reasonable suspicion (objective, documented behavioural signs), post-accident, and random testing for safety-sensitive roles.

Federal overlay (not Alaska law, but determinative for many Alaska jobs): U.S. Department of Transportation (DOT) drug-testing rules under 49 CFR Part 40 apply to safety-sensitive transportation workers (commercial drivers, maritime operators, aviation crews, transit personnel) regardless of Alaska law, and Alaska cannot loosen them. Federal contractors and federally regulated safety-sensitive positions remain zero-tolerance in practice. Alaska’s high share of DOT-regulated, maritime, aviation, oilfield and military-adjacent employment makes this overlay more important in Alaska than in most states — but no Alaska-specific DOT-testing count was located.

Drug testing of licensees/staff by the state: there is no routine state drug test for holding a handler permit or a licence in 3 AAC 306 as opened; the state’s controls are the background check (§11.8.3–11.8.4), the handler course/test, and the prohibition on consuming marijuana during a work shift in an onsite-consumption area (306.370(d)(1)) and on allowing intoxicated persons to enter/remain (306.310(b)(4)). Whether AMCO or an employer may require a test as a condition of a specific licence condition is Verify — it is not a general rule in the text opened.

11.9 Research and university cannabis and hemp work

11.9.1 No dedicated state research licence in 3 AAC 306

No “research licence” or university exemption appears in 3 AAC 306 as opened. A university that wished to cultivate, manufacture or test marijuana for research would, on the current text, need the ordinary licence for that activity (cultivation, manufacturing or testing facility) or must work with seized/confiscated material under law-enforcement authority (see §11.9.2), or with hemp under the Division of Agriculture programme (Run 2, Part 7), or under a federal DEA registration for Schedule I research (adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I federally — see the federal baseline). Whether the board has ever issued a licence for a university research purpose, or granted a research-related condition, is Unavailable — no such licence was located in board packets reviewed. Do not state that UAF holds a marijuana establishment licence; no evidence for that was found.

11.9.2 University of Alaska Fairbanks — what is documented

Stable-isotope provenance research (documented, dated, primary university source): UAF’s Alaska Stable Isotope Facility (Geophysical Institute), under Matthew Wooller, used stable-isotope analysis (carbon, oxygen, nitrogen, hydrogen) of marijuana samples — initially confiscated samples provided by the UAF Police Department with help from federal, state and local law enforcement, later samples from the DEA and the Alaska Bureau of Alcohol and Drug Enforcement — to determine where marijuana was grown, distinguishing Alaska-grown (high-latitude water signature) from imported (lower-latitude) product, and working toward distinguishing indoor from outdoor grows and building an Alaska isotope map. The work began about two years before the June 2007 report, supported initially by a University of Alaska President’s Special Projects Fund grant, and required a DEA permit for the laboratory to work with marijuana.

Source: UAF Geophysical Institute, “Alaska scientist uses isotopes to trace marijuana” — https://www.gi.alaska.edu/alaska-science-forum/alaska-scientist-uses-isotopes-trace-marijuana — accessed 2026-11-01 (undated republication of a 2007 report; the underlying work is 2005–2007, not current research — do not present it as a 2026 programme).

Secondary claims that could not be verified to a primary source — flagged, not adopted: a secondary aggregator (State Regs Today, “Research and Development in Alaska,” undated, accessed 2026-11-01) claims that UAF’s College of Natural Science and Mathematics established a “Cannabis Center of Excellence” in 2019 focused on plant biology, cultivation, genetics, safety and efficacy; that the UAF Institute of Arctic Biology received an NIH grant in 2016 to study cannabis compounds and seizures in epilepsy; and that UAF received a $1.2m private-company grant in 2016 for non-psychoactive cannabis compounds and epilepsy. None of these three claims was verified against a UAF, NIH or grantor primary source in this run — Verify all three before repeating them. They are included here only so the parent agent can see what a reader may encounter elsewhere. No current (2024–2026) UAF cannabis/hemp research programme, publication list or hemp trial was located — Unavailable. The Division of Agriculture’s hemp programme and the University of Alaska’s agricultural extension (School of Natural Resources and Extension) are the plausible current touchpoints for hemp agronomy, but no joint UAF–Division hemp trial was located in this run.

11.9.3 Other research touchpoints

  • State reference laboratory (proposed, not built): the 2026 Laboratory Testing Working Group discussed “the necessity for and potential funding mechanisms for a state reference laboratory” and increasing the Environmental Health Laboratory’s role through a reimbursable services agreement. No state reference laboratory was shown to exist at cut-off — Verify.
  • Public-health research: the secondary aggregator also claims Alaska Department of Health and Social Services support for the University of Alaska’s Center for Behavioral Health Research and Services (CBHRS) to study the impact of recreational legalisation. That claim is likewise secondary-only — Verify.
  • Hemp research: hemp research registrations, if any, fall under 11 AAC 40 (Division of Agriculture) and are covered in Run 2, Part 7, not here. No marijuana-specific state research funding programme was located in AS 17.38 or 3 AAC 306.

Part 12 — Consumer and Social Rules

PART 12 — CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke shop rules

Alaska has no drug-paraphernalia crime for marijuana accessories. AS 17.38.020(1) makes it lawful for a person 21 or older to possess, use, display, purchase or transport “marijuana accessories” or one ounce or less of marijuana, and the closing paragraph of AS 17.38.020 makes it lawful for a person 21 or older “to manufacture, possess, or purchase marijuana accessories, or to distribute or sell marijuana accessories to a person who is 21 years of age or older.” Secondary summaries agree: “There are no laws in Alaska punishing the possession, sale, or cultivation of marijuana paraphernalia” and Alaska is described as the only state without a criminal paraphernalia law (LegalClarity, undated secondary summaries, accessed 2026-11-01).

Consequences and limits:

  • A “smoke shop” selling only accessories (pipes, vaporizers, papers, etc.) to adults 21+ needs no AMCO marijuana establishment licence for that activity alone — but the moment it sells marijuana or marijuana products, it needs a retail licence, and the moment it sells hemp-derived intoxicating products it enters the Division of Agriculture hemp rules (Run 1, Part 1; Run 2, Part 7). No smoke-shop-specific licence, zoning or age-verification rule beyond the 21+ accessory-sale rule in AS 17.38.020 and general business licensing (AS 43.70) was located — Verify whether any municipality (notably Anchorage) imposes a separate tobacco/smoke-shop licence that also captures cannabis accessories.
  • Local ordinances may restrict paraphernalia sale/possession even though state law does not criminalise it — the secondary claim that “some local municipalities have their own ordinances restricting the sale or possession of paraphernalia” was not verified against a named ordinance in this run — Verify before naming a municipality.
  • Accessories sold by a licensed retail store are subject to the store’s general rules (no sale to under-21s, no display visible to the general public from a public right-of-way under AS 17.38.070(a)(1), packaging/labelling rules for any marijuana product sold with them). A retail store may not display marijuana/product visibly from a public right-of-way — the accessory itself is not the regulated product, but the display rule still bites on any product alongside it.
  • Federal paraphernalia law (21 U.S.C. § 863) is not displaced by Alaska law; interstate shipment of paraphernalia remains a federal question — Verify for any specific shipment. No Alaska paraphernalia seizure/return procedure was located beyond the general 306.740(e) law-enforcement disposal rule for marijuana/product.

12.2 THC driving limits and impairment testing; vehicle rules

12.2.1 No numeric THC driving limit in Alaska — impairment-based

Alaska has no per se THC blood limit for driving. The 15 Sept 2026 secondary survey relied on in this run (Marijuana Herald, “Marijuana DUI Laws by State,” updated 15 Sept 2026) lists Alaska as “Impairment-based — No specific THC limit,” in contrast to Colorado’s 5 ng/mL permissible inference and Washington/Illinois/Montana’s 5 ng/mL standards. The governing Alaska statute is AS 28.35.030 (driving while intoxicated): a person commits the crime if they operate/drive a motor vehicle or operate an aircraft or watercraft (1) while under the influence of intoxicating liquor or any controlled substance; (2) at or above the 0.08 alcohol thresholds on a chemical test within four hours; or (3) while under the combined influence of intoxicating liquor and a controlled substance. The 0.08 thresholds are alcohol-only; there is no parallel THC number in AS 28.35.030 as opened. Prosecution for marijuana-impaired driving therefore requires proof of being “under the influence” of a controlled substance to the statutory standard, not proof of a THC concentration.

Impairment testing: Alaska’s implied-consent law (AS 28.35.031–28.35.032) requires a driver lawfully arrested for driving while intoxicated to submit to a chemical test (breath for alcohol; blood/urine for drugs where applicable), with refusal carrying its own penalties. No Alaska-specific roadside THC test (oral-fluid device) mandate, THC breathalyser adoption, or drug-recognition-expert (DRE) programme figure was located in this run — Unavailable; do not state that Alaska uses a roadside THC device as a matter of law. Blood THC and metabolite evidence is admissible as part of an impairment case, but a positive THC test alone, without impairment evidence, does not satisfy the statute as written — that is the legal position, not a guarantee of how any individual case is charged. Commercial-vehicle drivers are governed separately by AS 28.33.030 (operating a commercial motor vehicle while under the influence of an alcoholic beverage, inhalant or controlled substance), with sentencing by reference to AS 28.35.030.

Minors: AS 28.35.280 (minor operating a vehicle after consuming alcohol) is alcohol-specific; no parallel under-21 THC driving statute was located — Verify before advising on under-21 marijuana driving, as the general AS 28.35.030 impairment offence still applies regardless of age.

12.2.2 Vehicle possession, consumption and open containers — the rule Run 1 left open, now answered in part

Run 1 left “a statewide marijuana open-container rule” as Verify. The position found in this run is:

  • Consumption in a vehicle is public consumption. Secondary guidance consistently treats smoking/vaping marijuana in a motor vehicle on a public roadway or in a place accessible to the public (including a parking lot) as public consumption, punishable by a fine of up to $100 (AS 17.38.040 — the public-consumption provision; Run 1 carries the same $100 figure). No person, driver or passenger, may lawfully smoke in a moving or parked vehicle on a public roadway under this rule.
  • Open marijuana container — AS 28.35.029 (as described in 2015–2016 legislative sectional analyses for SB 30 / CSHB 79, 29th Legislature): a person may not drive a motor vehicle when there is an open marijuana container in the passenger compartment. “Open marijuana container” is defined narrowly: a receptacle (or marijuana accessory) that contains marijuana, is open or has a broken seal, and there is evidence that marijuana has been consumed in the vehicle (in one sectional-analysis version: “any amount of marijuana is removed while in the vehicle”). All three elements must be present — an unsealed dispensary bag alone, without signs of in-vehicle consumption, does not trigger the violation on the secondary summary of the statute (LegalClarity, “Open Container Law Alaska,” accessed 2026-11-01). Exceptions: the container is in the trunk; or, in a trunkless vehicle or on a motorcycle, behind the last upright seat if enclosed in another container. The partition and large-vehicle-for-hire exceptions that apply to alcohol do not extend to marijuana on that summary.
  • Penalty/classification: the open-container violation is an infraction, not a misdemeanour or felony, on the secondary summary (fine per the Alaska Supreme Court bail schedule — amount Verify, not stated here). This is a secondary-only summary of AS 28.35.029; the current text of AS 28.35.029 was not directly opened in this run — Verify against akleg.gov before quoting the definition or penalty. The Run 1 “Verify” flag on a statewide open-container rule is therefore narrowed to “definition and penalty Verify against current AS 28.35.029 text,” not retired entirely.
  • Consumer transport generally: an adult 21+ may transport up to 1 oz (and the retail-sale equivalent of 7 g concentrate) in a vehicle, in a sealed, labelled container, not accessible for consumption while driving; the product must not be open in the passenger compartment within the AS 28.35.029 definition above. Transport across state lines, even to another legal state, remains a federal offence and an Alaska offence outside AS 17.38.020 — see Part 10 (Run 2) for interstate travel.
  • No delivery to consumers: see §12.5 — a retail store may not deliver to a consumer’s home; the consumer must be physically present (or use the exterior-window pickup in §12.5).

12.3 Record clearing for marijuana offences (reconciling Run 1’s HB 239)

12.3.1 The baseline: Alaska had essentially no general expungement

Before 2026, Alaska had no general expungement of adult convictions. The only sealing route was AS 12.62.180, limited to records that, beyond a reasonable doubt, resulted from mistaken identity or a false accusation. A valid conviction generally could not be sealed at all; a gubernatorial pardon forgives but does not delete the record; and a set-aside is possible only where a court suspended imposition of sentence at the outset under AS 12.55.085, with prosecutor and defendant consent, and even a set-aside leaves the record visible. There is no automatic “clean slate” sealing in Alaska. (Secondary summary: DocDraft, “Expunging a Criminal Record in Alaska,” reviewed 19 Aug 2026, accessed 2026-11-01 — secondary, but consistent with the statutory structure.)

12.3.2 HB 239 (2026) — the new, narrow marijuana sealing route (enacted, reconciling Run 1)

Run 1 recorded HB 239 as an omnibus crime bill carrying the former HB 81 concept, enacted 2026 without the Governor’s signature, with chapter/effective-date details flagged as conflicting. The position confirmed by secondary reporting in this run (Marijuana Moment, June 2026; NORML, 2 June and 18 June 2026) is:

  • What passed: HB 239 cleared the Senate 20–0 on 19 May 2026 and the House 39–1 on 20 May 2026, and became law without Governor Dunleavy’s signature (Chapter 9, SLA 2026, law without Governor’s signature; House Journal message 18 June 2026; one secondary source dates the law-without-signature to approximately 18 June 2026, another to the 18 June 2026 House Journal message — date conflict, Verify against the akleg.gov bill history and the Session Laws chapter number).
  • Who is eligible: a person convicted of possessing less than one ounce of marijuana, who was 21 years of age or older at the time of the offence, and who was not convicted of any other criminal charges in the relevant case.
  • What it does: on the eligible person’s request, no agency may release information about that criminal case — i.e. the record is withheld from public release, not erased, and government agencies retain access for background and statistical purposes. It is not automatic: the eligible person must proactively file a request with the requisite state agencies.
  • Sunset on the request requirement: the requirement to proactively request non-release is sunsetted on 1 January 2028 (Marijuana Moment, June 2026) — i.e. after that date the mechanism changes; the exact post-sunset operation (automatic withholding vs. a different procedure) was not stated in the secondary reports opened and is Verify against the enrolled HB 239 text.
  • Origin: the cannabis provision was initially introduced as standalone HB 81 (Rep. David Nelson (R)) and folded into the HB 239 omnibus.
  • What it does NOT do: it does not seal or expunge convictions for more than one ounce, for any other offence in the same case, for under-21 possession, for sale/manufacture/delivery, or for any non-marijuana offence; it does not create a general expungement right; and it does not delete the record from government systems. A secondary source notes a “limited 2024 change” for certain marijuana possession (DocDraft) — that reference is unreconciled with HB 239 (2026) and may reflect an earlier, narrower provision or an error; Verify whether any 2024 marijuana record provision predates HB 239, and do not cite a 2024 marijuana expungement as law without that check.

Reconciliation statement for the parent agent: Run 1’s HB 239 entry stands (enacted without signature, 2026), with the following refinements from this run: votes 20–0 / 39–1 and the <1 oz + 21+ + no-other-charges eligibility test are now sourced to June 2026 secondary reporting; the chapter number, the exact law-without-signature date, the effective date, and the post-1-Jan-2028 operation remain Verify against the enrolled text on akleg.gov. The HB 239 sealing route does not change any handler-permit or licence disqualification in §11.8.3 — a sealed-from-public-release record is not the same as a conviction that never occurred, and whether a sealed record still counts for 306.010(d)/306.700(f) purposes is Verify.

12.3.3 Pardons and other relief (unchanged)

The Governor’s pardon power remains the main relief for an accurate conviction that falls outside HB 239’s narrow class, processed with the Board of Parole; a pardon does not delete the record. No Alaska marijuana-specific pardon programme or mass pardon was located in this run — Unavailable as a separate programme; do not confuse HB 239 sealing with a pardon.

12.4 Onsite consumption and events

12.4.1 Onsite consumption — current law (3 AAC 306.370, eff. 11 Apr 2019; am. 7 Aug 2021; am. 8 Dec 2024)

Onsite consumption is not a standalone licence: it is an endorsement to a retail marijuana store licence (3 AAC 306.370, 306.100(d)(7), 306.110(c) — an endorsement is valid only in conjunction with a licence, transfers only with the licence, expires if the licence expires, and is suspended if the licence is suspended). Renewal is annual, at the time of renewal of the underlying retail licence (306.370(h)).

Where consumption is permitted (306.370(a)): (1) edible marijuana product only, in an indoor or outdoor area, regardless of whether the store is freestanding; (2) by any method, in an indoor or outdoor area of a freestanding licensed retail store. A non-freestanding store therefore cannot offer smoking/vaping onsite — edibles only.

Per-person per-day sale limits for onsite consumption (306.370(b)) — these are stricter than the general retail caps in Run 1 and must not be conflated with them:

Product Onsite limit per person per day
Marijuana bud/flower 1 gram
Edible marijuana products 25 milligrams of THC
Vaping device containing marijuana concentrate 0.3 grams of concentrate; concentrate may not be consumed onsite by any means other than vaping as described

What the endorsement holder may do (306.370(c)): sell food/beverages not containing marijuana or alcohol; allow a person to remove purchased product from the premises if packaged under 306.345.

What the endorsement holder may not do (306.370(d)): allow any licensee/employee/agent to consume during a work shift; allow tobacco/tobacco products in the consumption area; allow a person to bring in or consume product not purchased at that store; sell/offer/deliver at a price less than the price regularly charged during the same calendar week; sell/offer/deliver an unlimited amount for a fixed price over a set period; sell/offer/deliver on any one day at prices less than those charged the general public that day; encourage/permit an organised game/contest involving consuming marijuana or awarding marijuana/product as prizes; or advertise/promote any practice prohibited under this section.

Consumption-area characteristics (306.370(e)): if any-method consumption is permitted, the area must be isolated from other store areas, separated by walls and a secure door, with access only from the retail store; the store must provide a smoke-free monitoring area for employees; an indoor smoking area must have a ventilation system separate from other areas, directing air outside through a filtration system sufficient to remove visible smoke, consistent with building codes/ordinances, and adequate to eliminate odour at the property line; an outdoor area must be found by the board to be compatible with surrounding uses (neighbouring uses, air-intake vents, sight-obscuring wall/fence, objections of owners/residents/occupants within 250 linear feet or the local-government notification distance, whichever is greater, and any other relevant information).

Application (306.370(f)): board-prescribed form with the 306.100 endorsement fee; operating plan describing security (doors/locks, windows, diversion prevention, under-21 access prevention), ventilation (if smoking indoors: signed/approved by a licensed mechanical engineer, sufficient to remove visible smoke, consistent with codes/ordinances), overconsumption monitoring, unconsumed-product disposal/packaging under 306.345, and prevention of outside product; detailed diagram (store premises, serving areas, ventilation exhaust points, employee monitoring area, doors/windows/exits, access control points); title/lease/sole-possession documentation if the area is not already part of the approved licensed premises; and for outdoor areas, an affidavit of mailed notice to owners/residents/occupants within 250 linear feet or the local distance, whichever is greater.

Holder duties (306.370(g)): destroy all unconsumed marijuana left abandoned/unclaimed in the consumption area per the operating plan and 306.740; monitor patrons at all times, specifically for overconsumption; display all warning signs required under 306.360 and 306.365 in the consumption area, visible to all consumers; provide, at no cost, written dosage/safety materials for each product type sold for onsite consumption; package/label all onsite product under 306.345; and comply with any local-government or board conditions on the endorsement.

Local protest right (separate from the retail-licence protest): local governments retain a separate right to protest an onsite-consumption endorsement application (306.370, former 306.365(h) in the older numbering); the board denies unless the protest is arbitrary, capricious and unreasonable, and may impose local-government-recommended conditions unless they are arbitrary, capricious and unreasonable (see Run 1, Part 3, for the general protest rule at 306.060).

Operating count — still Unavailable (Run 1 position unchanged): no official endorsement count was located in this run. At least Good Titrations (Fairbanks, opened 20 Apr 2021, per Run 1) and Cannabis Corner (Ketchikan, per Run 1) are reported operating; a 2025 Sitka code change (see §12.5.3) and a 2026 consumption facility expected near the Sitka Cruise Terminal are secondary-reported, not officially counted. The 22 Sept 2026 AO-360 proposal to remove onsite purchase limits (customers could not leave possessing above the standard possession limit) is proposed only.

Section-numbering note: an older filed-regulation excerpt still circulating (labelled 3 AAC 306.365) shows different per-transaction limits (1 g / 10 mg / 0.25 g per transaction) and a different endorsement structure. That excerpt predates the 29 May 2025 renumbering to 306.370 and the 8 Dec 2024 amendments. The 306.370 text in the 29 May 2025 compilation is the text to cite; do not cite the 306.365 excerpt as current law.

12.4.2 Events other than onsite consumption

  • Trade shows (306.760): see §11.7.3 — display only, no sale/distribution, handler-permit handling only.
  • Sponsorship (306.770(j)): a licensee may sponsor an industry trade show, charitable event, sports event/competition, concert, or other board-approved event where no more than 30% of participants/audience is reasonably expected to be under 21; consumption games/competitions and child-appealing promotional materials/activities are banned (306.770(k)).
  • Private events / cannabis clubs: no “cannabis club” or private-event consumption licence was located in 3 AAC 306. Consumption at a private event is governed by the general rules: consumption is lawful only in a non-public place (AS 17.38.020(4) — “nothing in this chapter permits the consumption of marijuana in public”), and a retail store may not allow consumption on its premises except under an onsite endorsement (306.310(b)(2)). Whether a truly private, non-commercial gathering in a private residence is lawful turns on the public/private distinction in AS 17.38.020 and AS 17.38.900’s definition of “public place” — Verify the current definition before advising on a specific venue. No “temporary event licence” (as in some other states) exists in Alaska law as opened.
  • No consumption on licensed premises except onsite endorsement; no consumption by staff on shift; no tobacco in the consumption area (306.370(d)(1)–(2)).

12.5 Cannabis tourism and visitor purchase rules (including cruise passengers)

12.5.1 Visitors may purchase — no residency requirement for consumers

Alaska imposes no residency requirement on retail consumers. Any adult 21 or older with valid photographic identification may purchase from a licensed retail store, subject to the same per-transaction caps as residents (Run 1: 1 oz / 7 g concentrate / 5,600 mg THC per person per day under 306.355 — note the Run 1 wording “per person per day” vs. one secondary source’s “per transaction” wording; the 306.355 text as summarised in Run 1 is “per person per day” and that is the figure carried here; Verify the exact 306.355 wording if the distinction matters for a compliance question). Identification verification is required (3 AAC 306.350); tribal identifications were added as acceptable identification by the 24 Sept 2021 amendment to 306.350(b)/306.701(b). There is no visitor registration, no visitor purchase registry, and no medical-card requirement for adult-use purchase. A registered medical patient (97 active at 31 Dec 2025, Run 1) buys through the same adult-use stores with no price or tax distinction.

12.5.2 The visitor’s consumption problem — the central tourism constraint

Purchase is easy; lawful consumption as a visitor is hard, because:

  1. Public consumption is prohibited (AS 17.38.020(4); fine up to $100 under AS 17.38.040).
  2. Consumption in a vehicle on a public roadway or publicly accessible place is public consumption (§12.2.2).
  3. Hotels, rental accommodation and cruise ships may prohibit consumption by contract/policy (see §12.6 and §12.5.3).
  4. The only licensed public consumption option is an onsite-consumption endorsement area (§12.4.1), and the operating count is Unavailable, with coverage concentrated in a handful of towns.
