Visitors should therefore be advised to consume only in a private place with the owner’s permission, or in a licensed onsite-consumption area, and not in parks, streets, waterfronts, parking lots or vehicles. No official Alaska visitor-cannabis guide was located — the Juneau visitor page below is a destination-marketing page, not a statute.

12.5.3 Cruise passengers — ship rules override port legality

  • On board, marijuana is prohibited even in Alaska ports. Every major cruise line follows U.S. federal law on board; Carnival lists marijuana/cannabis/CBD items first among prohibited items, Norwegian’s prohibited-items list expressly includes medical marijuana, including during transfers, in terminals, on shore excursions and at private islands (secondary summaries of cruise-line policies, Cruzely, accessed 2026-11-01 — https://www.cruzely.com/can-i-bring-marijuana-on-a-cruise/ ; CruisesNooze, accessed 2026-11-01). Consequences secondarily reported include confiscation, a $500 Carnival penalty example, fines, and removal from the ship. A cruise passenger may not bring marijuana back on board, and may not consume it on board, even if it was lawfully purchased in an Alaska port and even if the ship is docked.
  • In port, Alaska law applies on land: a passenger 21+ may lawfully purchase from a licensed store in Juneau, Ketchikan, Skagway, Sitka and other ports, and may lawfully consume only in a non-public place or a licensed onsite area. The Juneau visitor authority states the rule plainly: product “cannot be consumed in a public area, nor can it be taken aboard a cruise ship, commercial vehicle, boat or airplane” (Travel Juneau, “Cannabis Rules in Juneau, Alaska,” accessed 2026-11-01 — https://www.traveljuneau.com/discover-juneau/cannabis/).
  • Sitka as the worked example (secondary, Verify against the Sitka code): Sitka passed one of Alaska’s first local cannabis ordinances on 24 Feb 2015; it prohibits public consumption anywhere within city limits (street, parks, waterfront, docks). In 2025 the Sitka Assembly approved a code change (reported as passing 6–1, after first reading at 6–1 in Run 1’s terms) allowing licensed retailers to offer onsite consumption, driven in part by cruise-visitor demand — visitors with no private space were consuming illegally in public — with a consumption facility near the Sitka Cruise Terminal (adjacent to AKO Marijuana) expected to open in summer 2026 (The Joint Sitka FAQ, secondary, accessed 2026-11-01 — https://thejointsitka.com/faq.html). Whether that facility opened, and whether Sitka’s final ordinance matches the secondary summary, is Verify — do not present the summer-2026 opening as confirmed.
  • Air travel: marijuana may not be taken on a commercial aircraft (federal law; TSA/airline policy) and, for licensees, may not be transported as unaccompanied airline luggage/cargo (see §11.4.4). For consumers, the same federal prohibition applies; the “may not be taken aboard… an airplane” warning on the Juneau visitor page reflects that.
  • Ferries and boats: see Part 10 (Run 2) for the Alaska Marine Highway / federal-waters analysis; for consumers, the Juneau visitor page’s “commercial vehicle, boat” warning and the cruise-line policies above are the practical position, and no Alaska statute was located that authorises consumer possession on a ferry in federal waters — Verify for any specific sailing.
  • No official tourism-sales figure: no official figure for visitor/cruise-passenger cannabis sales was located (Run 1, Part 6, same position). Do not estimate a tourism share of Alaska sales.

12.5.4 Exterior-window / curbside pickup — not delivery

A licensed retail store may serve a consumer through a walk-up or drive-through exterior window under 3 AAC 306.380 (added 13 Aug 2023, am. 8 Dec 2024), unless prohibited by local law, provided: an operating plan/modification depicting the window area and its video surveillance is board-approved; the area immediately outside the window is under the licensee’s sole possession/control and includes no public property (streets, sidewalks, public parking lots); internet/telephone orders must be paid for and picked up in person by the consumer placing the order; all window orders must be placed by the consumer from a menu; no marijuana/product may be displayed at the window; before completing a drive-through sale the licensee/employee must physically view and inspect the consumer’s photographic identification, and each person in a vehicle at the drive-through window must be 21+ unless the person is the consumer’s own child, grandchild or ward not older than seven seated as required by law in a car/booster seat; all sales occur within the licensed premises (satisfied by electronic/internet payment, mobile point-of-sale, or payment transferred through the window into the store interior); the window area must be video-recorded and records stored under 306.720; and the window must not be in a separate building, a movable structure/trailer, or accessible only through another licence type’s premises or overlapping premises. This is pickup, not delivery: the consumer still comes to the store. The Covid-era curbside rules (former 306.995) are repealed; 306.380 is the current route.

12.6 Landlord and tenant rules

No cannabis-specific landlord/tenant statute was located in AS 17.38 or 3 AAC 306. The position is therefore governed by general Alaska landlord/tenant law (AS 34.03, the Uniform Residential Landlord and Tenant Act as adopted in Alaska) plus the lease itself, plus the local-option and licensing rules for commercial premises:

  • Residential: a landlord may prohibit smoking (including marijuana smoking) and cultivation in the leased premises by lease term, and may treat a violation as a lease breach. Secondary lease forms for Alaska routinely include a smoking policy (permitted only in stated areas, or prohibited on the premises and common areas) and define “smoking” to include pipes, cigars, electronic vaporising/aerosol devices and other devices for inhaling tobacco, marijuana or similar substances, with damage from smoking (deodorising, sealing/painting, carpet/pad replacement) chargeable against the deposit as not ordinary wear and tear. A tenant’s lawful home grow under AS 17.38.020 does not override a lease prohibition — the statute makes the grow lawful under state criminal/civil law, not immune from a private lease term. Eviction for a lease violation follows AS 34.03 procedures; no cannabis-specific eviction protection was located. Whether a landlord may prohibit non-smoking medical use (e.g. edibles) by a registered patient in a way that engages disability/housing law is Verify — no Alaska case or statute on that point was located.
  • No disclosure duty located: secondary landlord-disclosure summaries state that Alaska does not require disclosure of a marijuana-use policy in a lease (disclosure “not required but still recommended”). That is secondary-only — Verify against AS 34.03 before advising a landlord that no disclosure is needed.
  • Commercial (licensed premises): a licensed establishment must prove a right to possession of the licensed premises (306.020(b), 306.035(b)(4) on request on renewal). The 2026 board transfer packets show the commercial reality: a lease for a licensed retail premises (Anchorage, 3231 Spenard Road, Unit 1) makes loss/revocation/suspension/formal cancellation of required state or municipal cannabis licences an event of default, with termination on uncured default and AMCO enforcement notice. A commercial landlord is not a licensee by virtue of leasing to a licensee, but any direct/indirect financial interest in the licensed business brings the landlord within the 306.015(e)(1) financial-interest disclosure regime — Verify the lease structure before assuming a landlord is outside the licensing regime.
  • Public housing / federally assisted housing: federal law (including the federal public-housing marijuana rules) is not displaced by Alaska law; a tenant in federally assisted housing may face federal consequences for marijuana use/possession even where Alaska law permits it. No Alaska-specific public-housing cannabis rule was located — Verify for any specific housing authority.
  • Local-option overlay: a commercial lease in a jurisdiction that later adopts a local-option ban does not save the licence — a licence in a banned area is void 90 days after the election/ordinance takes effect (3 AAC 306.250, summarised in the 29 May 2025 text at p. 33), and the lease does not create a right to operate.

12.7 Gun ownership conflicts

Federal law is the operative conflict; Alaska law does not resolve it.

  • 18 U.S.C. § 922(g)(3) makes it unlawful for any person who is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance to ship, transport, possess or receive firearms or ammunition. Because adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I federally (and, on the given baseline, no Alaska commercial activity clearly counts as state-licensed medical Schedule III activity), a person who uses marijuana in Alaska is, for federal firearms purposes, an unlawful user of a controlled substance, even though the use is lawful under Alaska law. The ATF’s 2011 open letter to federal firearms licensees directs dealers not to transfer firearms to a person where there is reasonable cause to believe the person is an unlawful user, including where the person holds a medical marijuana registry card, and ATF Form 4473 asks (Question 21e/21f in different form versions) whether the applicant is an unlawful user of or addicted to marijuana or any other controlled substance, with a warning that marijuana use/possession remains unlawful under federal law regardless of state legalisation. Answering falsely is a separate federal felony (18 U.S.C. § 1001).
  • Wilson v. Lynch (9th Cir. 2016): the Ninth Circuit — whose jurisdiction includes Alaska — upheld the federal ban as applied to medical marijuana cardholders against a Second Amendment challenge, holding that the government could rely on a card as reasonable cause to believe the holder uses marijuana. That decision is binding in the Ninth Circuit unless and until overruled.
  • Hemani (2026) — Verify against the slip opinion (no holding, date or scope adopted here): secondary sources in 2026 describe a Supreme Court decision in United States v. Hemani (2026) narrowing § 922(g)(3) — one secondary source describes it as a unanimous decision that occasional marijuana use alone does not authorise the federal government to infringe Second Amendment rights, with scope Verify against the slip opinion; another secondary source, from before the decision, describes Hemani as argued 2 Mar 2026 with decision pending. These secondary accounts conflict on the status and scope of Hemani and were not verified against the slip opinion in this run — Verify Hemani’s holding, date and scope before advising any person that the federal ban no longer applies to them. The safe statement at cut-off is: § 922(g)(3) remains on the books, Wilson v. Lynch remains Ninth Circuit law, and any narrowing from Hemani is Verify against the slip opinion as to scope (ATF interim-rule claim deleted). The given federal baseline (Schedule III for state-licensed medical only, adult-use Schedule I) does not, by itself, remove the § 922(g)(3) problem for Alaska adult-use consumers, because their use is adult-use, not state-licensed medical on the Run 1 analysis.
  • Alaska state law: no Alaska statute was located that protects a marijuana user (medical or adult-use) from the federal firearms disability, or that creates a state firearms disability for marijuana users beyond federal law. Secondary claims that “Alaska prohibits gun ownership by medical cannabis patients,” that “obtaining a medical card invalidates a firearm licence,” or that a patient whose card has expired “is free to obtain a firearm licence” (AlaskaStateCannabis.org, undated) conflate federal disability with a supposed Alaska disability and were not verified against any Alaska statute — do not repeat them as Alaska law. Alaska’s permitless-carry law (AS 11.61.220, AS 18.65.700 et seq.) was not shown to contain a marijuana-user disqualifier in this run — Verify. Spouses/household members who do not themselves use marijuana are not disabled by another person’s use, on the federal statute’s individual-user terms.
  • Practical advice boundary: this section is legal information, not advice to any individual about possessing a firearm while using marijuana. Any person in that position should obtain advice on the current federal position, including Hemani and any ATF guidance, before acquiring or retaining a firearm.

12.8 Pet and veterinary products

No Alaska veterinary cannabis rule was located in AS 17.38, AS 17.37, 3 AAC 306 or 11 AAC 40. The position is therefore:

  • Marijuana for animals is not authorised by the medical or adult-use chapters. AS 17.37 (medical) authorises use by a qualifying patient (a human with a debilitating medical condition, certified by a physician); it does not authorise veterinary prescription or administration of marijuana to an animal. AS 17.38 (adult-use) authorises use by persons 21+; it does not authorise administration to animals. A veterinarian who prescribed or dispensed marijuana for an animal would be outside both chapters and would face federal controlled-substance and Alaska veterinary-practice issues — Verify against the Alaska Board of Veterinary Examiners’ position, as no board guidance was located.
  • Hemp-derived CBD for pets is in the general hemp grey area, not a veterinary authorisation. Secondary sources list Alaska among states where hemp-derived CBD is “completely legal” for pets (PawCBD, undated secondary summary), while the American Veterinary Medical Association’s position, quoted secondarily, is that “State laws legalizing use in people do not apply to cannabis use in animals.” The FDA has not approved CBD for animal use (with the narrow exception of the human drug Epidiolex, not a veterinary product). Alaska’s hemp programme (11 AAC 40) endorses no consumable product containing delta-9 THC and no delta-8/THCA/THCV/THCP/HHC/synthetic product (Run 1), but a compliant hemp CBD product (delta-9-removed broad-spectrum/isolate) sold through the hemp channel is not, by that fact alone, a veterinary medicine. No Alaska rule was located that either authorises or specifically prohibits a veterinarian from discussing hemp CBD with a pet owner, or that sets a pet-CBD labelling, dosing or testing standard — Unavailable. A secondary directory claim that “cannabis products for pet care or veterinary treatments are not legally available for purchase in Homer” (LeafyThings, Homer directory, undated) is a local-directory assertion, not a statute, and is not adopted as law.
  • Veterinary PDMP point (not a cannabis authorisation): Alaska’s controlled-substance prescription database (PDMP, AS 17.30.200) originally required veterinarians to register/report; HB 56 (33rd Legislature, enrolled) exempted veterinarians from PDMP requirements for Schedule II–IV substances prescribed/dispensed to treat an animal (AS 17.30.200(v) as added). That exemption concerns the PDMP, not marijuana, and marijuana (Schedule I adult-use / Schedule VIA state) is not a Schedule II–IV veterinary prescription in any event. Do not cite HB 56 as a veterinary cannabis authorisation.
  • Poisoning / accidental ingestion: no Alaska-specific pet-poisoning reporting rule was located; the general veterinary and poison-control routes apply. Owners should be warned, as a practical matter, that THC is toxic to dogs/cats at sufficient doses — that is a veterinary-medicine fact, not an Alaska rule, and no Alaska dose threshold was located.

12.9 Medical marijuana in schools

The rule is a non-accommodation rule, not an administration programme.

AS 17.37.040(d) (restrictions on medical use):

“(d) Nothing in this chapter requires any accommodation of any medical use of marijuana (1) in any place of employment; (2) in any correctional facility, medical facility, or facility monitored by the department or the Department of Administration; (3) on or within 500 feet of school grounds; (4) at or within 500 feet of a recreation or youth center; or (5) on a school bus.”

AS 17.37.040(a) further provides that a patient, primary caregiver or alternate caregiver may not engage in medical use in a way that endangers any person’s health or well-being, or in plain view of or in a place open to the general public (possession in a public place is not thereby prohibited if within the chapter’s possession limits), and a patient found by a preponderance of the evidence to have knowingly violated the chapter is precluded from obtaining or using a registry card for one year (306.040(b) — actually AS 17.37.040(b); citation corrected here).

Consequences:

  • No school is required to allow medical marijuana on school grounds, within 500 feet of school grounds, or on a school bus, and no school employee is required to administer it. Alaska has no “school administration of medical marijuana” statute of the kind enacted in some other states (e.g. laws permitting a parent/caregiver to administer non-smokable medical cannabis on school property). No Alaska school-district medical-marijuana policy was located in this run — Verify for any specific district (Anchorage, Fairbanks, Juneau, Mat-Su) before advising a family, as districts may have their own policies within the non-accommodation rule.
  • Minors as patients: a qualifying patient who is a minor requires a parent/legal guardian’s written consent and agreement to serve and control dosage (AS 17.37.030 as summarised in secondary sources — the current text of 17.37.030 was not directly opened in this run, Verify). Even for a minor patient, the school-grounds/bus non-accommodation rule in 17.37.040(d)(3) and (5) applies — the chapter does not create a right to use at school.
  • Adult-use at school: AS 17.38 does not permit consumption in public, and a school is a public place for this purpose; the 500-foot buffer for licensed establishments (3 AAC 306.010(a)) is a separate licensing buffer, not a student-use rule. Possession by a person under 21 remains unlawful under AS 17.38 (see Run 1, Part 1) and under school disciplinary codes, which are outside this report’s scope.
  • Hemp/CBD at school: no Alaska rule was located that specifically addresses hemp-derived CBD (non-intoxicating) possession or administration at school — Unavailable. Do not assume the medical-marijuana non-accommodation rule does or does not cover a compliant hemp CBD product; the school’s own policy and the product’s THC status would both matter, and neither was resolved in this run.

Conflicting figures / choices made in this report

  1. Renewal licence fees: pre-2025 schedule ($7,000/$1,400 etc., Run 1) vs 29 May 2025 text ($5,000/$1,000, plus $600 renewal application fee and $1,000 late fee). Choice: the 29 May 2025 text governs; the older schedule is superseded.
  2. Handler-permit fee: Run 1 carried $50 as secondary. Choice: confirmed by 306.100(e) ($50 new and renewed).
  3. Onsite endorsement fees: Run 1 carried $1,000 + $2,000 as secondary summaries. Choice: confirmed in structure — $1,000 new application, $600 renewal application, $2,000 annual endorsement fee (306.100(a), (b), (d)(7)).
  4. Onsite per-person limits: older 306.365 excerpt (1 g / 10 mg / 0.25 g per transaction) vs current 306.370 (1 g / 25 mg / 0.3 g per day). Choice: 306.370 (29 May 2025 text) governs.
  5. Giveaways: older 306.310 text and the current MJ-03 form (free-product ban) vs current 306.310 as amended in 2023 (ban removed). Choice: the current regulation text governs; the form lag is flagged, not resolved.
  6. Waste notice period: DEC’s cannabis-waste summary (three-day board notice) vs 306.740(c)(1) repealed 16 Oct 2022. Choice: the repeal governs; no three-day notice in current law.
  7. Fingerprint fee: $48.25 (superseded) vs $50.00 — unresolved, Verify (see Verify #9).
  8. PCE figures: base rate 19.92 cents (prior year) vs 20.38 cents (FY2026); community-facility 70 kWh vs 500 kWh — unresolved in part, Verify (see Verify #35). The “commercial customers receive no PCE subsidy” point is not in conflict.
  9. Firearms / Hemani: secondary sources conflict on whether Hemani was decided and what it held — unresolved, Verify (see Verify #31).
  10. HB 239 law-without-signature date: approximately 18 June 2026 vs 18 June 2026 House Journal message — unresolved, Verify (see Verify #24).
  11. Retail cap wording: “per person per day” vs “per transaction” — unresolved in wording, Verify (see Verify #36); the numeric caps (1 oz / 7 g / 5,600 mg) are not in conflict.
